Dig Deeper: Matthew — Whole-Book Sweep

Mode: Multi-Passage Sweep. Fifty-six pericopes, Matthew 1:1–28:20, following the book overview’s 56 preaching units exactly. Primary text (observation): SBLGNT with the MorphGNT word index, _texts/greek-nt-sblgnt/01-Matt.txt and _index/01-Matt.tsv. It has 1,068 verses; 17:21, 18:11 and 23:14 are not in the text. Each unit was read in Greek before any English. Citation of record: NA28 text and apparatus, _texts/logos-exports/02-New-Testament/01-Matthew-NA28.txt and 01-Matthew-NA28-apparatus.txt. Greek OT: Swete, _texts/greek-lxx-swete/ (observation). The Rahlfs–Hanhart text exports in _texts/logos-exports/ were used wherever a Greek OT wording carries weight. There is no Rahlfs apparatus in the folder. Hebrew OT: WLC, _texts/hebrew-wlc/, checked with tools/find.py; BHS text and apparatus exports where a reading is cited. Study text: NASB95, 01-Matthew-NASB95.txt. Pulpit text: ESV, declared for the series. The export is Crossway’s 2025 US text, not the ESV Anglicised. Book-overview context: Matthew/book-overview-matthew.md, Draft v0.2 (1 October 2026), upgraded from the claim audit Matthew/dig-deeper-matthew-claim-audit.md. Earlier sweep: Matthew/dig-deeper-matthew-whole-book.md (16 July 2026). It predates the current method and is left untouched. It was opened only at the checking stage (see Checking Stage). Date: 2 October 2026 Post-audit note: the sweep’s candidates and its three structural claims were tested the same day by independent auditors, in Matthew/dig-deeper-matthew-claim-audit-2.md. Where they differ, the audit supersedes this report. In particular, Headline 1, Convergent 1 and Pitfall 1 are reduced to a few deliberate links: 6:10 → 26:42; 5:39 → 26:67, with Isa 50:6; and 26:52. The lexical distribution is no greater than chance, and four of the chains are inherited from Mark, so the design reading is low–moderate. Convergent 3’s shepherd design is moderate, with Ezekiel 34 as its hub. ἀπὸ τότε (“from then on”) at 26:16 is an echo, not a third hinge. For the ratings of the Tensions §8 rows, read the audit’s table.

How to Read This Sweep

This is planning material, not pulpit preparation. Fifty-six pericopes worked at proportional depth cannot each get what a solo run gives one passage. Four consequences govern everything below.

Every cross-passage claim is synthetic and capped at moderate confidence until a claim audit has tested it. The exception is a chain verified by lemma or phrase against the SBLGNT index; those say verified. Treat everything else as a candidate.

For any ⭐ pericope, run a full solo dig before the pulpit. Sweep depth is enough to plan a series and to test an overview. It is not enough to preach from.

Each pericope is worked in the same order:

  1. Its position in the book (the two Phase 0.6 questions).
  2. Its structure.
  3. The text-first findings.
  4. A Synoptic check.
  5. The Old Testament citations (Tool 11). The Citation Triad is applied to every direct quotation; allusions get a lighter treatment.
  6. The internal echoes (Move 4).
  7. The translations, with a pulpit-divergence line.
  8. A Difficulty and a Pitfall where either is live.
  9. A note on what the pericope does to the overview.

The units are not of even depth, and you should know why. Units 1–14, 17–20, 25–28, 33–36, 38, 41–45, 49 and 50 were worked at full sweep depth. The other twenty-two (15–16, 21–24, 29–32, 37, 39–40, 46–48, 51–56) were finished more briefly, so that the run could be completed at reasonable cost. They keep the same order of work and the same checking of counts, but they have fewer findings, and they apply the full source-context treatment only to direct quotations. Unit 37 is the shorter version: a fuller draft was overwritten by mistake during the run. Three of the briefer units carry the passion (51–56), so a solo dig there matters more than usual.

Warrant tags.

  • [T]: derivable from the text on the page.
  • [I]: a reasonable inference from it.
  • [S]: supplied by a secondary source and held provisionally.
  • [S: audit]: settled by the claim audit and not re-derived.

Counts name their edition, because a count belongs to a text, not to a book. Greek and Hebrew stand in their own script with an English gloss on every occurrence.

One independence caveat, stated plainly. The overview this sweep consumes was drafted in this project yesterday, by the same model. A sweep that re-reads its own overview can only agree with it, unless it deliberately works from the Greek first. Every unit below was read in the SBLGNT before the overview’s rows for it were consulted. Where the sweep agrees with the overview, it says confirmed. Where it adds or disagrees, the point is collected in Book-Overview Tensions.

Headline Findings

Five book-level findings that emerged from several units converging. Each was verified against the corpus rather than recalled.

1. The passion enacts the Sermon on the Mount against its preacher, word for word. A cluster of words occurs in the Sermon (or the mission discourse) and then not again until the night of the trial. All counts are SBLGNT, verified by lemma.

  • ἔνοχος (“liable, guilty”): only 5:21–22 and 26:66. The one who taught that anger makes a man “guilty before the court” is declared guilty by the court.
  • ῥαπίζω (“to slap”): only 5:39 and 26:67.
  • ὀμνύω (“to swear”): “do not swear at all” (5:34) returns in Peter’s oath (26:74).
  • ἀρνέομαι (“to deny”): only 10:33 and 26:70, 72.
  • ψευδομαρτυρία (“false testimony”): only 15:19 and 26:59.
  • πειρασμός (“temptation”): only 6:13 and 26:41.
  • ῥύομαι (“to deliver”): only 6:13 and 27:43.
  • γενηθήτω τὸ θέλημά σου (“your will be done”): 6:10 and 26:42.

The prayer Jesus taught is the prayer he prays in Gethsemane. The conduct he forbade is the conduct used against him. [T] for every chain; the design reading is [I], moderate–high (units 10, 11, 20, 30, 51, 52).

2. The infancy and the temptation are answered in the passion. Again, the counts are SBLGNT and verified.

  • ὄναρ (“dream”): six times. Five dreams protect the child (1:20–2:22). The sixth, given to Pilate’s wife, declares the man righteous (27:19).
  • Ναζωραῖος (“Nazarene”): only 2:23 and 26:71.
  • “King of the Jews”: only in Gentile mouths, at 2:2 and 27:11, 29, 37.
  • χαρὰ μεγάλη (“great joy”): only 2:10 and 28:8.
  • ἡ ἁγία πόλις (“the holy city”): only 4:5 and 27:53.
  • The devil’s εἰ υἱὸς εἶ τοῦ θεοῦ (“if You are the Son of God”, 4:3, 6) is said again at the cross (27:40), and only Matthew has it there.

The book’s first and last acts are built on one frame. [T]/[I], high (units 2–4, 6, 52–55).

3. Wherever Swete and Rahlfs divide, Matthew stands with Rahlfs, and he often stands nearer the Hebrew than either. The sweep found five places where the two Greek OT editions differ in a verse Matthew uses. Matthew agrees with Rahlfs in all five:

  • Hos 6:6 καὶ οὐ θυσίαν (“and not sacrifice”, 9:13; 12:7); Swete has ἢ θυσίαν (“rather than sacrifice”).
  • Isa 7:14 ἕξει (“will be with child”, 1:23); Swete has λήμψεται (“will conceive”).
  • Isa 8:23 ὁδὸν θαλάσσης (“by the way of the sea”, 4:15).
  • Theodotion Dan 12:3 ἐκλάμψουσιν (“will shine forth”, 13:43).
  • Isa 29:13 (15:8). Matthew lacks Swete’s ἐν τῷ στόματι αὐτοῦ (“with its mouth”). This one is new in this sweep.

Where Matthew departs from both Greek texts, the departure is regularly towards the Hebrew:

  • the potter (27:9–10)
  • the key of David (16:19 ↔︎ Isa 22:22)
  • the last line of Mic 7:6 (10:36)
  • κατασκευάσει (“will prepare”, 11:10)
  • the Decalogue order (5:21, 27; 19:18)
  • Moses before Elijah (17:3)
  • Jer 31:15 and Hos 11:1 (2:15, 18)

Two cautions apply. Rahlfs is an eclectic text, and some of its readings may themselves have been drawn towards the New Testament in transmission [S]. And no LXX apparatus is in the folder. So the finding is about the two printed editions, not about the Greek text Matthew read (units 2, 4, 7, 10, 17, 20, 21, 26, 30, 33, 34, 53).

4. The mission’s restriction is lifted only at the end, and baptism falls silent until then.

  • Restriction. “The lost sheep of the house of Israel” occurs only at 10:6 and 15:24 [T].
  • “All the nations”. ἔθνος (“nation”) is first at 4:15 (“Galilee of the nations”) and last at 28:19. The phrase “all the nations” gathers at 24:9, 14 and 25:32 before 28:19, where it is in the case and wording of Dan 7:14 OG [T]/[S: audit].
  • Baptism. βαπτίζω (“to baptise”) occurs only in chapter 3 and at 28:19 (SBLGNT, verified). Between the Jordan and the mountain, no one in Matthew is baptised.
  • The disciples. Their first call is undone at the arrest: ἀφέντες (“leaving”) at 4:20, 22 and 26:56.
  • The women. The two disciple verbs, ἀκολουθέω (“to follow”) and διακονέω (“to serve”), are given to them at the cross (27:55). The διακονέω line runs 4:11 → 8:15 → 20:28 → 25:44 → 27:55, all SBLGNT.

(Units 5, 7, 15, 19, 31, 48, 51, 54, 56.)

5. Blood and silver carry the book’s verdict, and the forgiveness of sins is moved to the cup.

  • Blood. αἷμα (“blood”) runs from “all the righteous blood … upon you” (23:35) through the covenant blood (26:28) to five uses in 27:4–25 (SBLGNT, verified). λαός (“people”) runs from “he will save his people from their sins” (1:21) to “his blood on us” (27:25).
  • Forgiveness. Matthew does not give John’s baptism the words εἰς ἄφεσιν ἁμαρτιῶν (“for the forgiveness of sins”, Mark 1:4; Luke 3:3). ἄφεσις (“forgiveness”) occurs in Matthew only at 26:28, at the cup [T].
  • Pouring. ἐκχυννόμενον (“poured out”) joins 23:35 to 26:28 [T].
  • Silver. ἀργύριον (“silver”) bought the betrayal (26:15), the potter’s field (27:7) and the guards’ lie about the resurrection (28:12, 15) [T].

The overview’s Trap 3 corrective, which reads 27:25 with 1:21 and 26:28, is confirmed from the text and strengthened by these verbal links (units 5, 44, 50, 53, 55).

Book-Overview Active Layer (Phase 0.5)

The four threads were extracted from overview v0.2 before any pericope was worked, and held in peripheral vision.

Thread What is live across the book
Christological trajectory Origins. The son of David and of Abraham, and Immanuel (1:1, 23). Sonship. The obedient Son who is Israel’s story relived (2:15; 4:1–11), and the Servant (8:17; 12:18–21). Authority. The Lord of the Sabbath and one greater than the temple (12:6, 8). The Son of the living God (16:16). The one who comes on the clouds (26:64). The end. The one given all authority, who is “with you” to the end (28:18–20).
Intertextual map Isaiah (7–9, 29, 40, 42, 53, 56, 61). The Psalms (2, 8, 22, 69, 78, 110, 118). Daniel 7 and 12. Hosea (6:6, 11:1). Jeremiah (7, 31, and the crux at 27:9). Micah 5 and 7. Zechariah 9, 11 and 13. Exodus–Deuteronomy (the temptation, the antitheses, the covenant blood). The ten formula citations are concentrated in chapters 1–4. For Move 2, read Swete, Rahlfs and the WLC together.
Preaching traps (1) The Sermon as a new law or an impossible one. (2) The formula quotations as proof-texts of prediction. (3) 27:25 and chapter 23 turned against “the Jews”. (4) Matthew 24 as a timetable. (5) 16:18 as a confessional battleground. (6) The Commission as “Go”. (7) 25:31–46 as pure social ethics or pure missions.
Presenting situation A Scripture-literate, largely Jewish-Christian community in tension with the synagogue leadership, receiving Gentiles. Written after the event, “to this day” (27:8; 28:15). [I]: a reconstruction from the book’s own features, not a text statement.

Book-Level Positional Frame (Phase 0.6)

What the preceding movement set up. For a Gospel, the preceding movement is the canon it presupposes. Matthew’s first words, βίβλος γενέσεως (“book of the genesis”), are the words of Gen 2:4 and 5:1 (Swete) [T]. The genealogy (1:1–17) recalls the whole story from Abraham, through David and the exile, to the Christ. That story has been left with a promised son of David, a scattered people, and a promised presence of God with them [I], high.

Why the book exists here. It exists because Israel’s Messiah was rejected by Israel’s leaders, handed over to the Gentiles, crucified and raised. Matthew’s answer is twofold:

  • his fulfilment formulas, built on πληρόω (“to fulfil”), ten times in the narrator’s voice (1:22 to 27:9) [T];
  • the claim that the one rejected now has all authority and sends his disciples to all the nations (28:18–20) [T].

The sweep adds one strand. The book is just as concerned with whether the disciples will do what Jesus taught. It ends on τηρεῖν πάντα ὅσα ἐνετειλάμην (“to keep all that I commanded”, 28:20). The passion shows the Sermon’s teaching broken by every party except Jesus (Headline 1) [I], moderate–high.

Implication for every pericope. Read each passage for two things:

  • what it contributes to the question “who is this?” (8:27; 12:23; 16:13–16; 21:10; 26:63; 27:54);
  • what it teaches the disciple to keep.

Where a pericope seems self-contained, the positional question asks what it plants for the passion and the commission.

Book-level Genre (Tool 12). Narrative gospel, with five long discourses (5–7, 10, 13, 18, 24–25). Each discourse is closed by the formula καὶ ἐγένετο ὅτε ἐτέλεσεν ὁ Ἰησοῦς (“and when Jesus had finished”, 7:28; 11:1; 13:53; 19:1; 26:1) [T]. The narrative is more orderly than Mark’s. Matthew compresses the miracle stories and expands the teaching, and he groups material by theme: ten miracle stories in 8–9; seven parables in 13; seven woes in 23 [T]/[I].

Book-level Bible Timeline (Tool 14). The events stand at the hinge of the timeline. The promises to Abraham and David, and the end of the exile counted in the genealogy (1:17), are declared fulfilled. The Son of Man is crucified and raised. Three things are foretold but not narrated:

  • the gospel preached in the whole world (24:14; 26:13);
  • the coming of the Son of Man (24:30; 26:64);
  • the end of the age (28:20).

The reader stands between the commission and that end [T].

1. Matthew 1:1–17 — The Book of the Genesis ⭐

Position. Q1: nothing precedes it; the book’s first verse is its title. What it provides is the frame for everything after it: who this is (Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ (“Jesus Christ”)), whose son he is (υἱοῦ Δαυὶδ υἱοῦ Ἀβραάμ (“the son of David, the son of Abraham”)), and which story he finishes [T]. Q2: it must stand first because 1:18 cannot be told without it. The line breaks at 1:16 (ἐγεννήθη (“was born”), not ἐγέννησεν (“was the father of”)), and 1:18–25 exists to explain the break [T]. It also stands first because the book opens with the words that open Adam’s family history (Gen 5:1), so the whole Gospel is set as a new beginning inside the old story [I], high.

Structure. Title (1:1). Three descents of the line: Abraham to David (1:2–6a), David to the deportation (1:6b–11), the deportation to the Christ (1:12–16). Summary (1:17) [T]. 1:1 names the son of David first and the son of Abraham second; 1:2–16 then runs Abraham → David → the Christ, so the title is answered in reverse order [T]. The narrator’s own count, three times δεκατέσσαρες (“fourteen”), closes the unit (1:17) [T].

Text-first findings.

  • The first two words are Genesis. Βίβλος γενέσεως (“the record of the genealogy”, NASB95; “book of the genesis”) stands in Swete only at Gen 2:4 and Gen 5:1 [T], verified (phrase search; both returned). Gen 2:4 is the heavens and the earth; Gen 5:1 is Adam’s line, Hebrew זֶה סֵפֶר תּוֹלְדֹת אָדָם (“this is the book of the generations of Adam”), the only סֵפֶר תּוֹלְדֹת (“book of the generations”) in the WLC [T], verified. γένεσις (“genealogy, birth, genesis”) stands twice in Matthew (SBLGNT): 1:1 and 1:18 [T], verified. NA28 also reads γένεσις (“birth”) at 1:18; the majority text reads γέννησις (“birth”) there (NA28 apparatus) [T]. The heading and the birth story share one noun, and that noun is the title of the first book of the Bible. High.
  • “Jesus Christ” brackets the genealogy. Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ (“of Jesus Christ”) stands in Matthew only at 1:1 and 1:18 (SBLGNT and NA28 phrase search) [T], verified. Χριστός (“Christ, Messiah”) occurs in 16 verses of Matthew (SBLGNT); four of them fall in 1:1–18 (1:1, 16, 17, 18) [T], verified. The genealogy is the definition of the title before the title is used in the story (2:4) [I].
  • The chain and its one break. ἐγέννησεν (“was the father of”) occurs 39 times in 1:2–16 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. At 1:16 the active verb stops: Ἰωσὴφ τὸν ἄνδρα Μαρίας, ἐξ ἧς ἐγεννήθη Ἰησοῦς (“Joseph the husband of Mary, by whom Jesus was born”) [T]. The relative ἐξ ἧς (“by whom”) is feminine, so Jesus is born of Mary, not of Joseph [T]. The passive leaves the agent unnamed, and 1:18–25 names it: ἐκ πνεύματος ἁγίου (“by the Holy Spirit”, 1:18, 20) [T]. The form ἐγεννήθη (“was born”) occurs again in Matthew only at 26:24, of Judas: “it would have been good for that man if he had not been born” (SBLGNT) [T], verified; the link is verbal only (uncertain as design).
  • Four women, then Mary, then the Spirit. ἐκ τῆς (“by”, feminine) introduces Θαμάρ (“Tamar”, 1:3), Ῥαχάβ (“Rahab”, 1:5), Ῥούθ (“Ruth”, 1:5) and τῆς τοῦ Οὐρίου (“the [wife] of Uriah”, 1:6) [T]. ἐκ τῆς occurs at 1:3, 1:5 (twice) and 1:6, and elsewhere in Matthew only in unrelated uses (13:41; 15:18; 17:5; 24:17; SBLGNT) [T], verified. The fifth woman is introduced with ἐξ ἧς (“by whom”, 1:16), and the next ἐκ (“of, by”) of a parent is ἐκ πνεύματος ἁγίου (“by the Holy Spirit”, 1:18) [T]. Each of the four is irregular in the Tanak’s own telling: Tamar conceives by her father-in-law (Gen 38), Rahab is a πόρνη (“harlot”) of Jericho (Josh 2:1 Swete), Ruth is a Moabite (Ruth 1:4), and Bathsheba is left unnamed and defined by Uriah, a Hittite (2 Sam 11:3) [T] for each source. What they share as a group is [I]; the text shows only the pattern of irregular unions that ends in the unique one of 1:16–18 (moderate–high).
  • Uriah is named; Bathsheba is not. ἐκ τῆς τοῦ Οὐρίου (“by the [wife] of Uriah”, 1:6). The genealogy names the wronged husband, not the woman. This brings 2 Sam 11–12 into the line of David at the point where David is first called τὸν βασιλέα (“the king”, 1:6) [T]. τὸν βασιλέα (“the king”) is said of David alone in the list; the majority text adds ὁ βασιλεύς (“the king”) of Solomon too (NA28 apparatus, 1:6) [T]. The king is named as king in the verse that recalls his sin. Moderate–high.
  • The exile is the hinge. μετοικεσία (“deportation”) occurs four times in Matthew (SBLGNT), all in 1:11–17, and nowhere else in the New Testament [T], verified. The genealogy is turned on Babylon twice (1:11, 12) and the summary names it twice (1:17) [T]. Exile and its end are not named again in the book, but the next quotation with a place of mourning is Rachel at Ramah (2:18; Jer 31:15), whose context is the exile (see unit 4) [I].
  • Spellings that do not come from the Greek Bible. Ῥαχάβ (“Rahab”) is spelled so only here in the New Testament; Heb 11:31 and Jas 2:25 have Ῥαάβ, as does Swete Josh 2:1 [T], verified. Βόες (“Boaz”) is Matthew’s alone (1:5 twice); Luke 3:32 has Βόος, as do Swete Ruth 4:21 and 1 Chr 2:11–12 [T], verified. NA28 reads Ἀσάφ (“Asaph”, 1:7–8) and Ἀμώς (“Amos”, 1:10); the majority text reads Ἀσά (“Asa”) and Ἀμών (“Amon”) (NA28 apparatus) [T]. The list is not copied from one Greek source [I].
  • Gaps in the second fourteen. Between Ἰωράμ (“Joram”) and Ὀζίας (“Uzziah”, 1:8) the WLC of 1 Chr 3:11–12 has Ahaziah, Joash and Amaziah; between Josiah and Jeconiah (1:11) it has Jehoiakim (1 Chr 3:15–16) [T]. Swete 1 Chr 3:11 spells Ahaziah Ὀζειά (“Ozeia”), close to Matthew’s Ὀζίας (“Uzziah”); Rahlfs spells it Οχοζια (“Ochozia”) [T], checked in both. A scribal leap from one “Ozias” to the next would explain the omission of three kings, but this is [I], uncertain. The selection may equally serve the count of 1:17 [I].
  • Shealtiel, father of Zerubbabel. 1:12 reads Σαλαθιὴλ δὲ ἐγέννησεν τὸν Ζοροβαβέλ (“Shealtiel was the father of Zerubbabel”). WLC 1 Chr 3:19 reads וּבְנֵי פְדָיָה זְרֻבָּבֶל (“the sons of Pedaiah: Zerubbabel”); Swete and Rahlfs 1 Chr 3:19 both read υἱοὶ Σαλαθιήλ· Ζοροβάβελ (“the sons of Shealtiel: Zerubbabel”) [T]. Matthew stands with the Greek of Chronicles here, and with Ezra 3:2 and Hag 1:1 (Zerubbabel “son of Shealtiel”), against the WLC of 1 Chr 3:19 [T]. Luke 3:27 has the same pair [T].

Synoptic check. Mark has no genealogy and no birth narrative. Luke’s genealogy (Luke 3:23–38) stands after the baptism, not before the birth. It ascends from Jesus to τοῦ Ἀδὰμ τοῦ θεοῦ (“of Adam, of God”, Luke 3:38), runs from David through Nathan (Luke 3:31), names no women, and qualifies the sonship ὡς ἐνομίζετο (“as was supposed”, Luke 3:23) [T]. Matthew descends, stops at Abraham, runs through Solomon and the kings, names four women and Mary, and makes the qualification by grammar (ἐξ ἧς (“by whom”) and the passive, 1:16) [T]. Both lists meet at Σαλαθιήλ (“Shealtiel”) and Ζοροβαβέλ (“Zerubbabel”) (Luke 3:27) [T]. Matthew’s Adam is present only by allusion, in the words of Gen 5:1 (1:1) [I].

Tool 11.

Gen 2:4; 5:1 → 1:1 (high — exact two-word phrase, unique to these two places in Swete). Source context: Gen 2:4 closes the seven days and opens the garden; Gen 5:1 opens the line from Adam to Noah with the reminder that God made man בִּדְמוּת אֱלֹהִים (“in the likeness of God”), κατ’ εἰκόνα θεοῦ (“according to the image of God”, Swete) [T]. Both mark a new movement inside the story of creation. Book usage: the book’s first words; no earlier use. OT-to-OT: the plene spelling תּוֹלְדוֹת (“generations”), with both vowel letters and no suffix, stands in the WLC only at Gen 2:4 and Ruth 4:18 [T], verified (surface search), and Ruth 4:18 opens the line of Perez to David that Matthew follows in 1:3–6. Swete Ruth 4:18 renders it αὗται αἱ γενέσεις Φάρες (“these are the generations of Perez”) [T]. A rabbinic reading joins Gen 2:4 and Ruth 4:18 on that spelling and sees the Messiah in Perez’s line (Gen. Rab. 12:6) [S], to be verified in Logos. Divergence: none; Swete and Rahlfs agree (Rahlfs Gen 2:4; 5:1 checked) [T]. Matthew stands with the Greek (the Hebrew of Gen 2:4 has no סֵפֶר (“book”)), but Gen 5:1 matches both. What it adds: Jesus’ genealogy is placed where Adam’s was. The book opens as a second Genesis, and the man of 1:16 is set beside the man made in God’s image [I], high.

Ruth 4:18–22; 1 Chr 2:4–15; 3:10–19 → 1:3–6, 7–12 (high — the line followed name by name). Source context: Ruth closes with the line from Perez to David, after the kinsman-redeemer has taken the Moabitess (Ruth 4:13–17). 1 Chr 2:4 names Tamar as mother of Perez and Zerah, Θαμὰρ ἡ νύμφη αὐτοῦ (“Tamar his daughter-in-law”, Swete) [T]. 1 Chr 3:10–19 runs the kings from Solomon to Jeconiah and the exile [T]. Book usage: the first use; Chronicles returns at 23:35 (2 Chr 24:20–22) [S: audit]. OT-to-OT: Ruth 4:18–22 and 1 Chr 2:9–15 give the same line; Chronicles adds the kings. Ruth’s line, with its Genesis heading (see above), is already a reading of David’s origin through a foreign woman [I]. Divergence: Hebrew רָם (“Ram”, Ruth 4:19; 1 Chr 2:9); Swete Ruth 4:19 and Rahlfs Ruth 4:19 Ἀρράν (“Arran”); Swete 1 Chr 2:9 ends καὶ Ἀράμ (“and Aram”); Matthew Ἀράμ (“Aram”, 1:3–4) [T]. Shealtiel → Zerubbabel follows the Greek of 1 Chr 3:19 against the WLC (above). Nearest: the Greek of Chronicles, but not slavishly (Ῥαχάβ, Βόες) [T]/[I]. What it adds: the genealogy is read out of the last book of the Tanak and its four-chapter preface to Israel’s history, with Ruth’s ending set into it [I].

Gen 38 → 1:3 (moderate–high — named figure). Move 1: Tamar, twice widowed and refused Judah’s third son, sits veiled at Enaim, and Judah says צָדְקָה מִמֶּנִּי (“she is more righteous than I”, Gen 38:26) [T]. Gen 38 gives no ethnicity for her; Judah’s own wife is the Canaanite (Gen 38:2) [T].

Josh 2; 6:25 → 1:5 (moderate–high — named figure). Move 1: the harlot of Jericho who hides the spies, confesses the LORD, and lives “in the midst of Israel” (Josh 6:25) [T]. Her marriage to Salmon is not in the Tanak [T].

2 Sam 11–12 → 1:6 (moderate–high — named figure). Move 1: David takes Uriah’s wife and has Uriah killed. Nathan’s verdict, “you are the man”, and the death of the first child follow; Solomon is born afterwards and named יְדִידְיָה (“beloved of the LORD”, 2 Sam 12:25) [T].

Internal: Plants §1:18–25 — γένεσις (“birth”) and Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ (“of Jesus Christ”) at 1:1 and 1:18 only (high, verified). Plants §1:20; 9:27 → 21:15; 22:41–45 — υἱὸς Δαυίδ (“son of David”); see the overview’s echo table (high). Plants §3:9; 8:11; 28:19 — Ἀβραάμ (“Abraham”), in 6 verses of Matthew (SBLGNT: 1:1, 2, 17; 3:9; 8:11; 22:32), verified; “children to Abraham from these stones” (3:9) is the next use (moderate–high). Plants §15:22 — the Canaanite woman (moderate; Rahab is the only woman of the four called a Canaanite by her story). Plants §11:16; 12:39–45; 23:36; 24:34 — γενεά (“generation”) stands four times in 1:17 and then only as “this generation” under judgement (13 occurrences in 10 verses, SBLGNT, verified) [T]; synthetic, moderate. Plants §2:2; 27:11, 37 — τὸν βασιλέα (“the king”) is David’s title at 1:6; the next “king” is the question of 2:2 (moderate).

Translations. NASB95 renders Χριστός (“Christ”) as “the Messiah” at 1:1, 16 and 17; the ESV has “Christ” and “the Christ” [T], checked. NASB95 1:6 supplies “Bathsheba who had been the wife of Uriah”; the ESV’s “the wife of Uriah” keeps the Greek’s silence about her name [T]. NASB95 prints “Asa” and “Amon” (1:7–8, 10); the ESV prints “Asaph” and “Amos” (NA28) [T]. NASB95 “The record of the genealogy” hides βίβλος (“book”); the ESV keeps “The book of the genealogy”. Pulpit divergence: the congregation will hear “Asaph” and “Amos” among the kings, will not hear Bathsheba’s name (rightly), and will hear “Christ” where the NASB95 reader sees “Messiah”.

Difficulty. The count of fourteen. 1:2–6 gives fourteen names from Abraham to David inclusive. If David is not counted again, 1:6b–11 gives fourteen from Solomon to Jeconiah, and 1:12–16 gives thirteen from Shealtiel to Jesus [T]. Options: (a) David is counted twice, ending the first set and opening the second; (b) Jeconiah is counted twice, before and after the deportation; (c) Jesus and “the Christ” are counted as two (the summary says ἕως τοῦ χριστοῦ (“to the Christ”)); (d) the number is structural, and the Hebrew letters of דָּוִד (“David”) sum to fourteen (ד = 4, ו = 6, ד = 4) [S]. The text does not say which. Nothing in the unit depends on the arithmetic; what depends on it is the narrator’s own claim that history is ordered [T].

Pitfall. What it looks like: skipping the genealogy as a list, or preaching the four women as “Jesus welcomes sinners”. Why wrong: the list is the book’s argument, not its preface. It defines “Christ” before the story uses the word, and it bends at 1:16 so that the reader must ask how this son is born. The women are not chosen as moral examples. Tamar is “more righteous” than Judah, and the sin named at 1:6 is David’s, not Bathsheba’s. Corrective: preach 1:1–17 as Matthew’s answer to “who is this?”: the son of Abraham and David, the end of the exile, a new Genesis. Let the irregular births of the line lead to the birth that has no father in the list.

Book-overview note: Confirms Gen 2:4; 5:1 → 1:1 and the Chronicles/Ruth line, from the text first. Extends: (1) Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ (“of Jesus Christ”) at 1:1 and 1:18 only, an inclusio with γένεσις; (2) the plene תּוֹלְדוֹת (“generations”) at Gen 2:4 and Ruth 4:18 only (WLC), which joins the two OT sources of 1:1–6; (3) Shealtiel → Zerubbabel follows Swete and Rahlfs 1 Chr 3:19 against the WLC; (4) Swete’s Ὀζειά (“Ozeia”) for Ahaziah at 1 Chr 3:11 as a possible cause of the three-king gap (uncertain). Contradicts, in part: the echo table calls Tamar Canaanite “by the narrative of the Tanak” (Gen 38). Gen 38 gives no ethnicity for Tamar (WLC and Swete 38:6); only Judah’s wife is Canaanite (38:2). Rahab alone of the four is Canaanite by her story; that row should drop Tamar or move her to [I].

2. Matthew 1:18–25 — Jesus, Immanuel ⭐

Position. Q1: the genealogy broke its own pattern at 1:16. Mary is named with ἐξ ἧς (“by whom”), and Jesus ἐγεννήθη (“was born”) with no father as subject [T]. Q2: this unit exists to explain that break. It opens by repeating the title’s words, Τοῦ δὲ Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ ἡ γένεσις (“now the birth of Jesus Christ”, 1:18; compare 1:1), and it shows how a son not fathered by Joseph becomes the legal “son of David” through Joseph’s obedience: Joseph is addressed Ἰωσὴφ υἱὸς Δαυίδ (“Joseph, son of David”, 1:20), takes Mary, and names the child (1:25) [T]. It also gives the two names that the rest of the book will fill, Jesus and Immanuel [T].

Structure. The problem (1:18–19). The dream and the angel’s word (1:20–21). The narrator’s first fulfilment formula and citation (1:22–23). Joseph’s obedience (1:24–25) [T]. The naming formula frames the unit: καλέσεις τὸ ὄνομα αὐτοῦ Ἰησοῦν (“you shall call his name Jesus”, 1:21) and ἐκάλεσεν τὸ ὄνομα αὐτοῦ Ἰησοῦν (“he called his name Jesus”, 1:25), with καλέσουσιν τὸ ὄνομα αὐτοῦ Ἐμμανουήλ (“they shall call his name Immanuel”, 1:23) in the centre [T]. τὸ ὄνομα αὐτοῦ (“his name”) occurs in Matthew only at 1:21, 23 and 25 (SBLGNT) [T], verified.

Text-first findings.

  • The Spirit fills the gap the genealogy left. εὑρέθη ἐν γαστρὶ ἔχουσα ἐκ πνεύματος ἁγίου (“she was found to be with child by the Holy Spirit”, 1:18); τὸ γὰρ ἐν αὐτῇ γεννηθὲν ἐκ πνεύματός ἐστιν ἁγίου (“for the Child who has been conceived in her is of the Holy Spirit”, 1:20) [T]. The narrator tells the reader first; the angel tells Joseph second [T]. The preposition ἐκ (“by, of”) is the one that carried the mothers in 1:3–6 and Mary in 1:16 (ἐξ ἧς, “by whom”) [T]. The Holy Spirit is named next at the baptism, ἐν πνεύματι ἁγίῳ (“with the Holy Spirit”, 3:11), and last in the name of 28:19 [T].
  • Joseph is “righteous”, and the righteous thing is mercy. Ἰωσὴφ δὲ ὁ ἀνὴρ αὐτῆς, δίκαιος ὢν καὶ μὴ θέλων αὐτὴν δειγματίσαι (“Joseph her husband, being a righteous man and not wanting to disgrace her”, 1:19) [T]. He plans λάθρᾳ ἀπολῦσαι αὐτήν (“to send her away secretly”) [T]. δίκαιος (“righteous”) is the book’s first adjective of moral character; it occurs in 14 verses of Matthew (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The participles make his righteousness and his unwillingness to shame her one disposition, but the text does not say whether καί (“and”) is additive or concessive [I]. ἀπολύω (“to send away”) is the verb of divorce at 5:31–32 and 19:3–9 [T], verified (lemma, SBLGNT).
  • The name is explained. καλέσεις τὸ ὄνομα αὐτοῦ Ἰησοῦν, αὐτὸς γὰρ σώσει τὸν λαὸν αὐτοῦ ἀπὸ τῶν ἁμαρτιῶν αὐτῶν (“you shall call his name Jesus, for he will save his people from their sins”, 1:21) [T]. The γάρ (“for”) presupposes the Hebrew name יְהוֹשֻׁעַ (“Joshua”) / יֵשׁוּעַ (“Jeshua”), with יָשַׁע (“to save”) in it; the wordplay does not work in Greek [I], high. The emphatic αὐτός (“he himself”) puts the saving in the child, not in the people’s effort [T].
  • “His people” and “their sins” are both answered at the end. ἁμαρτία (“sin”) occurs in 7 verses of Matthew (SBLGNT): 1:21; 3:6; 9:2, 5, 6; 12:31; 26:28 [T], verified. ἄφεσις (“forgiveness”) occurs once, at 26:28, εἰς ἄφεσιν ἁμαρτιῶν (“for the forgiveness of sins”) [T], verified. Matthew does not attach the phrase to John’s baptism, where Mark has it (Mark 1:4; compare Matt 3:1–2), and adds it to the cup, where Mark lacks it (Mark 14:24) [T]. λαός (“people”) occurs in 14 verses (SBLGNT); its last uses are πᾶς ὁ λαός (“all the people”, 27:25), who take his blood on themselves, and the λαός to whom the guards’ lie is told (27:64) [T], verified. The saving “from their sins” of 1:21 is placed at the cup, and “his people” next stand at the cross saying “his blood be on us”. [T] for the wording, [I] for the irony; synthetic, moderate–high.
  • The first formula names the Lord as speaker. τοῦτο δὲ ὅλον γέγονεν ἵνα πληρωθῇ τὸ ῥηθὲν ὑπὸ κυρίου διὰ τοῦ προφήτου λέγοντος (“now all this took place to fulfil what was spoken by the Lord through the prophet”, 1:22) [T]. ὑπὸ κυρίου διὰ τοῦ προφήτου (“by the Lord through the prophet”) stands at 1:22 and 2:15 only (SBLGNT) [T], verified. Both are about the son (τέξεται υἱόν, “she will bear a son”, 1:23; τὸν υἱόν μου, “my son”, 2:15) [T]. Of the ten formulae, ἵνα πληρωθῇ τὸ ῥηθέν (“that what was spoken might be fulfilled”) stands at 1:22; 2:15; 4:14; 12:17; 21:4; ὅπως πληρωθῇ (“so that … might be fulfilled”) at 2:23; 8:17; 13:35; and τότε ἐπληρώθη (“then was fulfilled”) at 2:17; 27:9 (SBLGNT) [T], verified.
  • Immanuel is translated with Isaiah’s own Greek. ὅ ἐστιν μεθερμηνευόμενον Μεθ’ ἡμῶν ὁ θεός (“which translated means, ‘God with us’”, 1:23) [T]. μεθερμηνεύω (“to translate”) occurs once in Matthew (SBLGNT) [T], verified. μεθ’ ἡμῶν ὁ θεός (“God with us”) is the wording of Swete Isa 8:8 and 8:10 and of Rahlfs Isa 8:8 (Rahlfs 8:10 reads μεθ’ ἡμῶν κύριος ὁ θεός, “the Lord God is with us”) [T], checked. The gloss is not Matthew’s coinage; it is the refrain of the next chapter of Isaiah (see Tool 11).
  • Five dreams, and a sixth. κατ’ ὄναρ (“in a dream”, 1:20). ὄναρ (“dream”) occurs six times in the New Testament, all in Matthew: 1:20; 2:12, 13, 19, 22; 27:19 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The five of the prologue guide Joseph and the magi; the sixth is Pilate’s wife, who sends word μηδὲν σοὶ καὶ τῷ δικαίῳ ἐκείνῳ (“have nothing to do with that righteous man”, 27:19) [T]. The first dream is given to a δίκαιος (“righteous man”, 1:19) and the last is about one [T]; synthetic, moderate.
  • Joseph obeys without a word. ἐγερθεὶς δὲ ὁ Ἰωσὴφ ἀπὸ τοῦ ὕπνου ἐποίησεν ὡς προσέταξεν αὐτῷ ὁ ἄγγελος κυρίου (“Joseph awoke from his sleep and did as the angel of the Lord commanded him”, 1:24) [T]. Joseph never speaks in Matthew [T]. The pattern “angel speaks → Joseph rises → does exactly that” returns at 2:13–14 and 2:19–21 [T]. ἄγγελος κυρίου (“angel of the Lord”) stands at 1:20, 24; 2:13, 19, and at 28:2 as ἄγγελος γὰρ κυρίου (“for an angel of the Lord”) (SBLGNT) [T], verified: the angel of the birth is the angel of the tomb.
  • “Until.” οὐκ ἐγίνωσκεν αὐτὴν ἕως οὗ ἔτεκεν υἱόν (“he did not know her until she gave birth to a son”, 1:25) [T]. The clause guards the virginal conception up to the birth; what follows the birth is not the narrator’s subject here [T]/[I]. The majority text reads τὸν υἱὸν αὐτῆς τὸν πρωτότοκον (“her firstborn son”), assimilated to Luke 2:7 (NA28 apparatus) [T].

Synoptic check. Mark has no birth narrative. Luke’s annunciation is to Mary, not Joseph (Luke 1:26–38). Luke has the same naming formula, καὶ τέξῃ υἱόν, καὶ καλέσεις τὸ ὄνομα αὐτοῦ Ἰησοῦν (“you will bear a son, and you will call his name Jesus”, Luke 1:31), addressed to her [T]. Both make Mary a παρθένος (“virgin”) betrothed to Joseph of David’s house (Luke 1:27), and both ascribe the conception to the Holy Spirit (Luke 1:35) [T]. Matthew alone gives Joseph’s dilemma, the dream, the explanation of the name (“he will save his people from their sins”) and the citation of Isa 7:14 [T]. Luke explains no name and cites nothing here; Luke 1:35 ends in “Son of God” [T].

Tool 11.

Isa 7:14 (with 8:8, 10) → 1:23 (high — formula quotation). Source context: the Syro-Ephraimite crisis. Aram and Israel march on Jerusalem, and “the heart of the house of David” shakes (Isa 7:2). Ahaz refuses to ask for a sign. Isaiah turns on him: שִׁמְעוּ־נָא בֵּית דָּוִד (“hear now, house of David”, 7:13), Ἀκούσατε δή, οἶκος Δαυείδ (“hear now, house of David”, Swete) [T]. The sign is given to the house of David against its king’s unbelief. Before the child knows good from evil, the two hostile kings will be gone (7:15–16); but Assyria will flood Judah “up to the neck”, and the land is addressed as עִמָּנוּ אֵל (“Immanuel”, 8:8) [T]. The nations’ plans fail, כִּי עִמָּנוּ אֵל (“for God is with us”, 8:10) [T]. The unit then runs on through darkness to the light of 8:23–9:1 and the child of 9:5–6 on David’s throne [T]. The cited verse is the sign at the centre of a judgement that ends in a royal birth. Book usage: first quotation of the book; first use of Isaiah. Isaiah returns at 3:3 (40:3), 4:15–16 (8:23–9:1), 8:17 (53:4), 12:18–21 (42:1–4), 13:14–15 (6:9–10), 15:8–9 (29:13) and 21:13 (56:7), named six times [T] (overview inventory, after reading). Matthew follows Isaiah’s own order inside the prologue: Isa 7:14 at 1:23, then Isa 8:23–9:1 at 4:15–16 (see unit 7) [T]. OT-to-OT: Gen 16:11 (Ishmael) and Gen 17:19 (Isaac) carry the same birth formula, τέξεται/τέξῃ υἱόν, καὶ καλέσεις τὸ ὄνομα αὐτοῦ (“she will bear / you will bear a son, and you will call his name”). In Swete the clause υἱὸν καὶ καλέσεις τὸ ὄνομα αὐτοῦ (“a son, and you will call his name”) stands only at Gen 16:11, Gen 17:19 and Isa 7:14 [T], verified. Isaiah’s sign is already shaped as a patriarchal annunciation, and Gen 17:19 is to Abraham [I], moderate. Divergence: WLC הָעַלְמָה הָרָה (“the young woman [is/will be] pregnant”); Swete ἡ παρθένος ἐν γαστρὶ λήμψεται (“the virgin will conceive”); Rahlfs ἡ παρθένος ἐν γαστρὶ ἕξει (“the virgin will be with child”) [T]. Matthew reads ἕξει (“will be with child”), with Rahlfs against Swete: a Greek variant, not Matthew’s own change [T]. Both Greek texts read καλέσεις (“you will call”, singular); the WLC וְקָרָאת (“and she/you will call”). Matthew’s καλέσουσιν (“they will call”) is the NT’s own Greek, matching neither [T]; codex D and some Fathers assimilate it to καλέσεις (NA28 apparatus) [T]. παρθένος (“virgin”) for עַלְמָה (“young woman”) is the LXX’s choice, and Matthew takes it over; it is a translation difference with theological weight, to be handled from Logos, not settled here [S]. Nearest text: Rahlfs, with Matthew’s own verb at the end. What it adds: the sign to the unbelieving house of David is given to Joseph, “son of David”, who believes. “God with us” in Isaiah is a promise inside judgement, and the one so named is born into a line just traced through the exile (1:11–17) [I]. The plural “they will call” makes the naming the confession of a people [I], moderate.

Gen 16:11; 17:19 → 1:21 (moderate — the annunciation formula, type-scene). Move 1: in Gen 16:11 the angel of the LORD names Ishmael to Hagar in the wilderness; in Gen 17:19 God names Isaac to Abraham, with the covenant to be established with him [T]. Matthew’s ἄγγελος κυρίου (“angel of the Lord”) with τέξεται δὲ υἱὸν καὶ καλέσεις (“she will bear a son, and you shall call”) combines both [T].

Ps 130:8 (Swete 129:8) → 1:21 (uncertain–moderate). Move 1: וְהוּא יִפְדֶּה אֶת־יִשְׂרָאֵל מִכֹּל עֲוֺנֹתָיו (“and he will redeem Israel from all his iniquities”); Swete καὶ αὐτὸς λυτρώσεται τὸν Ἰσραὴλ ἐκ πασῶν τῶν ἀνομιῶν αὐτοῦ (“and he himself will redeem Israel from all his lawless deeds”) [T]. The shape matches (emphatic “he”, the people, “from” their sins); the verbs and nouns differ [T]. In the psalm the subject is the LORD [T].

Gen 28:16 → 1:24 (uncertain — new candidate). Move 1: ἀπὸ τοῦ ὕπνου (“from sleep”) stands in Swete only at Gen 28:16, where Jacob wakes from the Bethel dream and says, “Surely the LORD is in this place” [T], verified (Swete phrase search). In the New Testament it occurs only at Matt 1:24 and Acts 20:9 [T], verified. Joseph, son of Jacob (1:16), wakes from a dream about “God with us”; Gen 28:15 had just said ἰδοὺ ἐγὼ μετὰ σοῦ (“behold, I am with you”) [T]. The match is two words and a setting; flag for the lead, not for preaching [I].

Internal: Answers §1:1, 16 — γένεσις (“birth”) and Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ (“of Jesus Christ”) at 1:1 and 1:18 only (high, verified); the passive of 1:16 explained (high). Plants §26:28 — sins forgiven (high, verified). Plants §27:25 — “his people” (moderate–high, synthetic). Plants §28:20 — μεθ’ ἡμῶν (“with us”, 1:23) and μεθ’ ὑμῶν (“with you”, 28:20); μεθ’ ὑμῶν stands also at 17:17 and 26:29 (SBLGNT), verified; the book closes on the promise its first citation named (high). Plants §27:19 — dream and righteous man (moderate, synthetic). Plants §28:2 — the angel of the Lord (moderate–high, verified). Plants §2:15 — the Lord speaking through the prophet of the son (high, verified). Plants §5:31–32; 19:3–9 — ἀπολύω (“to send away, divorce”) (moderate).

Translations. NASB95 1:19 “a righteous man”; ESV “a just man”. The ESV breaks the link to δικαιοσύνη (“righteousness”, 3:15; 5:6, 10, 20; 6:1, 33; 21:32; SBLGNT, verified) for the hearer [T], checked. NASB95 “send her away secretly”; ESV “divorce her quietly”: the ESV makes audible that ἀπολύω (“to send away”) is the verb of 5:31–32 and 19:3–9 [T]. At 1:23 NASB95 “shall be with child” keeps ἐν γαστρὶ ἕξει (“will be with child”); ESV “shall conceive” renders the Swete wording, not Matthew’s [T]. At 1:25 NASB95 “kept her a virgin until” interprets; ESV “knew her not until” keeps the idiom [T]. Pulpit divergence: the congregation will hear Joseph called “just”, not “righteous”, and will hear “divorce”; the preacher should name δίκαιος (“righteous”) if the 5:20 thread is to be heard.

Difficulty. παρθένος (“virgin”) and עַלְמָה (“young woman”). Isaiah’s Hebrew uses a word for a young woman of marriageable age; both Greek texts render it παρθένος (“virgin”) [T]. Options: (a) the Hebrew already implies an unmarried virgin, and the LXX made it explicit; (b) Isaiah’s sign had a near fulfilment in his own day, and Matthew reads it as a pattern completed in Jesus, using the Greek word that fits the fulfilment; (c) the whole of Isa 7–9, with the child on David’s throne (9:5–6), is the fulfilment in view, not 7:14 alone [S]. Matthew’s own claim does not rest on the Hebrew word: he narrates the virginal conception first (1:18, 20) and cites the text second [T]. Take the lexical question to Logos (HALOT; Wildberger; Motyer) before preaching on the word.

Pitfall. What it looks like: preaching 1:23 as a single prediction fulfilled once, cut off from Isaiah 7–8, or preaching “God with us” as a general comfort. Why wrong: the sign in Isaiah is given to the faithless house of David inside a judgement, and “God with us” is the refrain of an invasion (8:8) before it is a defiant hope (8:10). Matthew sets the citation inside a story about a righteous man’s obedience and a name that means salvation “from their sins”. Corrective: preach the name Jesus (1:21) and the name Immanuel (1:23) together. He saves from sin, and in him God is present; let 26:28 and 28:20 show where the book takes both names.

Book-overview note: Confirms from the text: Rahlfs ἕξει (“will be with child”) against Swete λήμψεται (“will conceive”); καλέσουσιν (“they will call”) as Matthew’s own; μεθ’ ἡμῶν ὁ θεός (“God with us”) from Isa 8:8, 10; the 1:23 / 28:20 inclusio; and the 1:21 → 26:28 answer. Extends: (1) ὑπὸ κυρίου διὰ τοῦ προφήτου (“by the Lord through the prophet”) only at 1:22 and 2:15, both about the son; (2) the sixth ὄναρ (“dream”, 27:19) joins the first dream to the second δίκαιος (“righteous”) pair; (3) Rahlfs Isa 8:10 has κύριος ὁ θεός (“the Lord God”), so only Swete has the exact phrase twice; (4) ἄγγελος κυρίου (“angel of the Lord”) at the birth and at the tomb (28:2); (5) new uncertain candidate Gen 28:15–16 (ἀπὸ τοῦ ὕπνου, “from sleep”). Refines: the overview’s Gen 16:11; 17:19 row (moderate) can add that the clause stands in Swete only there and at Isa 7:14, verified.

3. Matthew 2:1–12 — The King of the Jews and the Magi

Position. Q1: 1:18–25 gave the child his names and placed him, through Joseph, in David’s house. It did not say where he was born [T]. Q2: this unit supplies the place (Βηθλέεμ (“Bethlehem”), 2:1, 5, 6, 8) and the title that the first half of the book did not use: βασιλεὺς τῶν Ἰουδαίων (“King of the Jews”, 2:2) [T]. It also sets the two responses the whole book will trace. Outsiders from the east come to worship; the king in Jerusalem, and “all Jerusalem with him”, are troubled, and the chief priests and scribes know the text but do not go [T]. Without this unit, 2:13–23 would have no hunted child and no reason for Egypt [T].

Structure. Arrival and question (2:1–2). Herod troubled; the council and the Scripture (2:3–6). Herod’s secret enquiry and false errand (2:7–8). The star, the house, worship and gifts (2:9–11). The dream and the other road (2:12) [T]. The verb προσκυνέω (“to worship”) binds it: the magi come προσκυνῆσαι (“to worship”, 2:2), Herod claims he will προσκυνήσω (“worship”, 2:8), and the magi προσεκύνησαν (“worshiped”, 2:11) [T].

Text-first findings.

  • The title is first asked by Gentiles. Ποῦ ἐστιν ὁ τεχθεὶς βασιλεὺς τῶν Ἰουδαίων; (“Where is He who has been born King of the Jews?”, 2:2) [T]. βασιλεὺς / βασιλεῦ / βασιλέα τῶν Ἰουδαίων (“King of the Jews”) stands next at 27:11 (Pilate), 27:29 (the soldiers’ Χαῖρε, “Hail”) and 27:37 (the charge over the cross) (SBLGNT phrase search, all three cases) [T], verified. The magi’s question at the birth is answered by the inscription at the death. High.
  • Two kings in one verse. ἐν ἡμέραις Ἡρῴδου τοῦ βασιλέως (“in the days of Herod the king”, 2:1) is set against ὁ τεχθεὶς βασιλεύς (“he who has been born King”, 2:2) [T]. Herod is called ὁ βασιλεύς (“the king”) at 2:1, 3, 9 [T]. The last royal title in the genealogy was David’s (1:6); the next is the child’s [T].
  • Worship, true and false. προσκυνέω (“to worship”) stands in 13 verses of Matthew (SBLGNT); the first three are 2:2, 8, 11 [T], verified. The next two are the devil’s demand and Jesus’ refusal (4:9, 10), and the last two are the risen Lord worshipped (28:9, 17) [T]. Herod’s ὅπως κἀγὼ ἐλθὼν προσκυνήσω αὐτῷ (“that I too may come and worship him”, 2:8) is the book’s first lie, and it uses the book’s key word for the right response to Jesus [T]/[I]. πεσόντες προσεκύνησαν (“they fell to the ground and worshiped”) at 2:11 is the posture the devil asks for at 4:9, πεσὼν προσκυνήσῃς (“fall down and worship”) [T].
  • “Secretly”, twice, and the two uses differ. λάθρᾳ (“secretly”) occurs twice in Matthew (SBLGNT): Joseph’s plan to spare Mary shame (1:19) and Herod’s secret summons of the magi (2:7) [T], verified. The righteous man’s secrecy protects; the king’s secrecy plots [I], moderate.
  • The scribes have the right answer and do nothing. The council is πάντας τοὺς ἀρχιερεῖς καὶ γραμματεῖς τοῦ λαοῦ (“all the chief priests and scribes of the people”, 2:4). Their answer is οὕτως γὰρ γέγραπται διὰ τοῦ προφήτου (“for this is what has been written by the prophet”, 2:5) [T]. This is the first of ten uses of γράφω (“to write”) in Matthew (SBLGNT: 2:5; 4:4, 6, 7, 10; 11:10; 21:13; 26:24, 31; 27:37) [T], verified. The first γέγραπται (“it is written”) is spoken by those who will plot his death (26:3–4), and the last use of the verb is the charge they secure (27:37) [T]; synthetic, moderate.
  • Bethlehem is no longer “little”. οὐδαμῶς ἐλαχίστη εἶ (“you are by no means least”, 2:6), where Micah has צָעִיר (“little”) and the Greek ὀλιγοστός (“few, least”) [T]. The inversion is Matthew’s own (see Tool 11). The ruler comes from the least place, and the place is made great by the ruler [I].
  • The “leaders” of Judah, and the “governor”. ἐν τοῖς ἡγεμόσιν Ἰούδα (“among the leaders of Judah”, 2:6) [T]. ἡγεμών (“leader, governor”) stands in 9 verses of Matthew (SBLGNT): 2:6; 10:18; then seven times of Pilate in 27–28 (27:2, 11, 14, 15, 21, 27; 28:14) [T], verified. The word that honours Bethlehem among Judah’s leaders becomes the title of the man who condemns the ruler from Bethlehem [I], uncertain–moderate as design.
  • Great joy. ἐχάρησαν χαρὰν μεγάλην σφόδρα (“they rejoiced exceedingly with great joy”, 2:10) [T]. χαρὰ μεγάλη (“great joy”) returns once, at the empty tomb: μετὰ φόβου καὶ χαρᾶς μεγάλης (“with fear and great joy”, 28:8) (SBLGNT phrase search) [T], verified. The joy at his birth and the joy at his rising are the book’s only “great joy” (moderate–high).
  • The road home. δι’ ἄλλης ὁδοῦ ἀνεχώρησαν (“they left by another way”, 2:12) [T]. ἀναχωρέω (“to withdraw, depart”) occurs in 10 verses of Matthew (SBLGNT); four are in chapter 2 (2:12, 13, 14, 22), and the verb is used again of Jesus’ withdrawal to Galilee (4:12) and before danger (12:15; 14:13; 15:21) [T], verified. In the prologue, withdrawal is how the child is kept alive [I].

Synoptic check. No Markan or Lukan parallel. Luke places the birth in Bethlehem too, πόλιν Δαυὶδ ἥτις καλεῖται Βηθλέεμ (“the city of David which is called Bethlehem”, Luke 2:4), but his visitors are shepherds of Israel, told by an angel (Luke 2:8–20) [T]. Luke cites no Scripture for Bethlehem; Matthew puts Micah in the mouth of Jerusalem’s council [T]. Both have “great joy” at the birth: Luke 2:10, χαρὰν μεγάλην (“great joy”), to the shepherds [T].

Tool 11.

Mic 5:1 (Hebrew; Swete and Rahlfs 5:2; English 5:2) + 2 Sam 5:2 → 2:6 (high — formula citation, with γέγραπται). Source context: Micah 4:9–5:5 moves from the siege of Zion and its humiliated judge, struck “on the cheek with a rod” (Mic 4:14 Hebrew), to a ruler from Bethlehem Ephrathah. מִמְּךָ לִי יֵצֵא לִהְיוֹת מוֹשֵׁל בְּיִשְׂרָאֵל (“from you one will go out for me to be ruler in Israel”), וּמוֹצָאֹתָיו מִקֶּדֶם מִימֵי עוֹלָם (“and his goings out are from of old, from days of eternity”, 5:1) [T]. Israel is given up “until the time when she who is in labour has borne” (5:2). Then וְעָמַד וְרָעָה בְּעֹז יְהוָה (“and he will stand and shepherd in the strength of the LORD”, 5:3), and he will be great עַד־אַפְסֵי־אָרֶץ (“to the ends of the earth”) [T]. The cited verse is a royal birth-promise set between a humiliated ruler and a shepherd-king whose greatness reaches the nations [T]. Book usage: first citation of the Twelve in Matthew. Micah returns at 10:35–36 (Mic 7:6) [S] (overview inventory, after reading); the struck cheek of Mic 4:14 stands behind 27:30 for the overview [S: audit]. OT-to-OT: Matthew ends the citation with ὅστις ποιμανεῖ τὸν λαόν μου τὸν Ἰσραήλ (“who will shepherd my people Israel”). σὺ ποιμανεῖς τὸν λαόν μου τὸν Ἰσραήλ (“you will shepherd my people Israel”) stands in Swete only at 2 Sam 5:2 and 1 Chr 11:2, the tribes’ words to David at his anointing over all Israel [T], verified (Swete phrase search; also present in Rahlfs Samuel and Chronicles). The two texts were already joined in the Hebrew: Micah’s ruler will רָעָה (“shepherd”, 5:3), and David was told אַתָּה תִרְעֶה אֶת־עַמִּי (“you will shepherd my people”, 2 Sam 5:2) [T]. Matthew completes Micah’s thought with the David text Micah echoes [I], moderate–high. Divergence: Matthew departs from both Greek texts and the Hebrew in four places. (1) γῆ Ἰούδα (“land of Judah”) for אֶפְרָתָה (“Ephrathah”), Swete and Rahlfs οἶκος (τοῦ) Ἐφράθα (“house of Ephratha”) [T]. (2) οὐδαμῶς ἐλαχίστη (“by no means least”) reverses צָעִיר (“little”), ὀλιγοστός (“least”) [T]. (3) ἐν τοῖς ἡγεμόσιν (“among the leaders”) where WLC has בְּאַלְפֵי (“among the thousands/clans”) and the Greek ἐν χιλιάσιν (“among the thousands”); the same consonants pointed אַלֻּפֵי would mean “chiefs” [I]. (4) ἡγούμενος (“a ruler”) for מוֹשֵׁל (“ruler”) and ἄρχοντα (“ruler”): ἡγούμενον (“leader”) stands in Swete 1 Chr 11:2 and in Rahlfs 2 Sam 5:2 and 1 Chr 11:2 (Swete 2 Sam 5:2 has εἰσηγούμενος, “leader”) [T]. Rahlfs Mic 5:1 agrees with Swete here [T], checked. All four are the NT’s own Greek, shaped towards the David text and towards the honour of Bethlehem; none is a Greek variant [T]/[I]. Matthew omits “his goings out are from of old” [T]. What it adds: the child is David’s kind of king, a shepherd, and Micah’s context brings a ruler who is struck before he shepherds and whose greatness reaches the ends of the earth. The magi, who came from those ends, are the first sign of it [I], moderate–high.

Isa 60:3, 6; Ps 72:10–11, 15 (Swete 71) → 2:11 (moderate). Move 1: Isaiah 60 is Zion’s dawn: πορεύσονται βασιλεῖς τῷ φωτί σου (“kings will walk by your light”, 60:3), and from Sheba they come φέροντες χρυσίον, καὶ λίβανον (“bringing gold, and frankincense”, 60:6 Swete) [T]. χρυσίον καὶ λίβανον (“gold and frankincense”) stands together in Swete only at Isa 60:6 [T], verified (phrase search; positive control: the phrase returned the verse). Ps 72 is the royal psalm: kings of Sheba bring gifts, and προσκυνήσουσιν αὐτῷ πάντες οἱ βασιλεῖς (“all kings will worship him”, Swete 71:11) [T]. Matthew has χρυσόν (“gold”), not χρυσίον, and the gift-bearers are not called kings [T]. The pairing of gold and frankincense and the verb of worship are the evidence; the third gift, σμύρνα (“myrrh”), has no source in either text [T].

Num 23:7; 24:17 → 2:1–2, 9 (uncertain–moderate). Move 1: Balaam is summoned ἐξ ὀρέων ἀπ’ ἀνατολῶν (“from the mountains of the east”, Num 23:7 Swete) to curse Israel and blesses instead; his last oracle sees ἀνατελεῖ ἄστρον ἐξ Ἰακώβ (“a star shall rise out of Jacob”, 24:17) [T]. In Matthew ἀπὸ ἀνατολῶν (“from the east”) stands at 2:1; 8:11; 24:27 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. A seer from the east, a star, a ruler, and a hostile king (Balak / Herod) are the shared pattern; the noun differs (ἄστρον / ἀστήρ, “star”) [T].

Ps 2:2 → 2:4 (uncertain — new candidate). Move 1: παρέστησαν οἱ βασιλεῖς τῆς γῆς καὶ οἱ ἄρχοντες συνήχθησαν ἐπὶ τὸ αὐτὸ κατὰ τοῦ κυρίου καὶ κατὰ τοῦ χριστοῦ αὐτοῦ (“the kings of the earth stood, and the rulers were gathered together against the LORD and against his anointed”, Swete) [T]. A king συναγαγὼν (“gathering together”, 2:4) the rulers and asking where ὁ χριστός (“the Christ”) is born is the same shape [I]. Ps 2:7 is heard at 3:17. The verb is common, so this stays uncertain.

Internal: Plants §27:11, 29, 37 — “King of the Jews” (high, verified). Plants §4:9–10; 28:9, 17 — προσκυνέω (“to worship”) (high, verified). Plants §28:8 — great joy (moderate–high, verified). Plants §8:11 — ἀπὸ ἀνατολῶν (“from the east”): the magi are the first of the “many from east and west” (moderate–high, verified). Plants §21:10 — Jerusalem troubled at his birth, shaken at his entry: ἐταράχθη … πᾶσα Ἱεροσόλυμα (“all Jerusalem was troubled”, 2:3), ἐσείσθη πᾶσα ἡ πόλις (“all the city was stirred”, 21:10) (moderate). Plants §26:3–4 — the chief priests gathered (moderate). Answers §1:6 — the royal title passes from David to the child (moderate). Plants §27:2–27 — ἡγεμών (“leader, governor”) (uncertain–moderate, verified). Plants §9:36; 26:31 — the shepherd: ποιμαίνω (“to shepherd”) occurs only at 2:6, and ποιμήν (“shepherd”) at 9:36; 25:32; 26:31 (SBLGNT), verified; the ruler who will shepherd Israel sees the crowds “like sheep without a shepherd” and is struck as the shepherd (moderate–high).

Translations. NASB95 “magi” (2:1, 7, 16); ESV “wise men”, which hides that μάγοι (“magi”) is the word for Bar-Jesus the μάγος (“magician”, Acts 13:6, 8) [T]. The ESV also uses “wise men” for σοφούς (“wise men”) at 23:34, so the pulpit text gives two Greek words one English name [T], checked. At 2:2, 9 NASB95 “in the east”; ESV “when it rose”. The ESV breaks the ἀνατολή (“east”) chain (2:1; 8:11; 24:27) but lets Num 24:17’s “rise” be heard [S]; both are defensible. At 2:4 NASB95 “the Messiah”; ESV “the Christ”. At 2:6 the ESV’s “rulers … a ruler” gives ἡγεμόσιν (“leaders”) and ἡγούμενος (“ruler”) the same English, as the Greek nearly does; NASB95 “leaders … a Ruler”. Pulpit divergence: “wise men” will be heard; the preacher may want to say “magi” once, and to note that their trade is not commended by the text, only their worship.

Pitfall. “Be like the wise men: seek Jesus and give him your best.” The text does not commend the magi’s method or their gifts; it shows the King received by outsiders and threatened by insiders who held the Scriptures. Corrective: preach the title (2:2 with 27:37) and the contrast between the ones who knew where and the ones who came.

Book-overview note: Confirms Mic 5:1 + 2 Sam 5:2, the King-of-the-Jews frame, and Isa 60:6 / Ps 72 (moderate). Extends: (1) χρυσίον καὶ λίβανον (“gold and frankincense”) stands together in Swete only at Isa 60:6, verified; (2) Matthew’s ἡγούμενος (“ruler”) matches the ἡγούμενον (“leader”) of 1 Chr 11:2 and Rahlfs 2 Sam 5:2, so the David text supplied two words, not one; (3) Mic 5:3 Hebrew וְרָעָה (“and he will shepherd”) already links Micah to 2 Sam 5:2, so the conflation follows a link inside the Hebrew; (4) λάθρᾳ (“secretly”) at 1:19 and 2:7 only; (5) χαρὰ μεγάλη (“great joy”) at 2:10 and 28:8 only; (6) ἡγεμών (“leader, governor”) 2:6 → Pilate; (7) new uncertain candidate Ps 2:2 → 2:4.

4. Matthew 2:13–23 — Out of Egypt; Rachel Weeping; a Nazarene ⭐

Position. Q1: 2:1–12 left a troubled king who had been deceived, and a child worshipped in Bethlehem [T]. Q2: this unit exists to move the child from Bethlehem, the city of the promise (2:6), to Nazareth, the place of his name (2:23), by way of Egypt. On that road the son re-lives Israel’s story: he goes down to Egypt, is called out as God’s son, and is recalled with the words spoken to Moses (2:15, 20) [T]/[S: audit]. It also explains why the Messiah from Bethlehem is known as a Galilean from Nazareth (2:22–23; compare 21:11; 26:71) [T]. Three of the prologue’s five fulfilment formulae stand here (2:15, 17, 23) [T].

Structure. Three scenes, each closed by a fulfilment formula [T]:

Verses Scene Formula
2:13–15 Dream 2; flight to Egypt by night ἵνα πληρωθῇ (“that … might be fulfilled”): Hos 11:1
2:16–18 Herod’s slaughter in Bethlehem τότε ἐπληρώθη (“then was fulfilled”): Jer 31:15
2:19–23 Dreams 3 and 4; return; Galilee; Nazareth ὅπως πληρωθῇ (“so that … might be fulfilled”): “the prophets”

The command-and-obedience pair is repeated word for word: Ἐγερθεὶς παράλαβε τὸ παιδίον καὶ τὴν μητέρα αὐτοῦ (“Get up! Take the Child and His mother”, 2:13, 20) and ὁ δὲ ἐγερθεὶς παρέλαβε τὸ παιδίον καὶ τὴν μητέρα αὐτοῦ (“so he got up and took the Child and His mother”, 2:14, 21) [T]. τὸ παιδίον καὶ τὴν μητέρα αὐτοῦ (“the Child and His mother”) stands in Matthew only at these four verses (SBLGNT) [T], verified.

Text-first findings.

  • The second formula names the son, through the Hebrew. Ἐξ Αἰγύπτου ἐκάλεσα τὸν υἱόν μου (“out of Egypt I called my son”, 2:15) [T]. WLC Hos 11:1 reads וּמִמִּצְרַיִם קָרָאתִי לִבְנִי (“and out of Egypt I called my son”); Swete and Rahlfs both read ἐξ Αἰγύπτου μετεκάλεσα τὰ τέκνα αὐτοῦ (“out of Egypt I called back his children”) [T], checked in all three. The point of the citation, “my son”, exists only in the Hebrew. High. The citation is placed when the family goes into Egypt (2:14–15), not when it comes out (2:21): the son is already the one to be called out [T]/[I].
  • “My son” before the Voice says it. τὸν υἱόν μου (“my son”) stands in Matthew at 2:15 and 21:37 (the vineyard owner’s son); ὁ υἱός μου (“my son”) at 3:17 and 17:5 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. God’s first word about Jesus in the book, spoken through the prophet, is “my son”; his first word from heaven is the same (3:17). [T]; synthetic, high for the chain.
  • The hunt. μέλλει γὰρ Ἡρῴδης ζητεῖν τὸ παιδίον τοῦ ἀπολέσαι αὐτό (“for Herod is going to search for the Child to destroy Him”, 2:13) [T]. ἀπόλλυμι (“to destroy”) with Jesus as object stands at 2:13, 12:14 (the Pharisees’ plot) and 27:20 (the crowd persuaded “to destroy Jesus”) [T], checked in the 17 verses of the lemma (SBLGNT). The plot at the end of the book is foreshadowed in its second chapter [I], high.
  • Herod “was mocked”. ἰδὼν ὅτι ἐνεπαίχθη ὑπὸ τῶν μάγων (“when Herod saw that he had been tricked by the magi”, 2:16) [T]. ἐμπαίζω (“to mock”) occurs in 5 verses of Matthew (SBLGNT): 2:16; 20:19; 27:29, 31, 41 [T], verified. The king who was mocked has the children killed; at the end the soldiers and the chief priests mock the King [T]/[I], high.
  • Pharaoh’s pattern, not Pharaoh’s words. ἀνεῖλεν πάντας τοὺς παῖδας (“he killed all the male children”, 2:16) [T]. ἀναιρέω (“to kill”) occurs once in Matthew (SBLGNT) [T], verified. It is the verb of Exod 2:15, ἐζήτει ἀνελεῖν Μωυσῆν· ἀνεχώρησεν δὲ Μωυσῆς (“he sought to kill Moses; but Moses withdrew”) [S: audit]. The order to kill the Hebrew boys (Exod 1:22) shares the pattern, not the wording [T].
  • “They are dead.” τεθνήκασιν γὰρ οἱ ζητοῦντες τὴν ψυχὴν τοῦ παιδίου (“for those who sought the Child’s life are dead”, 2:20) [T]. The plural, when only Herod has died (2:19), is the plural of Exod 4:19 [S: audit] (confirmed and strengthened; see Tool 11). Joseph is sent εἰς γῆν Ἰσραήλ (“into the land of Israel”, 2:20, 21); the phrase occurs in Matthew only in these two verses (SBLGNT) [T], verified.
  • Mourning that refuses comfort. καὶ οὐκ ἤθελεν παρακληθῆναι (“and she refused to be comforted”, 2:18) [T]. The passive of παρακαλέω (“to comfort”) occurs in Matthew only at 2:18 and 5:4, μακάριοι οἱ πενθοῦντες, ὅτι αὐτοὶ παρακληθήσονται (“blessed are those who mourn, for they shall be comforted”) (SBLGNT, phrase search on both forms; 9 verses for the lemma, the other seven active) [T], verified. The first beatitude-promise answers Rachel’s refusal. Synthetic, moderate–high.
  • The Galilean turn. Archelaus βασιλεύει (“is reigning”, 2:22) over Judea; Joseph ἐφοβήθη (“was afraid”) and ἀνεχώρησεν εἰς τὰ μέρη τῆς Γαλιλαίας (“withdrew to the regions of Galilee”) [T]. This is the first use of Γαλιλαία (“Galilee”) in Matthew; the last is 28:16, the mountain of the commission (16 verses, SBLGNT) [T], verified. The withdrawal into Galilee is repeated at 4:12, with the same verb [T].
  • “Through the prophets”, plural, and ὅτι. ὅπως πληρωθῇ τὸ ῥηθὲν διὰ τῶν προφητῶν ὅτι Ναζωραῖος κληθήσεται (“so that what was spoken through the prophets might be fulfilled: ‘He shall be called a Nazarene’”, 2:23) [T]. This is the only formula with the plural προφητῶν (“prophets”) and with ὅτι (“that”) in place of λέγοντος (“saying”) (compare 1:22; 2:15, 17; 4:14) [T]. No OT text reads this sentence [T]. Matthew signals a summary of prophetic speech, not a quotation [I], high. Ναζωραῖος (“Nazarene”) returns once, from the servant-girl in the high priest’s courtyard (26:71) [T], verified; Mark’s form is Ναζαρηνός (“Nazarene”, Mark 1:24; 10:47; 14:67; 16:6) [T].

Synoptic check. No Markan or Lukan parallel to the flight, the slaughter or the return. Luke has no Egypt: the family returns from Jerusalem εἰς πόλιν ἑαυτῶν Ναζαρέθ (“to their own town Nazareth”, Luke 2:39), so Nazareth is home before the birth (Luke 1:26) [T]. Matthew gives no home before Bethlehem and explains Nazareth as a refuge chosen by a dream (2:22–23) [T]. Mark introduces Jesus as ἀπὸ Ναζαρὲτ τῆς Γαλιλαίας (“from Nazareth of Galilee”, Mark 1:9) with no explanation [T].

Tool 11.

Hos 11:1 → 2:15 (high — formula quotation). Source context: Hosea 11 is the LORD speaking as a parent. כִּי נַעַר יִשְׂרָאֵל וָאֹהֲבֵהוּ (“when Israel was a youth I loved him”, 11:1); the more he called, the more they went away to the Baals (11:2); he taught Ephraim to walk (11:3). Judgement follows: they will not return to Egypt but Assyria will be their king, כִּי מֵאֲנוּ לָשׁוּב (“because they refused to return”, 11:5) [T]. Then the turn: אֵיךְ אֶתֶּנְךָ אֶפְרַיִם (“how can I give you up, Ephraim?”, 11:8), and the promise that they will come trembling כְצִפּוֹר מִמִּצְרַיִם (“like a bird from Egypt”, 11:11) [T]. The cited verse is a look back at the exodus, at the head of a chapter that runs from love to judgement to a second coming-out from Egypt [T]. It is not a prediction in its own context [T]. Book usage: the first Twelve citation was Mic 5:1 (2:6). Hosea returns at 9:13 and 12:7 (Hos 6:6) [S] (overview inventory, after reading). OT-to-OT: Hosea’s “my son” is the exodus title of Exod 4:22, בְּנִי בְכֹרִי יִשְׂרָאֵל (“Israel is my son, my firstborn”), Swete Υἱὸς πρωτότοκός μου Ἰσραήλ (“Israel is my firstborn son”) [T]. That is the speech Moses is sent with three verses after Exod 4:19, which Matthew uses at 2:20 [T]. The two Exodus-shaped verses of 2:15 and 2:20 sit three verses apart in Exodus 4 [T]; synthetic, moderate–high. Divergence: see above; Swete and Rahlfs agree with each other against the WLC (μετεκάλεσα, “I called back”; τὰ τέκνα αὐτοῦ, “his children”). Matthew’s ἐκάλεσα (“I called”) and τὸν υἱόν μου (“my son”) are the Hebrew, translated afresh [T]. A substantive difference: the Greek has no “son” [T]. Nearest: WLC. What it adds: Jesus is the son Israel was called to be, and Hosea’s own chapter promises a new coming-out from Egypt after exile (11:11). The fulfilment is the filling of a pattern, not a forecast [I], high.

Exod 4:19 (with 2:15; 1:22) → 2:20, 13–16 (high — confirmed and strengthened) [S: audit]. Accepted as audited: τεθνήκασιν (“they are dead”) occurs in Swete only at Exod 4:19, 1 Sam 4:17 and 4 Macc 12:4, and in the New Testament only at Matt 2:20; Rahlfs Exod 4:19 opens with the plus ἐτελεύτησεν ὁ βασιλεὺς Αἰγύπτου (“the king of Egypt died”), which Swete lacks, giving Matthew’s order of a king’s death and then “they are dead” (2:19–20); Exod 2:15 stands behind 2:13–14 [S: audit]. Text-first extension: the “son” of Exod 4:22 (above) follows in the same speech [T]. What it adds: the child is a new Moses, recalled from exile when the killing king is dead; and the one Moses was sent to call “my son” is the child himself [I].

Jer 31:15 (Swete and Rahlfs 38:15) → 2:18 (high — formula quotation). Source context: Jer 30–31 is the Book of Consolation. Its centre is return from exile: הִנְנִי מֵבִיא אוֹתָם מֵאֶרֶץ צָפוֹן (“behold, I am bringing them from the north country”), with עִוֵּר וּפִסֵּחַ הָרָה וְיֹלֶדֶת (“the blind and the lame, the pregnant woman and she who gives birth”, 31:8) [T]. At Ramah, where the exiles were gathered for Babylon (Jer 40:1), Rachel weeps and will not be comforted (31:15). The LORD answers at once: מִנְעִי קוֹלֵךְ מִבֶּכִי (“keep your voice from weeping”), וְשָׁבוּ מֵאֶרֶץ אוֹיֵב (“and they will return from the land of the enemy”, 31:16), וְיֵשׁ־תִּקְוָה לְאַחֲרִיתֵךְ (“there is hope for your future”, 31:17) [T]. Ephraim is then called בֵּן יַקִּיר (“a dear son”, 31:20), and the chapter reaches the new covenant (31:31–34) [T]. The cited verse is the lament that the next verse comforts. Book usage: the first citation of Jeremiah, and the first of three formulae naming him (2:17; 27:9; with 16:14) [S] (overview inventory). Τότε ἐπληρώθη (“then was fulfilled”) is used only here and at 27:9, the potter’s field and Judas’ silver: the two formulae that introduce a death, and the two that name Jeremiah [T], verified. OT-to-OT: Rachel died and was buried בְּדֶרֶךְ אֶפְרָתָה הִוא בֵּית לָחֶם (“on the way to Ephrath, that is, Bethlehem”, Gen 35:19; Swete αὕτη ἐστὶν Βηθλέεμ, “this is Bethlehem”) [T]. Ephrathah is Micah’s word in the citation of 2:6 (Mic 5:1) [T]. So Jer 31:15, Gen 35:19 and Mic 5:1 are already joined in the OT by Rachel’s tomb on the road to Bethlehem [I], moderate–high. Divergence: WLC נְהִי בְּכִי תַמְרוּרִים (“lamentation, bitter weeping”), רָחֵל מְבַכָּה עַל־בָּנֶיהָ מֵאֲנָה לְהִנָּחֵם עַל־בָּנֶיהָ כִּי אֵינֶנּוּ (“Rachel weeping for her children; she refuses to be comforted for her children, because he is not”) [T]. Swete and Rahlfs agree: θρήνου καὶ κλαυθμοῦ καὶ ὀδυρμοῦ (“of lament and weeping and mourning”), Ῥαχὴλ ἀποκλαιομένη οὐκ ἤθελεν παύσασθαι ἐπὶ τοῖς υἱοῖς αὐτῆς, ὅτι οὐκ εἰσίν (“Rachel weeping would not cease for her sons, because they are not”) [T], checked. Matthew has κλαυθμὸς καὶ ὀδυρμὸς πολύς (“weeping and great mourning”), κλαίουσα τὰ τέκνα αὐτῆς (“weeping for her children”) and οὐκ ἤθελεν παρακληθῆναι (“she refused to be comforted”) [T]. παρακληθῆναι (“to be comforted”) is the Hebrew לְהִנָּחֵם (“to be comforted”) against the Greek παύσασθαι (“to cease”); οὐκ ἤθελεν (“she would not”) and οὐκ εἰσίν (“they are not”, plural) are with the Greek [T]. The majority text adds θρῆνος καί (“lamentation and”), assimilating to the LXX (NA28 apparatus) [T]. Classification: a mixed text, Greek in frame, Hebrew in the key verb; the change is substantive, because it brings “comfort” into the citation [T]/[I]. Nearest: the WLC for the sense, the Greek for the syntax. What it adds: Rachel’s lament is the cry of the exile, and the next verse in Jeremiah is “they will return”. Matthew places the cry in Bethlehem at the slaughter, and the very next scene is a return to the land of Israel (2:19–21) [T]/[I]. “Comfort” is then promised to mourners (5:4) [T].

“Through the prophets”: Isa 11:1; Judg 13:5, 7 → 2:23 (uncertain for any single source; moderate that a cluster is meant). Move 1. (a) Isa 11:1: וְנֵצֶר מִשָּׁרָשָׁיו יִפְרֶה (“and a branch from his roots will bear fruit”) [T]. נֵצֶר (“branch”) stands as a noun at Isa 11:1 and 60:21 (WLC surface search; the other hits are the verb נָצַר, “to guard”) [T]. Swete renders it ἄνθος (“flower”), so the wordplay works only in Hebrew [T]. Isa 11:1 is the root of Jesse, David’s father (1:5–6) [T]. (b) Judg 13:5: כִּי־נְזִיר אֱלֹהִים יִהְיֶה הַנַּעַר (“for the boy shall be a Nazirite of God”), וְהוּא יָחֵל לְהוֹשִׁיעַ אֶת־יִשְׂרָאֵל (“and he shall begin to save Israel”) [T]. Swete: ναζεὶρ θεοῦ (“a Nazir of God”, 13:5) and ἅγιον θεοῦ (“holy one of God”, 13:7); Rahlfs: ναζιραῖον (“Nazirite”, 13:5, 7) and ναζιραῖος θεοῦ (“a Nazirite of God”, 16:17) [T], checked. Judg 13:5 Swete also has αὐτὸς ἄρξεται τοῦ σῶσαι τὸν Ἰσραήλ (“he himself will begin to save Israel”), the shape of 1:21, αὐτὸς γὰρ σώσει (“for he himself will save”) [T]. Jesus is no Nazirite by Matthew’s account (11:19) [T]. OT-to-OT finding: the Hebrew birth formula הָרָה וְיֹלֶדֶת / וְיֹלַדְתְּ בֵּן (“pregnant and bearing a son”) stands in the WLC only at Gen 16:11, Judg 13:5, 7 and Isa 7:14 [T], verified (surface search; Isa 7:14 is the positive control). Without בֵּן (“son”), הָרָה וְיֹלֶדֶת occurs once more, at Jer 31:8, in the chapter of Rachel [T], verified. The texts Matthew cites at 1:23 and 2:18, and the leading candidate for 2:23, share the one Hebrew formula of a promised birth [T] for the distribution; [I], uncertain–moderate, as Matthew’s design.

Internal: Answers §1:21 — αὐτὸς … σώσει (“he himself will save”) and Judg 13:5 (uncertain). Answers §1:22 — ὑπὸ κυρίου διὰ τοῦ προφήτου (“by the Lord through the prophet”) at 1:22 and 2:15 only (high, verified). Plants §3:17; 17:5; 21:37 — “my son” (high, verified). Plants §5:4 — comfort for mourners (moderate–high, verified). Plants §12:14; 27:20 — “to destroy him” (high). Plants §20:19; 27:29, 31, 41 — the mocking (high, verified). Plants §27:9 — τότε ἐπληρώθη (“then was fulfilled”) and Jeremiah (high, verified). Plants §4:12 — ἀναχωρέω (“to withdraw”) into Galilee (high, verified). Plants §26:71 — “Nazarene” from the servant-girl (moderate). Plants §28:16 — Galilee first and last (moderate–high, verified). Plants §8:12 and the six “weeping and gnashing” — κλαυθμός (“weeping”) stands in 7 verses (SBLGNT): Rachel’s at 2:18, then six times of judgement (8:12; 13:42, 50; 22:13; 24:51; 25:30), verified; the link is verbal only (uncertain as design).

Translations. At 2:16 NASB95 and ESV both have “tricked” for ἐνεπαίχθη (“was mocked”), which hides the ἐμπαίζω (“to mock”) chain to 27:29, 31, 41, where both have “mocked” [T], checked. NASB95 “slew all the male children” and ESV “killed all the male children” both supply “male” for παῖδας (“boys, children”). At 2:18 NASB95 “they were no more”, ESV “they are no more”: the ESV keeps the Greek present, οὐκ εἰσίν (“they are not”). Pulpit divergence: none bearing; but the preacher should restore “mocked” at 2:16 if the 27:29 echo is to be heard.

Difficulty. “He shall be called a Nazarene.” No OT text says it [T]. Options: (a) a wordplay on נֵצֶר (“branch”, Isa 11:1), the Davidic shoot; (b) an allusion to the Nazirite (Judg 13:5, 7), holy to God from the womb and a saviour of Israel; (c) a summary of the prophets’ witness that the Messiah would be despised (Nazareth’s obscurity; compare John 1:46), drawing on texts such as Isa 53:2–3 and Ps 22:7 [S]; (d) more than one of these at once, which the plural “prophets” and the ὅτι (“that”) of summary permit [I]. The corpus shows that (a) is Hebrew-only and (b) is clearer in Rahlfs than in Swete [T]. Take to Logos before preaching a single source.

Pitfall. What it looks like: “Matthew proves Jesus is the Messiah from prophecies that came true”, with Hos 11:1 and Jer 31:15 read as forecasts. Why wrong: Hos 11:1 looks back at the exodus and Jer 31:15 mourns the exile; each is followed in its own chapter by a promise of return (Hos 11:11; Jer 31:16–17). Matthew’s point depends on the context, and at 2:15 on the Hebrew. Corrective: preach Israel’s story in the son: called out of Egypt, mourned in exile, brought back to the land. Then let the comfort refused in Ramah be promised in 5:4.

Book-overview note: Confirms from the text: Hos 11:1 through the Hebrew; Jer 31:15 with παρακληθῆναι (“to be comforted”) from the Hebrew; the Exod 4:19 row as audited. Extends: (1) τὸν υἱόν μου (“my son”) 2:15 → ὁ υἱός μου (“my son”) 3:17, verified; (2) Exod 4:22 (“Israel is my son, my firstborn”) stands three verses after Exod 4:19, so 2:15 and 2:20 draw on one Exodus speech; (3) 2:18 → 5:4: the passive of παρακαλέω (“to comfort”) only twice, verified; (4) Gen 35:19 joins Rachel to Ephrath/Bethlehem and so to Mic 5:1 at 2:6; (5) the Hebrew birth formula הָרָה וְיֹלֶדֶת בֵּן (“pregnant and bearing a son”) at Gen 16:11, Judg 13:5, 7 and Isa 7:14 only, plus Jer 31:8 — joins 1:23, 2:18 and the Judg 13 candidate for 2:23; (6) Judg 13:5 Swete αὐτὸς … σῶσαι (“he himself … to save”) with 1:21. Refines: the overview’s Judg 13:5 row should note that Swete has ναζεὶρ (“Nazir”) and ἅγιον θεοῦ (“holy one of God”), Rahlfs ναζιραῖος (“Nazirite”).

5. Matthew 3:1–17 — The Voice in the Wilderness; the Beloved Son ⭐

Position. Q1: 2:13–23 brought the son out of Egypt and settled him in Galilee, in Nazareth (2:22–23), and closed the prologue’s run of dreams [T]. It left two things unsaid: what the child grown up would announce, and whether the Father would speak in his own voice what he had spoken through the prophet, “my son” (2:15) [T]. Q2: this unit supplies both. The kingdom is announced, first by John (3:2), in the words Jesus will use (4:17) [T]. And the heaven speaks: Οὗτός ἐστιν ὁ υἱός μου (“This is My … Son”, 3:17) [T]. It must stand here, before the wilderness, because the tempter’s condition, εἰ υἱὸς εἶ τοῦ θεοῦ (“if You are the Son of God”, 4:3, 6), tests the very word the Voice has just spoken [T]. Ἐν δὲ ταῖς ἡμέραις ἐκείναις (“now in those days”, 3:1) ties the adult ministry loosely to the Nazareth years; the phrase occurs in Matthew only here (SBLGNT) [T].

Structure. Two halves, each opened by παραγίνεται (“came/arrived”, historic present) [T]:

Verses Section
3:1–6 John arrives (παραγίνεται, 3:1): message, Isaiah, Elijah’s dress, the crowds confessing
3:7–10 Pharisees and Sadducees: vipers, fruit, Abraham, the axe
3:11–12 The Stronger One who comes after: Spirit and fire; the winnowing fork
3:13–15 Jesus arrives (παραγίνεται, 3:13): John’s refusal; “to fulfil all righteousness”
3:16–17 Heavens opened; the Spirit of God; the Voice

παραγίνομαι (“to arrive”) occurs in Matthew only at 2:1 (the magi), 3:1 (John) and 3:13 (Jesus) (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The parallel of 3:1 and 3:13 sets the forerunner and the one who comes after side by side [T]. ἰδού (“behold”) opens each event of 3:16–17 [T].

Text-first findings.

  • John is given Jesus’ own words. Μετανοεῖτε, ἤγγικεν γὰρ ἡ βασιλεία τῶν οὐρανῶν (“Repent, for the kingdom of heaven is at hand”, 3:2) stands word for word at 4:17, and nowhere else in Matthew (SBLGNT phrase search) [T], verified. At 10:7 the Twelve are sent with ἤγγικεν ἡ βασιλεία τῶν οὐρανῶν (“the kingdom of heaven is at hand”) without μετανοεῖτε (“repent”) [T], verified. Mark gives John a different message: κηρύσσων βάπτισμα μετανοίας εἰς ἄφεσιν ἁμαρτιῶν (“proclaiming a baptism of repentance for the forgiveness of sins”, Mark 1:4) [T]. In Matthew the forerunner and the Son proclaim the same sentence; John is not a lesser preacher with a lesser message [T]/[I], high. This is the first of the book’s 32 uses of “kingdom of heaven” (31 verses, SBLGNT; regex on βασιλεία in any case followed by τῶν οὐρανῶν) [T].
  • “Forgiveness of sins” is taken from John and given to the cup. ἄφεσις (“forgiveness”) occurs in Matthew once, at 26:28: τὸ αἷμά μου τῆς διαθήκης τὸ περὶ πολλῶν ἐκχυννόμενον εἰς ἄφεσιν ἁμαρτιῶν (“My blood of the covenant, which is poured out for many for forgiveness of sins”) (SBLGNT; lemma search, with 26:28 as its own positive control) [T], verified. The phrase εἰς ἄφεσιν ἁμαρτιῶν (“for the forgiveness of sins”) stands in Mark only at 1:4, of John’s baptism [T], verified. Matthew keeps the confession — ἐξομολογούμενοι τὰς ἁμαρτίας αὐτῶν (“as they confessed their sins”, 3:6) — and drops the forgiveness [T]. What John’s baptism announced in Mark is, in Matthew, done only by the blood of the covenant. [T] for the distribution; [I] for the design, high. This answers 1:21: “he will save his people ἀπὸ τῶν ἁμαρτιῶν αὐτῶν (”from their sins“)” [T].
  • Elijah is dressed, not named. ἔνδυμα … ἀπὸ τριχῶν καμήλου καὶ ζώνην δερματίνην περὶ τὴν ὀσφὺν αὐτοῦ (“a garment of camel’s hair and a leather belt around his waist”, 3:4) [T]. Swete 2 Kgs 1:8 (Swete “2Ki” = 4 Kingdoms): ἀνὴρ δασὺς καὶ ζώνην δερματίνην περιεζωσμένος τὴν ὀσφὺν αὐτοῦ (“a hairy man, with a leather belt bound around his waist”), and the king answers Ἠλειοὺ ὁ Θεσβείτης οὗτός ἐστιν (“it is Elijah the Tishbite”) [T]. ζώνην δερματίνην (“leather belt”) occurs in Swete only at 2 Kgs 1:8 (phrase search) [T], verified; Rahlfs agrees [T], checked. Ἠλίας (“Elijah”) is not named until 11:14, αὐτός ἐστιν Ἠλίας (“he himself is Elijah”); the name stands in 9 verses, none before 11:14 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The reader is shown first and told later [I].
  • John’s words return in Jesus’ mouth, three times over. (1) Γεννήματα ἐχιδνῶν (“You brood of vipers”, 3:7) stands in Matthew at 3:7; 12:34; 23:33 (SBLGNT), and the last two are Jesus’ [T], verified. (2) πᾶν … δένδρον μὴ ποιοῦν καρπὸν καλὸν ἐκκόπτεται καὶ εἰς πῦρ βάλλεται (“every tree that does not bear good fruit is cut down and thrown into the fire”, 3:10) is repeated verbatim by Jesus at 7:19, and only there [T], verified. (3) συνάξει τὸν σῖτον αὐτοῦ εἰς τὴν ἀποθήκην (“He will gather His wheat into the barn”, 3:12) returns in the parable of the weeds: τὸν δὲ σῖτον συναγάγετε εἰς τὴν ἀποθήκην μου (“but gather the wheat into my barn”, 13:30) [T]; ἀποθήκη (“barn”) stands at 3:12; 6:26; 13:30 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. John’s judgement preaching is not superseded by Jesus; Jesus adopts it. Synthetic, verified; high for the chain.
  • The only “wrath” in the book. φυγεῖν ἀπὸ τῆς μελλούσης ὀργῆς (“to flee from the wrath to come”, 3:7) [T]. ὀργή (“wrath”) occurs once in Matthew (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The judgement vocabulary of the unit — ἀξίνη (“axe”), ῥίζα (“root”), πῦρ (“fire”, 3:10, 11, 12), ἄχυρον (“chaff”), πυρὶ ἀσβέστῳ (“with unquenchable fire”) — is concentrated here; ἄχυρον (“chaff”) is a Matthean hapax (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The first target is the religious leadership: πολλοὺς τῶν Φαρισαίων καὶ Σαδδουκαίων (“many of the Pharisees and Sadducees”, 3:7), a pairing Matthew alone makes (Luke 3:7 has τοῖς … ὄχλοις, “the crowds”) [T]. The pair returns at 16:1, 6, 11, 12 — the leaven chapter (Σαδδουκαῖος, “Sadducee”, 7 verses, SBLGNT) [T], verified.
  • Abraham’s children from stones. Πατέρα ἔχομεν τὸν Ἀβραάμ (“We have Abraham for our father”, 3:9) is refused as a ground: δύναται ὁ θεὸς ἐκ τῶν λίθων τούτων ἐγεῖραι τέκνα τῷ Ἀβραάμ (“God is able from these stones to raise up children to Abraham”) [T]. This answers 1:1, υἱοῦ Ἀβραάμ (“the son of Abraham”): the descent the genealogy traces is not a guarantee to others who claim it [T]/[I]. Abraham’s next appearance is 8:11, “many will come from east and west and recline … with Abraham”, while “the sons of the kingdom” are cast out (8:12) [T].
