Ἀβραάμ (“Abraham”) stands in 6 verses (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The play אֲבָנִים / בָּנִים (“stones” / “sons”) is [S] and works only in a Semitic retelling [I], uncertain.
  • The coming one is “behind me”. ὁ δὲ ὀπίσω μου ἐρχόμενος ἰσχυρότερός μού ἐστιν (“He who is coming after me is mightier than I”, 3:11) [T]. ὀπίσω (“after, behind”) occurs in Matthew in 6 verses: 3:11; 4:19; 10:38; 16:23; 16:24; 24:18 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The first is John’s, of Jesus; the next is Jesus’ call, δεῦτε ὀπίσω μου (“follow Me”, lit. “come behind me”, 4:19) [T]. ὁ ἐρχόμενος (“the one who is coming”) returns as John’s question, σὺ εἶ ὁ ἐρχόμενος; (“are You the Expected One?”, 11:3), and as the crowd’s acclamation (21:9) and the last word to Jerusalem (23:39) [T], verified (phrase search; 3:11 has the participle split by ὀπίσω μου). ἰσχυρός (“strong”) returns at 12:29, “the strong man”, who must be bound [T], verified.
  • Jesus’ first words: “to fulfil all righteousness”. Ἄφες ἄρτι, οὕτως γὰρ πρέπον ἐστὶν ἡμῖν πληρῶσαι πᾶσαν δικαιοσύνην (“Permit it at this time; for in this way it is fitting for us to fulfill all righteousness”, 3:15) [T]. These are the first words Jesus speaks in the book [T]. πληρόω (“to fulfil”) has been the narrator’s word four times (1:22; 2:15, 17, 23); here it passes to Jesus [T]. It occurs in 16 verses (SBLGNT) [T], verified. δικαιοσύνη (“righteousness”) occurs in 7 verses: 3:15; 5:6, 10, 20; 6:1, 33; 21:32 (SBLGNT) [T], verified, and the last is John again: ἦλθεν … Ἰωάννης … ἐν ὁδῷ δικαιοσύνης (“John came to you in the way of righteousness”, 21:32) [T]. πρέπω (“to be fitting”) occurs only here (SBLGNT) [T], verified. ἡμῖν (“for us”) includes John [T]. The exchange (3:14–15) has no parallel in Mark or Luke [T].
  • The Voice speaks to the hearers, not to Jesus. Οὗτός ἐστιν ὁ υἱός μου ὁ ἀγαπητός, ἐν ᾧ εὐδόκησα (“This is My beloved Son, in whom I am well-pleased”, 3:17) [T]. Mark and Luke have Σὺ εἶ (“You are”) and ἐν σοί (“in You”) (Mark 1:11; Luke 3:22) [T]. The third person makes the baptism a public declaration, and the transfiguration Voice repeats it verbatim (17:5) [T], verified. ἀγαπητός (“beloved”) and εὐδοκέω (“to be well pleased”) each occur in Matthew only at 3:17; 12:18; 17:5 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. At 12:18 the narrator renders Isa 42:1 with these two words, where Swete has ὁ ἐκλεκτός μου (“my chosen”) and προσεδέξατο (“accepted”) [T]. Matthew’s own translation of the Servant song tells the reader how to hear the Voice [T]/[I], high.

Synoptic check. Matthew follows Mark’s order (Isaiah, dress, baptism, Spirit, Voice) and shares the preaching of 3:7–10, 12 with Luke almost word for word (Luke 3:7–9, 17) [T]. Moves: the Malachi half of Mark’s composite citation (Mark 1:2, Mal 3:1) is held back until 11:10, where Jesus applies it to John [T]. Omits: εἰς ἄφεσιν ἁμαρτιῶν (“for the forgiveness of sins”, Mark 1:4), given instead to 26:28 (above) [T]. Adds: the kingdom sentence for John (3:2); “Pharisees and Sadducees” as addressees (3:7); the dialogue of 3:14–15 [T]. Alters: Mark’s σχιζομένους τοὺς οὐρανούς (“the heavens being torn open”, Mark 1:10) becomes ἠνεῴχθησαν οἱ οὐρανοί (“the heavens were opened”, 3:16) [T]; σχίζω (“to tear”) is then kept for 27:51 alone, the veil and the rocks (SBLGNT) [T], verified, so Matthew drops Mark’s tearing-inclusio (Mark 1:10; 15:38) at its first end [I]. Mark’s τὸ πνεῦμα (“the Spirit”) becomes πνεῦμα θεοῦ (“the Spirit of God”); the anarthrous phrase recurs only at 12:28, ἐν πνεύματι θεοῦ (“by the Spirit of God”), of the exorcisms (SBLGNT phrase search on both cases) [T], verified. Mark’s ὡς (“as”) becomes ὡσεί (“as”, “like”) (3 verses in Matthew: 3:16; 9:36; 14:21) [T]. “Untie the strap of his sandals” (Mark 1:7) becomes τὰ ὑποδήματα βαστάσαι (“to carry the sandals”, 3:11) [T].

Tool 11.

Isa 40:3 → 3:3 (high — formula quotation). Source context: Isaiah 40 opens the comfort of Jerusalem: נַחֲמוּ נַחֲמוּ עַמִּי (“comfort, comfort my people”, 40:1), כִּי נִרְצָה עֲוֺנָהּ (“for her iniquity is pardoned”, 40:2) [T]. The voice calls for a road for YHWH; every valley is raised (40:4); וְנִגְלָה כְּבוֹד יְהוָה (“and the glory of the LORD will be revealed”, 40:5); and the herald to Zion cries הִנֵּה אֱלֹהֵיכֶם (“behold your God”, 40:9), who comes with might and as a shepherd (40:10–11) [T]. The road is for YHWH’s own return to Zion [T]. Book usage: the first citation of Isaiah by name (Ἠσαΐας, “Isaiah”, 6 verses: 3:3; 4:14; 8:17; 12:17; 13:14; 15:7, SBLGNT, verified); Isaiah already stood behind 1:23 (Isa 7:14) [T]. The formula here is unusual: οὗτος γάρ ἐστιν ὁ ῥηθείς (“for this is the one referred to”), identifying a person, not ἵνα πληρωθῇ (“that it might be fulfilled”) [T]. Compare 11:10, οὗτός ἐστιν περὶ οὗ γέγραπται (“this is the one about whom it is written”), again of John [T]. Both John formulae use οὗτός ἐστιν (“this is”), the words of the king’s identification of Elijah in Swete 2 Kgs 1:8 [T]; synthetic, uncertain–moderate as design. OT-to-OT: Mal 3:1 takes up Isa 40:3’s פַּנּוּ־דֶרֶךְ (“prepare a way”) for the messenger before the LORD’s coming to his temple [T] (Hebrew), and Mal 3:23 names the messenger אֵלִיָּה הַנָּבִיא (“Elijah the prophet”) [T]. Matthew supplies Malachi’s half of the pair at 11:10, 14 [T]. Divergence: (1) The WLC accents divide קוֹל קוֹרֵא (“a voice is calling”) from בַּמִּדְבָּר פַּנּוּ (“in the wilderness prepare”), and the parallel line has בָּעֲרָבָה (“in the desert”) with the second verb, so in the Hebrew the road is built in the wilderness [T]. Swete and Rahlfs read φωνὴ βοῶντος ἐν τῇ ἐρήμῳ (“a voice of one crying in the wilderness”), placing the voice there [T], checked in both. Matthew follows the Greek, and his narrative depends on it: John is κηρύσσων ἐν τῇ ἐρήμῳ (“preaching in the wilderness”, 3:1) [T]. (2) Swete and Rahlfs end τὰς τρίβους τοῦ θεοῦ ἡμῶν (“the paths of our God”); Matthew, with Mark 1:3, reads τὰς τρίβους αὐτοῦ (“His paths”) [T]. The change lets κυρίου (“of the Lord”) stand for Jesus without “our God” pinning it to the Father [I], moderate–high. Classification: (1) a translation choice the NT keeps; (2) the NT’s own Greek, inherited from Mark. Nearest: Swete and Rahlfs (they agree). What it adds: the road prepared is the road for YHWH’s return to comfort Zion; Matthew sets Jesus on it. The comfort of Isa 40:1 is the comfort promised to mourners at 5:4 (synthetic, moderate).

2 Kgs 1:8 → 3:4 (high — verbal, Swete-only phrase). Source context: Ahaziah’s messengers describe the man who met them; the king knows him by his dress. WLC אִישׁ בַּעַל שֵׂעָר וְאֵזוֹר עוֹר אָזוּר בְּמָתְנָיו (“a hairy man, with a leather girdle bound about his loins”) [T]. The chapter is Elijah’s judgement on a king who sought another god (2 Kgs 1:3, 16), with fire from heaven (1:10, 12) [T]. Book usage: first Elijah reference; named at 11:14; 16:14; 17:3–12; 27:47, 49 [T]. OT-to-OT: Mal 3:23 (Swete 4:4), the promise of Elijah’s return before the day of the LORD [T]; Zech 13:4, prophets who will no longer wear אַדֶּרֶת שֵׂעָר (“a hairy mantle”, Swete δέρριν τριχίνην, “a hair cloak”) to deceive [T] (the true prophet’s dress, worn falsely). What it adds: John comes as Elijah, the prophet of fire and of the turn of hearts, before the day [I], high.

Mal 3:19 (Swete 4:1) → 3:10–12 (moderate — not in the overview; related to audit candidate 14). Move 1: הִנֵּה הַיּוֹם בָּא בֹּעֵר כַּתַּנּוּר (“behold, the day is coming, burning like an oven”); the arrogant become קַשׁ (“stubble”), and לֹא־יַעֲזֹב לָהֶם שֹׁרֶשׁ וְעָנָף (“it will leave them neither root nor branch”) [T]. Swete: ἡμέρα ἔρχεται (“a day is coming”), καλάμη (“stubble”), οὐ μὴ ὑπολειφθῇ ἐξ αὐτῶν ῥίζα οὐδὲ κλῆμα (“neither root nor branch will be left of them”) [T]. Shared: the coming day/wrath, root, fire, and burnt chaff-like stubble (3:7, 10, 12) [T]; the wording is partial (Matthew has ἄχυρον, “chaff”, not καλάμη, “stubble”) [T]. Elijah’s promise follows four verses later (Mal 3:23), and John is dressed as Elijah (3:4) [T]. Synthetic, moderate. Note Swete’s versification: its Mal 4:1 begins with the end of MT 3:18 [T].

Ps 2:7; Isa 42:1; Gen 22:2 → 3:17 (moderate–high as a composite; no one source is cited). Source context: (a) Ps 2:7, the king’s decree, בְּנִי אַתָּה אֲנִי הַיּוֹם יְלִדְתִּיךָ (“you are my son; today I have begotten you”); Swete and Rahlfs Υἱός μου εἶ σύ (“you are my son”) [T]. The nations rage against the LORD and his anointed (2:1–2) and are given to the son (2:8) [T]. (b) Isa 42:1, הֵן עַבְדִּי … בְּחִירִי רָצְתָה נַפְשִׁי נָתַתִּי רוּחִי עָלָיו (“behold my servant … my chosen, in whom my soul delights; I have put my Spirit upon him”) [T]. Swete and Rahlfs insert Ἰακὼβ … Ἰσραήλ (“Jacob … Israel”) and read προσεδέξατο (“accepted”) [T], checked in both. (c) Gen 22:2, אֶת־בִּנְךָ אֶת־יְחִידְךָ אֲשֶׁר־אָהַבְתָּ (“your son, your only one, whom you love”); Swete and Rahlfs τὸν υἱόν σου τὸν ἀγαπητόν (“your beloved son”), and again at Swete 22:12, 16 [T]. Book usage: “my son” was first God’s word through Hosea (2:15) [T]; Isa 42:1–4 is cited in full at 12:18–21 [T]. OT-to-OT: the Spirit is given to the Servant in Isa 42:1 and to the Davidic shoot in Isa 11:2 [T]. Divergence: “beloved” is the Greek’s rendering of יָחִיד (“only one”) in Gen 22 [T]; “well pleased” is nearer the Hebrew רָצְתָה (“delights”) than Swete’s προσεδέξατο (“accepted”) [T]. Matthew’s own rendering at 12:18 (ὁ ἀγαπητός μου εἰς ὃν εὐδόκησεν ἡ ψυχή μου, “My Beloved in whom My soul is well-pleased”) agrees with neither Greek text and joins the Servant to the Voice [T]. Nearest: none; the Voice is Matthew’s (Mark’s) own composite Greek. What it adds: king (Ps 2), Servant endowed with the Spirit (Isa 42) and beloved son offered (Gen 22) are spoken over one man, and the Spirit descends before the Servant’s words are spoken [I], moderate–high. In Gen 22:1 ὁ θεὸς ἐπείραζεν τὸν Ἀβραάμ (“God tested Abraham”) stands immediately before “your beloved son” (Swete) [T]; in Matthew the son is πειρασθῆναι (“to be tempted/tested”, 4:1) immediately after (synthetic, uncertain–moderate).

Ezek 1:1 → 3:16 (moderate — not in the overview). Move 1: ἠνοίχθησαν οἱ οὐρανοί (“the heavens were opened”) occurs in Swete only at Ezek 1:1 (phrase search), and Rahlfs agrees [T], verified. WLC נִפְתְּחוּ הַשָּׁמַיִם וָאֶרְאֶה מַרְאוֹת אֱלֹהִים (“the heavens were opened and I saw visions of God”), by the river Chebar among the exiles [T]. Matthew’s change from Mark’s σχιζομένους (“being torn”) brings the scene nearer Ezekiel’s: a river, opened heavens, and “he saw” (εἶδεν, 3:16) [T]. Isa 63:19 (Swete 64:1), ἐὰν ἀνοίξῃς τὸν οὐρανόν (“if you open the heaven”), is the companion for the descent [T]. Within the NT only Matt 3:16 has ἠνεῴχθησαν οἱ οὐρανοί; Luke 3:21 has the infinitive ἀνεῳχθῆναι τὸν οὐρανόν (“the heaven was opened”) [T], verified.

Internal: Answers §2:15 — “my son”, now spoken from heaven (high, verified; see unit 4). Answers §1:21 — sins: confession here (3:6), forgiveness held for 26:28 (high for the distribution, verified). Answers §1:1 — Abraham’s children (moderate–high). Answers §1:18, 20 — the Spirit: the child “from the Holy Spirit” is the baptiser “with the Holy Spirit” (3:11) (moderate). Plants §4:17; 10:7 — the kingdom sentence (high, verified). Plants §26:45–46 — ἤγγικεν (“is at hand”) stands in 5 verses: the kingdom at 3:2; 4:17; 10:7, then ἤγγικεν ἡ ὥρα (“the hour is at hand”, 26:45) and ἤγγικεν ὁ παραδιδούς με (“the one who betrays Me is at hand”, 26:46) (SBLGNT phrase search; Mark has it only at 1:15; 14:42), verified; synthetic, moderate. Plants §4:3, 6 — the Son tested (high). Plants §4:19 — ὀπίσω (“behind”) (high, verified). Plants §7:19; 12:34; 13:30; 23:33 — John’s words in Jesus’ mouth (high, verified). Plants §11:3, 10, 14; 17:10–13; 21:25–32 — John’s identity and righteousness (high). Plants §12:18 — the Servant (high, verified). Plants §12:28 — πνεῦμα θεοῦ (“Spirit of God”) (high, verified). Plants §12:29 — the strong man (moderate). Plants §17:5 — the Voice verbatim (high, verified). Plants §26:28 — forgiveness (high). Plants §27:51 — the tearing kept for the veil (moderate). Plants §28:19 — βαπτίζω (“to baptise”) stands in 6 verses of Matthew, five in chapter 3 and then only 28:19 (SBLGNT) [T], verified; Father, Son and Spirit are present together at 3:16–17 and named together at 28:19 (high; agrees with the overview).

Translations. At 3:16 the ESV reads “the heavens were opened to him”; the NASB95 omits “to Him” [T], checked. NA28 prints °[αὐτῷ] (“to him”) in brackets; it is omitted by א* B and others and read by א¹ C and the majority (NA28 apparatus) [T]; SBLGNT omits it [T]. NA28 also brackets [τὸ] … [τοῦ] before πνεῦμα θεοῦ (“the Spirit of God”) and [καὶ] before ἐρχόμενον (“coming”) [T]; nothing in the findings rests on these. At 3:15 NASB95 “Permit it at this time” and ESV “Let it be so now” render ἄφες ἄρτι (“allow it now”) [T]. At 3:11 NASB95 “I am not fit to remove His sandals” has paraphrased βαστάσαι (“to carry”); the ESV “whose sandals I am not worthy to carry” keeps it [T]. ἤγγικεν is “is at hand” in both versions in all five places, so the 3:2 → 26:45–46 chain can be heard in either [T], checked. Pulpit divergence: the ESV’s “opened to him” (3:16) rests on bracketed text and makes the vision sound private to Jesus. The third-person Voice (3:17) points the other way. The preacher should not build on “to him”.

Difficulty. “To fulfil all righteousness” (3:15). Options: (a) Jesus does what God requires of the faithful Israelite, including a baptism of repentance he does not need, and so identifies with the people he will save [S]; (b) “righteousness” means God’s saving plan, which John and Jesus together (ἡμῖν, “for us”) bring to its fulfilment, as πληρόω (“to fulfil”) means elsewhere in the book [S]; (c) the baptism enacts in advance the Servant’s being “numbered with transgressors” (Isa 53:12), which the Voice’s echo of Isa 42:1 would support [S]/[I]. The text gives no gloss. The Sermon’s δικαιοσύνη (“righteousness”) texts (5:6, 10, 20; 6:1, 33) and 21:32 (“the way of righteousness”) should be read with it before choosing [I]. Take to Logos.

“With the Holy Spirit and fire” (3:11). Options: one baptism of purification (Spirit and fire together); or two baptisms, Spirit for the wheat and fire for the chaff (3:12). The fire of 3:10 and 3:12 is judgement, which favours the second for the sentence’s context [T]/[I], moderate.

Pitfall. What it looks like: “John preached law and judgement; Jesus brought grace.” Why wrong: Matthew gives John Jesus’ exact proclamation (3:2 = 4:17), and Jesus repeats John’s judgement word for word (3:10 = 7:19; 3:7 = 12:34; 23:33). The difference Matthew marks is not in the message but in the person: John baptises with water, and the one who comes after him is stronger and baptises with Spirit and fire (3:11). Even forgiveness is withheld from John’s baptism and given to the cup (26:28). Corrective: preach the continuity of message and the discontinuity of person. The unit’s climax is not a doctrine of baptism but the Voice’s “This is My beloved Son”.

Book-overview note: Confirms from the text: 3:15 as Jesus’ first words and the δικαιοσύνη (“righteousness”) set; ἀγαπητός / εὐδοκέω (“beloved” / “well pleased”) at 3:17; 12:18; 17:5 only; βαπτίζω (“to baptise”) only in chapter 3 and 28:19; 2 Kgs 1:8. Extends: (1) Matthew removes εἰς ἄφεσιν ἁμαρτιῶν (“for the forgiveness of sins”) from John’s baptism (Mark 1:4) and ἄφεσις (“forgiveness”) occurs only at 26:28 — the overview notes Matthew “adds” it at 26:28 but not that he took it from John; this should go in the 1:21 → 26:28 echo row. (2) John’s words are Jesus’ words in both directions: 3:2 = 4:17; 3:10 = 7:19; 3:7 → 12:34; 23:33; 3:12 → 13:30. (3) ἤγγικεν (“is at hand”) 3:2 → 26:45–46. (4) Two new Move-1 sources: Ezek 1:1 (ἠνοίχθησαν οἱ οὐρανοί, “the heavens were opened”, Swete-only phrase) and Mal 3:19 (Swete 4:1) behind 3:10–12, which joins audit candidate 14 (Mal 3:19–21 behind 13:41–43): Malachi’s day stands behind both John’s preaching and the harvest parable. (5) Isa 40:3 — Matthew follows the Greek in placing the voice “in the wilderness”, which his narrative needs (3:1); the overview row should say which text he is nearest. Refines: the Gen 22:2 row should note Gen 22:1, “God tested Abraham”, before the beloved son, as a possible bridge to 4:1 (uncertain–moderate).

6. Matthew 4:1–11 — If You Are the Son of God ⭐

Position. Q1: the Voice has said “This is My beloved Son” (3:17) [T]. Q2: this unit tests that word at once. Τότε (“then”, 4:1) joins the two scenes without a gap, and the tempter’s first words take up the Voice’s: Εἰ υἱὸς εἶ τοῦ θεοῦ (“If You are the Son of God”, 4:3, 6) [T]. The Son who came up out of the water now goes where Israel went after the sea, into the wilderness, to be tested and to hunger (Deut 8:2–3) [T]/[I]. It must come before the public ministry (4:12–17), because the authority Jesus will exercise and the worship he will receive (28:17–18) are first refused from the wrong giver (4:8–10) [T]/[I]. It also closes the Jordan scene: τότε ἀφίησιν αὐτόν (“then he permitted Him” / “then … left Him”) stands only at 3:15 and 4:11 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. John’s yielding opens the scene and the devil’s leaving closes it.

Structure. Setting (4:1–2); three tests, each a demand followed by γέγραπται (“it is written”) and a word from Deuteronomy (4:3–4; 4:5–7; 4:8–10); ending (4:11) [T].

Verses Place Demand Answer
4:3–4 wilderness “If You are the Son of God … stones … bread” Deut 8:3
4:5–7 the holy city, the temple’s pinnacle “If You are the Son of God, throw Yourself down” + Ps 91:11–12 Deut 6:16
4:8–10 a very high mountain “All these I will give You, if You fall down and worship me” Deut 6:13

The first two tests open with the conditional on the title; the third drops the title and states the price [T]. The movement rises from wilderness to temple to “a very high mountain” [T]. The devil, too, says γέγραπται (“it is written”, 4:6) [T].

Text-first findings.

  • The Spirit leads, God tests, the devil tempts. ἀνήχθη εἰς τὴν ἔρημον ὑπὸ τοῦ πνεύματος, πειρασθῆναι ὑπὸ τοῦ διαβόλου (“was led up by the Spirit into the wilderness to be tempted by the devil”, 4:1) [T]. The passive with ὑπό (“by”) twice gives two agents [T]. Deut 8:2 Swete: πᾶσαν τὴν ὁδὸν ἣν ἤγαγέν σε Κύριος ὁ θεός σου ἐν τῇ ἐρήμῳ, ὡς ἂν κακώσῃ σε καὶ ἐκπειράσῃ σε (“all the way which the Lord your God led you in the wilderness, to afflict you and test you”), and 8:3 ἐλιμαγχόνησέν σε (“he let you hunger”) [T]. Matthew’s sequence — led, wilderness, tested, hungry (ἐπείνασεν, “He became hungry”, 4:2) — is Deut 8:2–3’s sequence [T]. Three verses later Moses gives the reason: ὡς εἴ τις παιδεύσαι ἄνθρωπος τὸν υἱὸν αὐτοῦ (“as a man disciplines his son”, Deut 8:5, Swete; WLC אֶת־בְּנוֹ, “his son”) [T]. The chapter Jesus quotes first is the chapter that calls Israel’s wilderness testing the training of a son. [T] for the text; [I] for Matthew’s use, high. ἀνάγω (“to lead up”) occurs only here in Matthew (SBLGNT) [T], verified.
  • Forty days and forty nights. Mark 1:13 and Luke 4:2 have “forty days”; Matthew alone adds καὶ νύκτας τεσσεράκοντα (“and forty nights”, 4:2) [T]. The full phrase τεσσεράκοντα ἡμέρας καὶ τεσσεράκοντα νύκτας (“forty days and forty nights”) occurs in Swete at Gen 7:4, 12, 17 (the flood); Exod 24:18; 34:28; Deut 9:9, 18, 25; 10:10 (Moses on the mountain); and 1 Kgs 19:8 (Elijah to Horeb) (phrase search) [T], verified. In Exod 34:28 and Deut 9:9, 18 it is joined to ἄρτον οὐκ ἔφαγεν (“he did not eat bread”) [T]. Deut 9:18 is Moses’ second fast, for the sin of the golden calf: περὶ πασῶν τῶν ἁμαρτιῶν ὑμῶν (“because of all your sins”) [T]. Synthetic, moderate–high: Jesus fasts as Moses fasted, and Israel’s worship of the calf is the sin behind the fast; the third test is a demand for false worship [I].
  • “These stones”: John’s word turned into the devil’s. εἰπὲ ἵνα οἱ λίθοι οὗτοι ἄρτοι γένωνται (“command that these stones become bread”, 4:3) [T]. John had said δύναται ὁ θεὸς ἐκ τῶν λίθων τούτων ἐγεῖραι τέκνα τῷ Ἀβραάμ (“God is able from these stones to raise up children to Abraham”, 3:9) [T]. Luke has the singular τῷ λίθῳ τούτῳ (“this stone”, Luke 4:3) [T]. Matthew’s plural keeps the echo of 3:9 [I], moderate. υἱός (“son”), ἄρτος (“bread”) and λίθος (“stone”) stand together in only two verses of Matthew: 4:3 and 7:9 — “what man is there among you who, when his son asks for a loaf, will give him a stone?” (SBLGNT, lemma intersection) [T], verified. The Father who gives good things to those who ask (7:11) is the Father the Son trusts in 4:4 [I]; synthetic, moderate–high.
  • Bread “alone”; God “alone”. Both answers that use μόνος (“only, alone”) frame the unit: οὐκ ἐπ᾽ ἄρτῳ μόνῳ (“not on bread alone”, 4:4) and αὐτῷ μόνῳ λατρεύσεις (“serve Him only”, 4:10) [T]. μόνῳ (“only”) is in Deut 8:3 (WLC לְבַדּוֹ, “alone”) but not in Deut 6:13 in the WLC, Swete or Rahlfs [T], checked in all three. Swete’s nearest is δουλεύσατε αὐτῷ μόνῳ (“serve him only”, 1 Sam 7:3), Samuel’s call to put away foreign gods [T] (uncertain as a source). The added word makes the first and last answers rhyme [I].
  • “Worship” and “serve”: the commandment against images. At 4:10 Jesus says κύριον τὸν θεόν σου προσκυνήσεις καὶ αὐτῷ μόνῳ λατρεύσεις (“You shall worship the Lord your God, and serve Him only”) [T]. Deut 6:13 has תִּירָא (“you shall fear”) in the WLC and φοβηθήσῃ (“you shall fear”) in Swete and Rahlfs (and so Deut 10:20) [T], checked. In Swete προσκυνήσεις (“you shall worship”) followed by λατρεύσῃς (“you shall serve”) stands at Exod 20:5; 23:24; Deut 5:9: οὐ προσκυνήσεις αὐτοῖς οὐδὲ μὴ λατρεύσῃς αὐτοῖς (“you shall not worship them nor serve them”) (regex search) [T], verified. The verb that replaces “fear” is the verb of the second commandment, where the same pair forbids worship of images. [T] for the wording; [I], moderate–high, that the answer reads Deut 6:13 through the Decalogue. The word answers the devil’s πεσὼν προσκυνήσῃς μοι (“fall down and worship me”, 4:9) [T]. λατρεύω (“to serve”) occurs only here in Matthew (SBLGNT) [T], verified. Luke 4:8 has the same προσκυνήσεις (“you shall worship”) [T], so the change is in the shared tradition, not Matthew’s alone [T]/[I].
  • The testing passes to the leaders. πειράζω (“to tempt, test”) occurs in Matthew in 6 verses: 4:1, 3 (ὁ πειράζων, “the tempter”), then 16:1; 19:3; 22:18; 22:35, all of Pharisees, Sadducees or a lawyer “testing” Jesus (SBLGNT) [T], verified. ἐκπειράζω (“to put to the test”) occurs only at 4:7 [T], verified. After the wilderness the devil never again “tests” Jesus in Matthew; the religious leaders do. [T]; synthetic, high for the chain. At 22:18 Jesus asks τί με πειράζετε; (“why are you testing Me?”) [T]. The noun πειρασμός (“temptation”) stands only at 6:13 and 26:41 [T], verified: the Son was “led up” into testing; the disciples are to pray not to be “led” into it, and in Gethsemane are told to watch and pray for that reason [T]/[I].
  • The devil shows; Jesus shows. δείκνυσιν αὐτῷ πάσας τὰς βασιλείας τοῦ κόσμου (“showed Him all the kingdoms of the world”, 4:8) [T]. δείκνυμι (“to show”) occurs in Matthew at 4:8; 8:4; 16:21 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. At 16:21 ἤρξατο ὁ Ἰησοῦς δεικνύειν … ὅτι δεῖ αὐτὸν … παθεῖν (“Jesus began to show … that He must … suffer”), and two verses later he says to Peter Ὕπαγε ὀπίσω μου, Σατανᾶ (“Get behind Me, Satan!”, 16:23) [T]. The kingdoms without the cross are what the devil shows; the cross is what Jesus shows, and the objection to it is Satan’s again. Synthetic, moderate–high.
  • Two mountains. εἰς ὄρος ὑψηλὸν λίαν (“to a very high mountain”, 4:8) [T]. ὄρος ὑψηλόν (“high mountain”) occurs in Matthew at 4:8 and 17:1 only (SBLGNT phrase search) [T], verified. On the first the devil offers the kingdoms and their δόξα (“glory”); on the second Jesus’ face shines and the Voice of 3:17 is heard again (17:2, 5) [T]. The overview’s row 4:8–9 → 28:16–18 stands; 17:1 is the middle term [I], moderate–high.
  • The angels, and the ones he will not call. The devil quotes τοῖς ἀγγέλοις αὐτοῦ ἐντελεῖται περὶ σοῦ (“He will command His angels concerning You”, 4:6) [T]. When Jesus refuses, ἄγγελοι προσῆλθον καὶ διηκόνουν αὐτῷ (“angels came and began to minister to Him”, 4:11) [T]. At the arrest he says he could ask the Father and παραστήσει μοι … δώδεκα λεγιῶνας ἀγγέλων (“He will at once put at My disposal more than twelve legions of angels”, 26:53) [T]. Synthetic, moderate: the temple test returns in Gethsemane, and it is refused for the same reason (26:54, πῶς οὖν πληρωθῶσιν αἱ γραφαί, “how then will the Scriptures be fulfilled?”) [T]. διακονέω (“to serve, minister”) returns at 20:28: the Son of Man came οὐκ … διακονηθῆναι ἀλλὰ διακονῆσαι (“not to be served, but to serve”) [T] (6 occurrences in 5 verses, SBLGNT, verified).

Synoptic check. Mark 1:12–13 has two verses and no dialogue: “forty days”, πειραζόμενος ὑπὸ τοῦ Σατανᾶ (“being tempted by Satan”), the wild beasts, and angels serving [T]. Matthew shares the three tests with Luke 4:1–13 [T]. Matthew’s distinctives: “and forty nights” (4:2); the full Deut 8:3, including ἀλλ᾽ ἐπὶ παντὶ ῥήματι ἐκπορευομένῳ διὰ στόματος θεοῦ (“but on every word that proceeds out of the mouth of God”, 4:4), which Luke 4:4 lacks; “these stones” plural (Luke “this stone”); τὴν ἁγίαν πόλιν (“the holy city”, 4:5) where Luke has Ἰερουσαλήμ (“Jerusalem”); the mountain (Luke 4:5 has none) and the order, ending on the mountain (Luke ends at the temple); Ὕπαγε, Σατανᾶ (“Go, Satan!”, 4:10), which Luke lacks; and Mark’s angels, which Luke lacks [T]. Matthew’s order makes the last test the question of worship, and the book ends with worship on a mountain (28:16–17) [T]/[I]. Luke’s distinctives (for contrast): “all this authority … for it has been handed over to me” (Luke 4:6) — Matthew has no ἐξουσία (“authority”) in the offer, and keeps the word for 28:18 [T]/[I].

Tool 11.

Deut 8:3 → 4:4 (high — quotation with γέγραπται). Source context: Deut 8 is Moses’ sermon on remembering the wilderness. The LORD led Israel forty years לְנַסֹּתְךָ (“to test you”), to know what was in its heart, הֲתִשְׁמֹר מִצְוֹתָו אִם־לֹא (“whether you would keep his commandments or not”, 8:2) [T]. He let them hunger and fed them manna, so that they would know that man lives עַל־כָּל־מוֹצָא פִי־יְהוָה (“on everything that comes out of the mouth of the LORD”, 8:3) [T]. The LORD disciplines Israel as a man his son (8:5); the warning that follows is against forgetting him in plenty (8:11–17) [T]. Book usage: the first γέγραπται (“it is written”) of Jesus, and the first of three Deuteronomy citations in this unit [T]. OT-to-OT: the manna story itself (Exod 16:4) gives the purpose לְמַעַן אֲנַסֶּנּוּ (“that I may test them”) [T] (Hebrew, not gated). Divergence: the WLC has no word for “word”; כָּל־מוֹצָא (“all that comes out”) is general [T]. Swete and Rahlfs supply ἐπὶ παντὶ ῥήματι τῷ ἐκπορευομένῳ διὰ στόματος θεοῦ (“on every word that proceeds through the mouth of God”) [T], checked in both. Matthew follows the Greek, without τῷ (“the”) (SBLGNT and NA28) [T]. Classification: the Greek’s own interpretation, kept by the NT. Nearest: Swete and Rahlfs (they agree). What it adds: the Son, hungry in the wilderness, lives on what the Father says, and what the Father has just said is “This is My beloved Son” (3:17) [I], high.

Ps 91:11–12 (Swete 90:11–12) → 4:6 (high — quoted by the devil). Source context: Psalm 91 is the trust of one who dwells בְּסֵתֶר עֶלְיוֹן (“in the shelter of the Most High”, 91:1) [T]. The promise of angels is לִשְׁמָרְךָ בְּכָל־דְּרָכֶיךָ (“to guard you in all your ways”, 91:11) [T]. Then עַל־שַׁחַל וָפֶתֶן תִּדְרֹךְ (“you will tread on the lion and the cobra”, 91:13), Swete καταπατήσεις λέοντα καὶ δράκοντα (“you will trample lion and dragon”) [T]. Divergence: Swete ἐν ταῖς ὁδοῖς σου (“in your ways”); Rahlfs ἐν πάσαις ταῖς ὁδοῖς σου (“in all your ways”) [T], checked. Matthew omits the whole clause τοῦ διαφυλάξαι σε ἐν … ταῖς ὁδοῖς σου (“to guard you in your ways”); Luke 4:10 keeps τοῦ διαφυλάξαι σε (“to guard you”) [T]. Otherwise the devil’s text is the Greek verbatim [T]. Classification: an abridgement. Nearest: Swete/Rahlfs. What it adds: the devil quotes a promise for the one who walks in God’s ways, and drops “your ways”; the psalm’s next verse promises the trampling of the serpent [T]/[I], moderate.

Deut 6:16 → 4:7 (high). Source context: לֹא תְנַסּוּ אֶת־יְהוָה אֱלֹהֵיכֶם כַּאֲשֶׁר נִסִּיתֶם בַּמַּסָּה (“you shall not test the LORD your God as you tested him at Massah”) [T]. At Massah the people asked הֲיֵשׁ יְהוָה בְּקִרְבֵּנוּ אִם־אָיִן (“Is the LORD among us or not?”, Exod 17:7); Swete Εἰ ἔστιν Κύριος ἐν ἡμῖν ἢ οὔ; (“Is the Lord among us or not?”) [T]. Book usage: Deut 6 is the Shema chapter; 6:5 is cited at 22:37 [T]. OT-to-OT: Ps 95:8–9 recalls Massah as the day “your fathers tested me” [T] (Hebrew). Divergence: the WLC is plural (תְנַסּוּ, “you [pl.] shall test”; אֱלֹהֵיכֶם, “your [pl.] God”); Swete and Rahlfs are singular, οὐκ ἐκπειράσεις κύριον τὸν θεόν σου (“you shall not test the Lord your God”) [T], checked. Matthew follows the Greek singular [T]. Classification: translation choice; the singular suits a word to one Son. Nearest: Swete and Rahlfs. What it adds: Massah’s question was whether God is with his people. The book has named Jesus Ἐμμανουήλ … μεθ᾽ ἡμῶν ὁ θεός (“Immanuel … God with us”, 1:23) [T]. The Son does not stage a test of God’s presence [I]; synthetic, moderate.

Deut 6:13 (with Exod 20:5; Deut 5:9) → 4:10 (high). Source context: after the Shema (6:4–9) Moses warns that in a land full of good things Israel must not forget the LORD who brought it out of Egypt (6:10–12); then אֶת־יְהוָה אֱלֹהֶיךָ תִּירָא וְאֹתוֹ תַעֲבֹד (“you shall fear the LORD your God and serve him”, 6:13), and לֹא תֵלְכוּן אַחֲרֵי אֱלֹהִים אֲחֵרִים (“you shall not go after other gods”, 6:14) [T]. Book usage: see 4:7. OT-to-OT: Deut 10:20 repeats 6:13 [T]. Divergence: “fear” → “worship”, and μόνῳ (“only”) added; see the findings. The worship/serve pair is the Decalogue’s (Exod 20:5; Deut 5:9) [T], verified. Classification: the NT’s own Greek (shared with Luke 4:8). Nearest: none for the verb; Swete/Rahlfs for the rest. The overview’s note that some LXX manuscripts read προσκυνήσεις (“you shall worship”) stays [S]: the corpus has no LXX apparatus to check it [T]. What it adds: the price of the kingdoms is idolatry, and the answer is the first two commandments in one sentence [I], moderate–high.

Deut 34:1–4 → 4:8 (moderate — not in the overview). Move 1: Moses goes up the mountain, and ἔδειξεν αὐτῷ Κύριος πᾶσαν τὴν γῆν (“the Lord showed him all the land”, Swete Deut 34:1; WLC וַיַּרְאֵהוּ יְהוָה אֶת־כָּל־הָאָרֶץ, “and the LORD showed him all the land”) [T]. ἔδειξεν αὐτῷ (“he showed him”) occurs in 7 Swete verses, Deut 34:1 among them [T], verified. The LORD shows the land sworn to Abraham, Isaac and Jacob (34:4) [T]. The devil shows “all the kingdoms of the world” from a very high mountain and offers what the LORD had promised to give (Ps 2:8) [T]/[I]. Shared: mountain, “show”, “all”. Moderate.

Internal: Answers §3:17 — the title tested (high). Answers §3:9 — “these stones” (moderate). Answers §3:15 — τότε ἀφίησιν αὐτόν (“then he … him”, 3:15; 4:11 only) (high for the wording, verified; moderate as design). Answers §2:2, 8, 11 — προσκυνέω (“to worship”): the magi worship; Herod pretends; the devil demands it (high; overview). Answers §1:23 — “God with us” and Massah (moderate, synthetic). Plants §6:13; 26:41 — πειρασμός (“temptation”) (moderate–high, verified). Plants §7:9–11 — son, bread, stone (moderate–high, verified). Plants §14:33; 28:9, 17 — worship given to Jesus (high; overview). Plants §16:1; 19:3; 22:18, 35 — the leaders “test” him (high, verified). Plants §16:21–23 — “show” and “Satan” (moderate–high, verified). Plants §17:1 — the high mountain (moderate–high, verified). Plants §20:28 — διακονέω (“to serve”) (moderate). Plants §26:53–54 — the angels he will not summon (moderate). Plants §27:40, 43 — εἰ υἱὸς εἶ τοῦ θεοῦ (“if You are the Son of God”) at 4:3, 6; 27:40 only (SBLGNT phrase search) [T], verified, with Θεοῦ εἰμι υἱός (“I am the Son of God”, 27:43) (high; overview). Plants §28:18 — authority given by the Father, not by the devil (high; overview).

Translations. Both versions render πειράζω (“to tempt, test”) “tempted”/“tempter” at 4:1, 3 and “testing”/“test” at 16:1; 19:3; 22:18, 35 [T], checked. The chain from the tempter to the leaders is lost in both. At 4:10 NASB95 “Go, Satan!” and ESV “Be gone, Satan!” both follow NA28; the majority text adds ὀπίσω μου (“behind Me”) from 16:23 (NA28 apparatus) [T], so the KJV’s “Get thee hence” (no “behind me”) and some majority-text readers will differ; the echo of 16:23 is in the Greek either way. Pulpit divergence: none bearing; but the preacher who wants the 16:1–22:35 chain heard must say “tested” at 4:1.

Difficulty. Could the Son of God have sinned? The unit does not raise the question. It presents real tests with real hunger (ἐπείνασεν, “He became hungry”, 4:2) and real answers from Scripture [T]. The dogmatic question (peccability) should not govern the exposition [I].

Who leads? The Spirit leads (4:1), and the disciples are taught to pray μὴ εἰσενέγκῃς ἡμᾶς εἰς πειρασμόν (“do not lead us into temptation”, 6:13) [T]. Options: (a) the Son goes where the disciples ask not to be led, and goes for them [I]; (b) the petition asks for deliverance in testing, not exemption [S]. Take 6:13 and 26:41 together.

Pitfall. What it looks like: “Three ways to beat temptation: know your Bible, quote it back, and the devil will leave.” Why wrong: the unit is about who Jesus is, not first a method. Each test begins “If You are the Son of God”, and each answer is a word spoken to Israel in the wilderness, where Israel failed. The Son succeeds where the son Israel fell (Deut 8:5). The devil also quotes Scripture (4:6). Corrective: preach the Son’s obedience as the ground of the disciples’ hope, and only then as their pattern. The test returns at the cross in the same words (27:40), and he passes it by not coming down.

Book-overview note: Confirms from the text: the three Deuteronomy answers; προσκυνέω (“to worship”); εἰ υἱὸς εἶ τοῦ θεοῦ (“if You are the Son of God”) at 4:3, 6; 27:40 only; ἁγία πόλις (“holy city”) 4:5 → 27:53; Σατανᾶς (“Satan”) 4:10 → 16:23. Contradicts / refines: the overview’s “one deliberate departure from every text” at 4:10 is shared with Luke 4:8, so it belongs to the double tradition, not to Matthew’s own hand; and the replacing verb is the Decalogue’s προσκυνήσεις … λατρεύσῃς (“you shall worship … serve”, Exod 20:5; 23:24; Deut 5:9), which the overview should name. Extends: (1) Deut 8:2–5: led, wilderness, testing, hunger, and “son”; (2) “forty days and forty nights” is Matthew’s addition, and the Swete phrase belongs to the flood, Moses’ fasts (with Deut 9:18, the calf) and Elijah; (3) πειράζω (“to test”) passes from the devil to the leaders (16:1; 19:3; 22:18, 35), verified; (4) δείκνυμι (“to show”) 4:8 → 16:21; (5) ὄρος ὑψηλόν (“high mountain”) 4:8 → 17:1 only; (6) son–bread–stone 4:3 → 7:9 only; (7) τότε ἀφίησιν αὐτόν (“then … him”) 3:15 → 4:11 only; (8) a new Move-1 source, Deut 34:1–4 (the LORD “showed” Moses all the land).

7. Matthew 4:12–25 — Light in Galilee of the Nations; “Come Behind Me”

Position. Q1: the Son has been declared (3:17) and tested (4:1–11), but has not yet spoken in public or gathered anyone [T]. Q2: this unit turns the prologue into the ministry. It explains the place (Capernaum, by Isaiah’s word), gives the proclamation (4:17), calls the first four, and summarises the work in teaching, preaching and healing (4:23) that chapters 5–9 will fill out [T]. It holds the fifth and last fulfilment formula of the opening (4:14; after 1:22; 2:15, 17, 23) and the first of the two ἀπὸ τότε ἤρξατο (“from that time Jesus began”) hinges (4:17; 16:21) [T], verified (overview). It must come here: the mountain of 5:1 needs the crowds of 4:25 and the disciples of 4:18–22 (5:1 τοὺς ὄχλους … οἱ μαθηταί, “the crowds … His disciples”) [T].

Structure.

Verses Section
4:12–16 John handed over; the withdrawal; Capernaum; Isa 8:23–9:1
4:17 Ἀπὸ τότε ἤρξατο (“from that time … began”): the proclamation
4:18–22 Two pairs of brothers called
4:23–25 Summary: teaching, proclaiming, healing; fame in Syria; crowds from five regions

The two call scenes are built in parallel: εἶδεν … δύο ἀδελφούς (“He saw two brothers”, 4:18, 21) … οἱ δὲ εὐθέως ἀφέντες … ἠκολούθησαν αὐτῷ (“immediately they left … and followed Him”, 4:20, 22) [T]. 4:23 is repeated almost word for word at 9:35, and the two verses frame chapters 5–9 [T] (overview; checked).

Text-first findings.

  • John is “handed over” first. Ἀκούσας δὲ ὅτι Ἰωάννης παρεδόθη ἀνεχώρησεν (“Now when He heard that John had been taken into custody, He withdrew”, 4:12) [T]. παραδίδωμι (“to hand over”) occurs in 31 verses of Matthew (SBLGNT), and from 10:4 on it is the word for what will be done to Jesus (17:22; 20:18, 19; 26:2, 15–25, 45–48; 27:2, 26) [T], verified. The forerunner’s fate is told in the verb of the passion before Jesus has said a word in public. [T]/[I], high. The pattern repeats: Ἀκούσας δέ … ἀνεχώρησεν (“now when He heard … He withdrew”) at 14:13, after John’s death [T]. ἀναχωρέω (“to withdraw”) stands in 10 verses (SBLGNT), five of them in 2:12–4:12 (the magi, the family, Jesus), each in the shadow of a ruler’s threat or John’s arrest [T], verified.
  • Nazareth left, Capernaum settled. καταλιπὼν τὴν Ναζαρὰ ἐλθὼν κατῴκησεν εἰς Καφαρναούμ (“leaving Nazareth, He came and settled in Capernaum”, 4:13) [T]. ἐλθὼν κατῴκησεν (“came and settled”) occurs only at 2:23 and 4:13 (SBLGNT phrase search) [T], verified. Each settlement is followed by a fulfilment formula (2:23; 4:14) [T]. The first named the son “Nazarene”; the second sets him in the tribal lands of Zebulun and Naphtali [T]. The form Ναζαρά (“Nazareth”) occurs only here in Matthew; 2:23 has Ναζαρέτ (SBLGNT, lemma search) [T], verified; NA28 agrees, with variants (apparatus) [T].
  • The fifth formula follows the Hebrew where the Greek fails. The Greek of Isa 8:23 begins τοῦτο πρῶτον πίε / ποίει, ταχὺ ποίει (“drink / do this first, do it quickly”, Swete 9:1 / Rahlfs 8:23), a reading of the Hebrew’s כָּעֵת הָרִאשׁוֹן הֵקַל (“in the former time he brought into contempt”) that loses the sense [T], checked. Matthew omits it and gives only the names, as the Hebrew’s list does [T]. Γῆ Ζαβουλὼν καὶ γῆ Νεφθαλίμ (“the land of Zebulun and the land of Naphtali”, 4:15) repeats “land” as the WLC does (אַרְצָה … אַרְצָה, “land … land”); Swete and Rahlfs have χώρα … γῆ (“region … land”) [T]. ὁδὸν θαλάσσης (“by the way of the sea”) is the Hebrew דֶּרֶךְ הַיָּם (“the way of the sea”), present in Rahlfs and absent from Swete [T], checked (overview). Then εἶδεν (“saw”, past) and ἀνέτειλεν (“dawned”, past) follow the Hebrew perfects רָאוּ (“they saw”) and נָגַהּ (“it shone”) against the Greek ἴδετε (“see!”, imperative) and λάμψει (“will shine”, future) [T], checked in Swete and Rahlfs. Matthew’s tense is the Hebrew’s: the light has already dawned. High.
  • “Sitting in darkness”: the Hebrew word, the Psalm’s phrase. ὁ λαὸς ὁ καθήμενος ἐν σκοτίᾳ (“the people who were sitting in darkness”, 4:16) replaces the Hebrew הַהֹלְכִים (“who walk”) and the Greek πορευόμενος (“going”) [T]. The second line’s τοῖς καθημένοις (“those who were sitting”) renders the Hebrew יֹשְׁבֵי (“those who dwell / sit”), where Swete and Rahlfs have οἱ κατοικοῦντες (“those who dwell”) [T]. So Matthew’s participle is a fair rendering of the Hebrew in the second line, carried back into the first [T]/[I]. καθημένους ἐν σκότει (“sitting in darkness”) occurs in Swete only at Isa 42:7 and Ps 106:10 (MT 107:10), and Ps 106:10 continues καὶ σκιᾷ θανάτου (“and the shadow of death”) [T], verified (overview names both). Matthew’s ἐν χώρᾳ καὶ σκιᾷ θανάτου (“in the land and shadow of death”) agrees with Rahlfs (ἐν χώρᾳ καὶ σκιᾷ θανάτου) against Swete (ἐν χώρᾳ σκιᾷ θανάτου, without καί, “and”) [T], checked. Not in the overview.
  • The light “rose”. φῶς ἀνέτειλεν αὐτοῖς (“upon them a Light dawned”, 4:16) [T]. ἀνατέλλω (“to rise, dawn”) is the verb of the noun ἀνατολή (“east, rising”) at 2:1, 2, 9, where the magi saw the star ἐν τῇ ἀνατολῇ (“in the east / at its rising”) [T]. ἀνατέλλω occurs in Matthew at 4:16; 5:45; 13:6 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. φῶς ἀνέτειλεν (“light has risen”) occurs in Swete only at Ps 96:11 (MT 97:11), φῶς ἀνέτειλεν τῷ δικαίῳ (“light has risen for the righteous”), where the Hebrew reads זָרֻעַ (“is sown”) [T], verified; Rahlfs agrees [T]. Synthetic, uncertain–moderate as a source; moderate–high that 4:16 answers the star’s rising at 2:2.
  • Galilee “of the nations”: the first ἔθνη. Γαλιλαία τῶν ἐθνῶν (“Galilee of the Gentiles”, 4:15) [T]. ἔθνος (“nation, Gentile”) occurs in 14 verses of Matthew (SBLGNT); 4:15 is the first and 28:19, πάντα τὰ ἔθνη (“all the nations”), the last [T], verified. Both are in Galilee [T]. The overview’s row (4:15 → 28:19) is confirmed by the lemma’s distribution [T].
  • Mark’s “fulfilled” becomes the narrator’s formula. Mark has Jesus say Πεπλήρωται ὁ καιρὸς … μετανοεῖτε καὶ πιστεύετε ἐν τῷ εὐαγγελίῳ (“The time is fulfilled … repent and believe in the gospel”, Mark 1:15) [T]. Matthew drops both clauses from Jesus’ mouth and gives the “fulfilment” to the narrator (ἵνα πληρωθῇ, “to fulfil”, 4:14) [T]. Jesus’ sentence is the one John spoke (3:2) [T], verified (see unit 5). εὐαγγέλιον (“gospel”) never stands alone in Matthew: τὸ εὐαγγέλιον τῆς βασιλείας (“the gospel of the kingdom”) at 4:23; 9:35; 24:14, and τὸ εὐαγγέλιον τοῦτο (“this gospel”) at 26:13 (SBLGNT; lemma 4 verses) [T], verified. The third is preached “to all the nations” (24:14) [T].
  • “Come behind me”: the call, and the undoing. Δεῦτε ὀπίσω μου, καὶ ποιήσω ὑμᾶς ἁλιεῖς ἀνθρώπων (“Follow Me, and I will make you fishers of men”, 4:19) [T]. ὀπίσω (“behind”) first stood in John’s mouth, of Jesus (3:11) [T], verified (6 verses). The response is ἀφέντες … ἠκολούθησαν αὐτῷ (“they left … and followed Him”, 4:20, 22) [T]. The participle ἀφέντες (“leaving”) occurs in Matthew at 4:20, 22; 22:22; 26:56 (SBLGNT phrase search), and at 26:56 it is the disciples who leave him [T], verified (overview’s reversal confirmed). The sons of Zebedee leave τὸν πατέρα αὐτῶν (“their father”, 4:22) [T]; their mother returns at 20:20 and 27:56 [T] (Ζεβεδαῖος, “Zebedee”, 5 verses, SBLGNT, verified).
  • The summary is the book’s programme. διδάσκων … κηρύσσων … θεραπεύων (“teaching … proclaiming … healing”, 4:23) [T]. The three participles are filled in order: teaching (5–7), healing (8–9), and the authority passed on (10:1) [T]/[I]. μαλακία (“sickness”) occurs only at 4:23; 9:35; 10:1 (SBLGNT) [T], verified (overview). σεληνιαζομένους (“epileptics”, 4:24) returns only at 17:15 [T], verified. ἐν τῷ λαῷ (“among the people”, 4:23) takes up ὁ λαός (“the people”, 4:16) and 1:21, “his people” [T]. The crowds come from Galilee, Decapolis, Jerusalem, Judea and πέραν τοῦ Ἰορδάνου (“beyond the Jordan”, 4:25), the phrase of the citation (4:15) [T]; it occurs at 4:15, 25 and 19:1 only (SBLGNT) [T], verified.

Synoptic check. Mark 1:14–20 gives the arrest, the summary of preaching and the two calls [T]. Matthew adds: the move from Nazareth to Capernaum and the citation (4:13–16), and ἀπὸ τότε ἤρξατο (“from that time … began”, 4:17) [T]. Alters: Mark’s κηρύσσων τὸ εὐαγγέλιον τοῦ θεοῦ (“proclaiming the gospel of God”, Mark 1:14) becomes “the kingdom of heaven” in Jesus’ mouth (4:17) and “the gospel of the kingdom” in the summary (4:23) [T]. Mark’s ποιήσω ὑμᾶς γενέσθαι ἁλιεῖς (“I will make you become fishers”, Mark 1:17) loses γενέσθαι (“become”), which some witnesses restore (NA28 apparatus) [T]. Mark’s sons of Zebedee leave their father μετὰ τῶν μισθωτῶν (“with the hired men”, Mark 1:20); Matthew has them leave τὸ πλοῖον καὶ τὸν πατέρα (“the boat and their father”) [T]. The summary (4:23–25) gathers Mark 1:39 and 3:7–8. Mark 3:8 lists Idumea and Tyre and Sidon; Matthew has none of these, and has Decapolis (Δεκάπολις, “Decapolis”, only here in Matthew) and Syria (Συρία, “Syria”, only here) [T], verified.

Tool 11.

Isa 8:23–9:1 (Swete 9:1–2; English 9:1–2) → 4:15–16 (high — formula quotation). Source context: Isa 8 ends in darkness: those who turn from the law and testimony look to the earth, וְהִנֵּה צָרָה וַחֲשֵׁכָה (“and behold, distress and darkness”, 8:22) [T]. Then the turn: the land of Zebulun and Naphtali, first humbled (by the Assyrian invasion, [S]), will later be made glorious (8:23), and the people walking in darkness see a great light (9:1) [T]. The reason is given in 9:5–6: כִּי־יֶלֶד יֻלַּד־לָנוּ בֵּן נִתַּן־לָנוּ (“for a child is born to us, a son is given to us”), with names including אֵל גִּבּוֹר (“Mighty God”), on עַל־כִּסֵּא דָוִד (“on the throne of David”) [T]. Book usage: 1:23 cited Isa 7:14 (Immanuel). The name returns at Isa 8:8 and 8:10, עִמָּנוּ אֵל (“God is with us”), Swete μεθ᾽ ἡμῶν ὁ θεός (“God with us”), the phrase Matthew used to translate the name (1:23) [T], checked. Both Isaiah citations of the prologue come from one section, Isa 7:14–9:6, in which a child is promised, named “God with us”, and enthroned on David’s throne [T] for the texts; [I], high, that Matthew reads them as one. OT-to-OT: Isa 42:6–7, the Servant as לְאוֹר גּוֹיִם (“a light to the nations”), sent to bring out יֹשְׁבֵי חֹשֶׁךְ (“those who sit in darkness”) [T]; the Servant is cited in full at 12:18–21, ending “in His name the Gentiles will hope” [T]. Divergence: see the findings. Summary: Matthew omits the Greek’s misreading, keeps “land … land” and the past tenses with the Hebrew, has ὁδὸν θαλάσσης (“way of the sea”) and ἐν χώρᾳ καὶ σκιᾷ (“in the land and shadow”) with Rahlfs against Swete, and uses καθήμενος (“sitting”) from the Hebrew’s יֹשְׁבֵי (“dwellers / sitters”) with the wording of Ps 106:10 and Isa 42:7 [T]. NA28 reads ἐν σκότει (“in darkness”) with the Greek of Isa 9:1; SBLGNT reads ἐν σκοτίᾳ (“in darkness”) (NA28 apparatus: σκοτία א¹ B D W) [T]. Classification: a fresh rendering from the Hebrew, using Greek wording where it is good; the overview’s “Rahlfs vs Swete” case is confirmed and extended by a second Rahlfs agreement (καί, “and”). Nearest: the WLC for sense and tense; Rahlfs for wording. What it adds: the Messiah’s ministry begins in the region first struck when the northern tribes went into exile; the darkness is the exile’s, and the light is the child of Isa 9:5–6 [I], high.

Jer 16:16 → 4:19 (moderate). Move 1: הִנְנִי שֹׁלֵחַ לְדַוָּגִים רַבִּים (“behold, I am sending for many fishermen”, ketiv לדוגים) … וְדִיגוּם (“and they will fish for them”) [T]; Swete and Rahlfs ἰδοὺ ἐγὼ ἀποστέλλω τοὺς ἁλεεῖς τοὺς πολλούς … καὶ ἁλιεύσουσιν αὐτούς (“behold I send the many fishermen … and they will fish for them”) [T], checked. In context the fishers gather Israel for judgement (16:16–18), but the verses before promise a new exodus “from the land of the north” (16:14–15) [T]. The image is shared; the sense in Jeremiah is double. Moderate, and the preacher should not import the judgement.

Internal: Answers §2:22–23 — the Galilean withdrawal and the settling (ἐλθὼν κατῴκησεν, “came and settled”, 2:23; 4:13 only) (high, verified). Answers §2:2 — the rising light (moderate–high). Answers §1:23 — Immanuel’s section of Isaiah (high). Answers §3:2 — the same proclamation (high, verified). Answers §3:11 — ὀπίσω (“behind”) (moderate–high, verified). Plants §9:35; 10:1 — the summary and μαλακία (“sickness”) (high, verified). Plants §14:13 — “when He heard … He withdrew”, after John’s death (high). Plants §16:21 — the second ἀπὸ τότε ἤρξατο (high; overview). Plants §16:24 — ὀπίσω μου (“after Me”) (high). Plants §17:22; 20:18–19; 26:2 — παραδίδωμι (“to hand over”) (high, verified). Plants §20:20; 27:56 — Zebedee’s family (moderate). Plants §24:14; 28:19 — the gospel of the kingdom and the nations (high, verified). Plants §26:56 — ἀφέντες (“leaving”) reversed (high, verified). Plants §5:14 — φῶς (“light”): the light that dawned on the people is then given to the disciples, ὑμεῖς ἐστε τὸ φῶς τοῦ κόσμου (“You are the light of the world”) (φῶς, 6 verses, SBLGNT, verified; moderate–high, synthetic).

Translations. 4:12: NASB95 “had been taken into custody”, ESV “had been arrested” [T], checked. Both hide παρεδόθη (“was handed over”), which both render “delivered (over / up)” for Jesus at 17:22; 20:18; 26:2 [T]. 4:15: both “Galilee of the Gentiles”, and both “all the nations” (NASB95) / “all nations” (ESV) at 28:19 [T], so the ἔθνη (“nations”) frame is lost in English. 4:16: NASB95 “sitting … sitting” keeps καθήμενος (“sitting”); the ESV has “dwelling … dwelling”, which is the Hebrew’s sense but loses the link to Ps 107:10 and Isa 42:7 [T]. 4:19: both “Follow Me”, where the Greek is “come behind Me” [T]. Pulpit divergence: the ESV’s “arrested” (4:12) and “dwelling” (4:16) each hide a Greek link. The preacher should say “handed over” at 4:12 if the passion parallel is to be heard.

Pitfall. What it looks like: “Jesus calls us to be fishers of men: here is how to evangelise.” Why wrong: the promise is ποιήσω ὑμᾶς (“I will make you”), and the call is first δεῦτε ὀπίσω μου (“come behind Me”). The unit gives the disciples nothing to do yet but follow; they are not sent until 10:5 and not commissioned to the nations until 28:19. Corrective: preach the call as an act of Jesus’ authority and a promise of his making, set in the light that has already dawned (4:16).

Book-overview note: Confirms from the text: Isa 8:23–9:1 with ὁδὸν θαλάσσης (“way of the sea”) in Rahlfs not Swete; 4:23 = 9:35 → 10:1; ἀφέντες (“leaving”) 4:20, 22 → 26:56; 4:15 → 28:19 (now with ἔθνος, “nation”, first and last). Extends: (1) a second Rahlfs-against-Swete agreement in the same citation, ἐν χώρᾳ καὶ σκιᾷ θανάτου (“in the land and shadow of death”); (2) Matthew’s past tenses and “land … land” are the Hebrew’s, and the Greek’s τοῦτο πρῶτον πίε (“drink this first”) is dropped; (3) καθήμενος (“sitting”) is not “in neither” — it renders the Hebrew’s יֹשְׁבֵי (“dwellers, sitters”) in the second line, carried into the first (refines the map row); (4) both Isaiah citations of the prologue (1:23; 4:15–16) come from Isa 7:14–9:6, where Swete 8:8, 10 already has μεθ᾽ ἡμῶν ὁ θεός (“God with us”); (5) παρεδόθη (“handed over”, 4:12) → the passion; (6) ἐλθὼν κατῴκησεν (“came and settled”) 2:23; 4:13 only; (7) φῶς ἀνέτειλεν (“light rose”) with 2:2’s ἀνατολή and Ps 96:11 Swete (uncertain–moderate); (8) Mark’s “the time is fulfilled” becomes the narrator’s formula; (9) Jer 16:16 behind 4:19 (moderate), which the overview does not list.

8. Matthew 5:1–12 — The Blessed ⭐

Position. Q1: 4:23 promised teaching, proclaiming and healing, and 4:25 gathered crowds from every region of Israel [T]. The disciples have been called but not yet taught (4:18–22) [T]. Q2: this unit opens the first of the five discourses with the teaching 4:23 promised. Ἰδὼν δὲ τοὺς ὄχλους (“When Jesus saw the crowds”, 5:1) ties it to 4:25 [T]. It must come first in the Sermon, before any demand: the Sermon begins by pronouncing who is blessed, and the righteousness of 5:20 and the perfection of 5:48 are addressed to them [T]/[I]. The kingdom announced at 4:17 as “at hand” is here said to belong (ἐστιν, “is”) to the poor in spirit and the persecuted (5:3, 10) [T].

Structure.

Verses Section
5:1–2 Setting: the mountain; seated; disciples come; “he opened his mouth and taught”
5:3–6 Beatitudes 1–4, ending τὴν δικαιοσύνην (“righteousness”, 5:6)
5:7–10 Beatitudes 5–8, ending ἕνεκεν δικαιοσύνης (“for the sake of righteousness”, 5:10)
5:11–12 The ninth, in the second person: “you”, persecuted “because of Me”; the prophets before you

Devices [T]: (1) an inclusio: ὅτι αὐτῶν ἐστιν ἡ βασιλεία τῶν οὐρανῶν (“for theirs is the kingdom of heaven”) stands at 5:3 and 5:10 only (SBLGNT phrase search), verified; the six promises between are future (5:4–9). (2) Two halves of four, each closed by δικαιοσύνη (“righteousness”, 5:6, 10). (3) Each half has 36 words in the SBLGNT (5:3–6; 5:7–10), counted by script [T]; the NA28 text is the same in these verses [T], checked. The balance is [T] as a count; that it is designed is [I], moderate. (4) The shift to the second person at 5:11 (μακάριοί ἐστε, “Blessed are you”) turns the description into an address [T].

Text-first findings.

  • The setting: a mountain, a seat, an opened mouth. ἀνέβη εἰς τὸ ὄρος (“He went up on the mountain”, 5:1): the exact aorist occurs in Swete three times, all of Moses at Sinai; the setting carries the echo, and Torah follows (5:17–48) [S: audit] (Claim 2: confirmed with nuance, moderate). Text-first extension: Swete Ps 23:3 (MT 24:3) asks τίς ἀναβήσεται εἰς τὸ ὄρος τοῦ κυρίου; (“who shall ascend the mountain of the Lord?”), and its answer is καθαρὸς τῇ καρδίᾳ (“pure in heart”, 23:4), the phrase of 5:8 [T], verified (phrase search; Rahlfs agrees). The question of the psalm Matthew uses at 5:8 is the action of 5:1. Synthetic, moderate. καθίσαντος αὐτοῦ (“after He sat down”, 5:1) is the teacher’s posture; καθίζω (“to sit”) next describes the Son of Man on his throne (19:28; 25:31) and the scribes on Moses’ seat (23:2) (8 verses, SBLGNT) [T], verified. ἀνοίξας τὸ στόμα αὐτοῦ (“He opened His mouth”, 5:2) stands only here and at 17:27 (the fish’s mouth) (SBLGNT phrase search) [T], verified; the formal opening marks a solemn utterance [I].
  • The kingdom “is” theirs; the rest “will be”. ὅτι αὐτῶν ἐστιν (“for theirs is”, 5:3, 10) against αὐτοὶ παρακληθήσονται … κληρονομήσουσι … χορτασθήσονται … ἐλεηθήσονται … ὄψονται … κληθήσονται (“they shall be comforted … inherit … be satisfied … receive mercy … see … be called”, 5:4–9) [T]. The emphatic αὐτοί (“they”) stands in every future promise [T]. The kingdom is present possession; its gifts are still to come [I], high.
  • Poor, mourning, meek: Isaiah 61 as the frame. πτωχοί (“poor”), πενθοῦντες (“mourn”) and παρακληθήσονται (“shall be comforted”) follow Isa 61:1–2, which Matthew moves towards against Luke (Luke 6:21, κλαίοντες … γελάσετε, “weeping … you will laugh”) [S: audit] (Claim 13: confirmed with nuance; Isa 61 the frame for 5:3–5, high). Accepted, including the audit’s note that the Hebrew עֲנָוִים (“humble, poor”) stands behind Isa 61:1 (πτωχοῖς, “to the poor”) and Ps 37:11 (πραεῖς, “the meek”) [S: audit]; checked in the WLC [T]. Text-first extension: παρακληθήσονται (“shall be comforted”) answers Rachel, who οὐκ ἤθελεν παρακληθῆναι (“refused to be comforted”, 2:18); the passive of The passive of παρακαλέω (“to comfort, be comforted”) occurs in Matthew only at 2:18 and 5:4; the active (“to beg, urge”) is common (9 verses in all, SBLGNT) [T], verified (see unit 4). πενθέω (“to mourn”) returns only at 9:15, the wedding guests who cannot mourn while the bridegroom is with them [T], verified (2 verses).
  • The meek: the disciples, the teacher, the King. οἱ πραεῖς (“the gentle”, 5:5) [T]. πραΰς (“gentle, meek”) occurs in Matthew only at 5:5; 11:29 (πραΰς εἰμι, “I am gentle”); 21:5 (the King coming πραΰς, “gentle”) (SBLGNT) [T], verified (overview). The third beatitude describes Jesus before it describes his disciples. Synthetic, verified; high for the chain. κληρονομήσουσι τὴν γῆν (“shall inherit the earth”, 5:5): κληρονομέω (“to inherit”) returns at 19:29 (“inherit eternal life”) and 25:34 (κληρονομήσατε … βασιλείαν, “inherit the kingdom”) [T], verified (3 verses).
  • Hunger and thirst: the wilderness Son, and the feedings. οἱ πεινῶντες καὶ διψῶντες τὴν δικαιοσύνην (“those who hunger and thirst for righteousness”, 5:6) [T]. The one who taught it ἐπείνασεν (“became hungry”, 4:2) [T]. πεινάω (“to hunger”) and διψάω (“to thirst”) stand together again only at 25:35, 37, 42, 44, where the King is the one hungry and thirsty (διψάω, 5 verses, SBLGNT) [T], verified. χορτασθήσονται (“they shall be satisfied”) is the verb of the feedings: χορτάζω (“to fill, satisfy”) occurs only at 5:6; 14:20; 15:33; 15:37 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. Synthetic, moderate: the promise of 5:6 is first kept in bread (14:20; 15:37) [I]. Luke has οἱ πεινῶντες νῦν (“you who hunger now”, Luke 6:21) without “thirst” or “righteousness” [T].
  • Mercy received by the merciful. οἱ ἐλεήμονες, ὅτι αὐτοὶ ἐλεηθήσονται (“the merciful, for they shall receive mercy”, 5:7) [T]. ἐλεήμων (“merciful”) occurs only here (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The verb (MorphGNT lemma ἐλεάω, “to have mercy”) stands in 7 verses: 5:7; then the cry ἐλέησον (“have mercy”) of the blind, the Canaanite woman and the epileptic’s father (9:27; 15:22; 17:15; 20:30, 31); and 18:33, οὐκ ἔδει καὶ σὲ ἐλεῆσαι … ὡς κἀγὼ σὲ ἠλέησα; (“should you not also have had mercy … in the same way that I had mercy on you?”) [T], verified. 18:33 is the beatitude’s negative, told as a parable [I], high. ἔλεος (“mercy”) stands at 9:13; 12:7 (Hos 6:6) and 23:23 [T], verified.
  • Sons of God, called by Hosea’s word. οἱ εἰρηνοποιοί, ὅτι αὐτοὶ υἱοὶ θεοῦ κληθήσονται (“the peacemakers, for they shall be called sons of God”, 5:9) [T]. εἰρηνοποιός (“peacemaker”) occurs only here (SBLGNT) [T], verified. κληθήσονται … υἱοὶ θεοῦ (“they shall be called … sons of God”) occurs in Swete only at Hos 1:10 (MT 2:1): κληθήσονται καὶ αὐτοὶ υἱοὶ θεοῦ ζῶντος (“they too shall be called sons of the living God”) (regex search) [T], verified. Not in the overview. The Son has just been named υἱὸς θεοῦ (“Son of God”) by the Voice and tested on it (3:17; 4:3, 6) [T]. The plural returns at 5:45, ὅπως γένησθε υἱοὶ τοῦ πατρὸς ὑμῶν (“so that you may be sons of your Father”), for those who love their enemies [T]. Synthetic, moderate–high: the peacemakers share the Son’s name.
  • Persecuted “for righteousness” and “because of Me”. οἱ δεδιωγμένοι ἕνεκεν δικαιοσύνης (“those who have been persecuted for the sake of righteousness”, 5:10) is restated in 5:11 as ἕνεκεν ἐμοῦ (“because of Me”) [T]. The parallel sets Jesus where righteousness stood [T]/[I], high. ἕνεκεν ἐμοῦ (“for My sake”) recurs at 10:18, 39; 16:25 (SBLGNT phrase search) [T], verified. διώκω (“to persecute”) occurs in 6 verses (5:10, 11, 12, 44; 10:23; 23:34), and at 23:34 Jesus tells the scribes and Pharisees καὶ διώξετε ἀπὸ πόλεως εἰς πόλιν (“and persecute from city to city”) the prophets he sends [T], verified. So 5:12, “they persecuted the prophets who were before you”, is answered by the woes (23:29–37) [T]. ὀνειδίσωσιν (“insult”, 5:11) returns at 27:44, where the robbers ὠνείδιζον αὐτόν (“were insulting Him”) [T] (ὀνειδίζω, 3 verses, SBLGNT, verified). What the disciples will suffer “because of Me”, he suffers first. Synthetic, verified; high for the chain.
  • Righteousness closes each half. δικαιοσύνη (“righteousness”, 5:6, 10) is the second and third of its seven uses (3:15; 5:6, 10, 20; 6:1, 33; 21:32, SBLGNT) [T], verified (see unit 5). After 3:15, where Jesus “fulfils all righteousness”, the first two uses are a hunger for it and a persecution for it — not an achievement of it [T]/[I]. The demand of 5:20 comes after both [T].

Synoptic check. Luke 6:20–23 has four beatitudes in the second person, followed by four woes (6:24–26), on a level place (Luke 6:17) [T]. Matthew differs: nine beatitudes, the first eight in the third person; a mountain; πτωχοὶ τῷ πνεύματι (“poor in spirit”) where Luke has οἱ πτωχοί (“the poor”); “kingdom of heaven” where Luke has “kingdom of God”; “mourn … comforted” for Luke’s “weep … laugh”; “hunger and thirst for righteousness” for “hunger now”; and ἕνεκεν ἐμοῦ (“because of Me”) for ἕνεκα τοῦ υἱοῦ τοῦ ἀνθρώπου (“for the sake of the Son of Man”, Luke 6:22) [T]. The meek, the merciful, the pure in heart, the peacemakers and the persecuted for righteousness (5:5, 7–10) are Matthew’s alone [T]. Luke’s comfort stands in a woe, ἀπέχετε τὴν παράκλησιν ὑμῶν (“you have received your comfort”, Luke 6:24); Matthew’s in a blessing (5:4) [T]. Matthew’s additions are drawn from the Psalms and Isaiah (Ps 37:11; Ps 24:4; Isa 61:1–3) and from Hosea’s “sons of the living God” (Hos 1:10 Swete) [S: audit]/[T].

Tool 11.

Isa 61:1–3, 7 → 5:3–5 (high, as the frame — audited) [S: audit]. Accepted as audited (Claim 13). Text-first extension: Swete Isa 61:3 continues καὶ κληθήσονται γενεαὶ δικαιοσύνης (“and they shall be called generations of righteousness”) [T], so the passage that comforts the mourners also gives them a “called” name and “righteousness” — the vocabulary of 5:9–10 [T]; synthetic, uncertain–moderate. The servant figure of Isa 61:1, anointed by רוּחַ אֲדֹנָי יְהוִה (“the Spirit of the Lord GOD”), has just been seen in the Spirit’s descent (3:16) [T]/[I].

Ps 37:11 (Swete 36:11) → 5:5 (high — near-verbatim). Source context: Ps 37 is a wisdom psalm: do not fret over evildoers; they will be cut off, and those who wait for the LORD will inherit the land (37:1–11) [T]. The refrain יִירְשׁוּ־אָרֶץ (“they will inherit the land”) runs through it (37:9, 11, 22, 29, 34) [T] (WLC 37:9, 11, 29 read). Book usage: the Psalter’s first wisdom psalm in Matthew [I]. OT-to-OT: the land promise of Gen 15:7 and Num 14:31, where Swete has κληρονομήσουσιν τὴν γῆν (“they shall inherit the land”) of the wilderness children [T]. Divergence: Swete and Rahlfs Ps 36:11 read οἱ δὲ πραεῖς κληρονομήσουσιν γῆν (“but the meek shall inherit land”), without the article [T], checked. Matthew’s τὴν γῆν (“the earth”) has the article [T]. Swete Ps 36:9, two verses earlier in the same psalm, reads αὐτοὶ κληρονομήσουσιν τὴν γῆν (“they shall inherit the land”) — with Matthew’s αὐτοί (“they”) and his article; Rahlfs 36:9 has αὐτοὶ κληρονομήσουσιν γῆν, without the article [T], checked in both. κληρονομήσουσιν τὴν γῆν occurs in Swete at Num 14:31; Ps 36:9; Isa 60:21 (phrase search) [T], verified; Isa 61:7 has the words in another order [S: audit]. Classification: Matthew’s line is Ps 37:11’s subject with Ps 37:9’s predicate, in Swete’s form [I], moderate–high. Nearest: Swete. What it adds: the gentle do not seize; they wait for the LORD and are given the land [I]. γῆ (“land, earth”) is open: the land of Israel, or the whole earth (compare 28:18, ἐν οὐρανῷ καὶ ἐπὶ τῆς γῆς, “in heaven and on earth”) [T]/[I].

Ps 24:4 (Swete 23:4) → 5:8 (high for purity; moderate for “see God” — audited) [S: audit]. Accepted as audited (Claim 18). Text-first extensions: (1) the psalm’s question, τίς ἀναβήσεται εἰς τὸ ὄρος τοῦ κυρίου; (“who shall ascend the mountain of the Lord?”, Swete 23:3), is enacted by Jesus at 5:1 (above) [T], moderate. (2) Swete 23:4 begins ἀθῷος χερσίν (“innocent in hands”) before καθαρὸς τῇ καρδίᾳ (“pure in heart”) [T]; ἀθῷος χερσίν occurs in Swete only there (phrase search), verified. ἀθῷος (“innocent”) occurs in Matthew only at 27:4 and 27:24, where Pilate washes his hands and says Ἀθῷός εἰμι (“I am innocent”) [T], verified. Synthetic, uncertain–moderate: the verse that blesses the pure in heart also names the “innocent hands” Pilate claims and does not have [I]. (3) Swete 23:5 promises ἐλεημοσύνην (“mercy, alms”) where the WLC has צְדָקָה (“righteousness”) [T], checked. Righteousness (5:6), mercy (5:7) and purity of heart (5:8) stand in that order in Matthew; Ps 24:4–5 has purity, then righteousness (Hebrew) or mercy (Greek) [T]; uncertain as design.

Hos 1:10 Swete (MT 2:1) → 5:9 (moderate–high — not in the overview or the audit). Source context: Hosea 1 names the children לֹא עַמִּי (“Not My People”); 2:1 reverses it: בִּמְקוֹם אֲשֶׁר־יֵאָמֵר לָהֶם לֹא־עַמִּי אַתֶּם יֵאָמֵר לָהֶם בְּנֵי אֵל־חָי (“in the place where it was said to them, ‘You are not My people’, it will be said to them, ‘Sons of the living God’”) [T]. The next verse gathers Judah and Israel under one head (2:2; Swete 1:11) [T]. Book usage: Hosea was cited at 2:15 (Hos 11:1, through the Hebrew), and will be at 9:13 and 12:7 (Hos 6:6) [T]. OT-to-OT: “my son” of Hos 11:1 and the “sons of the living God” of Hos 2:1 are the same book’s two names for Israel restored [T]. Divergence: the WLC has the impersonal יֵאָמֵר (“it will be said”); Swete and Rahlfs have κληθήσονται (“they shall be called”) [T], checked. Swete has καὶ αὐτοί (“they too”), Rahlfs ἐκεῖ (“there”) [T], checked. Matthew has κληθήσονται and αὐτοί (“they”), with Swete; but αὐτοί is in all Matthew’s future promises (5:4–9), so it is not evidence by itself [I]. Nearest: Swete. Living God: τοῦ θεοῦ τοῦ ζῶντος (“of the living God”) occurs in Matthew at 16:16 (Peter: “the Son of the living God”) and 26:63 (the high priest’s oath) (SBLGNT phrase search) [T], verified. Synthetic, moderate: the disciples’ name (5:9) and Jesus’ confessed name (16:16) share Hosea’s phrase in its two halves. What it adds: the peacemakers are those whom God renames as his restored people, sons where they had been “not my people” [I], moderate–high.

Ps 107 (Swete 106) → 5:6; with 4:16 and 8:11 (moderate — not in the overview). Move 1: Swete 106:5 πεινῶντες καὶ διψῶντες (“hungry and thirsty”) — the phrase occurs in Swete and Rahlfs only here (phrase search), verified; WLC רְעֵבִים גַּם־צְמֵאִים (“hungry and thirsty”) [T]. Swete 106:9 ὅτι ἐχόρτασεν ψυχὴν κενήν, καὶ ψυχὴν πεινῶσαν ἐνέπλησεν ἀγαθῶν (“for he satisfied the empty soul and filled the hungry soul with good things”) [T]. The same psalm supplies καθημένους ἐν σκότει καὶ σκιᾷ θανάτου (“sitting in darkness and the shadow of death”, 106:10) at 4:16, and ἀπὸ ἀνατολῶν καὶ δυσμῶν (“from east and west”, 106:3), which in Swete occurs only there and in Pss. Sol. 11:3, and in Matthew only at 8:11, “many will come from east and west” [T], verified. The psalm is a song of τῶν λελυτρωμένων (“the redeemed”, 106:2) gathered from the lands [T]. Synthetic, moderate: one psalm of the redeemed exiles sounds at 4:16, 5:6 and 8:11.

Internal: Answers §2:18 — comfort refused, comfort promised (moderate–high, verified). Answers §3:15 — δικαιοσύνη (“righteousness”) (high, verified). Answers §3:17; 4:3, 6 — “sons of God” after the Son (moderate–high). Answers §4:2 — hunger (moderate). Answers §4:16 — Ps 107 (moderate, synthetic). Answers §4:17 — the kingdom “at hand” now “is theirs” (high). Plants §5:20; 6:1, 33 — righteousness demanded after it is blessed (high). Plants §5:44–45 — persecution, enemies, sons of the Father (high, verified). Plants §9:15 — mourning and the bridegroom (moderate). Plants §11:5 — the poor evangelised (high; audit). Plants §11:29; 21:5 — πραΰς (“gentle”) (high, verified). Plants §14:20; 15:37 — χορτάζω (“to satisfy”) (moderate, verified). Plants §16:16; 26:63 — the living God (moderate). Plants §18:33 — mercy (high, verified). Plants §23:29–37 — the persecuted prophets (high, verified). Plants §25:34–44 — inherit the kingdom; the hungry and thirsty King (moderate–high, verified). Plants §27:24 — ἀθῷος (“innocent”) (uncertain–moderate). Plants §27:44 — ὀνειδίζω (“to insult”) (moderate–high, verified).

Translations. 5:5: NASB95 “the gentle”, ESV “the meek” [T], checked. NASB95 has “gentle” at 5:5; 11:29; 21:5, so the πραΰς (“gentle”) chain is heard; the ESV has “meek” (5:5), “gentle” (11:29) and “humble” (21:5), so it is lost [T], checked. 5:10: NASB95 “who have been persecuted” keeps the perfect δεδιωγμένοι (“having been persecuted”); ESV “who are persecuted” [T]. 5:11: both have “falsely” (NASB95 “falsely say”; ESV “utter … falsely”), which renders ψευδόμενοι (“lying”), printed by NA28 in brackets °[ψευδόμενοι] and by SBLGNT without them [T]; the finding does not rest on it. 5:11 ὀνειδίσωσιν: NASB95 “insult” and ESV “revile”; each version uses the same verb at 27:44 (“insulting” / “reviled”) [T], checked. Pulpit divergence: the ESV’s “meek … gentle … humble” hides that the third beatitude describes Jesus (11:29) and the King (21:5). The preacher should say “meek” (or “gentle”) all three times.

Difficulty. “Poor in spirit.” Options: (a) the economically poor who are also humble before God, the עֲנָוִים (“humble, poor”) of Isa 61:1 and the Psalms [S]; (b) spiritual poverty only — those who know their need of God [S]; (c) the Isaiah frame and the Hebrew word keep both: the oppressed who wait on God [S: audit]/[I]. Ps 34:19 (Swete 33:19) ταπεινοὺς τῷ πνεύματι (“the humble in spirit”) and Isa 66:2 are the audit’s companions [S: audit]. Do not preach (b) as if the material sense were absent from Isaiah.

The order of 5:4 and 5:5. D, 33 and the Latin tradition put “the meek” before “those who mourn” (NA28 apparatus) [T]. NA28 and SBLGNT keep the order of most witnesses [T]. Nothing in the findings depends on it, but the 36-word halves do not change either way [T].

Pitfall. What it looks like: “Eight attitudes to cultivate if you want God’s blessing” — the Beatitudes as conditions. Why wrong: the form is a pronouncement (μακάριοι, “blessed are”), not a command; the kingdom “is” theirs already (5:3, 10); and the poor, the mourners and the persecuted are not virtues anyone sets out to acquire. Several describe Jesus first (the meek, 11:29; the hungry, 4:2; the persecuted, 5:11 “because of Me”; the insulted, 27:44). Corrective: preach the Beatitudes as the King’s declaration over those who belong to his kingdom, in the shape of his own life. The demands come after, and are addressed to the blessed (5:13–48).

Book-overview note: Confirms from the text: Isa 61 as the frame (audit), Ps 37:11, Ps 24:4, πραΰς (“gentle”) at 5:5; 11:29; 21:5 only. Extends: (1) the structure: inclusio 5:3 = 5:10 (verified), two halves of four each ending in δικαιοσύνη (“righteousness”), 36 + 36 words (SBLGNT); (2) Hos 1:10 Swete (MT 2:1) behind 5:9 — κληθήσονται … υἱοὶ θεοῦ occurs in Swete only there — a new map row, moderate–high, with “living God” at 16:16; 26:63; (3) Ps 107 (Swete 106) as one psalm sounding at 4:16 (106:10), 5:6 (106:5, 9) and 8:11 (106:3, “from east and west”, Swete-only phrase) — new, moderate; (4) Matthew’s τὴν γῆν (“the earth”) with αὐτοί (“they”) is Swete Ps 36:9, two verses before the cited 36:11, and Swete differs from Rahlfs there (article); (5) Ps 24:3 Swete “who shall ascend the mountain of the Lord?” joins the audited Ps 24:4 to the audited 5:1 ascent; and ἀθῷος χερσίν (“innocent in hands”, Ps 24:4 Swete) to 27:24 (uncertain–moderate); (6) χορτάζω (“to satisfy”) 5:6 → the feedings only; (7) ἐλεάω (“to have mercy”) 5:7 → 18:33; (8) διώκω (“to persecute”) 5:10–12 → 23:34; ὀνειδίζω (“to insult”) 5:11 → 27:44. Translations: the overview’s πραΰς row should note that the ESV breaks the chain (meek / gentle / humble) and the NASB95 keeps it (gentle ×3).

9. Matthew 5:13–20 — Salt, Light, and the Law Fulfilled ⭐

Position. Q1: the Beatitudes have pronounced the disciples blessed, and ended on the persecuted, “for so they persecuted the prophets who were before you” (5:12) [T]. Q2: this unit exists here because the blessed now receive their identity and their task, and because the Sermon cannot go on to “it was said … but I say” (5:21–48) until the speaker has said where he stands towards the Law and the Prophets. 5:17–20 is the hinge between the blessing and the demand [T] for the order, [I] for the function.

Structure. Two identity sayings, each opening Ὑμεῖς ἐστε (“You are”, 5:13, 14), with a warning (salt thrown out, 5:13) and a purpose (light seen, 5:16) [T]. Then four sentences on the Law: the programme (5:17), its ground in an ἀμήν (“truly”) saying (5:18), its consequence with οὖν (“then”, 5:19), and its demand with γάρ (“for”, 5:20) [T]. 5:17 and 7:12 frame the body of the Sermon: τὸν νόμον ἢ τοὺς προφήτας (“the Law or the Prophets”, 5:17) and ὁ νόμος καὶ οἱ προφῆται (“the Law and the Prophets”, 7:12) [T], verified (SBLGNT). The frame is the text’s; reading 5:17–7:12 as a single body is [I], high.

Text-first findings.

  • Identity comes before demand. Ὑμεῖς ἐστε (“You are”) is indicative, twice, and emphatic [T]. Nothing is commanded until 5:16 (λαμψάτω, “let … shine”). The salt saying is a warning about loss of identity, not a call to become salt: ἐὰν δὲ τὸ ἅλας μωρανθῇ (“but if the salt has become tasteless”, 5:13). μωραίνω (“to become tasteless”, literally “to be made foolish”) stands only here in Matthew, and its root returns in μωρός (“foolish”), the man who built on sand (7:26) [T]. The salt that “goes foolish” and the builder who is “foolish” bracket the Sermon’s warnings [I], moderate. Luke 14:34 has the same verb, so the wordplay is inherited, not Matthew’s own.
  • The light of Isaiah 9 is handed on. φῶς (“light”) stands in six verses of Matthew (SBLGNT): 4:16 (twice), 5:14, 5:16, 6:23, 10:27, 17:2 [T], verified. At 4:16 the people in darkness “saw a great light” (Isa 9:1 Hebrew). Ten verses later, the disciples are τὸ φῶς τοῦ κόσμου (“the light of the world”, 5:14). What dawned on Galilee of the nations now shines through the disciples. [I], moderate–high. λάμπω (“to shine”) occurs at 5:15, 5:16 and 17:2 only (verified), and Swete’s Isa 9:2 (WLC 9:1) has φῶς λάμψει ἐφ᾽ ὑμᾶς (“light will shine on you”), the verb Matthew did not use at 4:16 and uses here [T]. The link to Isaiah’s Greek is uncertain; the internal link is firm.
  • God is first called “Father” here. τὸν πατέρα ὑμῶν τὸν ἐν τοῖς οὐρανοῖς (“your Father who is in heaven”, 5:16) is the book’s first use of πατήρ (“father”) for God. Before 5:16 the word stands only at 2:22; 3:9; 4:21, 22, all of human fathers (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The aim of the good works is δοξάσωσιν (“they may glorify”) the Father. δοξάζω (“to glorify”) occurs in four verses: 5:16; 6:2 (the hypocrites “honoured by men”); 9:8; 15:31 (the crowds glorify God) [T], verified. 5:16 and 6:2 are set against each other, and 9:8 is the first time the crowds do what 5:16 hoped for.
  • “Do not think that I came.” Μὴ νομίσητε ὅτι ἦλθον (“do not think that I came”, 5:17) occurs twice, here and at 10:34 (“not to bring peace, but a sword”) [T], verified. Both correct a misreading of Jesus’ mission. ἦλθον (“I came”) makes the coming itself the subject [T]; that it implies a coming from beyond is [I], moderate.
  • “Abolish” is the word of the temple charge. καταλῦσαι (“to abolish”, 5:17, twice). καταλύω (“to abolish, destroy”) occurs at 5:17; 24:2; 26:61; 27:40, and nowhere else in Matthew [T], verified. At 26:61 the false witnesses say he claimed to be able καταλῦσαι τὸν ναόν (“to destroy the temple”), and at 27:40 the passers-by call him ὁ καταλύων τὸν ναόν (“you who are going to destroy the temple”). The one who said he did not come to abolish the Law is condemned and mocked as the one who would abolish the temple. Synthetic, verified: high for the chain; the irony is [I], moderate–high.
  • πληρῶσαι (“to fulfil”) is Jesus’ word before it is the narrator’s. πληρόω (“to fulfil”) stands in 16 verses (SBLGNT) [T], verified. Jesus speaks it twice of his own programme: 3:15 (πληρῶσαι πᾶσαν δικαιοσύνην, “to fulfil all righteousness”) and here. The contrast is not “keep” against “break” but καταλῦσαι (“to abolish”) against πληρῶσαι (“to fulfil”); the narrator’s ten formula quotations show what Matthew means by the second [I], high.
  • The Law outlasts heaven and earth “until all things take place”. ἕως ἂν παρέλθῃ ὁ οὐρανὸς καὶ ἡ γῆ … ἕως ἂν πάντα γένηται (“until heaven and earth pass away … until all is accomplished”, 5:18). Both phrases return in one place only: ἕως ἂν πάντα ταῦτα γένηται (“until all these things take place”, 24:34), and ὁ οὐρανὸς καὶ ἡ γῆ παρελεύσεται, οἱ δὲ λόγοι μου οὐ μὴ παρέλθωσιν (“heaven and earth will pass away, but my words will not pass away”, 24:35) [T], verified (SBLGNT phrase search: 5:18 and 24:34; 5:18 and 24:35). 5:18 sets the Law’s term at the passing of heaven and earth; 24:35 sets Jesus’ words beyond it. Synthetic, verified: high for the chain. The NA28 apparatus records that the Old Latin c adds 24:35 here at 5:18 (5:18 ⸇) [T]: one early reader already joined them.
  • Doing and teaching, and the measure of the kingdom. λύσῃ (“annuls”) and ποιήσῃ καὶ διδάξῃ (“keeps and teaches”, 5:19). λύω (“to loose, annul”) returns as the binding and loosing of 16:19 and 18:18 [T], verified (5:19; 16:19; 18:18; 21:2). ἐλάχιστος (“least”) is used twice here; it occurs also at 2:6 (Bethlehem “by no means least”) and 25:40, 45 (“the least of these my brothers”) [T], verified.
  • A righteousness that “surpasses”. ἐὰν μὴ περισσεύσῃ ὑμῶν ἡ δικαιοσύνη πλεῖον (“unless your righteousness surpasses”, 5:20). δικαιοσύνη (“righteousness”) stands in 7 verses (SBLGNT): 3:15; 5:6, 10, 20; 6:1, 33; 21:32 [T], verified. Five of the seven are in chapters 5–6. This is the first pairing of γραμματεῖς καὶ Φαρισαῖοι (“scribes and Pharisees”) in the book (γραμματεύς and Φαρισαῖος both first co-occur at 5:20) [T]. The pair returns as the addressees of the woes (23:13–29).

Synoptic check. 5:13 has a parallel at Luke 14:34 (μωρανθῇ, “has become tasteless”, in both) and a looser one at Mark 9:50 (ἄναλον, “unsalty”). 5:15 has Mark 4:21 and Luke 11:33. 5:18 has Luke 16:17, which says the same thing as a comparison: εὐκοπώτερον … τὸν οὐρανὸν καὶ τὴν γῆν παρελθεῖν ἢ τοῦ νόμου μίαν κεραίαν πεσεῖν (“it is easier for heaven and earth to pass away than for one stroke of the Law to fail”) [T]. 5:14a, 5:16, 5:17, 5:19 and 5:20 have no Synoptic parallel. The “you are” identity, the “Father” who is glorified, the programme of fulfilment, and the surpassing righteousness are Matthew’s own material [T].

Tool 11.

Isa 9:1–2 → 5:14–16 (via 4:16) (moderate–high as an internal hand-on; uncertain for λάμπω). Source context: the land of Zebulun and Naphtali, first humbled by Assyria, will be honoured; “the people who walked in darkness have seen a great light” (WLC 9:1: אוֹר גָּדוֹל, “a great light”; אוֹר נָגַהּ עֲלֵיהֶם, “light has shone on them”). The light is the birth of the royal child, with the throne of David and peace without end (9:5–6) [T]. Swete (9:2) and Rahlfs both read φῶς λάμψει ἐφ᾽ ὑμᾶς (“light will shine on you”), with the second person [T]. Book usage: cited by formula at 4:15–16. OT-to-OT: Isa 42:6 and 49:6 make the Servant φῶς ἐθνῶν (“a light of the nations”; Swete phrase only at Isa 49:6 and 51:4) [T]. What it adds: the light of 5:14 is not a general metaphor. It is the light of Isaiah’s royal child, which Matthew has just told the reader has dawned in Galilee; the disciples carry it to τοῦ κόσμου (“the world”).

Isa 2:2–3 → 5:14b (uncertain). Move 1 only: the mountain of the LORD’s house raised above the hills, and πάντα τὰ ἔθνη (“all the nations”) streaming to it (Swete 2:2) [T]. The speaker sits on a mountain (5:1), but “city on a hill” has no verbal tie to Isa 2.

Isa 40:8 → 5:18 with 24:35 (moderate). Move 1: “the word of our God stands for ever” (Swete τὸ δὲ ῥῆμα τοῦ θεοῦ ἡμῶν μένει εἰς τὸν αἰῶνα), set against grass that withers. Matthew’s pair 5:18 / 24:35 makes the same contrast, and at 24:35 places Jesus’ λόγοι (“words”) where Isaiah placed God’s word [I].

Internal: answers §4:16 — the light (high, verified). Answers §3:15 — πληρῶσαι (“to fulfil”) and δικαιοσύνη (“righteousness”) together (high; the overview has it). Plants §7:12 — the Law and the Prophets frame (high, verified). Plants §10:34 — “do not think that I came” (high, verified). Plants §24:34–35 — “until all things take place”; heaven and earth pass away (high, verified). Plants §26:61; §27:40 — καταλύω (“to destroy”) (high, verified; synthetic). Plants §9:8 — the crowds glorify God (moderate–high, verified). Plants §16:19; §18:18 — λύω (“to loose”) (moderate; the sense shifts from “annul” to “loose”). Plants §7:26 — μωρ- (“foolish”) (moderate). Plants §17:2 — λάμπω (“to shine”), his face shining like the sun (moderate).

Translations. NASB95 “annuls” and ESV “relaxes” for λύσῃ (5:19); both hide the link to “loose” at 16:19 and 18:18. NASB95 “keeps” for ποιήσῃ (“does”, 5:19), ESV “does”: the ESV keeps the “do” that runs through 7:24–26. NASB95 “has become tasteless” and ESV “has lost its taste” both lose μωρ- (“foolish”).
