Pulpit divergence: at 5:19 the congregation will hear “relaxes … does”, which is nearer the Greek’s doing than the NASB95’s “keeps”.

Difficulty. “Until all is accomplished” (5:18). Options: (a) until the end of the age (24:34–35 read eschatologically); (b) until Jesus’ own work is done, the cross and resurrection (27:51–54) [S]; (c) both, the second as the start of the first. The text gives the verbal link to 24:34–35 [T]; the choice between (a) and (b) is [I], and should not be settled from this verse alone.

Pitfall. Reading 5:17–20 as “Jesus keeps the Law so that we can be excused from it”, or as “Christians must keep every Mosaic command”. The first ignores 5:19–20, which raises the demand; the second ignores that the next 28 verses show what “fulfil” means by setting Jesus’ word beside the Law’s. Corrective: preach 5:17 as a claim about who Jesus is towards the Scriptures, and let 5:21–48 and 7:12 define what fulfilment looks like.

Book-overview note: confirms with something new and extends. The overview has 3:15 → 5:17 and the “Law and the Prophets” verses (5:17; 7:12; 11:13; 22:40). It does not have (1) καταλύω (“to abolish/destroy”) 5:17 → 26:61; 27:40, (2) the verbal pair 5:18 ↔︎ 24:34–35, or (3) 5:16 as the first naming of God as Father. All three are verified and should be added to the Echo Table.

10. Matthew 5:21–48 — “But I Say to You” ⭐

Position. Q1: 5:17–20 has set out the programme: not καταλῦσαι (“to abolish”) but πληρῶσαι (“to fulfil”), and a righteousness that must περισσεύσῃ (“surpass”, 5:20) [T]. Q2: this unit exists here because 5:20 is a claim that needs showing. Six cases set “it was said” beside “but I say”, and the unit ends with the question τί περισσὸν ποιεῖτε; (“what more are you doing?”, 5:47), which picks up the verb-root of 5:20 [T]. Without 5:21–48 the reader would not know what “fulfil” and “surpass” mean [I], high.

Structure. Six antitheses, each opening with a form of ἐρρέθη (“it was said”) and answered by ἐγὼ δὲ λέγω ὑμῖν (“but I say to you”) [T]. They fall into two sets of three. The full formula Ἠκούσατε ὅτι ἐρρέθη τοῖς ἀρχαίοις (“you have heard that the ancients were told”) stands at 5:21 and again at 5:33, where it is introduced by Πάλιν (“again”); the four others are shortened [T]. The third antithesis (5:31) is the shortest, Ἐρρέθη δέ (“it was said”), and hangs on the second [T]. The unit closes with a summary command joined by οὖν (“therefore”): Ἔσεσθε οὖν ὑμεῖς τέλειοι (“you are to be perfect”, 5:48) [T]. Reading 5:48 as the conclusion of all six, not only the sixth, is [I], moderate–high.

Text-first findings.

  • The formula is unique to this unit. ἐγὼ δὲ λέγω ὑμῖν (“but I say to you”) stands in exactly six verses of Matthew, all here: 5:22, 28, 32, 34, 39, 44 (SBLGNT phrase search) [T], verified. ἐρρέθη (“it was said”) stands in six verses, all here: 5:21, 27, 31, 33, 38, 43 [T], verified. τοῖς ἀρχαίοις (“to the ancients”) stands only at 5:21 and 5:33 [T], verified. The passive ἐρρέθη (“it was said”) leaves the speaker unnamed. It is not γέγραπται (“it is written”) and not the narrator’s τὸ ῥηθέν (“what was spoken”) [T]. Over against it stands an emphatic ἐγώ (“I”) [T]. That the “I” takes the place Moses held at Sinai is [I], moderate–high.
  • Matthew’s Decalogue follows the Hebrew order. 5:21 and 5:27 take “murder” and then “adultery”. So does 19:18 (Οὐ φονεύσεις, Οὐ μοιχεύσεις, “you shall not murder, you shall not commit adultery”) [T]. The WLC has the same order (Exod 20:13 לֹא תִּרְצָח, “you shall not murder”; 20:14 לֹא תִּנְאָף, “you shall not commit adultery”) [T]. Swete puts adultery first in both Decalogues (Exod 20:13–15: Οὐ μοιχεύσεις, Οὐ κλέψεις, Οὐ φονεύσεις, “adultery, stealing, murder”; Deut 5:17–18: adultery, murder) [T]. Rahlfs agrees with Swete in both places [T]. On the order, Matthew stands with the Hebrew against both Greek editions (high for the fact; that Matthew chose it is [I], moderate).
  • Every case moves from the act to its root. Murder → ὀργιζόμενος (“angry”, 5:22). Adultery → ὁ βλέπων … πρὸς τὸ ἐπιθυμῆσαι (“looks … with lust”, 5:28), already done ἐν τῇ καρδίᾳ αὐτοῦ (“in his heart”) [T]. ἐπιθυμέω (“to desire”) stands only at 5:28 and 13:17 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. γέεννα (“hell”) stands in 7 verses: 5:22, 29, 30; 10:28; 18:9; 23:15, 33 [T], verified. Three of the seven are in this unit.
  • Reconciliation comes before worship. ἄφες ἐκεῖ τὸ δῶρόν σου … πρῶτον διαλλάγηθι τῷ ἀδελφῷ σου (“leave your offering there … first be reconciled to your brother”, 5:24) [T]. θυσιαστήριον (“altar”) stands at 5:23, 24 and then only in 23:18–20, 35 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The next scene with an altar is the woes, where swearing “by the altar” is mocked (23:18–20) and Zechariah is killed “between the sanctuary and the altar” (23:35) [T]. ἀδελφός (“brother”) runs through 5:22–24 four times and returns at 5:47 [T].
  • Oaths: Peter swears, Jesus does not. μὴ ὀμόσαι ὅλως (“make no oath at all”, 5:34). ὀμνύω (“to swear”) stands in 8 verses: 5:34, 36; 23:16, 18, 20, 21, 22; 26:74 [T], verified. ὅρκος (“oath”) stands at 5:33; 14:7, 9; 26:72 [T], verified. The two passion uses are Peter: ἠρνήσατο μετὰ ὅρκου (“he denied it with an oath”, 26:72) and ἤρξατο … ὀμνύειν (“he began … to swear”, 26:74) [T]. Between them, the high priest says to Jesus Ἐξορκίζω σε (“I adjure you”, 26:63), and ἐξορκίζω (“to put under oath”) stands only there [T], verified. Jesus answers Σὺ εἶπας (“you have said it”, 26:64), not with an oath [T]. The disciple swears and lies; the Master, put under oath, speaks plainly. Synthetic, verified: high for the chain; the reading is [I], moderate–high. The overview has 26:72 against 10:33, but not against 5:33–37.
  • “Do not resist” is the Servant’s Greek. μὴ ἀντιστῆναι τῷ πονηρῷ· ἀλλ᾽ ὅστις σε ῥαπίζει εἰς τὴν δεξιὰν σιαγόνα (“do not resist an evil person; but whoever slaps you on your right cheek”, 5:39). ῥαπίζω (“to slap”) stands only at 5:39 and 26:67 in the New Testament (SBLGNT) [T], verified. ἀγγαρεύω (“to compel to carry”, 5:41) stands in Matthew only at 5:41 and 27:32, Simon made to carry the cross [T], verified (the overview has both). Text-first work adds the Isaiah cluster: see Tool 11. Matthew’s six words ἀντιστῆναι (“to resist”), ῥαπίζει (“slaps”), σιαγόνα (“cheek”), κριθῆναι (“to sue”), ἱμάτιον (“cloak”) and μὴ ἀποστραφῇς (“do not turn away”) all stand in Swete Isa 50:6–9 [T].
  • Love of enemies answers the persecuted of the Beatitudes. προσεύχεσθε ὑπὲρ τῶν διωκόντων ὑμᾶς (“pray for those who persecute you”, 5:44). διώκω (“to persecute”) stands in 6 verses: 5:10, 11, 12, 44; 10:23; 23:34 [T], verified. The blessed persecuted of 5:10–12 are told what to do with their persecutors. The result is ὅπως γένησθε υἱοὶ τοῦ πατρὸς ὑμῶν (“so that you may be sons of your Father”, 5:45), which answers υἱοὶ θεοῦ κληθήσονται (“they shall be called sons of God”, 5:9), the only other “sons of God” in Matthew (SBLGNT phrase search: 5:9 only) [T], verified.
  • “What more?” answers 5:20. περισσός (“more, beyond”) stands only at 5:37 and 5:47 [T], verified. τὸ δὲ περισσὸν τούτων ἐκ τοῦ πονηροῦ ἐστιν (“anything beyond these is of evil”, 5:37) and τί περισσὸν ποιεῖτε; (“what more are you doing?”, 5:47) [T]. With περισσεύσῃ (“surpasses”, 5:20) the root frames the unit [T]. The “more” of 5:47 is not more words (5:37), but more love [I], moderate–high.
  • “Perfect” is Deuteronomy’s word. τέλειος (“perfect”) stands at 5:48 (twice) and 19:21, “if you wish to be perfect” [T], verified. In Swete τέλειος ἔσῃ (“you shall be perfect”) stands only at Deut 18:13 [T], verified; the form τέλειος (“perfect”) stands in Swete at Gen 6:9 (Noah) and Deut 18:13, and outside the Tanak at Wis 9:6 and Sir 44:17 (Noah again) [T], verified; other forms of the adjective are spread more widely. The shape of 5:48, Ἔσεσθε … ὡς (“you shall be … as”), is the shape of Lev 19:2, Ἅγιοι ἔσεσθε, ὅτι ἐγὼ ἅγιος (“you shall be holy, for I am holy”) [T]. The Father’s likeness here is shown in one act: he gives sun and rain to the evil and the good (5:45) [T].

Synoptic check. The double tradition has the core of the last two antitheses, without the antithetic frame: Luke 6:27–36 (love enemies, the cheek, the cloak, giving, the “sinners” who do the same, “be merciful”, οἰκτίρμονες, Luke 6:36) [T]. Luke has τύπτοντι (“strikes”) where Matthew has ῥαπίζει (“slaps”); Luke lacks Matthew’s ἀντιστῆναι (“to resist”), κριθῆναι (“to sue”), the second mile, and μὴ ἀποστραφῇς (“do not turn away”) [T]. Luke has ἁμαρτωλοί (“sinners”) where Matthew has τελῶναι (“tax collectors”) and ἐθνικοί (“Gentiles”) [T]. Luke 12:58–59 has the adversary on the way, with λεπτόν (“small copper coin”) for Matthew’s κοδράντην (“cent”) [T]. Mark 9:43–47 has the eye and the hand, in the reverse order and as a warning to disciples, not tied to adultery [T]. Mark 10:11 and Luke 16:18 have the divorce saying without Matthew’s παρεκτὸς λόγου πορνείας (“except for the reason of unchastity”) [T]. The six-fold “it was said … but I say”, the murder–anger and oath antitheses, and τέλειος (“perfect”) are Matthew’s alone [T].

Tool 11.

Exod 20:13–14 / Deut 5:17–18 → 5:21, 27 (high; quotation). Source context: the Ten Words, spoken by God from the mountain to the whole assembly (Exod 20:1, 19; Deut 5:22) [T]. Book usage: cited again at 15:4 (the fifth commandment, against tradition) and 19:18–19 (to the rich man) [T]. OT-to-OT: the Hebrew order murder–adultery stands in both Decalogues; the Greek reverses it (see above). Jer 7:9 and Hos 4:2 list the same commandments in indictment [I]. What it adds: Matthew quotes the commandments in the Hebrew order and then goes behind them to the heart. Divergence: Swete and Rahlfs differ from the WLC in order only, a translational choice of the Greek tradition (Rahlfs and Swete agree); Matthew stands nearest the Hebrew.

Lev 19:2, 12, 17–18 → 5:22, 33, 39, 43–48 (moderate–high; a chapter-level frame, not in the overview as such). Source context: the “holiness” chapter opens with Ἅγιοι ἔσεσθε (“you shall be holy”, 19:2) [T]. It forbids swearing falsely by the Name (19:12), hating τὸν ἀδελφόν σου τῇ διανοίᾳ σου (“your brother in your mind”, 19:17; WLC בִּלְבָבֶךָ, “in your heart”), and taking vengeance (19:18, οὐκ ἐκδικᾶταί σου ἡ χείρ, “your hand shall not avenge you”), and then commands ἀγαπήσεις τὸν πλησίον σου ὡς σεαυτόν (“you shall love your neighbour as yourself”, 19:18) [T]. Book usage: Lev 19:18 is cited at 5:43; 19:19; 22:39 [T]. At 5:43 Matthew omits ὡς σεαυτόν (“as yourself”), which he keeps at 19:19 and 22:39 [T]. OT-to-OT: Deut 18:13 supplies τέλειος (“perfect”) where Lev 19:2 has ἅγιος (“holy”). What it adds: four of the six antitheses (anger at a brother in the heart, oaths, non-retaliation, love) touch a single Torah chapter, and 5:48 has its shape [I], moderate–high (synthetic).

“And hate your enemy” (5:43) (no source). The clause is in no Torah text. Swete phrase search for μισήσεις τὸν ἐχθρόν (“you shall hate the enemy”) returns nothing; the positive control ἀγαπήσεις τὸν πλησίον (“you shall love the neighbour”) returns Lev 19:18 [T], verified. That the clause states a common inference from “neighbour” is [I]/[S].

Deut 24:1 → 5:31 (high; quotation-summary). Source context: a regulation that presupposes divorce and limits remarriage to the first husband (24:1–4) [T]. βιβλίον ἀποστασίου (“a certificate of divorce”) stands in Swete at Deut 24:1, 3, then Isa 50:1 and Jer 3:8, where the LORD speaks of Israel’s divorce [T], verified. Book usage: returns at 19:7. What it adds: the case is closed at 19:3–9. Move 4 below notes 1:19.

Exod 21:24 (Lev 24:20; Deut 19:21) → 5:38 (high). The exact phrase ὀφθαλμὸν ἀντὶ ὀφθαλμοῦ (“an eye for an eye”) stands in Swete at these three places only [T], verified. All three are rules for judges, not for private revenge: at Deut 19:18 ἐξετάσωσιν οἱ κριταί (“the judges shall investigate”) [T]; for the others [I]. Deut 19:18 Swete also says the false witness ἀντέστη κατὰ τοῦ ἀδελφοῦ αὐτοῦ (“stood against his brother”), three verses before ὀφθαλμὸν ἀντὶ ὀφθαλμοῦ (“an eye for an eye”, 19:21) [T]. Matthew’s μὴ ἀντιστῆναι (“do not resist”, 5:39) uses the same verb [T]; the WLC has עָנָה בְאָחִיו (“testified against his brother”), so the link, if any, runs through the Greek (uncertain–moderate).

Lev 19:12; Num 30:3; Deut 23:22; Ps 50:14 → 5:33 (moderate–high). Move 1: no single verse has Matthew’s wording. ἀπόδος τῷ ὑψίστῳ τὰς εὐχάς σου (“pay your vows to the Most High”) is Ps 49:14 Swete (WLC 50:14) [T]. ἐπιορκέω (“to swear falsely”) stands in canonical Swete only at Zech 5:3 [T], verified. Matthew’s line is a summary of Torah and Psalm.

Isa 66:1 with Ps 48:3 → 5:34–35 (high; the overview has it). Source context: Isa 66:1 asks what house can be built for the God whose throne is heaven and whose footstool is earth (Swete ὑποπόδιον τῶν ποδῶν μου, “the footstool of my feet”; WLC הֲדֹם רַגְלָי, “the footstool of my feet”) [T]. Ps 48:3 (Swete 47:3): ἡ πόλις τοῦ βασιλέως τοῦ μεγάλου (“the city of the great King”; WLC קִרְיַת מֶלֶךְ רָב) [T]. What it adds: every oath-formula names something that belongs to God. Matthew’s Greek is nearer Swete than a fresh rendering would need to be, but the Hebrew and Greek agree here; nearest: both.

Isa 50:6–9 → 5:39–42 (moderate–high; new candidate 9 of the audit, now tested). Source context: the third Servant song. The Servant gives τὰς δὲ σιαγόνας μου εἰς ῥαπίσματα (“my cheeks to slaps”), τὸ δὲ πρόσωπόν μου οὐκ ἀπέστρεψα (“I did not turn away my face”, 50:6). Then: τίς ὁ κρινόμενός μοι; ἀντιστήτω μοι (“who is the one who goes to law with me? let him stand against me”, 50:8); and his accusers ὡς ἱμάτιον παλαιωθήσεσθε (“will wear out like a garment”, 50:9) [T] (Swete; Rahlfs reads δέδωκα for ἔδωκα at 50:6 and otherwise agrees). Evidence: ῥαπ- (“slap”) with σιαγών (“cheek”) stands in Swete only at Isa 50:6 and Hos 11:4; the positive control σιαγον- (“cheek”) returns 19 Swete verses, including Lam 3:30 and Mic 5:1 [T], verified. Matthew shares with Isa 50:6–9 six lexemes: ἀνθίστημι (“to resist”), ῥαπίζω (“to slap”), σιαγών (“cheek”), κρίνω (“to go to law”), ἱμάτιον (“cloak”) and ἀποστρέφω (“to turn away”) [T]. Of these, Luke 6:29–30 has only σιαγών and ἱμάτιον [T]. Hebrew vs Greek: the WLC has לְמֹרְטִים (“to those who pluck out [the beard]”) where Greek has ῥαπίσματα (“slaps”), and מִי־יָרִיב אִתִּי נַעַמְדָה יָּחַד (“who will contend with me? let us stand up together”, 50:8) for the Greek’s κρινόμενος … ἀντιστήτω (“goes to law … let him stand against”) [T]. Classification: the Greek is a fair rendering of 50:8 and an interpretive one at 50:6; Matthew stands nearest the Greek (Swete = Rahlfs). Book usage: Isa 50:6 is behind the passion at 26:67 (ἐράπισαν, “they slapped”) [S: audit] (high for the tradition; moderate for Matthew’s own wording). OT-to-OT: Lam 3:30 δώσει τῷ παίοντι αὐτὸν σιαγόνα (“let him give his cheek to the one who strikes him”) is the closest teaching-form of the same posture [T]. What it adds: Matthew’s own additions to the double tradition are the Servant’s words. The disciple is told to do what the Servant did, and the passion shows Jesus doing it (26:67) [I], moderate–high. Rating: moderate–high for 5:39–42 as a whole; the single-word ῥαπίζω link is moderate on its own.

Deut 18:13 (with Lev 19:2) → 5:48 (moderate–high). Source context: Deut 18:9–14 forbids the practices of the nations (divination, sorcery) and ends τέλειος ἔσῃ ἐναντίον Κυρίου τοῦ θεοῦ σου (“you shall be blameless before the LORD your God”; WLC תָּמִים תִּהְיֶה עִם יְהוָה, “you shall be whole with the LORD”) [T]. What it adds: Deut 18:13 sets Israel’s wholeness against τὰ ἔθνη (“the nations”); 5:47–48 sets the disciples’ “more” against the ἐθνικοί (“Gentiles”) in the next breath [T]. Deut 18:15 follows: the prophet like Moses [T]. Matthew’s Greek τέλειος is the Swete word; nearest: Greek (Hebrew agrees in sense).

Internal: answers §5:20 — περισσεύω / περισσός (“surpass” / “more”) (high, verified). Answers §5:9–12 — sons of God; the persecuted (high, verified). Answers §1:19 — Joseph, δίκαιος (“righteous”), planned λάθρᾳ ἀπολῦσαι αὐτήν (“to send her away secretly”); ἀπολῦσαι αὐτήν (“to divorce her”) stands only at 1:19 and 19:7 (SBLGNT phrase search) [T], verified. The book’s first “righteous” man is introduced in the act this unit addresses (moderate). Plants §26:63–74 — oath and adjuration (high, verified; synthetic). Plants §26:67; §27:32 — slapped, compelled (high, verified; the overview has it). Plants §19:18–21 — Decalogue order and τέλειος (“perfect”) (high, verified). Plants §19:3–9 — divorce (high). Plants §22:39 — love of neighbour (high). Plants §23:16–22 — oaths by the altar, by heaven and “the throne of God” (high). Plants §18:8–9 — eye and hand (high). Plants §18:17 — ἐθνικὸς καὶ ὁ τελώνης (“a Gentile and a tax collector”); ἐθνικός (“Gentile”) stands only at 5:47; 6:7; 18:17 [T], verified (moderate). Uncertain: ἀνατέλλει (“causes to rise”, 5:45) with 4:16 ἀνέτειλεν (“dawned”); ἀνατέλλω (“to rise”) stands at 4:16; 5:45; 13:6 only (verified), but the sun and the light are different images.

Translations. 5:21, 33: NASB95 “the ancients were told”; ESV “it was said to those of old” [T]. Both keep the passive. 5:22 Ῥακά (“raca”): NASB95 “You good-for-nothing”, ESV “insults” [T]; both drop the Aramaic word, which the Greek leaves unexplained. 5:39 τῷ πονηρῷ: NASB95 “an evil person”, ESV “the one who is evil” [T]. The ESV keeps the article, which matters for the link to 6:13 τοῦ πονηροῦ (“the evil one”/“evil”). 5:41 ἀγγαρεύσει: NASB95 and ESV “forces”; both lose the link to 27:32 (the overview notes this). 5:47 περισσόν: both “more” [T]. 5:48: NASB95 “you are to be perfect”, ESV “you therefore must be perfect” [T]. Pulpit divergence: at 5:39 the congregation will hear “the one who is evil”, which makes a later reference to 6:13 easier to draw; otherwise none bearing.

Difficulty. The exception clause (5:32; 19:9). παρεκτὸς λόγου πορνείας (“except for the reason of unchastity”) is Matthew’s alone [T]. The NA28 text at 5:32 is secure on the clause; the apparatus records the omission of the second half (καὶ ὃς ἐὰν … μοιχᾶται, “and whoever marries … commits adultery”) in D and some Old Latin [T]. Options: (a) πορνεία (“unchastity”) as adultery, permitting divorce; (b) as a marriage within forbidden degrees (Lev 18), making the union void; (c) as unchastity during betrothal, which fits 1:19 [S]. The text supplies the 1:19 link [T]; the choice is [I]. Also live: εἰκῇ (“without cause”, 5:22) is added by א² D K L W and the majority text; NA28 reads without it, with 𝔓⁶⁴ א* B [T]. The NASB95 and ESV both follow NA28. Preachers using the KJV-family will hear “without a cause”.

Pitfall. Reading the antitheses as Jesus correcting Moses (“the Old Testament said hate your enemy; Jesus said love”). Four of the six are Torah texts, and the Torah itself already forbids hatred of a brother in the heart and vengeance (Lev 19:17–18). The fifth clause, “hate your enemy”, is not Torah at all. Corrective: preach “it was said” as the Law as heard and applied, and “but I say” as the authority that goes to the Law’s root (5:17). The contrast is between hearers and the Speaker, not between Moses and Jesus.

Book-overview note: confirms with something new and extends. (1) The overview should note that ἐγὼ δὲ λέγω ὑμῖν (“but I say to you”) and ἐρρέθη (“it was said”) are each exactly six and confined to this unit. (2) New: Matthew’s Decalogue order (5:21, 27; 19:18) follows the Hebrew against Swete and Rahlfs. (3) New: Isa 50:6–9 shares six lexemes with 5:39–42, four of them Matthew’s own against Luke; the audit’s candidate 9 should be raised to moderate–high and the Echo Table row 5:39 ↔︎ 26:67 should cite it. (4) New: the oath chain 5:33–37 → 26:63 (ἐξορκίζω, “I adjure”) → 26:72, 74 (Peter’s oaths). (5) New: Lev 19 as a chapter frame for the antitheses, and Deut 18:13 for τέλειος (“perfect”).

11. Matthew 6:1–18 — In Secret; the Lord’s Prayer (⭐ for 6:9–13)

Position. Q1: 5:21–48 showed the “more” of the surpassing righteousness in relation to others, and ended on being like the Father (5:45, 48) [T]. Q2: this unit exists here because 6:1 turns the same δικαιοσύνη (“righteousness”, 5:20; 6:1) towards God, and asks who it is done for. It names the hypocrites for the first time, and it places the Father “in secret” at the centre of piety [T]. It also sets the prayer that the book will hear again in Gethsemane (26:39–42) [T].

Structure. A heading (6:1), then three acts of piety, each built to one pattern: ὅταν (“when”) … μὴ … ὡς οἱ ὑποκριταί (“not … like the hypocrites”) … ἀμὴν λέγω ὑμῖν, ἀπέχουσιν τὸν μισθὸν αὐτῶν (“truly I say to you, they have their reward in full”) … σὺ δέ (“but you”) … ὁ πατήρ σου ὁ βλέπων ἐν τῷ κρυπτῷ ἀποδώσει σοι (“your Father who sees in secret will reward you”) [T]. Giving (6:2–4), prayer (6:5–6), fasting (6:16–18). The prayer section is opened out by an insertion on the Gentiles’ many words, the model prayer and a comment on forgiveness (6:7–15) [T]. In the Sermon (5:3–7:27, 107 verses in the SBLGNT) the prayer begins at the 55th verse, just after the midpoint [T]; that it is placed as the Sermon’s centre is [I], moderate.

Text-first findings.

  • The refrain is triple and exact. τὸν μισθὸν αὐτῶν (“their reward”) stands at 6:2, 5, 16 and nowhere else (SBLGNT phrase search) [T], verified. ἀπέχω (“to have in full”) stands at 6:2, 5, 16, then 14:24 and 15:8 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. 15:8 is Isa 29:13: ἡ δὲ καρδία αὐτῶν πόρρω ἀπέχει ἀπ᾽ ἐμοῦ (“but their heart is far away from me”), cited against the Pharisees and called ὑποκριταί (“hypocrites”, 15:7) [T]. The hypocrites “have in full” their reward, and their heart “is far” from God: the same verb [I], moderate (the sense differs).
  • “Hypocrites” enters here. ὑποκριτής (“hypocrite”) stands in 13 verses: 6:2, 5, 16; 7:5; 15:7; 22:18; 23:13, 15, 23, 25, 27, 29; 24:51 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The first three uses are this refrain; the woes take six. The word is not yet aimed at a named group in 6:1–18, where the hypocrites are those who perform in the synagogues and streets (6:2, 5) [T]. At 15:7 and 23:13 it is aimed at scribes and Pharisees [T].
  • The Father who sees in secret. ὁ βλέπων ἐν τῷ (“who sees in …”) stands at 6:4, 6, 18 only [T], verified. κρυπτός (“secret”) stands at 6:4 (twice), 6:6 (twice) and 10:26; κρυφαῖος (“secret”) at 6:18 (twice), its only New Testament use [T], verified. πατήρ (“father”) of God stands ten times in 6:1–18 (6:1, 4, 6 twice, 8, 9, 14, 15, 18 twice; SBLGNT) [T], verified. Against this stands πρὸς τὸ θεαθῆναι αὐτοῖς (“to be noticed by them”, 6:1) and ὅπως φανῶσιν τοῖς ἀνθρώποις (“so that they may be seen by men”, 6:5, 16) [T]. φαίνω (“to appear, be seen”) turns up again at 23:27–28, the whitewashed tombs that φαίνονται (“appear”) beautiful outwardly [T], verified.
  • Address: “Our Father”, against Jesus’ “My Father”. The vocative πάτερ (“Father”) stands at 6:9; 11:25; 26:39, 42 [T], verified. The disciples are taught Πάτερ ἡμῶν (“our Father”, 6:9); Jesus prays Πάτερ μου (“my Father”, 26:39, 42) [T]. He never joins them in the plural [T] for these verses; the significance is [I], moderate.
  • Three “your” petitions, then four for “us”. ἁγιασθήτω τὸ ὄνομά σου, ἐλθέτω ἡ βασιλεία σου, γενηθήτω τὸ θέλημά σου (“hallowed be your name, your kingdom come, your will be done”) are three third-person aorist imperatives [T]. ὡς ἐν οὐρανῷ καὶ ἐπὶ γῆς (“as in heaven, so on earth”, 6:10) belongs most naturally to all three [I], moderate. Then δός, ἄφες, μὴ εἰσενέγκῃς, ῥῦσαι (“give, forgive, do not lead, deliver”) with ἡμῖν / ἡμᾶς (“us”) [T].
  • The prayer is prayed in the passion. γενηθήτω τὸ θέλημά σου (“your will be done”) stands only at 6:10 and 26:42; εἰς πειρασμόν (“into temptation”) only at 6:13 and 26:41 [T], verified (the overview has both). Text-first work adds two more. (1) ἐν οὐρανῷ καὶ ἐπὶ γῆς (“in heaven and on earth”) stands only at 6:10, and with the article (ἐπὶ τῆς γῆς) only at 28:18, πᾶσα ἐξουσία ἐν οὐρανῷ καὶ ἐπὶ τῆς γῆς (“all authority in heaven and on earth”) [T], verified (SBLGNT phrase search; NA28 apparatus at 6:10 records τῆς added by D K L Θ 𝔪). What the disciples ask to be done “as in heaven, so on earth”, the risen Son announces is given to him “in heaven and on earth” (synthetic, verified: high for the chain; the reading [I], moderate–high). (2) ῥύομαι (“to deliver”) stands only at 6:13 and 27:43, ῥυσάσθω νῦν εἰ θέλει αὐτόν (“let him deliver him now, if he wants him”) [T], verified. The petition the disciples are taught is thrown at Jesus on the cross, from Ps 22:9 (Swete 21:9 ῥυσάσθω αὐτόν, “let him deliver him”) [T].
  • “The evil one”, or “evil”. ἀπὸ τοῦ πονηροῦ (“from the evil one / from evil”, 6:13) is grammatically either [T]. τοῦ πονηροῦ stands at 5:37; 6:13; 12:35; 13:38 (SBLGNT phrase search) [T], verified. At 13:19 ὁ πονηρός (“the evil one”) snatches the seed and at 13:38 the weeds are οἱ υἱοὶ τοῦ πονηροῦ (“the sons of the evil one”), plainly personal [T]. πειρασμός (“temptation”) joins the tempter: πειράζω (“to tempt, test”) stands at 4:1, 3 (ὁ πειράζων, “the tempter”); 16:1; 19:3; 22:18, 35 [T], verified. The personal reading has textual support in the book; the neuter is not excluded [I], moderate.
  • Debts, and the parable that unfolds them. ὀφείλημα (“debt”) stands only at 6:12; ὀφειλέτης (“debtor”) at 6:12 and 18:24 (the servant who owed ten thousand talents) [T], verified. παράπτωμα (“transgression”) stands only at 6:14, 15 [T], verified. The comment 6:14–15 is the only part of the prayer Jesus explains [T], and 18:35 repeats its condition: ἐὰν μὴ ἀφῆτε (“if you do not forgive”) [T]. ἀφήκαμεν (“we have forgiven”, aorist, NA28 and SBLGNT) puts the disciples’ forgiving before the request; the majority text has the present ἀφίεμεν (“we forgive”) [T] (NA28 apparatus).

Synoptic check. The prayer has a Lukan parallel (Luke 11:2–4), shorter. Luke has Πάτερ (“Father”) without “our … in heaven”; he lacks “your will be done” and “but deliver us from evil” [T]. Luke has τὰς ἁμαρτίας ἡμῶν (“our sins”) where Matthew has τὰ ὀφειλήματα ἡμῶν (“our debts”), δίδου … τὸ καθ᾽ ἡμέραν (“keep giving … each day”) for δὸς … σήμερον (“give … today”), and the present ἀφίομεν (“we forgive”) for ἀφήκαμεν (“we have forgiven”) [T]. ἐπιούσιος (“daily”) stands only at Matt 6:11 and Luke 11:3 in the New Testament [T], verified. Mark 11:25 has the forgiveness saying, with ὁ πατὴρ ὑμῶν ὁ ἐν τοῖς οὐρανοῖς (“your Father who is in heaven”) and τὰ παραπτώματα (“transgressions”) [T]. The three-fold “in secret” frame (6:1–6, 16–18) and the warning against Gentile babbling (6:7–8) are Matthew’s alone [T]. βατταλογέω (“to babble”) stands only at 6:7 in the New Testament [T], verified.

Tool 11.

Isa 26:20 → 6:6 (moderate; not in the overview). Source context: after the song of the righteous city and the promise that the dead will rise (Isa 26:19), the LORD says: Βάδιζε, λαός μου, εἴσελθε εἰς τὰ ταμεῖά σου, ἀπόκλεισον τὴν θύραν σου, ἀποκρύβηθι μικρόν (“go, my people, enter your rooms, shut your door, hide yourself a little”, Swete 26:20), until the wrath has passed [T]. Rahlfs agrees (spelling ταμιεῖα) [T]. WLC: בֹּא בַחֲדָרֶיךָ וּסְגֹר דְּלָתְךָ (“enter your rooms and shut your door”) [T]. Matthew 6:6: εἴσελθε εἰς τὸ ταμεῖόν σου καὶ κλείσας τὴν θύραν σου (“go into your inner room, close your door”) [T]. ταμ(ι)εῖ- (“room”) with θύρα (“door”) stands in Swete only at Isa 26:20 and its Ode copy (Ode 4:20 Swete) [T], verified. Rival: 2 Kgs 4:33 (Swete 4 Kgdms), Elisha ἀπέκλεισεν τὴν θύραν … καὶ προσηύξατο (“shut the door … and prayed”) [T]; a prayer, but no “room”. Book usage: Isaiah is the book’s spine; no other use of Isa 26 is in the overview. Divergence: Hebrew and Greek agree; Matthew’s wording is close to the Greek imperative-plus-door, but singular. Nearest: Greek (Swete = Rahlfs), weakly. What it adds: the room in Isaiah is a hiding place for God’s people while judgement passes; ἀποκρύβηθι (“hide yourself”) shares its root with κρυπτός (“secret”) [T]. Rating: moderate.

Ezek 36:23 → 6:9 (moderate). Move 1: ἁγιάσω τὸ ὄνομά μου τὸ μέγα τὸ βεβηλωθὲν ἐν τοῖς ἔθνεσιν (“I will sanctify my great name, which has been profaned among the nations”; WLC וְקִדַּשְׁתִּי אֶת־שְׁמִי הַגָּדוֹל) [T]. God hallows his own name by restoring Israel. The passive ἁγιασθήτω (“hallowed be”) leaves God as the agent [T]; the link to Ezekiel is [I], moderate.

Exod 16:4 → 6:11, 13 (moderate). Move 1: ἄρτους ἐκ τοῦ οὐρανοῦ (“bread from heaven”), gathered τὸ τῆς ἡμέρας εἰς ἡμέραν (“a day’s portion every day”), ὅπως πειράσω αὐτούς (“that I may test them”) [T] (Swete). Bread and testing in one verse; Swete pairs ἀρτ- (“bread”) with πειρασ- (“test”) in canonical books only at Exod 16:4, Exod 20:20 (a false hit: no bread) and Ps 77:56 (a false hit) [T]. The Son has already been tested about bread (4:3–4, Deut 8:3) [T]. What it adds: the fourth and sixth petitions stand on the wilderness: daily bread given, and testing [I], moderate.

1 Chr 29:11 → 6:13 (doxology, apparatus only) (high as the source of the scribal addition). Source context: David’s prayer as the materials for the temple are given: σύ, Κύριε, ἡ μεγαλωσύνη καὶ ἡ δύναμις … ὅτι σὺ πάντων τῶν ἐν τῷ οὐρανῷ καὶ ἐπὶ τῆς γῆς δεσπόζεις (“yours, O Lord, is the greatness and the power … for you rule over all in heaven and on earth”, Swete) [T]; the WLC continues לְךָ יְהוָה הַמַּמְלָכָה (“yours, O LORD, is the kingdom”) [T]. Manuscripts: NA28 ends at πονηροῦ with א B D Z 0170 ƒ¹ l 2211 lat mae boᵖᵗ and Origen; ὅτι σοῦ ἐστιν ἡ βασιλεία καὶ ἡ δύναμις καὶ ἡ δόξα εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας, ἀμήν (“for yours is the kingdom and the power and the glory for ever, amen”) is read by K L W Δ Θ ƒ¹³ 33 𝔪 and others; the Didache has a two-member form without “the kingdom”; 1253 has a Trinitarian form; the apparatus itself cross-refers 1 Chr 29:11–13 [T] (the overview has this). What it adds: the doxology is David’s temple prayer turned into the Church’s response. Note: 1 Chr 29:11 contains ἐν τῷ οὐρανῷ καὶ ἐπὶ τῆς γῆς (“in heaven and on earth”), the phrase of 6:10 and 28:18 [T]; this is a link in the scribes’ text, not Matthew’s.

Internal: answers §5:20; §5:45–48 — righteousness, the Father (high). Answers §4:1–11 — πειράζω / πειρασμός (“test” / “temptation”), bread (moderate–high, verified). Plants §26:39–42 — “my Father … your will be done” (high, verified; the overview has it). Plants §26:41 — “into temptation” (high, verified; the overview has it). Plants §28:18 — “in heaven and on earth” (high for the chain, verified; not in the overview). Plants §27:43 — ῥύομαι (“to deliver”) (high for the chain, verified; not in the overview). Plants §18:23–35 — debtor, forgiveness, “if you do not forgive” (high). Plants §23 — ὑποκριταί (“hypocrites”), φαίνω (“to be seen”) (high, verified). Plants §13:19, 38 — the evil one (moderate). Answers §5:47 — ἐθνικοί (“Gentiles”) at 5:47 and 6:7 (moderate, verified).

Translations. 6:13 ἀπὸ τοῦ πονηροῦ: NASB95 and ESV both “from evil” [T]. Both choose the neuter and hide the personal reading the book supports (13:19, 38). NASB95 prints the doxology in brackets; the ESV omits it [T] (the overview has this). 6:12 ἀφήκαμεν: both “we also have forgiven” [T], keeping the aorist. 6:2 ἀπέχουσιν: NASB95 “they have their reward in full”, ESV “they have received their reward” [T]. 6:7 βατταλογήσητε: NASB95 “use meaningless repetition”, ESV “heap up empty phrases” [T]. Pulpit divergence: the ESV congregation hears the prayer end at “deliver us from evil”, without the doxology most of them say aloud; and both versions read “evil”, not “the evil one”.

Difficulty. ἐπιούσιος (“daily”, 6:11). The word occurs only here and Luke 11:3, and is unattested in earlier Greek [S]. Options: (a) “for the coming day” (from ἡ ἐπιοῦσα, “the next day”); (b) “necessary for existence” (ἐπί + οὐσία); (c) “for today” [S]. The Matthean σήμερον (“today”) counts against (a) only if “the coming day” is taken as tomorrow [I]. Exod 16:4 (a day’s portion) supports a “sufficient for the day” sense; the eschatological “bread of the coming age” reading is [S], and nothing in Matthew requires it.

Pitfall. Treating the Lord’s Prayer as a fixed text to be recited for its own power, or alternatively as a mere pattern with no words of its own. Both miss the frame. 6:7–8 forbids praying like the Gentiles who think they will be heard διὰ … πολυλογίᾳ (“for their many words”), because the Father already knows; and Οὕτως (“in this way”, 6:9) introduces a short prayer given to be prayed [T]. Corrective: preach the prayer from its frame: it is short because the Father knows, it begins with his name because the Father is the audience (6:1–6), and Jesus himself prays its third petition when the will is the cup (26:42).

Book-overview note: confirms with something new. The overview has the doxology, 6:10 → 26:42 and 6:13 → 26:41. It does not have (1) ἐν οὐρανῷ καὶ ἐπὶ γῆς (“in heaven and on earth”) 6:10 → 28:18, the only two places in Matthew; (2) ῥύομαι (“to deliver”) 6:13 → 27:43, the only two; (3) Isa 26:20 behind 6:6 (moderate). Items (1) and (2) are verified and belong in the Echo Table beside the Gethsemane row.

12. Matthew 6:19–34 — Treasure, the Eye, and Anxiety

Position. Q1: 6:1–18 set the Father “who sees in secret” against the reward of being seen, and ended on the reward the Father gives (6:18) [T]. Q2: this unit exists here because it turns from piety to possessions with the same question: where is the heart? It moves from treasure (6:19–21), to the eye (6:22–23), to two masters (6:24), and then to anxiety (6:25–34), which is joined to the master saying by Διὰ τοῦτο (“for this reason”, 6:25) [T]. The unit ends at δικαιοσύνη (“righteousness”, 6:33), the word that opened 6:1 and drove 5:20 [T].

Structure. Three short sayings in pairs, each a choice of two: earth or heaven (6:19–21), single or evil eye (6:22–23), God or mammon (6:24) [T]. Then one long argument, framed by μὴ μεριμνᾶτε (“do not worry”, 6:25) and μὴ οὖν μεριμνήσητε (“so do not worry”, 6:31, 34) [T]. Inside it are two “how much more” arguments, the birds (6:26) and the lilies and grass (6:28–30) [T]. The centre of the argument is οἶδεν γὰρ ὁ πατὴρ ὑμῶν ὁ οὐράνιος (“for your heavenly Father knows”, 6:32), and the command is ζητεῖτε δὲ πρῶτον (“but seek first”, 6:33) [T].

Text-first findings.

  • Treasure and the heart. θησαυρίζω (“to store up”) stands only at 6:19, 20; θησαυρός (“treasure”) in 8 verses: 2:11; 6:19, 20, 21; 12:35; 13:44, 52; 19:21 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The first treasures in the book are the magi’s, opened for the child (2:11) [T]. The rich man is told ἕξεις θησαυρὸν ἐν οὐρανοῖς (“you will have treasure in heaven”, 19:21), in the verse that also says Εἰ θέλεις τέλειος εἶναι (“if you wish to be perfect”) [T]. 19:21 joins 5:48 and 6:20 in one sentence (synthetic, verified: high for the chain).
  • The thief who breaks in. κλέπτης (“thief”) and διορύσσω (“to break in”) each stand only at 6:19, 20 and 24:43, where the householder would not have let his house διορυχθῆναι (“be broken into”) [T], verified. The thief who cannot reach heavenly treasure is the image of the Son of Man’s unexpected coming [T] for 24:43–44; the link is [I], moderate.
  • The “evil eye” is stinginess. ὁ ὀφθαλμός σου πονηρός (“your eye is evil/bad”) stands only at 6:23 and 20:15, where the vineyard owner asks: ἢ ὁ ὀφθαλμός σου πονηρός ἐστιν ὅτι ἐγὼ ἀγαθός εἰμι; (“or is your eye envious because I am generous?”) [T], verified. At 20:15 the phrase means grudging, envious. Deut 15:9 Swete uses the same idiom for refusing a needy brother (see Tool 11) [T]. The context of 6:19–24 is money [T]. So ἁπλοῦς (“clear”, 6:22, its only use in Matthew) is likely “generous, undivided” rather than optical [I], moderate–high.
  • Light again. λύχνος (“lamp”) stands only at 5:15 and 6:22 [T], verified; φωτεινός (“full of light”) only at 6:22 and 17:5, the “bright” cloud of the transfiguration [T], verified. φῶς (“light”, 6:23) continues the chain from 4:16 and 5:14–16 (see unit 9). σκότος (“darkness”) stands at 6:23 (twice); 8:12; 22:13; 25:30; 27:45 [T], verified: three of the later four are τὸ σκότος τὸ ἐξώτερον (“the outer darkness”), and the last is the darkness at the cross.
  • Solomon’s glory, and then only the Son of Man’s. δόξα (“glory”) stands in 6 verses: 4:8; 6:29; 16:27; 19:28; 24:30; 25:31 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The first two are human or worldly glory: the kingdoms of the world and “their glory”, shown by the devil (4:8), and Σολομὼν ἐν πάσῃ τῇ δόξῃ αὐτοῦ (“Solomon in all his glory”, 6:29) [T]. Every later use is the Son of Man’s glory at his coming [T]. Solomon appears again at 12:42: πλεῖον Σολομῶνος ὧδε (“something greater than Solomon is here”) [T]. The lily outdoes Solomon, and the Son of Man is greater than Solomon (synthetic, verified: moderate–high).
  • “Little faith” is first said here. ὀλιγόπιστοι (“you of little faith”, 6:30) [T]. ὀλιγόπιστος (“of little faith”) stands at 6:30; 8:26; 14:31; 16:8 [T], verified (the overview has the chain). Its first use is about food and clothing, before any storm [T].
  • Worry, the Gentiles and the Father who knows. μεριμνάω (“to worry”) stands in 6 verses: 6:25, 27, 28, 31, 34; 10:19 [T], verified. πάντα γὰρ ταῦτα τὰ ἔθνη ἐπιζητοῦσιν· οἶδεν γὰρ ὁ πατὴρ ὑμῶν (“for the Gentiles eagerly seek all these things; for your Father knows”, 6:32) repeats the pattern of 6:7–8: ὥσπερ οἱ ἐθνικοί … οἶδεν γὰρ ὁ πατὴρ ὑμῶν (“as the Gentiles do … for your Father knows”) [T]. Both times the Gentiles’ anxiety (many words, many needs) is answered by the Father’s knowledge [I], high. διαφέρω (“to be worth more”) stands at 6:26; 10:31; 12:12 [T], verified: the disciples are worth more than birds and sparrows, and a man more than a sheep.
  • “Seek first the kingdom and his righteousness.” δικαιοσύνη (“righteousness”) at 6:33 is the fifth and last use in the Sermon (5:6, 10, 20; 6:1, 33) [T], verified. βασιλεία (“kingdom”) and δικαιοσύνη (“righteousness”) stand together in a verse only at 5:10, 5:20 and 6:33 (SBLGNT lemma intersection) [T], verified. The kingdom is promised to those persecuted for righteousness (5:10), entered only by surpassing righteousness (5:20), and sought with his righteousness (6:33) [T]; that 6:33 resolves 5:20 by making it an object of seeking, not achievement, is [I], moderate–high. προστίθημι (“to add”) stands only at 6:27 and 6:33 [T], verified: worry cannot add a cubit; seeking the kingdom is “added” all these things.

Synoptic check. The material is double tradition, scattered in Luke: treasure at Luke 12:33–34, the eye at Luke 11:34–36, two masters at Luke 16:13 (verbatim except Luke’s οἰκέτης, “servant”), anxiety at Luke 12:22–31 [T]. Matthew has τὰ πετεινὰ τοῦ οὐρανοῦ (“the birds of the air”, 6:26) where Luke has τοὺς κόρακας (“the ravens”, Luke 12:24) [T]. Matthew alone has πρῶτον (“first”) and καὶ τὴν δικαιοσύνην αὐτοῦ (“and his righteousness”, 6:33): Luke 12:31 reads ζητεῖτε τὴν βασιλείαν αὐτοῦ (“seek his kingdom”) [T]. Matthew alone has 6:34 [T]. Luke has “treasure in heaven” with “sell your possessions and give alms” (Luke 12:33); Matthew keeps that pairing for 19:21 [T].

Tool 11.

Deut 15:7–11 → 6:22–23 (with 6:1–4, 12) (moderate–high for 6:22–23; not in the overview). Source context: the year of release (ἔτος τῆς ἀφέσεως, “the year of release”, Deut 15:9 Swete; WLC שְׁנַת הַשְּׁמִטָּה). Israel must open its hand to the needy brother and not refuse him because the release of debts is near [T]. Swete 15:9: πρόσεχε σεαυτῷ μὴ γένηται ῥῆμα κρυπτὸν ἐν τῇ καρδίᾳ σου … καὶ πονηρεύσηται ὁ ὀφθαλμός σου τῷ ἀδελφῷ σου τῷ ἐπιδεομένῳ, καὶ οὐ δώσεις αὐτῷ (“take heed to yourself lest there be a hidden word in your heart … and your eye be evil towards your brother in need, and you not give to him”) [T]. WLC: וְרָעָה עֵינְךָ בְּאָחִיךָ הָאֶבְיוֹן (“and your eye be evil towards your needy brother”) [T]. Evidence: ὀφθαλμ- (“eye”) with πονηρ- (“evil”) stands in 29 Swete verses, most of them “evil in the eyes of the LORD” in Kings; the idiom of a person’s own “evil eye” in refusing to give stands at Deut 15:9, with Sir 14:8, 10 and 34:13 outside the Tanak [T], verified (regex). WLC Prov 22:9 (טוֹב־עַיִן, “good of eye”, gives bread to the poor) and 28:22 (רַע עָיִן, “evil of eye”, hastens after wealth) carry the same idiom [T]. Book usage: Deut 15 has no earlier use in Matthew. OT-to-OT: Prov 22:9 and 28:22 are the wisdom form of Deuteronomy’s law. What it adds: the eye saying is about giving, set between treasure and mammon. Deut 15:9 also has πρόσεχε (“take heed”, compare 6:1), κρυπτόν (“hidden”, compare 6:4, 6) and καρδία (“heart”, compare 6:21), and its ἄφεσις (“release”) is the release of debts (compare 6:12) [T]. These are common words; the cluster is [I], moderate. The idiom is in both Hebrew and Greek; nearest: both. Rating: moderate–high for the idiom at 6:23; moderate for the wider cluster.

1 Kgs 10 / 2 Chr 9 → 6:29 (moderate). Move 1: Solomon’s wealth and splendour, seen by the queen of Sheba (1 Kgs 10:4–7) [T]. The queen returns at 12:42, as βασίλισσα νότου (“the queen of the South”) [T]. 1 Chr 29:25 Swete: ἔδωκαν αὐτῷ δόξαν βασιλέως (“they gave him royal glory”), such as no king before him had [T].

Isa 40:6–8 → 6:30 (moderate). Move 1: Πᾶσα σὰρξ χόρτος, καὶ πᾶσα δόξα ἀνθρώπου ὡς ἄνθος χόρτου (“all flesh is grass, and all the glory of man like the flower of grass”, Swete 40:6) [T]. χόρτος (“grass”) and δόξα (“glory”) stand together in Isa 40:6 and in Matt 6:29–30 [T]. Isa 40:8 was proposed for 5:18 / 24:35 (unit 9) [I].

Ps 147:9; Job 38:41 → 6:26 (uncertain). Move 1: God feeds the young ravens (Swete Ps 146:9; Job 38:41) [T]. Luke’s κόρακας (“ravens”) is nearer; Matthew’s “birds” is general.

Prov 27:1 → 6:34 (uncertain–moderate). Move 1: μὴ καυχῶ τὰ εἰς αὔριον (“do not boast about tomorrow”, Swete) [T]. αὔριον (“tomorrow”) stands in Matthew only at 6:30 and 6:34 (twice) [T], verified.

Internal: answers §2:11 — treasures opened for the child (moderate, verified). Answers §4:8 — the glory of the kingdoms; δόξα (“glory”) chain (moderate–high, verified). Answers §6:7–8 — the Gentiles; the Father knows (high). Answers §5:20; §6:1 — righteousness (high, verified). Answers §5:14–16 — light, lamp (high, verified). Plants §19:21 — treasure in heaven, perfect (high, verified). Plants §20:15 — the evil eye (high, verified; not in the overview). Plants §24:43 — thief, break in (high for the chain, verified). Plants §12:42 — Solomon (moderate). Plants §8:26; §14:31; §16:8 — little faith (high, verified). Plants §10:19, 29–31 — do not worry; worth more (moderate–high, verified).

Translations. 6:22 ἁπλοῦς: NASB95 “clear”, ESV “healthy” [T]. Both choose the optical sense and lose “generous, single”. 6:23 πονηρός: both “bad” [T]. 20:15 the same phrase: NASB95 “is your eye envious”, ESV “do you begrudge my generosity” [T]. The ESV removes the “eye” from 20:15 altogether, so the pulpit congregation cannot hear the link. 6:24 μαμωνᾷ: NASB95 “wealth”, ESV “money” [T]; both drop the Aramaic loan-word. 6:27 ἡλικίαν … πῆχυν (“stature/span … cubit”): NASB95 “a single hour to his life”, ESV “a single hour to his span of life” [T]. 6:33: NASB95 “His kingdom”, ESV “the kingdom of God” [T]; see Difficulty. Pulpit divergence: at 6:33 the ESV congregation will hear “of God”, which rests on bracketed text; and at 20:15 they will not hear “eye”.

Difficulty. The bracketed [τοῦ θεοῦ] at 6:33. NA28 prints τὴν βασιλείαν [τοῦ θεοῦ] καὶ τὴν δικαιοσύνην αὐτοῦ (“the kingdom [of God] and his righteousness”) with the brackets; the SBLGNT omits τοῦ θεοῦ [T]. The apparatus records א (and k, l, sa, bo, Eusebius) without “of God”; B has the order “righteousness … kingdom” without it; the majority text has it [T]. Matthew’s usual phrase is ἡ βασιλεία τῶν οὐρανῶν (“the kingdom of heaven”); ἡ βασιλεία τοῦ θεοῦ (“the kingdom of God”) is rare in Matthew [S], so “of God” is easier to explain as a scribal addition [I]. Nothing turns on it, since αὐτοῦ (“his”) already points to the Father of 6:32 [T]. A finding should not rest on “of God” here.

Pitfall. Preaching 6:25–34 as a technique for managing anxiety (“worry achieves nothing, so relax”). The argument is not psychological. Its ground is οἶδεν γὰρ ὁ πατήρ (“for your Father knows”, 6:32), its contrast is “the Gentiles” (6:32), and its cure is a new object of seeking, “the kingdom and his righteousness” (6:33) [T]. Corrective: preach the Father’s knowledge and the kingdom’s priority; ὀλιγόπιστοι (“you of little faith”, 6:30) diagnoses worry as small faith in the Father, not as poor self-management.

Book-overview note: extends. Add to the Echo Table: (1) ὁ ὀφθαλμός σου πονηρός (“your eye is evil”) 6:23 → 20:15, the only two places, which fixes the sense of 6:22–23 as generosity; (2) the δόξα (“glory”) chain: 4:8 and 6:29 (worldly glory), then only the Son of Man’s (16:27; 19:28; 24:30; 25:31); (3) κλέπτης / διορύσσω (“thief” / “break in”) 6:19–20 → 24:43. Add Deut 15:9 to the Intertextual Map, Torah, moderate–high.

13. Matthew 7:1–12 — Judging, Asking, the Golden Rule

Position. Q1: 6:19–34 ended with the Father who knows and gives (6:32–33) [T]. Q2: this unit exists here because the Sermon’s body must close where it opened. It turns from possessions to the brother (7:3–5), back to the Father who gives to those who ask (7:7–11), and ends with ὁ νόμος καὶ οἱ προφῆται (“the Law and the Prophets”, 7:12), the phrase of 5:17 [T]. 7:12 closes the frame of 5:17–7:12 (see unit 9) [T], verified.

Structure. Three short blocks. Judging and the brother (7:1–5), with one saying on holy things and swine (7:6). Asking, seeking, knocking, and the “how much more” of the Father (7:7–11). The summary rule, joined by οὖν (“therefore”, 7:12) [T]. The οὖν of 7:12 does not follow 7:11 closely; it is best read as gathering the whole body of the Sermon [I], moderate–high.

Text-first findings.

  • Judging, and its echoes. κρίνω (“to judge”) stands in 4 verses of Matthew: 5:40 (to “sue”); 7:1, 2; 19:28 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. At 19:28 the Twelve will sit on twelve thrones κρίνοντες (“judging”) the tribes of Israel [T]. The prohibition of 7:1 does not abolish judgement; it reserves it [I], moderate. μέτρον (“measure”) stands only at 7:2 and 23:32, πληρώσατε τὸ μέτρον τῶν πατέρων ὑμῶν (“fill up the measure of your fathers”) [T], verified. Uncertain as design.
  • “Hypocrite” in the singular, aimed at the disciple. ὑποκριτά (“you hypocrite”, 7:5) is the fourth of 13 uses (SBLGNT), and the only one addressed to a hearer of the Sermon in the singular [T]. The hypocrisy of 6:2–16 was performance before others; here it is blindness about oneself [T]. The speck and the log are about τοῦ ἀδελφοῦ σου (“your brother”, three times, 7:3–5) [T]. κάρφος (“speck”) and δοκός (“log”) stand only at 7:3, 4, 5 [T], verified.
  • Bread and stone: the tempter’s request answered. λίθος (“stone”) and ἄρτος (“bread”) stand together in a verse only at 4:3 and 7:9 (SBLGNT lemma intersection) [T], verified. At 4:3 the tempter says: εἰπὲ ἵνα οἱ λίθοι οὗτοι ἄρτοι γένωνται (“command that these stones become bread”). At 7:9 no father μὴ λίθον ἐπιδώσει αὐτῷ (“will give him a stone”) when his son asks for bread [T]. The Son refused to make bread for himself, trusting his Father; now he tells disciples that the Father will not give a stone for bread. Synthetic, verified: high for the chain; the reading [I], moderate–high. Luke lacks bread and stone here (see Synoptic check).
  • “You, being evil.” εἰ οὖν ὑμεῖς πονηροὶ ὄντες (“if you then, being evil”, 7:11) [T]. πονηροὶ ὄντες (“being evil”) stands only at 7:11 and 12:34, γεννήματα ἐχιδνῶν, πῶς δύνασθε ἀγαθὰ λαλεῖν πονηροὶ ὄντες; (“you brood of vipers, how can you speak good things, being evil?”) [T], verified. The same diagnosis is spoken to disciples and to Pharisees [T]. πόσῳ μᾶλλον (“how much more”) stands only at 7:11 and 10:25 [T], verified.
  • Asking: the second half of 6:8. αἰτέω (“to ask”) stands at 5:42 and 6:8 (before you ask), and then in five verses of 7:7–11 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The Father who “knows what you need before you ask him” (6:8) is the Father who gives ἀγαθά (“good things”) τοῖς αἰτοῦσιν αὐτόν (“to those who ask him”, 7:11) [T]. The three imperatives αἰτεῖτε, ζητεῖτε, κρούετε (“ask, seek, knock”) are present tense, “keep asking” [T] for the tense; the force is [I], moderate. ζητεῖτε (“seek”) repeats 6:33 [T].
  • The rule that sums the Law. οὗτος γάρ ἐστιν ὁ νόμος καὶ οἱ προφῆται (“for this is the Law and the Prophets”, 7:12) [T]. The same summary returns at 22:40: ἐν ταύταις ταῖς δυσὶν ἐντολαῖς ὅλος ὁ νόμος κρέμαται καὶ οἱ προφῆται (“on these two commandments hang all the Law and the Prophets”) [T]. νόμος (“law”) stands in 8 verses: 5:17, 18; 7:12; 11:13; 12:5; 22:36, 40; 23:23 [T], verified. Twice in Matthew the whole Law and Prophets is summed in a single demand of love: 7:12 and 22:40 [I], high.

Synoptic check. Judging and the measure: Luke 6:37–38, where Luke adds “condemn not … forgive … give” and the “good measure, pressed down” [T]. The speck and log: Luke 6:41–42 [T]. Asking: Luke 11:9–13. Luke’s pairs are fish/serpent and egg/scorpion (Luke 11:11–12); Matthew’s are bread/stone and fish/serpent [T]. The bread/stone pair is Matthew’s: NA28 Luke 11:11 reads without it, with 𝔓⁴⁵ 𝔓⁷⁵ B; א A C D and the majority text add ἄρτον μὴ λίθον ἐπιδώσει αὐτῷ (“bread — will he give him a stone?”), which the apparatus marks as a parallel from Matthew [T]. So the link to 4:3 is Matthew’s too. Luke’s Father gives πνεῦμα ἅγιον (“the Holy Spirit”, Luke 11:13); Matthew’s gives ἀγαθά (“good things”, 7:11) [T]. The golden rule: Luke 6:31, in the love-of-enemies section, without οὗτος γάρ ἐστιν ὁ νόμος καὶ οἱ προφῆται (“for this is the Law and the Prophets”) [T]. 7:6 has no parallel; the Law-and-Prophets clause at 7:12 is Matthew’s [T].

Tool 11.

Lev 19:18 → 7:12 (moderate–high; conceptual). Move 1: ἀγαπήσεις τὸν πλησίον σου ὡς σεαυτόν (“you shall love your neighbour as yourself”, Swete; WLC וְאָהַבְתָּ לְרֵעֲךָ כָּמוֹךָ) [T]. “As yourself” is the measure of 7:12’s “as you want them to do to you” [I]. Book usage: Lev 19:18 is cited at 5:43; 19:19; 22:39; at 22:40 it is half of the Law-and-Prophets summary [T]. What it adds: 7:12 is the Sermon’s own form of the second great commandment [I], moderate–high.

Tob 4:15 → 7:12 (outside the Tanak; moderate as background). Move 1: ὃ μισεῖς μηδενὶ ποιήσῃς (“what you hate, do to no one”, Swete) [T]. Matthew’s form is positive [T]. Flag for the “outside the Tanak” note.

Exod 22:30 (Swete 22:31) → 7:6 (uncertain–moderate). Move 1: ἄνδρες ἅγιοι ἔσεσθέ μοι· καὶ κρέας θηριάλωτον οὐκ ἔδεσθε, τῷ κυνὶ ἀπορίψατε αὐτό (“you shall be holy men to me; you shall not eat flesh torn by beasts; throw it to the dog”, Swete; WLC לַכֶּלֶב תַּשְׁלִכוּן אֹתוֹ, “to the dog you shall throw it”) [T]. ἅγι- (“holy”) with κυν- (“dog”) stands in Swete at Exod 22:31, but the regex also returns nine false hits from προσκυνέω (“to worship”) [T], verified; Exod 22:31 is the only real pairing in the Torah. In Exodus, what is unclean goes to the dogs; in Matthew, what is holy must not [I]. Rating: uncertain–moderate.

Internal: answers §4:3 — stones and bread (high for the chain, verified; not in the overview). Answers §5:17 — the Law and the Prophets (high, verified; the overview has it). Answers §6:8; §6:33 — asking; seeking (high, verified). Answers §6:2–16 — ὑποκριτής (“hypocrite”) (high). Plants §22:40 — the Law and the Prophets summed (high). Plants §12:34 — πονηροὶ ὄντες (“being evil”) (high for the chain, verified). Plants §19:28 — κρίνω (“to judge”) (moderate). Plants §8:30–32 — χοῖρος (“swine”) stands only at 7:6 and in the Gadarene scene (8:30, 31, 32) [T], verified (uncertain as design). Plants §13:45–46 — μαργαρίτης (“pearl”) stands only at 7:6 and 13:45, 46 [T], verified (moderate: the pearl of great price is the kingdom).

Translations. 7:3 δοκός: NASB95 and ESV “log” [T]. 7:12: NASB95 “treat people the same way you want them to treat you”; ESV “whatever you wish that others would do to you, do also to them” [T]. The ESV keeps the “do” (ποιῶσιν / ποιεῖτε, “do” twice), which links to the doing of 7:21, 24, 26 [T] for the ESV wording; the NASB95 “treat” loses it. 7:11 πονηροὶ ὄντες: both “being evil” / “who are evil” [T]. Pulpit divergence: at 7:12 the ESV’s “do … do” is nearer the Greek than the NASB95’s “treat”; none other bearing.

Pitfall. Preaching 7:1 as a ban on all moral discernment (“Jesus said, do not judge”). The same unit requires discernment about dogs and swine (7:6), and the next calls for recognising false prophets by their fruit (7:15–20) [T]. Corrective: 7:1–5 forbids the judging that sees the brother’s speck and not one’s own log, and puts the judge under the same measure; it does not forbid seeing clearly (διαβλέψεις, “you will see clearly”, 7:5).

Book-overview note: extends. Add to the Echo Table: λίθος + ἄρτος (“stone” + “bread”) 4:3 → 7:9, the only two verses with both, and Matthew’s own against Luke. Note under Live sources that 7:12 and 22:40 are the book’s two summaries of the Law and the Prophets.

14. Matthew 7:13–29 — Two Ways, Two Trees, Two Houses

Position. Q1: 7:12 closed the body of the Sermon with “the Law and the Prophets” [T]. Q2: this unit exists here because the Sermon cannot end on instruction alone. It ends by dividing hearers: two gates, two kinds of tree, two claims on “Lord, Lord”, two builders [T]. It then closes the first discourse with the formula Καὶ ἐγένετο ὅτε ἐτέλεσεν (“and when he had finished”, 7:28) and the crowds’ verdict on his ἐξουσία (“authority”, 7:29), the word the next two chapters will prove [T].

Structure. Four pairs. Narrow and wide (7:13–14). Good and rotten trees, framed by ἀπὸ τῶν καρπῶν αὐτῶν ἐπιγνώσεσθε αὐτούς (“by their fruits you will know them”, 7:16, 20) [T]. Saying and doing (7:21–23). Wise and foolish builders, hearing and doing or not doing (7:24–27) [T]. The two builder paragraphs are word for word the same except for the verbs of doing, the adjective, the foundation and the end: οὐκ ἔπεσεν (“it did not fall”) / ἔπεσεν (“it fell”) [T]. Then the narrator’s close (7:28–29).

Text-first findings.

  • Jesus repeats John word for word. πᾶν δένδρον μὴ ποιοῦν καρπὸν καλὸν ἐκκόπτεται καὶ εἰς πῦρ βάλλεται (“every tree that does not bear good fruit is cut down and thrown into the fire”, 7:19) is John’s sentence at 3:10 (SBLGNT phrase search on the shared wording: 3:10 and 7:19 only) [T], verified. 3:10 has οὖν (“therefore”) and the axe at the root; 7:19 has neither [T]. καρπός (“fruit”) stands in 14 verses, starting at 3:8, 10 [T], verified. The forerunner’s warning is taken up unchanged by the one he announced [I], high. The overview does not have this pair.
  • False prophets and lawlessness. ψευδοπροφήτης (“false prophet”) stands only at 7:15; 24:11, 24 [T], verified. ἀνομία (“lawlessness”) stands at 7:23; 13:41; 23:28; 24:12 [T], verified (the overview has both). 24:11–12 joins the two: false prophets arise and ἀνομία (“lawlessness”) increases [T]. The Sermon’s last warning is about the end-time [I], moderate–high.
  • Wolves and sheep. λύκος (“wolf”) stands only at 7:15 and 10:16 [T], verified. At 7:15 wolves come ἐν ἐνδύμασι προβάτων (“in sheep’s clothing”); at 10:16 the disciples are sent ὡς πρόβατα ἐν μέσῳ λύκων (“as sheep in the midst of wolves”) [T]. The figures are reversed: here the danger is inside the flock, there the flock is sent out among the danger [I], moderate–high.
  • “Lord, Lord” and the will of my Father. Κύριε κύριε (“Lord, Lord”) stands only at 7:21, 22 and 25:11 [T], verified (the overview has it). At 7:21 the criterion is ὁ ποιῶν τὸ θέλημα τοῦ πατρός μου (“the one who does the will of my Father”) [T]. θέλημα (“will”) stands in 6 verses: 6:10; 7:21; 12:50; 18:14; 21:31; 26:42 [T], verified. This is the first time in the book that Jesus says τοῦ πατρός μου (“my Father”) [T] (πατήρ verses before 7:21 are human fathers or “your/our Father”; checked against the lemma list). In the Sermon πατήρ (“father”) of God stands 17 times (SBLGNT lemma list, 5:16–7:21): fifteen times “your Father” (ὑμῶν / σου), once “our Father” in the prayer he gives (6:9), and only at the judgement scene “my Father” (7:21) [T], verified; the significance is [I], moderate–high.
  • The Judge speaks the sufferer’s psalm. ἀποχωρεῖτε ἀπ᾽ ἐμοῦ οἱ ἐργαζόμενοι τὴν ἀνομίαν (“depart from me, you who practise lawlessness”, 7:23). See Tool 11. Ὁμολογήσω (“I will declare”, 7:23) is the verb of 10:32: ὁμολογήσω κἀγὼ ἐν αὐτῷ (“I also will confess him”) [T]. ὁμολογέω (“to confess, declare”) stands at 7:23; 10:32 (twice); 14:7 [T], verified. What he will “confess” at the judgement is either the disciple (10:32) or “I never knew you” (7:23) [I], moderate–high.
  • Wise and foolish: the virgins. φρόνιμος (“wise”) and μωρός (“foolish”) are set side by side only at 7:24, 26 and in the parable of the virgins (25:2, 3, 4, 8, 9) [T], verified (lemma lists: φρόνιμος 7:24; 10:16; 24:45; 25:2, 4, 8, 9; μωρός 5:22; 7:26; 23:17; 25:2, 3, 8). The virgins’ parable also has Κύριε κύριε (“Lord, Lord”, 25:11) and the refusal οὐκ οἶδα ὑμᾶς (“I do not know you”, 25:12) [T]. 7:21–27 and 25:1–13 share three elements: wise and foolish, “Lord, Lord”, and “I do not know you” (synthetic, verified: high for the chain). The overview has only the second and third.
  • Rain, rivers, winds — and the storm next. ἄνεμος (“wind”) stands in 9 verses; the first two are 7:25, 27, and the next two are 8:26, 27, the storm stilled [T], verified. The house that stands in the winds is followed by the boat in which the disciples fear the winds, and the question ποταπός ἐστιν οὗτος ὅτι καὶ οἱ ἄνεμοι … ὑπακούουσιν; (“what kind of man is this, that even the winds … obey him?”, 8:27) [T]. Synthetic, verified: moderate for design.
  • Authority, and “their” scribes. ἐξεπλήσσοντο οἱ ὄχλοι ἐπὶ τῇ διδαχῇ αὐτοῦ (“the crowds were amazed at his teaching”, 7:28). ἐπὶ τῇ διδαχῇ αὐτοῦ (“at his teaching”) stands only at 7:28 and 22:33, after the Sadducees are silenced [T], verified. ἐξουσία (“authority”) stands in 9 verses: 7:29; 8:9; 9:6, 8; 10:1; 21:23 (twice), 24, 27; 28:18 [T], verified. The first is the crowd’s verdict on his teaching; the last is his own word, πᾶσα ἐξουσία (“all authority”, 28:18). Matthew has οἱ γραμματεῖς αὐτῶν (“their scribes”, 7:29) where Mark 1:22 has οἱ γραμματεῖς (“the scribes”) [T]; NA28 prints αὐτῶν, and the majority text omits it [T] (apparatus).

Synoptic check. Luke has the narrow door (Luke 13:24, θύρας, “door”, not πύλης, “gate”), the trees (Luke 6:43–45), “Lord, Lord” as a question, τί δέ με καλεῖτε· Κύριε κύριε, καὶ οὐ ποιεῖτε ἃ λέγω; (“why do you call me ‘Lord, Lord’ and not do what I say?”, Luke 6:46), the two builders (Luke 6:47–49), and “depart from me” in a different setting (Luke 13:27) [T]. Luke’s builder digs deep and the river breaks against the house; Matthew’s has rain, rivers and winds in a triple, and the foolish man builds ἐπὶ τὴν ἄμμον (“on the sand”), where Luke’s builds ἐπὶ τὴν γῆν χωρὶς θεμελίου (“on the ground without a foundation”) [T]. Matthew alone has the wolves in sheep’s clothing (7:15), the repeat of 3:10 (7:19), the prophesying, exorcising and miracles “in your name” (7:22), and φρόνιμος / μωρός (“wise” / “foolish”) [T]. 7:28b–29 is Mark 1:22 almost verbatim, moved to the end of the Sermon [T].

Tool 11.

Ps 6:9 → 7:23 (high; the overview has it). Source context: Ps 6 is a sufferer’s prayer: “my bones are troubled … I am weary with my groaning” (6:3, 7) [T]. At 6:9 he turns on his enemies: Swete ἀπόστητε ἀπ᾽ ἐμοῦ πάντες οἱ ἐργαζόμενοι τὴν ἀνομίαν, ὅτι εἰσήκουσεν Κύριος τῆς φωνῆς τοῦ κλαυθμοῦ μου (“depart from me, all you workers of lawlessness, for the Lord has heard the voice of my weeping”) [T]. Rahlfs agrees [T]. WLC: סוּרוּ מִמֶּנִּי כָּל־פֹּעֲלֵי אָוֶן (“depart from me, all you workers of wickedness”) [T]. Wording: οἱ ἐργαζόμενοι τὴν ἀνομίαν (“who practise lawlessness”) is a Psalter formula in Swete (Pss 6:9; 35:13; 52:5; 91:8, 10; 93:4; 118:3) [T], verified; ἀπόστητε ἀπ᾽ ἐμοῦ (“depart from me”) with it stands only at Ps 6:9 [T], verified. Matthew uses his own verb ἀποχωρεῖτε (“depart”) and drops πάντες (“all”) [T]. NA28 apparatus: L Θ ƒ¹³ 1424 add πάντες (“all”), and Θ ƒ¹³ read ἀναχωρεῖτε (“withdraw”) [T]; the scribes heard the psalm. Luke 13:27 has the Psalm’s verb ἀπόστητε and πάντες, but ἐργάται ἀδικίας (“workers of unrighteousness”) [T]. Matthew keeps the Psalm’s object (ἀνομία, “lawlessness”); Luke keeps its verb. Book usage: first Psalm quotation in Jesus’ mouth in Matthew [I] (the overview’s map lists none earlier). OT-to-OT: the Hebrew פֹּעֲלֵי אָוֶן (“workers of wickedness”) is a Psalter refrain for the enemies of the righteous [T] for the listed Swete verses. What it adds: the words of the afflicted righteous one, whose prayer God has heard, become the Judge’s sentence. The one who will speak them is himself the sufferer of the passion psalms [I], moderate–high. Nearest: Greek (Swete = Rahlfs) for ἀνομία; Matthew’s own verb. Divergence: the Greek ἀνομία (“lawlessness”) narrows the Hebrew אָוֶן (“wickedness, trouble”); a translational choice that suits Matthew’s Law theme (5:17–20) [I].

Jer 21:8 (with Deut 30:15, 19) → 7:13–14 (moderate). Move 1: Ἰδοὺ ἐγὼ δέδωκα πρὸ προσώπου ὑμῶν τὴν ὁδὸν τῆς ζωῆς καὶ τὴν ὁδὸν τοῦ θανάτου (“behold, I set before you the way of life and the way of death”, Swete Jer 21:8) [T], spoken as Jerusalem faces Babylon. Deut 30:15: τὴν ζωὴν καὶ τὸν θάνατον (“life and death”) [T]. Matthew has ἀπώλεια (“destruction”), not θάνατος (“death”), against ζωή (“life”) [T]. ζωή (“life”) stands in 7 verses, always of the life of the age to come (7:14; 18:8, 9; 19:16, 17, 29; 25:46) [T], verified.

Ezek 13:10–14 → 7:24–27 (with 7:15) (moderate; not in the overview). Source context: the prophets who cry “Peace” when there is no peace build a flimsy wall and whitewash it [T]. Swete 13:11: ἔσται ὑετὸς κατακλύζων … καὶ πνεῦμα ἐξαῖρον (“there will be a flooding rain … and a violent wind”), and the wall πεσεῖται (“will fall”); 13:14 its θεμέλια (“foundations”) are laid bare [T]. WLC: גֶּשֶׁם שׁוֹטֵף … וְרוּחַ סְעָרוֹת (“a flooding rain … and a stormy wind”) [T]. Matthew shares rain, flood, wind, falling and foundation (τεθεμελίωτο, “it had been founded”, 7:25), and the context of false prophets (7:15) [T]; it shares no rare word with the Greek [T]. Rating: moderate as a thematic allusion; Hebrew and Greek agree, so no “nearest” can be named.

Ezek 22:27 (with Zeph 3:3) → 7:15 (moderate). Move 1: Swete Ezek 22:27, οἱ ἄρχοντες αὐτῆς … ὡς λύκοι ἁρπάζοντες ἁρπάγματα (“her princes … like wolves tearing prey”), and the next verse turns to her prophets who see false visions [T]. λυκ- (“wolf”) with ἁρπα- (“seize”) stands in Swete only at Gen 49:27 (Benjamin) and Ezek 22:27 [T], verified. Matthew’s λύκοι ἅρπαγες (“ravenous wolves”) is nearest Ezek 22:27 Greek.

Internal: answers §3:10 — the tree and the fire, verbatim (high, verified; not in the overview). Answers §5:13 — μωραίνω / μωρός (“become foolish” / “foolish”); see unit 9 (moderate). Answers §5:17; §5:20 — doing (ποιέω) and the Law (high). Answers §6:10 — the Father’s will (high, verified). Plants §10:16 — wolves and sheep (moderate–high, verified). Plants §10:32 — ὁμολογέω (“to confess”) (moderate–high, verified). Plants §8:26–27 — the winds (moderate). Plants §16:18 — rock, build (moderate; the overview has it). Plants §22:33 — astonished at his teaching (high for the chain, verified). Plants §24:11–12, 24 — false prophets, lawlessness (high, verified). Plants §25:1–13 — wise/foolish, “Lord, Lord”, “I do not know you” (high, verified; synthetic). Plants §28:18 — ἐξουσία (“authority”) (high, verified).

Translations. 7:23 οἱ ἐργαζόμενοι τὴν ἀνομίαν: NASB95 “you who practice lawlessness” (in small capitals, marking an OT quotation); ESV “you workers of lawlessness” [T]. The ESV keeps the Psalter’s “workers”. 7:14 τεθλιμμένη: NASB95 “narrow”, ESV “hard” [T]. The ESV’s “hard” catches the root θλίβω (“to press, afflict”), whose noun θλῖψις (“tribulation”) returns at 13:21; 24:9, 21, 29 [I] for the link. 7:28 ἐξεπλήσσοντο: NASB95 “amazed”, ESV “astonished” [T]; both keep the same verb at 22:33. Pulpit divergence: none bearing. The NASB95 alone signals that 7:23 is a quotation.

Pitfall. Treating the four pairs as four separate tests (a narrow life, good deeds, orthodox confession, Bible study). The text links them: the trees are judged by fruit (7:16–20), the claimants are judged by doing the Father’s will (7:21), and the builders by doing “these words” (7:24, 26) [T]. Prophesying and miracles “in your name” do not count (7:22) [T]. Corrective: preach the one criterion the unit repeats, hearing and doing the words of Jesus, which is the doing of the Father’s will; and preach “I never knew you” as relational, not as a failed checklist.

Book-overview note: confirms with something new and extends. Add to the Echo Table: (1) 3:10 → 7:19, John’s sentence repeated verbatim by Jesus; (2) the φρόνιμος / μωρός (“wise” / “foolish”) pairing at 7:24–26 and 25:2–9, which joins the overview’s existing “Lord, Lord” row into a three-element chain; (3) ἐπὶ τῇ διδαχῇ αὐτοῦ (“at his teaching”) 7:28 → 22:33. Add Ezek 13:10–14 and Ezek 22:27 to the Intertextual Map at moderate. In the Ps 6:9 row, note that Matthew keeps the Psalm’s ἀνομία (“lawlessness”) and Luke its verb, and that L Θ ƒ¹³ assimilate πάντες (“all”).

15. Matthew 8:1–17 — The Leper, the Centurion, the Fever: “He Took Our Weaknesses”

Position. Q1: the Sermon ended with the crowds astonished that he taught ὡς ἐξουσίαν ἔχων (“as one having authority”, 7:29) [T]. Q2: this unit exists here because authority in word must now be shown in deed. The first deeds after the mountain are three healings of people on the edge of Israel’s worship: a leper, a Gentile’s servant, a woman. The unit ends with the narrator’s formula citation (8:17), which tells the reader how to read them [T]/[I].

Structure. The leper (8:1–4); the centurion’s servant (8:5–13); Peter’s mother-in-law (8:14–15); the evening summary and the formula (8:16–17) [T]. Three healings, then a summary and Scripture, is the first of three triads of deeds in chapters 8–9 [I], moderate.

Text-first findings.

  • The leper is touched, and sent to the priest. καθαρίζω (“to make clean”) stands twice in 8:2–3, and again in Matthew only at 10:8; 11:5; 23:25, 26 (SBLGNT, 7 occurrences in 6 verses, verified) [T]. It runs from this healing to the commission (10:8, “cleanse lepers”) and to John’s answer (11:5), then turns against the scribes’ cups (23:25–26). The man is sent εἰς μαρτύριον αὐτοῖς (“as a testimony to them”, 8:4), wording Matthew shares with Mark 1:44 [T].
  • The centurion’s faith, and the many from east and west. οὐδὲ … ἐν τῷ Ἰσραὴλ τοσαύτην πίστιν εὗρον (“I have not found such great faith with anyone in Israel”, 8:10). πίστις (“faith”) occurs first here in Matthew; its eight occurrences (SBLGNT) are 8:10; 9:2, 22, 29; 15:28; 17:20; 21:21; 23:23 [T], verified. Matthew alone joins to it the saying about πολλοὶ ἀπὸ ἀνατολῶν καὶ δυσμῶν (“many from east and west”) reclining with Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, while the υἱοὶ τῆς βασιλείας (“sons of the kingdom”) are thrown out (8:11–12). Luke has the saying elsewhere (13:28–29) [T]. A Gentile believes; Abraham’s table opens (cf. 1:1) [I], high.
  • She “was serving him”. διηκόνει αὐτῷ (“she began waiting on Him”, 8:15). Mark 1:31 has αὐτοῖς (“them”); Matthew narrows the service to Jesus [T]. διακονέω (“to serve”) then runs: angels (4:11), this woman (8:15), the Son of Man who came not to be served but to serve (20:28), the judgement scene (25:44), the women at the cross (27:55) [T], verified (6 occurrences in 5 verses). Synthetic, moderate: the first and last human servants in the narrative are women.
  • The formula reads the healings as the Servant’s work. Αὐτὸς τὰς ἀσθενείας ἡμῶν ἔλαβεν καὶ τὰς νόσους ἐβάστασεν (“He Himself took our infirmities and carried away our diseases”, 8:17) [T]. ἀσθένεια (“weakness”) occurs only here in Matthew; νόσος (“disease”) at 4:23, 24; 8:17; 9:35; 10:1 (SBLGNT, verified) — the word of the summaries that frame the ministry and are handed to the Twelve [T]. βαστάζω (“to carry”) is John’s word at 3:11 (“I am not fit to carry his sandals”) [T].

Synoptic check. Matthew takes the leper (Mark 1:40–45) and the fever (Mark 1:29–31) from Mark, and the centurion from the tradition he shares with Luke (Luke 7:1–10). He moves the leper to follow the Sermon and drops Mark’s account of the leper’s disobedience (Mark 1:45). He compresses the centurion story: the man comes himself, with no Jewish elders (Luke 7:3–5). He adds the east-and-west saying (8:11–12) and the formula citation (8:17), both of which are his own [T].

Tool 11. Isa 53:4 → 8:17 (high — formula quotation; in the overview’s “nearer the Hebrew” list, re-checked here). Source context: the fourth Servant Song. The speakers confess that they misjudged the Servant: “Surely our griefs he himself bore … yet we esteemed him stricken.” Text: WLC חֳלָיֵנוּ (“our sicknesses”) … וּמַכְאֹבֵינוּ (“our pains”); Swete and Rahlfs alike οὗτος τὰς ἁμαρτίας ἡμῶν φέρει (“he bears our sins”) [T]. Matthew renders the Hebrew, not the Greek. Category 3 of the triage: the Greek reading is not demoted; Matthew simply cites a different text, and his point (healing) needs the Hebrew. Book usage: first Isaiah 53 citation; the Servant returns at 12:18–21 (Isa 42) and the “many” at 20:28; 26:28. OT-to-OT: Isa 53:4 belongs to the same Song as 53:11–12, whose “many” Matthew uses later. What it adds: the healings are not merely signs of power. They are the Servant taking what afflicts his people upon himself. The road from 8:17 runs to the cross.

Ps 107:3 → 8:11 (moderate) — ἀπὸ ἀνατολῶν καὶ δυσμῶν (“from east and west”) of the redeemed gathered; Move 1 only. The overview lists it; agreed.

Internal: answers §1:1 — “son of Abraham”: Gentiles at Abraham’s table (moderate–high). Plants §10:8; §11:5 — καθαρίζω (high, verified). Plants §15:28 — the second Gentile whose faith Jesus praises (high, verified by the πίστις chain). Plants §20:28; §27:55 — διακονέω (moderate–high, verified). Plants §12:18–21; §20:28 — the Servant (high).

Translations. NASB95 “took our infirmities and carried away our diseases”; ESV “took our illnesses and bore our diseases”. The ESV’s “illnesses” is closer to ἀσθένεια as Matthew uses it; both keep the healing sense. Pulpit divergence: none bearing.

Pitfall. Reading 8:17 as a promise that faith secures healing now. The text says the Servant bears sickness, and the Servant goes to the cross; the healings are signs of that work. The corrective: preach 8:17 with Isa 53 entire, and with 20:28.

Book-overview note: confirms the 8:17 row. Extends it: Matthew’s διηκόνει αὐτῷ (8:15, against Mark’s αὐτοῖς) makes the woman’s service a response to Jesus, and opens the διακονέω chain that ends at 27:55.

16. Matthew 8:18–9:8 — Who Is This? Following, the Storm, Gadara, Authority to Forgive (⭐ for 9:1–8)

Position. Q1: 8:1–17 showed authority over disease. Q2: this unit widens it to the sea, the demons and sin itself, and asks the question the book has been planting: ποταπός ἐστιν οὗτος (“what kind of man is this?”, 8:27). Between the healings and the storm stand two would-be followers (8:18–22), so that the question “who is this?” is asked by people deciding whether to follow him [T]/[I]. The unit ends with the first charge of blasphemy (9:3), which the trial will repeat (26:65) [T].

Structure. Two would-be followers (8:18–22); the storm (8:23–27); the two demoniacs of Gadara (8:28–34); the paralytic forgiven and healed (9:1–8) [T]. The first three scenes share movement across the sea (εἰς τὸ πέραν, “to the other side”, 8:18, 28) and back (9:1) [T].

Text-first findings.

  • The first Son of Man saying is a homeless one. ὁ δὲ υἱὸς τοῦ ἀνθρώπου οὐκ ἔχει ποῦ τὴν κεφαλὴν κλίνῃ (“but the Son of Man has nowhere to lay His head”, 8:20) [T]. Of the overview’s 30 Son of Man occurrences (SBLGNT), this is the first. The title enters the book without glory; the authority saying follows at 9:6 [T].
  • The disciples follow him into the storm. ἐμβάντι αὐτῷ εἰς τὸ πλοῖον ἠκολούθησαν αὐτῷ οἱ μαθηταὶ αὐτοῦ (“when He got into the boat, His disciples followed Him”, 8:23). ἀκολουθέω (“to follow”) stands at 8:19, 22 and 23 [T], verified. Matthew sets the storm as the immediate test of the following just discussed [I], high.
  • The storm is a σεισμός. ἰδοὺ σεισμὸς μέγας ἐγένετο ἐν τῇ θαλάσσῃ (“there arose a great storm on the sea”, 8:24). Mark 4:37 has λαῖλαψ (“windstorm”). σεισμός (“shaking, earthquake”) occurs in Matthew only at 8:24; 24:7; 27:54; 28:2 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. Synthetic, moderate: Matthew’s word for the storm is the word for the shaking at the cross and the tomb.
  • “Save, Lord; we are perishing.” Κύριε, σῶσον, ἀπολλύμεθα (8:25). Mark 4:38 has Διδάσκαλε, οὐ μέλει σοι (“Teacher, do You not care…?”). Matthew turns a reproach into a prayer, and names him κύριος (“Lord”) [T]. Jesus answers ὀλιγόπιστοι (“you men of little faith”, 8:26) before he calms the sea; in Mark the rebuke follows [T]. ὀλιγόπιστος (“of little faith”) stands at 6:30; 8:26; 14:31; 16:8 (SBLGNT) [T], verified.
  • The demons know the Son and the timetable. υἱὲ τοῦ θεοῦ … ἦλθες ὧδε πρὸ καιροῦ βασανίσαι ἡμᾶς; (“Son of God, have You come here to torment us before the time?”, 8:29). πρὸ καιροῦ (“before the time”) occurs in Matthew only here, and in the NT elsewhere only at 1 Cor 4:5 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The demons assume a fixed time of judgement; Jesus has brought it early. The town begs him to leave (8:34) [T].
  • Authority to forgive, given “to men”. ἐξουσίαν ἔχει ὁ υἱὸς τοῦ ἀνθρώπου ἐπὶ τῆς γῆς ἀφιέναι ἁμαρτίας (“the Son of Man has authority on earth to forgive sins”, 9:6). The crowds glorify God τὸν δόντα ἐξουσίαν τοιαύτην τοῖς ἀνθρώποις (“who had given such authority to men”, 9:8) — plural, Matthew’s own ending, against Mark 2:12’s “we have never seen anything like this” [T]. ἐξουσία (“authority”) runs 7:29; 8:9; 9:6, 8; 10:1; 21:23–27; 28:18 (SBLGNT, 10 occurrences in 9 verses) [T], verified. The plural looks ahead to the authority given to the Twelve (10:1) and to the binding and loosing of 16:19; 18:18 [I], moderate.
  • The first blasphemy charge. Οὗτος βλασφημεῖ (“this fellow blasphemes”, 9:3). βλασφημέω (“to blaspheme”) occurs only at 9:3; 26:65; 27:39 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The scribes’ charge here becomes the high priest’s verdict and the passers-by’s mockery. And Jesus knows τὰς ἐνθυμήσεις αὐτῶν (“their thoughts”, 9:4); ἐνθυμέομαι (“to think, ponder”) occurs only at 1:20 (Joseph) and 9:4 [T], verified.

Synoptic check. Matthew shares the storm, the demoniac and the paralytic with Mark (4:35–5:20; 2:1–12) and the two would-be followers with Luke (9:57–60). He doubles the demoniac (δύο δαιμονιζόμενοι, “two demon-possessed men”, 8:28) and shortens the scene to a third of Mark’s length, dropping the man’s request to follow (Mark 5:18–20). He moves the paralytic after the sea crossing and omits the roof. He adds ὀλιγόπιστοι, the order of rebuke before calm, σεισμός, “Save, Lord”, “before the time” and “to men” [T].

Tool 11. Jonah 1:4–6 → 8:24–25 (moderate — narrative pattern with two verbal contacts). Source context: the LORD hurls a storm on the sea; the sailors are afraid; Jonah sleeps below; the captain wakes him, “that God may save us and we may not perish”. Swete Jon 1:5 ἐκάθευδεν (“he was sleeping”), 1:6 μὴ ἀπολώμεθα (“that we may not perish”) [T]. Matthew 8:24 has ἐκάθευδεν — the form Mark lacks (καθεύδων, Mark 4:38) — and ἀπολλύμεθα (“we are perishing”) [T]. ἐκάθευδεν occurs in the NT only at Matt 8:24; in Swete at Jon 1:5 among nine places (SBLGNT, Swete; verified). Book usage: Jonah is named at 12:39–41 and 16:4, “something greater than Jonah”. What it adds: the sleeping figure in a storm is the reverse of Jonah — not fleeing God, but the one whom wind and sea obey. Uncited by the overview; new candidate, moderate, unaudited.

Ps 107:23–30 (Swete 106) (moderate — Move 1 only): sailors cry to the LORD, and he stills the storm. 106:29 Swete ἐσίγησαν τὰ κύματα (“the waves were stilled”) [T]. The question “what kind of man is this?” is answered by the psalm: only the LORD does this [I].

Internal: answers §1:20 — ἐνθυμέομαι: Joseph pondering, Jesus reading thoughts (moderate, verified). Plants §26:65; §27:39 — blasphemy (high, verified). Plants §27:54; §28:2 — σεισμός (moderate, verified; synthetic). Plants §14:31; §16:8 — ὀλιγόπιστοι (high, verified). Plants §10:1; §28:18 — ἐξουσία (high, verified). Plants §14:22–33 — the second sea scene, where the question is answered: “truly You are God’s Son” (high).

Translations. σεισμός at 8:24: NASB95 and ESV both “a great storm”, which hides the link to 27:54 and 28:2 (“earthquake”). Text: 8:28 NA28 Γαδαρηνῶν (“Gadarenes”); the apparatus records Γερασηνῶν (“Gerasenes”, latt sa) and Γεργεσηνῶν (“Gergesenes”, א² and the majority) [T]. Both English versions follow NA28. Pulpit divergence: none beyond what both versions share.

Pitfall. Preaching the storm as “Jesus calms the storms of your life”. Matthew’s question is christological: ποταπός ἐστιν οὗτος. The corrective: preach the answer the book gives at 14:33, and let the storm be about who he is before it is about what he does for us.

Book-overview note: confirms the σεισμός (“shaking”) chain the overview names. Extends: the overview has no Jonah row for 8:24–25; new candidate, moderate.

17. Matthew 9:9–17 — Mercy and Not Sacrifice

Position. Q1: 9:1–8 ended with the crowds glorifying God “who had given such authority to men” (9:8), after the first charge of blasphemy (9:3). The authority to forgive has been shown; the question is now whom it reaches [T]. Q2: this unit exists here because forgiveness must find its company. The tax collector is called, the table is filled with τελῶναι καὶ ἁμαρτωλοί (“tax collectors and sinners”, 9:10), and the first of two citations of Hos 6:6 gives the reason. It also sets the bridegroom’s presence against fasting, and so plants the first hint of the passion (9:15) before any plot exists (12:14) [T]/[I].

Structure. The call of Matthew (9:9); the meal and the Pharisees’ question (9:10–13); John’s disciples’ question about fasting (9:14–17). Each question is put to someone other than Jesus or about him (9:11 to the disciples; 9:14 by John’s disciples), and each is answered by a saying of Jesus (9:12–13; 9:15–17) [T]. The two questions both begin Διὰ τί (“why?”, 9:11, 14) [T].

Text-first findings.

  • The tax collector is named Matthew, here and at 10:3 only. Μαθθαῖος (“Matthew”) occurs at 9:9 and 10:3 (SBLGNT), and the second adds ὁ τελώνης (“the tax collector”) [T], verified. Mark 2:14 names him Λευίν (“Levi”). The call is the shortest in the book: Ἀκολούθει μοι (“Follow Me”) and ἀναστὰς ἠκολούθησεν αὐτῷ (“he got up and followed Him”, 9:9) [T]. That this is the evangelist’s self-reference is [S] (see the overview, Presenting Situation).
  • “Go and learn what this means.” πορευθέντες δὲ μάθετε τί ἐστιν· Ἔλεος θέλω καὶ οὐ θυσίαν (“but go and learn what this means: I desire mercy and not sacrifice”, 9:13). μανθάνω (“to learn”) occurs at 9:13; 11:29; 24:32 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The scholars are sent back to school on a verse of Hosea, and the next “learn” is μάθετε ἀπ’ ἐμοῦ (“learn from Me”, 11:29). The lesson moves from a text to a person [I], moderate.
  • Mercy is a chain the English breaks. ἔλεος (“mercy”) occurs at 9:13; 12:7; 23:23 only (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The cognate verb ἐλεάω (“to have mercy”; MorphGNT files ἐλεέω under this spelling) stands at 5:7; 9:27; 15:22; 17:15; 18:33 (twice); 20:30, 31 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. Hos 6:6 is quoted in 9:13, and fourteen verses later two blind men cry Ἐλέησον ἡμᾶς (“Have mercy on us”, 9:27). What God desires (9:13) is what the outcasts then ask of Jesus (9:27).
