Synthetic, verified; high for the chain, moderate as design.
  • “I came … to call.” οὐ γὰρ ἦλθον καλέσαι δικαίους ἀλλὰ ἁμαρτωλούς (“for I did not come to call the righteous, but sinners”, 9:13). Jesus says ἦλθον (“I came”) with an infinitive of purpose at 5:17 (twice), 9:13 and 10:34–35 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. Μὴ νομίσητε ὅτι ἦλθον (“do not think that I came”) stands at 5:17 and 10:34 only [T], verified. The “I came” sayings say what the coming is for: to fulfil, to call sinners, to bring a sword. The NA28 records the Majority addition εἰς μετάνοιαν (“to repentance”, from Luke 5:32) and does not print it [T].
  • Mourning, not only fasting. Mark 2:19 asks whether the wedding guests can νηστεύειν (“fast”). Matthew asks whether they can πενθεῖν (“mourn”, 9:15) [T]. πενθέω (“to mourn”) occurs at 5:4 and 9:15 only (SBLGNT) [T], verified. “Blessed are those who mourn” (5:4) has its occasion named: the bridegroom taken away [I], moderate–high.
  • The first hint of the passion. ἐλεύσονται δὲ ἡμέραι ὅταν ἀπαρθῇ ἀπ’ αὐτῶν ὁ νυμφίος (“but the days will come when the bridegroom is taken away from them”, 9:15). ἀπαίρω (“to take away”) occurs once in Matthew [T], verified. νυμφίος (“bridegroom”) returns only in the parable of the ten virgins (25:1, 5, 6, 10) [T], verified. The bridegroom who is taken away (9:15) is the bridegroom who comes at midnight (25:6). Synthetic, verified; moderate–high.
  • Both are preserved. Matthew alone ends the wineskin saying with καὶ ἀμφότεροι συντηροῦνται (“and both are preserved”, 9:17); Mark 2:22 and Luke 5:38–39 lack it [T]. συντηρέω (“to preserve”) occurs once in Matthew [T]. The new does not destroy the old; it keeps it. This sits with 5:17 (“not to abolish but to fulfil”) and with the scribe who brings out καινὰ καὶ παλαιά (“new things and old”, 13:52) [I], moderate–high. καινός (“new”) occurs at 9:17; 13:52; 26:29; 27:60 (SBLGNT) [T], verified.
  • “Worse” begins here. χεῖρον σχίσμα (“a worse tear”, 9:16). χείρων (“worse”) occurs at 9:16; 12:45; 27:64 only (SBLGNT) [T], verified. See the overview’s echo table (12:45 → 27:64).

Synoptic check. The unit follows Mark 2:13–22 closely [T]. Matthew (1) names the tax collector Matthew; (2) makes the questioners at 9:14 “the disciples of John”, where Mark 2:18 is impersonal; (3) inserts Hos 6:6 with πορευθέντες δὲ μάθετε (“go and learn”), which neither Mark nor Luke has; (4) changes “fast” to “mourn” in 9:15a; (5) adds “both are preserved” (9:17). Each change is Matthew’s own [T] for the wording, [I] for the purpose.

Tool 11.

Hos 6:6 → 9:13 (high — quotation, without formula). Source context: YHWH laments over Ephraim and Judah: τὸ δὲ ἔλεος ὑμῶν ὡς νεφέλη πρωινή (“your mercy is like a morning cloud”, Hos 6:4 Swete). Therefore he has “hewn them by the prophets” (6:5). Then the reason: כִּי חֶסֶד חָפַצְתִּי וְלֹא־זָבַח וְדַעַת אֱלֹהִים מֵעֹלוֹת (“for I delight in loyal love and not sacrifice, and the knowledge of God rather than burnt offerings”, 6:6 WLC; חֶסֶד, 2617, and זֶבַח, 2077, gated). The next verse accuses them: “like Adam they transgressed the covenant” (6:7). The verse is a prophetic verdict on worship without covenant loyalty [T]. Text: Swete reads ἔλεος θέλω ἢ θυσίαν (“I desire mercy rather than sacrifice”). Rahlfs reads ἔλεος θέλω καὶ οὐ θυσίαν (“I desire mercy and not sacrifice”), Matthew’s exact words, and the Hebrew has וְלֹא (“and not”) [T]. Matthew stands with Rahlfs and the Hebrew; this is a Swete–Rahlfs divergence, not a Matthean departure (as the overview already says). Book usage: first use of Hosea since 2:15 (Hos 11:1); cited again at 12:7 [T]. OT-to-OT: the theme of Hos 6:6 is the prophets’ own (1 Sam 15:22; Ps 40:7; Mic 6:6–8) [I]; no second text is cited here. What it adds: the Pharisees’ objection is answered with the prophets’ charge against Israel’s worship. The table with sinners is ἔλεος (“mercy”), חֶסֶד (“loyal love”), and the separation they defend is the “sacrifice” God did not desire.

Isa 62:5; Hos 2:16–20 → 9:15 (moderate). Move 1 only: the LORD as Israel’s bridegroom. Jesus calls himself ὁ νυμφίος (“the bridegroom”) without comment.

Internal: Answers §5:4 — the mourners, now named (high on the wording, verified). Plants §9:27; §12:7; §23:23 — ἔλεος / ἐλεάω (“mercy / have mercy”) (high, verified). Plants §25:1–13 — the bridegroom (moderate–high, verified). Plants §11:19 — τελωνῶν φίλος καὶ ἁμαρτωλῶν (“a friend of tax collectors and sinners”): the meal of 9:10 becomes the charge of 11:19 (high, verified: ἁμαρτωλός (“sinner”) at 9:10, 11, 13; 11:19; 26:45). Plants §26:45 — the last ἁμαρτωλῶν (“of sinners”): “the Son of Man is betrayed into the hands of sinners”. The friend of sinners is handed over to sinners (moderate, verified; synthetic). Answers §1:21 — “he will save his people from their sins”: here he calls sinners (moderate). Answers §5:17 and plants §13:52 — new and old, both kept (moderate–high).

Translations. At 9:13 (and 12:7) the NASB95 reads “I DESIRE COMPASSION, AND NOT SACRIFICE”; at 23:23 it reads “mercy”; at 9:27 “Have mercy”. The ESV reads “mercy” at 9:13, 12:7 and 23:23. The NASB95 also uses “compassion” for σπλαγχνίζομαι (“to be moved with compassion”) at 9:36, so in the study text one English word covers two Greek words and the ἔλεος (“mercy”) chain is broken. At 9:15 NASB95 “attendants of the bridegroom”, ESV “wedding guests” for οἱ υἱοὶ τοῦ νυμφῶνος (“the sons of the bridal chamber”). Pulpit divergence: none bearing; the ESV keeps the mercy chain the study text breaks.

Pitfall. “Jesus ate with sinners, so the church should be inclusive.” The unit’s reason is not inclusion but calling: οὐ … ἦλθον καλέσαι (“I did not come to call”, 9:13), and the doctor comes to the sick (9:12). Mercy here is God’s covenant love that calls and heals. The corrective: preach 9:12–13 as the physician’s errand, with Hos 6:6 as God’s verdict on a righteousness that keeps its distance.

Book-overview note: Extends. (1) The overview’s English table should add 9:13; 12:7: the NASB95’s “compassion” breaks the ἔλεος (“mercy”) chain to 23:23 and 9:27, and collides with “felt compassion” (σπλαγχνίζομαι, 9:36). (2) The echo table can add 9:15 → 25:1–13 (νυμφίος, “bridegroom”, verified) and 9:15 → 5:4 (πενθέω, “to mourn”, only two uses).

18. Matthew 9:18–38 — Daughter, Son of David, and the Shepherdless Crowd

Position. Q1: chapters 8–9 have shown authority over disease, storm, demons and sin, and 9:13 has named mercy as what God desires [T]. Q2: this unit completes the cycle of deeds with the cases that matter most for 11:5 — the dead raised, the blind seeing, the mute speaking — and then closes the frame opened at 4:23 with the same words (9:35). It ends on the shepherdless crowd and the prayer for workers, which is why chapter 10 follows [T]/[I].

Structure. A ruler’s daughter and a bleeding woman, the second set inside the first (9:18–26). Two blind men (9:27–31). A mute demoniac, and the two verdicts (9:32–34). The summary that repeats 4:23 (9:35), and the compassion and the harvest (9:36–38) [T]. Three healings of “touch” come in a row: she touched his garment (9:20–21), he took her hand (9:25), he touched their eyes (9:29) [T].

Text-first findings.

  • The girl is already dead. Ἡ θυγάτηρ μου ἄρτι ἐτελεύτησεν (“my daughter has just died”, 9:18). In Mark 5:23 she ἐσχάτως ἔχει (“is at the point of death”) [T]. Matthew removes the messengers who bring news of death (Mark 5:35) and so makes the request a request for resurrection from the first line: ζήσεται (“she will live”, 9:18) [T]/[I]. The ruler προσεκύνει (“was bowing down”, 9:18), one of the ten places where Jesus is the one worshipped (see the overview’s Microscript) [T].
  • “She was raised.” ἐκράτησεν τῆς χειρὸς αὐτῆς, καὶ ἠγέρθη τὸ κοράσιον (“he took her by the hand, and the girl got up”, 9:25). ἠγέρθη (“was raised, got up”) occurs at 8:15; 9:25; 14:2; 27:64; 28:6, 7 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The form used of the girl is the form the angel uses of Jesus: ἠγέρθη γάρ (“for He has risen”, 28:6). Synthetic, verified; moderate as design. In 10:8 the Twelve are told νεκροὺς ἐγείρετε (“raise the dead”), and in 11:5 νεκροὶ ἐγείρονται (“the dead are raised”) [T].
  • The tassel. She touched τοῦ κρασπέδου τοῦ ἱματίου αὐτοῦ (“the fringe of His cloak”, 9:20). Mark 5:27 has only τοῦ ἱματίου (“the cloak”); Luke 8:44 agrees with Matthew [T]. κράσπεδον (“tassel, fringe”) occurs at 9:20; 14:36; 23:5 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. At 23:5 the scribes and Pharisees lengthen theirs. The tassels commanded in Num 15:38–39 (κράσπεδα, Swete and Rahlfs) are worn by Jesus, and the unclean woman is healed through them [I], moderate.
  • “Saved,” three times. σωθήσομαι … σέσωκέν σε … ἐσώθη (“I will be saved … has saved you … was saved”, 9:21–22) [T]. σῴζω (“to save”) occurs in 13 verses (SBLGNT), from 1:21 (“he will save his people”) to 27:40–42 (“save yourself … he cannot save himself”) [T], verified. Θάρσει, θύγατερ (“take courage, daughter”, 9:22): θαρσέω (“to take courage”) occurs at 9:2, 9:22 and 14:27 only [T], verified. Matthew adds the word; Mark 5:34 has only Θυγάτηρ (“daughter”).
  • “Son of David” first on human lips. Ἐλέησον ἡμᾶς, υἱὲ Δαυίδ (“Have mercy on us, Son of David”, 9:27). The title is the narrator’s at 1:1 and the angel’s for Joseph at 1:20; at 9:27 it is first spoken to Jesus, by the blind [T], verified (Δαυίδ (“David”) in 15 verses, SBLGNT). The plea answers 9:13: ἐλεάω (“to have mercy”) follows ἔλεος (“mercy”) within fourteen verses [T], verified. The blind see who he is before the scholars do [I], high.
  • Doublets that plant chapter 12 and chapter 20. The two blind men (9:27–31) recur at 20:29–34, again two, again Ἐλέησον ἡμᾶς … υἱὸς Δαυίδ (“have mercy on us … Son of David”, 20:30, 31) [T]. The mute demoniac (9:32–34) recurs at 12:22–24, there blind and mute; and the Pharisees’ verdict ἐν τῷ ἄρχοντι τῶν δαιμονίων ἐκβάλλει τὰ δαιμόνια (“he casts out the demons by the ruler of the demons”, 9:34) recurs at 12:24 [T], verified (ἄρχοντι τῶν δαιμονίων, “the ruler of the demons”, at 9:34 and 12:24 only). 9:34 is the seed of 12:22–37 [I], high.
  • Two verdicts on one deed. The crowds: Οὐδέποτε ἐφάνη οὕτως ἐν τῷ Ἰσραήλ (“nothing like this has ever been seen in Israel”, 9:33). The Pharisees: “by the ruler of the demons” (9:34) [T]. The unit ends the deeds of 8–9 on a divided response, which 11:2–12:50 will develop [I].
  • The frame closes, and passes on. 9:35 repeats 4:23 word for word: διδάσκων ἐν ταῖς συναγωγαῖς αὐτῶν καὶ κηρύσσων τὸ εὐαγγέλιον τῆς βασιλείας καὶ θεραπεύων πᾶσαν νόσον καὶ πᾶσαν μαλακίαν (“teaching in their synagogues and proclaiming the gospel of the kingdom, and healing every disease and every sickness”) [T], verified; the last phrase passes to the Twelve at 10:1 (μαλακία, “sickness”, at 4:23; 9:35; 10:1 only) [T], verified (see the overview).
  • The shepherdless are harassed and thrown down. ἐσκυλμένοι καὶ ἐρριμμένοι (“harassed and thrown down”, 9:36). σκύλλω (“to harass”) occurs once in Matthew; ῥίπτω (“to throw”) at 9:36; 15:30; 27:5 [T], verified. The words are strong: the crowd is a flock mauled and cast down [I]. ἐσπλαγχνίσθη (“He felt compassion”) is the first of five uses of σπλαγχνίζομαι (9:36; 14:14; 15:32; 18:27; 20:34) [T], verified.
  • The harvest’s Lord sends. δεήθητε … τοῦ κυρίου τοῦ θερισμοῦ ὅπως ἐκβάλῃ ἐργάτας (“beseech the Lord of the harvest to send out workers”, 9:38). ἐκβάλῃ (“send out”, literally “throw out”) is the verb of the exorcisms two verses before (9:33, 34) [T]. θερισμός (“harvest”) returns only at 13:30, 39, where “the harvest is the end of the age” [T], verified. The mission is eschatological harvest [I], moderate–high.

Synoptic check. 9:18–26 abbreviates Mark 5:21–43 sharply (Mark’s 23 verses become 9). Matthew drops Jairus’ name, the crowd’s pressing, the woman’s story of doctors and the “who touched me?” exchange, and the Aramaic of Mark 5:41 [T]. He adds that the girl is already dead (9:18), “take courage” (9:22), “from that hour” (9:22) and the report going out (9:26). 9:27–34 has no Markan parallel at this point; it anticipates Mark 10:46–52 and Mark 3:22 [T]. 9:36 draws Mark 6:34 forward and changes its ὡς (“as”) to ὡσεί (“like”) [S: audit]. 9:37–38 is double tradition with Luke 10:2 [T].

Tool 11.

Num 27:17 → 9:36 (high for the phrase; moderate for the Joshua link) [S: audit]. Accepted as audited. ὡσεὶ πρόβατα (“like sheep”) occurs in Swete only at Num 27:17 and Ps 79:2, and Matthew’s ὡσεί (“like”) is his own change to Mark 6:34. Text-first work extends the audit at one point: the shepherd vocabulary was planted at 2:6, where Micah’s ruler ποιμανεῖ τὸν λαόν μου τὸν Ἰσραήλ (“will shepherd my people Israel”). ποιμαίνω (“to shepherd”) occurs only at 2:6, and ποιμήν (“shepherd”) at 9:36; 25:32; 26:31 [T], verified. The shepherdless crowd is the people the child of Bethlehem was born to shepherd [I], moderate–high.

Num 15:38–39 → 9:20 (moderate). Move 1 only: Israel’s tassels are commanded “so that you may see them and remember all the commandments of the LORD” (15:39). The woman touches the commandment’s sign on Jesus’ cloak.

Ezek 34:4–5, 16 → 9:36 (moderate). Move 1 only: Israel’s shepherds did not seek τὸ ἀπολωλός (“the lost”, Swete 34:4), so the sheep were scattered; YHWH says Τὸ ἀπολωλὸς ζητήσω (“I will seek the lost”, 34:16). Unit 19 takes this up at 10:6.

Internal: Answers §2:6 — the shepherd (high, verified). Answers §9:13 — mercy asked for (high, verified). Replants §1:1, 20 — “Son of David” (high). Plants §11:5 — dead raised, blind see, mute speak (high). Plants §12:22–24 — the demoniac and the verdict (high, verified). Plants §20:29–34 — the two blind men (high). Plants §28:6 — ἠγέρθη (“he was raised”) (moderate, verified). Plants §28:15 — διαφημίζω (“to spread abroad”) occurs at 9:31 and 28:15 only [T], verified: the blind men spread his fame against his order, and the guards’ lie “is spread” to this day. The first and last spreading of a report (moderate as design). Plants §13:30, 39 — the harvest (moderate–high, verified).

Translations. At 9:36 NASB95 “distressed and dispirited”, ESV “harassed and helpless”. Both soften ἐρριμμένοι (“thrown down”). At 9:22 NASB95 “Daughter, take courage; your faith has made you well”, ESV “Take heart, daughter; your faith has made you well”. Both render σέσωκεν (“has saved”) as “made well”, so the σῴζω (“save”) chain from 1:21 is hidden in both. Pulpit divergence: none bearing.

Difficulty. 9:34 is printed in the NA28 inside ⸋ ⸌ marks: it is omitted by D a k syˢ and Hilary [T]. The verse is not bracketed, and the omission is Western. Its doublet at 12:24 is secure [T]. Nothing in this unit rests on 9:34 alone.

Book-overview note: Extends. (1) διαφημίζω (“to spread abroad”, 9:31; 28:15 only) is an early↔︎late echo the overview does not list. (2) The overview’s English table can add 9:22, where both versions turn σῴζω (“to save”) into “made well”.

19. Matthew 10:1–23 — Sent Like Sheep Among Wolves

Position. Q1: the shepherdless crowd and the prayer for workers (9:36–38) ask for exactly what 10:1 supplies: workers, with the shepherd’s own authority [T]. Q2: the unit exists here because the deeds of 8–9 must now be multiplied. The Twelve receive the last words of the 4:23 = 9:35 frame (10:1) and are told to do the deeds of chapters 8–9 (10:8). Then the discourse turns from mission to persecution (10:16–23), so that the sent share the sender’s reception before the reader has seen that reception at its worst (12:14) [T]/[I].

Structure. The Twelve given authority and named (10:1–4). The charge: where to go, what to say, what to do, what to carry, how to enter and leave a house (10:5–15). The turn, Ἰδοὺ ἐγὼ ἀποστέλλω ὑμᾶς (“behold, I send you out”), and the persecutions to come: councils and synagogues, governors and kings, families, cities (10:16–23) [T]. The unit is bracketed by “Israel”: τὰ πρόβατα τὰ ἀπολωλότα οἴκου Ἰσραήλ (“the lost sheep of the house of Israel”, 10:6) and τὰς πόλεις τοῦ Ἰσραήλ (“the cities of Israel”, 10:23) [T].

Text-first findings.

  • The Twelve receive what Jesus has done. ἔδωκεν αὐτοῖς ἐξουσίαν … θεραπεύειν πᾶσαν νόσον καὶ πᾶσαν μαλακίαν (“He gave them authority … to heal every disease and every sickness”, 10:1) [T]. ἐξουσία (“authority”) occurs in 9 verses (SBLGNT): 7:29 (teaching), 8:9, 9:6, 8 (forgiving), 10:1 (given to the Twelve), 21:23–27 (questioned) and 28:18 (“all authority”) [T], verified. The list of 10:8 — sick healed, dead raised, lepers cleansed, demons cast out — is chapters 8–9 in summary: the leper (8:2–4), the dead girl (9:25), the demoniacs (8:28–34; 9:32–33) [T]/[I], high.
  • “Apostles”, once. τῶν δὲ δώδεκα ἀποστόλων (“of the twelve apostles”, 10:2). ἀπόστολος (“apostle”) occurs only here in Matthew [T], verified. The book’s word is μαθητής (“disciple”); “apostle” names the twelve at the moment of their sending (ἀπέστειλεν, “sent”, 10:5) [I].
  • Matthew the tax collector, and Judas who handed him over. Μαθθαῖος ὁ τελώνης (“Matthew the tax collector”, 10:3; Mark 3:18 has no label) [T], verified. Ἰούδας ὁ Ἰσκαριώτης ὁ καὶ παραδοὺς αὐτόν (“Judas Iscariot, the one who betrayed Him”, 10:4) [T]. παραδίδωμι (“to hand over”) is the keyword of the discourse: 10:4 (Judas hands over Jesus), 10:17, 19 (“they will hand you over”), 10:21 (“brother will hand over brother”) [T]. It was used first of John (4:12, παρεδόθη, “had been taken into custody”); it will be used of Jesus (17:22; 20:18, 19; 26:2 and on) [T], verified (31 verses, SBLGNT). John is handed over, Jesus will be, and so will the disciples: one verb for the forerunner, the Lord and the sent. Synthetic, verified; high for the chain.
  • Only to Israel, for now. Εἰς ὁδὸν ἐθνῶν μὴ ἀπέλθητε … πορεύεσθε δὲ μᾶλλον πρὸς τὰ πρόβατα τὰ ἀπολωλότα οἴκου Ἰσραήλ (“do not go in the way of the Gentiles … but rather go to the lost sheep of the house of Israel”, 10:5–6). The phrase “the lost sheep of the house of Israel” occurs at 10:6 and 15:24 only (SBLGNT) [T], verified. Yet the same discourse says ἀχθήσεσθε … εἰς μαρτύριον αὐτοῖς καὶ τοῖς ἔθνεσιν (“you will be brought … as a testimony to them and to the Gentiles”, 10:18) [T]. The restriction already strains inside the discourse that imposes it. It is lifted at 28:19 with the same noun, ἔθνη (“nations, Gentiles”) (see the overview’s echo table) [T].
  • The message is the King’s, word for word. Ἤγγικεν ἡ βασιλεία τῶν οὐρανῶν (“the kingdom of heaven has come near”, 10:7). The same announcement is John’s (3:2) and Jesus’ (4:17), there with γάρ (“for”) [T], verified (ἤγγικεν γὰρ ἡ βασιλεία at 3:2; 4:17; ἤγγικεν ἡ βασιλεία at 10:7). John, Jesus and the Twelve preach one sentence [T]; the disciples are given no message of their own [I], high.
  • Worthiness is the discourse’s measuring word. ἄξιος (“worthy”) occurs in 7 verses (SBLGNT): 3:8; 10:10, 11, 13 (twice), 37 (twice), 38; 22:8 [T], verified. In the first half the question is which house is worthy of the disciple (10:11–13); in the second half, which disciple is worthy of Jesus (10:37–38) [T]. The word turns round in the middle of the chapter [I], high. 22:8 closes the chain: οἱ δὲ κεκλημένοι οὐκ ἦσαν ἄξιοι (“those who were invited were not worthy”).
  • Peace, and its opposite. ἡ εἰρήνη ὑμῶν (“your peace”, 10:13, twice). εἰρήνη (“peace”) occurs in Matthew only at 10:13 and 10:34 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The greeting of peace that may return to the sender (10:13) is set against οὐκ ἦλθον βαλεῖν εἰρήνην ἀλλὰ μάχαιραν (“I did not come to bring peace, but a sword”, 10:34) [T]. See unit 20.
  • Sodom, and the day of judgement. ἀνεκτότερον ἔσται γῇ Σοδόμων … ἐν ἡμέρᾳ κρίσεως (“it will be more tolerable for the land of Sodom … in the day of judgement”, 10:15). ἀνεκτός (“tolerable”) occurs at 10:15; 11:22, 24 only; ἐν ἡμέρᾳ κρίσεως (“in the day of judgement”) at 10:15; 11:22, 24; 12:36 only; Σόδομα (“Sodom”) at 10:15; 11:23, 24 only [T], all verified. The threat made against the town that refuses the disciples (10:15) is made in the next chapter against the towns that refused Jesus (11:20–24). Synthetic, verified; high.
  • Sheep, wolves, serpents, doves. Ἰδοὺ ἐγὼ ἀποστέλλω ὑμᾶς ὡς πρόβατα ἐν μέσῳ λύκων (“behold, I send you out as sheep in the midst of wolves”, 10:16). λύκος (“wolf”) occurs at 7:15 and 10:16 only, and at 7:15 the wolves are false prophets in sheep’s clothing [T], verified. The sheep of 9:36 and 10:6 are now the disciples themselves [T]. φρόνιμοι ὡς οἱ ὄφεις (“shrewd as serpents”) is Matthew’s own (Luke 10:3 lacks it) [T].
  • The disciples’ path is Jesus’ path, in advance. The disciples will be handed over to συνέδρια (“councils”, 10:17) — Jesus stands before τὸ συνέδριον ὅλον (“the whole Council”, 26:59). They will be scourged, μαστιγώσουσιν (10:17) — μαστιγόω (“to scourge”) recurs at 20:19 (of Jesus) and 23:34 (of the prophets Jesus sends) [T], verified. They will stand ἐπὶ ἡγεμόνας (“before governors”, 10:18) — ἡγεμών (“governor”) is Pilate’s title eight times in 27:2–28:14, and first stands at 2:6, ἐν τοῖς ἡγεμόσιν Ἰούδα (“among the leaders of Judah”) [T], verified. Synthetic, verified; high for the chains.
  • Endurance to the end. ὁ δὲ ὑπομείνας εἰς τέλος οὗτος σωθήσεται (“it is the one who has endured to the end who will be saved”, 10:22) is repeated verbatim at 24:13, and ὑπομένω (“to endure”) occurs only there [T], verified (see the overview). The mission discourse borrows its language from the end-time discourse (Mark 13:9–13) [T].

Synoptic check. Matthew builds 10:1–23 from three Markan blocks and double tradition [T]. The call and list follow Mark 3:13–19 (with ὁ τελώνης, “the tax collector”, added). The charge follows Mark 6:7–11, but Matthew forbids even the staff (10:10, μηδὲ ῥάβδον, “nor a staff”, against Mark 6:8 εἰ μὴ ῥάβδον μόνον, “except a staff only”). 10:5b–6, “not to the Gentiles”, has no parallel anywhere. 10:17–22 is Mark 13:9–13, moved from the end-time discourse into the mission, with μαστιγώσουσιν (“they will scourge”) for Mark’s δαρήσεσθε (“you will be beaten”) and with καὶ τοῖς ἔθνεσιν (“and to the Gentiles”) added. 10:16a is Luke 10:3 (ἄρνας, “lambs”, there); 10:16b and 10:23 are Matthew’s own [T].

Tool 11.

Jer 50:6 (Swete 27:6) → 10:6 (moderate–high). Source context: in the oracle against Babylon, YHWH turns to his people: צֹאן אֹבְדוֹת הָיָה עַמִּי רֹעֵיהֶם הִתְעוּם (“my people have been lost sheep; their shepherds have led them astray”, Jer 50:6 WLC; אבד, 6, gated). Swete and Rahlfs (27:6) read Πρόβατα ἀπολωλότα ἐγενήθη ὁ λαός μου (“my people has become lost sheep”) [T]. The context is return from exile: “they will come and seek the LORD … an everlasting covenant” (50:4–5) [T]. Text: πρόβατα ἀπολωλότα (“lost sheep”) occurs in Swete only at Jer 27:6 (positive control: the search returns Jer 27:6 and nothing else; the singular πρόβατον ἀπολωλός stands at Ps 118:176 Swete) [T], verified. Matthew stands with the Greek; the Hebrew says the same. Book usage: first use of Jeremiah after 2:17–18 (Rachel weeping, from Jer 31:15, also an exile text) [T]. OT-to-OT: Ezek 34 is the companion: shepherds who did not seek τὸ ἀπολωλός (“the lost”, 34:4), and YHWH who will seek it (34:16) and set over them “one shepherd, my servant David” (34:23) [I]. Jeremiah and Ezekiel already share the picture of Israel as a flock lost by its shepherds [I]. What it adds: “lost sheep” names Israel as the exiled flock awaiting its return. The Twelve are sent as the shepherd’s workers to gather it — and in 15:24 Jesus says the same of himself.

Mic 7:6 → 10:21 (moderate–high; quoted at 10:35–36, see unit 20). Move 1: ἐπαναστήσονται τέκνα ἐπὶ γονεῖς (“children will rise up against parents”, 10:21) uses Micah’s verb ἐπαναστήσεται (“will rise up”, Mic 7:6 Swete and Rahlfs) [T]. The words are Mark 13:12’s [T]. The full quotation follows at 10:35–36.

Gen 3:1 → 10:16 (uncertain). ὁ ὄφις ἦν φρονιμώτατος (“the serpent was the shrewdest”, Gen 3:1 Swete and Rahlfs) is the only Swete verse with φρονιμώτατος (“shrewdest”) (positive control passed) [T]. The serpent’s shrewdness is proverbial, so the echo may be only the proverb.

Ezek 22:27; Zeph 3:3 → 10:16 (uncertain). Move 1 only: in both, Jerusalem’s rulers and judges are ὡς λύκοι (“like wolves”, Swete). If heard, the wolves of 10:16 are Israel’s own leaders, which fits 10:17 (“their synagogues”) [I].

Internal: Answers §9:36–38 — workers sent to the shepherdless (high). Answers §4:23; §9:35 — the frame handed on (high, verified). Answers §3:2; §4:17 — one message (high, verified). Answers §4:12 and plants §17:22; §20:18–19; §26:2 — παραδίδωμι (“to hand over”) (high, verified; synthetic). Plants §11:20–24 — Sodom, the day of judgement (high, verified). Plants §15:24 — the lost sheep (high, verified). Plants §24:9–14 — the end-time discourse repeats 10:17–22 (high, verified). Plants §26:59; §27:2 — council and governor (high, verified; synthetic). Plants §28:19 — the Gentiles (high). Opening material: answers §2:6 (ἡγεμών, “leader, governor”, and ποιμαίνω, “to shepherd”, in Micah’s text) (moderate).

Translations. At 10:16 NASB95 “shrewd as serpents”, ESV “wise as serpents” for φρόνιμοι (“shrewd, prudent”). The ESV’s “wise” makes it sound like σοφοί (“wise”, 11:25), which is a different word and in 11:25 a word of judgement. At 10:23 NASB95 “you will not finish going through the cities of Israel until the Son of Man comes”, ESV “you will not have gone through all the towns of Israel before the Son of Man comes”. Pulpit divergence: 10:16 — the congregation will hear “wise as serpents”, which can be heard as approval of a quality 11:25 condemns. Note it if the sermon makes anything of “wisdom”.

Difficulty. 10:23, “until the Son of Man comes.” Category: eschatological; one of the book’s cruxes. Options [S]: (a) the resurrection and the commission (28:16–20); (b) the judgement on Jerusalem in AD 70; (c) the parousia, with the mission to Israel unfinished until then; (d) a saying whose horizon is the whole church’s mission to Israel to the end. Text-first data: ἔλθῃ ὁ υἱὸς τοῦ ἀνθρώπου (“the Son of Man comes”) occurs at 10:23 and 25:31 only (SBLGNT), and at 25:31 it is the coming in glory to judge [T], verified. The NA28 apparatus records several Western expansions of the first half of the verse (D it) [T]; none bears on the crux. Take this to Logos before it is preached.

Pitfall. “Don’t take anything with you — trust God for provision.” The rule of 10:9–10 is grounded in ἄξιος γὰρ ὁ ἐργάτης τῆς τροφῆς αὐτοῦ (“for the worker is worthy of his support”, 10:10): the receiving household feeds the worker. It is about the urgency and dependence of this mission, not a rule against provision for all mission. The corrective: preach the commission as the extension of Jesus’ own authority and message (10:1, 7), and the poverty as the sign that the kingdom is a gift (δωρεὰν ἐλάβετε, δωρεὰν δότε, “freely you received, freely give”, 10:8).

Book-overview note: Extends. (1) ἄξιος (“worthy”) turns round across 10:10–13 and 10:37–38 (verified), and εἰρήνη (“peace”) stands only at 10:13 and 10:34 (verified). Both bind the two halves of the discourse. (2) The παραδίδωμι (“hand over”) chain — John (4:12), the disciples (10:17–21), Jesus (17:22 on) — is not in the echo table. (3) The overview’s Intertextual Map lacks Jer 50:6 (Swete 27:6) for 10:6 and 15:24; it lists Ezek 34 only for 9:36.

20. Matthew 10:24–42 — Not Peace but a Sword; the Cup of Cold Water

Position. Q1: 10:16–23 announced that the Twelve will be handed over, scourged and hated. It did not say why. 10:24–25 gives the reason: the disciple shares the teacher’s lot, and the teacher has already been called Βεελζεβούλ (“Beelzebul”, 10:25) [T]. The rest of the unit tells the sent how to face that lot: whom to fear, whom to confess, what it costs, and who will receive them [T]. Q2: the unit exists here because the charge it names has been made once in the narrative (9:34, “by the ruler of the demons”) and will be made openly at 12:24. The discourse prepares the Twelve for the reception of their master before chapters 11–12 show it. It ends on receiving (10:40–42), and the next movement (11:2–12:50) is a story of who receives John and Jesus and who does not [T]/[I].

Structure. The disciple and the teacher (10:24–25). Three times “do not fear”: μὴ οὖν φοβηθῆτε (“therefore do not fear”, 10:26), μὴ φοβεῖσθε (“do not fear”, 10:28), μὴ οὖν φοβεῖσθε (“so do not fear”, 10:31), with confession and denial as the conclusion (10:32–33) [T]. Not peace but a sword (10:34–36). Three times οὐκ ἔστιν μου ἄξιος (“is not worthy of Me”, 10:37 twice, 38), and the paradox of losing and finding (10:39) [T]. Receiving and reward: δέχομαι (“to receive”) six times in 10:40–41, μισθός (“reward”) three times in 10:41–42 [T], verified. The discourse closes with the formula of 11:1 [T].

Text-first findings.

  • The Beelzebul charge is planted here, and answered in chapter 12. εἰ τὸν οἰκοδεσπότην Βεελζεβοὺλ ἐπεκάλεσαν (“if they have called the head of the house Beelzebul”, 10:25) [T]. Βεελζεβούλ (“Beelzebul”) occurs only at 10:25; 12:24; 12:27 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The narrative has not yet reported the name. 9:34 had only “by the ruler of the demons” [T]. Jesus quotes the charge before the narrator records it. The house image runs on: οἰκοδεσπότης (“head of the house”, 10:25), οἰκιακοί (“members of his household”, 10:25, 36; only there, verified), and in 12:25–29 the divided house and the strong man’s house [T]. Synthetic, moderate–high.
  • “Do not fear” three times, and one fear commanded. φοβέομαι (“to fear”) stands four times in 10:26–31 (SBLGNT, verified), three times forbidden and once commanded: φοβεῖσθε δὲ μᾶλλον τὸν δυνάμενον καὶ ψυχὴν καὶ σῶμα ἀπολέσαι ἐν γεέννῃ (“but rather fear Him who is able to destroy both soul and body in hell”, 10:28) [T]. The reason for not fearing is the Father’s care: the sparrow does not fall ἄνευ τοῦ πατρὸς ὑμῶν (“apart from your Father”, 10:29). διαφέρω (“to be worth more”) makes the argument from lesser to greater at 6:26; 10:31; 12:12, and only there [T], verified.
  • What is said in the dark is to be said in the light. ὃ λέγω ὑμῖν ἐν τῇ σκοτίᾳ, εἴπατε ἐν τῷ φωτί (“what I tell you in the darkness, speak in the light”, 10:27). σκοτία (“darkness”) and φῶς (“light”) stand together in Matthew only at 4:16 and 10:27 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. At 4:16 the people “sitting in darkness saw a great light” (Isa 8:23–9:1). The light that dawned on Galilee is now to be spoken by the disciples from the housetops. Synthetic, moderate. The hidden and the revealed also open a chain: ἀποκαλύπτω (“to reveal”) occurs only at 10:26; 11:25; 11:27; 16:17 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. “Nothing hidden will not be revealed” (10:26) is answered in the next chapter: the Father hides and reveals, and the Son reveals the Father (11:25–27).
  • Confession and denial, enacted by Peter. ὁμολογέω (“to confess”) and ἀρνέομαι (“to deny”), each twice, with ἔμπροσθεν τῶν ἀνθρώπων (“before men”) and ἔμπροσθεν τοῦ πατρός μου (“before My Father”), 10:32–33 [T]. ἀρνέομαι (“to deny”) occurs only at 10:33; 26:70; 26:72, and at 26:70 Peter denies ἔμπροσθεν πάντων (“before them all”) [T], verified (already in the overview’s echo table). This is also the first time in the discourse that Jesus says “My Father” (10:32–33; before this, “your Father”, 10:20, 29) [T].
  • The sword, and the household divided. οὐκ ἦλθον βαλεῖν εἰρήνην ἀλλὰ μάχαιραν (“I did not come to bring peace, but a sword”, 10:34). εἰρήνη (“peace”) occurs only at 10:13 and 10:34 (SBLGNT) [T], verified; the peace that the disciples give to a worthy house (10:13) is not the peace of the world [I]. μάχαιρα (“sword”) occurs only here and in the arrest (26:47, 51, 52 three times, 55) [T], verified. At the arrest a disciple draws a μάχαιρα and is told πάντες γὰρ οἱ λαβόντες μάχαιραν ἐν μαχαίρῃ ἀπολοῦνται (“all those who take up the sword shall perish by the sword”, 26:52) [T]. The sword Jesus brings divides households; it is not one his disciples wield. Synthetic, verified for the chain; moderate as design.
  • “Worthy of Me”: the measure turns on Jesus. ἄξιος (“worthy”) in 10:10–13 asked which house is worthy of the disciple; in 10:37–38 it asks which disciple is worthy of Jesus (see unit 19) [T]. The first cross in the book is here: ὃς οὐ λαμβάνει τὸν σταυρὸν αὐτοῦ (“he who does not take his cross”, 10:38). σταυρός (“cross”) occurs at 10:38; 16:24; 27:32, 40, 42 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The word is used before any prediction of the passion (16:21) [T].
  • Losing the ψυχή. ὁ ἀπολέσας τὴν ψυχὴν αὐτοῦ ἕνεκεν ἐμοῦ εὑρήσει αὐτήν (“he who has lost his life for My sake will find it”, 10:39). ψυχή (“soul, life”) stands 4 times in 10:28–39, then at 11:29 (“rest for your souls”), 12:18 (“My soul is well-pleased”), 16:25–26, 20:28 (“to give His life a ransom for many”) and 26:38 [T], verified (16 occurrences in 11 verses, SBLGNT). The disciple loses his ψυχή for Jesus’ sake; Jesus gives his ψυχή for many (20:28) [I], moderate.
  • The first cup is a cup of cold water. ὃς ἂν ποτίσῃ ἕνα τῶν μικρῶν τούτων ποτήριον ψυχροῦ μόνον εἰς ὄνομα μαθητοῦ (“whoever in the name of a disciple gives to one of these little ones even a cup of cold water to drink”, 10:42) [T]. ποτίζω (“to give to drink”) occurs at 10:42; 25:35, 37, 42; 27:48 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. At 25:35 the King says ἐδίψησα καὶ ἐποτίσατέ με (“I was thirsty, and you gave Me something to drink”), and at 25:40 the deed is done ἑνὶ τούτων τῶν ἀδελφῶν μου τῶν ἐλαχίστων (“to one of these brothers of Mine, even the least of them”) [T]. At 27:48 the one “given to drink” is Jesus on the cross, with sour wine [T]. The drink given to “one of these little ones” here is the drink given to the King in 25:35–40. Synthetic, verified for the chain; moderate–high as an echo. The NA28 apparatus records D and the Latin reading ἐλαχίστων τούτων (“these least”) at 10:42, assimilating to 25:40 [T], verified; the scribes heard the link. ποτήριον (“cup”) also begins here and runs to the cup Jesus must drink (20:22–23; 26:27, 39) [T], verified.
  • “In the name of”, three times. εἰς ὄνομα προφήτου … εἰς ὄνομα δικαίου … εἰς ὄνομα μαθητοῦ (“in the name of a prophet … of a righteous man … of a disciple”, 10:41–42) [T]. εἰς ὄνομα (“in the name”) without the article occurs only in these verses; εἰς τὸ ὄνομα (“into the name”) only at 28:19 (SBLGNT) [T], verified; 18:20 has εἰς τὸ ἐμὸν ὄνομα (“in My name”). The receiving of 10:40 is a chain of sending: ὁ ἐμὲ δεχόμενος δέχεται τὸν ἀποστείλαντά με (“he who receives Me receives Him who sent Me”) [T].

Synoptic check. The unit gathers sayings that Luke has in four places and Mark in two [T]. 10:24–25a is Luke 6:40; the slave and master, and the whole Beelzebul clause (10:25b), are Matthew’s own. 10:26–33 is Luke 12:2–9, but Luke has “the Son of Man will confess him before the angels of God” (Luke 12:8); Matthew has ὁμολογήσω κἀγώ … ἔμπροσθεν τοῦ πατρός μου (“I will also confess him before My Father”). 10:34–36 is Luke 12:51–53, where Luke has διαμερισμόν (“division”) for Matthew’s μάχαιραν (“sword”). 10:37–38 is Luke 14:26–27, where Luke has μισεῖ (“hate”) and οὐ δύναται εἶναί μου μαθητής (“cannot be My disciple”); Matthew has ὁ φιλῶν … ὑπὲρ ἐμέ (“he who loves … more than Me”) and οὐκ ἔστιν μου ἄξιος (“is not worthy of Me”), so that ἄξιος is Matthew’s word [T]. 10:39 has parallels at Mark 8:35 and Luke 17:33. 10:42 is Mark 9:41, where the cup is ὕδατος (“of water”) given “because you belong to Christ”; Matthew adds ψυχροῦ (“cold”) and moves Mark’s “one of these little ones” (Mark 9:42) into the cup saying [T].

Tool 11.

Mic 7:6 → 10:35–36 (high — quotation without formula). Source context: the prophet’s lament over a land with no godly person left (Mic 7:1–2). Judges take bribes and no friend can be trusted (7:3–5). Then the household turns on itself: כִּי־בֵן מְנַבֵּל אָב בַּת קָמָה בְאִמָּהּ כַּלָּה בַּחֲמֹתָהּ אֹיְבֵי אִישׁ אַנְשֵׁי בֵיתוֹ (“for son treats father contemptuously, daughter rises up against her mother, daughter-in-law against her mother-in-law; a man’s enemies are the men of his own household”, Mic 7:6 WLC) [T]. The very next verse turns: “But as for me, I will watch for the LORD; I will wait for the God of my salvation” (7:7). Swete reads ὑπομενῶ ἐπὶ τῷ θεῷ τῷ σωτῆρί μου (“I will wait for God my Saviour”), and Rahlfs the same [T]; the Hebrew has אוֹחִילָה (“I will wait”, 3176 gated) and יִשְׁעִי (“my salvation”). The chapter ends with the shepherding of the flock (7:14, Ποίμαινε λαόν σου, “shepherd Your people”, Swete) and with God who θελητὴς ἐλέους ἐστίν (“delights in mercy”, 7:18 Swete; Hebrew חָפֵץ חֶסֶד) [T]. Text: Matthew’s ἐχθροὶ τοῦ ἀνθρώπου οἱ οἰκιακοὶ αὐτοῦ (“a man’s enemies will be the members of his household”) has no “all”. Swete reads ἐχθροὶ πάντες ἀνδρὸς οἱ ἐν τῷ οἴκῳ αὐτοῦ (“all a man’s enemies are those in his house”), and Rahlfs ἐχθροὶ ἀνδρὸς πάντες οἱ ἄνδρες οἱ ἐν τῷ οἴκῳ αὐτοῦ (“all the men in his house are a man’s enemies”). The Hebrew has no “all” [T], verified. Swete and Rahlfs differ slightly (Rahlfs has οἱ ἄνδρες, “the men”, like the Hebrew אַנְשֵׁי). Matthew’s verb διχάσαι (“to set against”) and his οἰκιακοί (“members of the household”) are his own Greek. Matthew stands nearest the Hebrew for the last line; the rest is his own rendering. Book usage: Micah was quoted at 2:6 (Mic 5:1, 3), for the ruler who “will shepherd My people Israel” [T]. 10:21 already used Micah’s verb (ἐπαναστήσονται, “will rise up”; see unit 19). OT-to-OT: within Micah, the shepherd of 5:3 (cited at 2:6) and the shepherd prayer of 7:14 frame the household lament of 7:6 [T]/[I]. What it adds: Jesus quotes Micah’s picture of a society so corrupt that a man’s own house is against him. In Micah the next word is “I will wait for the God of my salvation”. The discourse has just said ὁ δὲ ὑπομείνας εἰς τέλος οὗτος σωθήσεται (“the one who has endured to the end will be saved”, 10:22), with ὑπομένω (“to endure”) and σῴζω (“to save”) [T]. The verse Matthew does not quote (Mic 7:7) is the promise the discourse has already given. Synthetic, moderate.

1 Sam 14:45; 2 Sam 14:11; 1 Kgs 1:52 → 10:29–30 (uncertain). Move 1 only: “not one hair of his head shall fall to the ground” is an oath formula of protection. Swete 1 Sam 14:45 has πεσεῖται (“will fall”) and τριχὸς τῆς κεφαλῆς (“hair of the head”) with ἐπὶ τὴν γῆν (“to the ground”) [T]. Matthew splits the formula between the sparrow that “will not fall to the ground” and the hairs “all numbered”. It may be no more than the idiom.

Internal: Plants §12:24–29 — Βεελζεβούλ (“Beelzebul”) and the house (high, verified). Plants §11:25–27; §16:17 — ἀποκαλύπτω (“to reveal”) (high, verified). Answers §4:16 — darkness and light (moderate, verified). Plants §26:70–72 — denial (high, verified; in the overview). Plants §26:51–52 — the sword (moderate, verified). Plants §16:24–25 — cross and ψυχή (high; 16:24–25 repeats 10:38–39 as a doublet). Plants §25:35–42 — the drink given to the least (moderate–high, verified). Plants §28:19 — “into the name” (moderate). Plants §20:22–23; §26:39 — the cup (moderate, synthetic).

Translations. At 10:32–33 NASB95 has “confesses … confess”, ESV “acknowledges … acknowledge” for ὁμολογέω (“to confess”). At 10:41–42 the NASB95 keeps “in the name of a prophet … in the name of a righteous man … in the name of a disciple”; the ESV has “because he is a prophet … because he is a righteous person … because he is a disciple”. The ESV’s sense is defensible, but it removes the threefold εἰς ὄνομα (“in the name”) and so the link to “baptising them in the name” (28:19). At 10:25 NASB95 has “slave”, ESV “servant” for δοῦλος (“slave”). Pulpit divergence: 10:41–42 — the congregation will not hear “in the name” three times. If the sermon makes anything of receiving in Jesus’ name, read it from the Greek or the NASB95.

Book-overview note: Extends. (1) The ποτίζω (“give to drink”) chain 10:42 → 25:35–42 → 27:48 is not in the echo table, though Trap 7 cites 10:42. D’s ἐλαχίστων (“least”) at 10:42 shows that scribes heard 25:40 in it. (2) Mic 7:6 is rated high; add that Matthew’s last line stands with the Hebrew (no “all”) against both Greek texts, and that Mic 7:7 (ὑπομενῶ … σωτῆρι, “I will wait … Saviour”) lies behind 10:22. (3) The Βεελζεβούλ (“Beelzebul”) chain (10:25 → 12:24, 27) and ἀποκαλύπτω (“to reveal”, 10:26 → 11:25, 27 → 16:17) are not in the echo table.

21. Matthew 11:1–19 — Are You the One Who Is Coming?

Position. Q1: the second discourse closed (11:1), and chapters 8–9 have shown the deeds that 4:23 and 9:35 summarised [T]. Q2: this unit exists here because the deeds must now be interpreted, and the first to ask is the forerunner himself, from prison. John hears τὰ ἔργα τοῦ χριστοῦ (“the works of Christ”, 11:2) and asks whether this is ὁ ἐρχόμενος (“the Coming One”, 11:3) — the title he himself gave at 3:11 [T]. The answer opens the movement of division (11:2–16:20): who receives him and who does not [I], high.

Structure. John’s question and Jesus’ answer (11:2–6); Jesus on John (11:7–15); the children in the market place (11:16–19) [T]. An inclusio: τὰ ἔργα τοῦ χριστοῦ (“the works of the Christ”, 11:2) and ἐδικαιώθη ἡ σοφία ἀπὸ τῶν ἔργων αὐτῆς (“wisdom is vindicated by her deeds”, 11:19) [T]. Luke 7:35 has τέκνων (“children”) where Matthew has ἔργων (“works”); the frame is Matthew’s [T].

Text-first findings.

  • “The Christ” in the narrator’s voice. 11:2 is the first time since the prologue (1:1, 16, 17, 18; 2:4) that the narrator uses ὁ χριστός (“the Christ”), and he uses it of the deeds [T]. ἔργον (“work, deed”) stands in 6 verses (SBLGNT): 5:16; 11:2; 11:19; 23:3, 5; 26:10 [T], verified.
  • The answer is a catalogue of chapters 8–9, in Isaiah’s words. τυφλοὶ ἀναβλέπουσιν … χωλοὶ … λεπροὶ … κωφοὶ … νεκροὶ ἐγείρονται … πτωχοὶ εὐαγγελίζονται (“the blind receive sight … the lame … lepers … the deaf … the dead are raised … the poor have the gospel preached to them”, 11:5) [T]. Each item has been shown: lepers (8:2–4), the paralysed (9:2–7), the dead (9:18–26), the blind (9:27–31), the deaf-mute (κωφός, “mute, deaf”, 9:32–33). κωφός first occurs at 9:32 and here (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. The poor were blessed at 5:3: πτωχός (“poor”) 5:3; 11:5; 19:21; 26:9, 11 [T], verified.
  • “Blessed is he who does not take offence at me.” μακάριός ἐστιν ὃς ἐὰν μὴ σκανδαλισθῇ ἐν ἐμοί (11:6). σκανδαλίζω (“to cause to stumble, take offence”) occurs in 13 verses of Matthew (SBLGNT, 14 occurrences); here first of Jesus himself as the stumbling-block, then Nazareth (13:57), the Pharisees (15:12), and the disciples on the last night (26:31, 33) [T], verified. Synthetic, high for the chain: the offence John is warned against is the offence the Twelve will take.
  • John is Elijah, and the messenger before the Lord. αὐτός ἐστιν Ἠλίας ὁ μέλλων ἔρχεσθαι (“he himself is Elijah who was to come”, 11:14). Ἠλίας (“Elijah”) stands in 9 verses (SBLGNT), from 11:14 to the cry from the cross (27:47, 49) [T], verified. The book that calls John Elijah twice (11:14; 17:12–13) has bystanders mistake Jesus’ cry for a call to Elijah [T].
  • “The Son of Man came eating and drinking.” A τελωνῶν φίλος καὶ ἁμαρτωλῶν (“friend of tax collectors and sinners”, 11:19) — the charge of 9:11 turned into a nickname [T]. Wisdom is justified by her works: σοφία (“wisdom”) occurs only at 11:19; 12:42; 13:54 (SBLGNT), each time of Jesus [T], verified. The overview’s Sir 51 frame for 11:25–30 begins here [I], moderate.

Synoptic check. The whole unit is double tradition with Luke 7:18–35, and close in wording. Matthew adds τὰ ἔργα τοῦ χριστοῦ (11:2), the Elijah identification (11:14, absent from Luke 7), and “he who has ears, let him hear” (11:15); he places the violence saying here (Luke 16:16 has it elsewhere); and he has ἔργων for Luke’s τέκνων (11:19) [T].

Tool 11. Mal 3:1 + Exod 23:20 → 11:10 (high — quotation with γέγραπται). Source context: Exod 23:20, the angel sent before Israel in the wilderness to bring them into the land; Mal 3:1, the messenger who prepares the way before the LORD, who will come suddenly to his temple. The Hebrew of Malachi reads וּפִנָּה־דֶרֶךְ לְפָנָי (“and he will clear a way before me”). Text: Matthew’s opening, Ἰδοὺ ἐγὼ ἀποστέλλω τὸν ἄγγελόν μου πρὸ προσώπου σου (“behold, I send My messenger ahead of you”), is Swete Exod 23:20 word for word. His second clause, ὃς κατασκευάσει τὴν ὁδόν σου (“who will prepare your way”), follows the sense of the Hebrew פִנָּה (“clear, prepare”) against Swete and Rahlfs Mal 3:1 ἐπιβλέψεται ὁδόν (“he will survey the way”) [T]. Malachi’s “before me” becomes “before you” [T]. Book usage: Isa 40:3 at 3:3 (the voice preparing the way); Malachi returns at 17:10–13 (Elijah). OT-to-OT: Malachi itself echoes Exodus: a messenger before the people becomes a messenger before the LORD [I], moderate–high. What it adds: the second person “your way” puts Jesus where the LORD stands in Malachi. Matthew shares the composite and the change of person with Mark 1:2 and Luke 7:27 [T].

Isa 35:5–6; 26:19; 61:1 → 11:5 (high — a mosaic). Swete Isa 35:5–6 τυφλῶν … κωφῶν … χωλός; 26:19 ἐγερθήσονται οἱ ἐν τοῖς μνημείοις (“those in the tombs will be raised”); 61:1 εὐαγγελίσασθαι πτωχοῖς (“to preach good news to the poor”) [T]. Lepers are in none of these; their place in the list comes from Matthew’s own narrative (8:2–4; 10:8) [I], high. The overview’s Isa 61 row (audited, high) is confirmed [S: audit].

Internal: answers §3:11 — ὁ ἐρχόμενος (“the Coming One”; high). Answers §8:1–9:34 — the deeds catalogued (high). Plants §21:9; §23:39 — ὁ ἐρχόμενος in the psalm cry (verified: the participle stands at 11:3; 21:9; 23:39, SBLGNT; high). Plants §17:10–13; §27:47–49 — Elijah (high, verified). Plants §26:31–33 — σκανδαλίζω (high, verified).

Translations. 11:12 βιάζεται … βιασταὶ ἁρπάζουσιν: NASB95 “suffers violence, and violent men take it by force”; ESV “has suffered violence, and the violent take it by force”. The ESV footnote offers “has been coming violently” [S]. Pulpit divergence: none bearing; both close the ambiguity the same way.

Difficulty. 11:12 is a known crux: is the kingdom attacked (passive) or forcefully advancing (middle), and are the βιασταί (“violent men”) its enemies or its eager entrants? Options: (a) opponents, from Herod’s prison to the cross; (b) eager entrants (Luke 16:16’s “everyone forces his way in”) [S]. In Matthew’s context the prison of 11:2 favours (a) [I], moderate. Take it to Logos before preaching.

Pitfall. Reading John’s question as a loss of faith to be scolded. Jesus answers with Scripture and deeds, then praises John as the greatest born of women (11:11). The corrective: preach the question as the right one, and the answer as Isaiah fulfilled.

Book-overview note: confirms the Mal 3:1 / Exod 23:20 and Isa 35/61 rows. Extends: Mal 3:1’s κατασκευάσει (“will prepare”) stands nearer the Hebrew than either Greek text — a candidate for the “nearer the Hebrew” list, though the change is shared with Mark and Luke.

22. Matthew 11:20–30 — Woe, Revelation, and Rest ⭐

Position. Q1: 11:16–19 ended with “this generation” refusing both John and the Son of Man [T]. Q2: this unit exists here because the refusal now has names — Chorazin, Bethsaida, Capernaum — and because the book must say why some see and others do not before chapters 12–13 divide the hearers. The woes say who refused; the thanksgiving says that seeing is the Father’s gift; the invitation says that the gift is offered to the weary [T]/[I], high.

Structure. Woes on the cities (11:20–24); thanksgiving to the Father (11:25–26); the Son’s claim (11:27); the invitation (11:28–30) [T]. Three movements, each turning on a different audience: the cities, the Father, the weary [T].

Text-first findings.

  • The cities did not repent. ὅτι οὐ μετενόησαν (“because they did not repent”, 11:20). μετανοέω (“to repent”) stands at 3:2; 4:17; 11:20, 21; 12:41 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The call of John and of Jesus (3:2; 4:17) is now answered with refusal, and the last use is Nineveh, which did repent (12:41) [T]. Synthetic, high for the chain.
  • Gentile cities and Sodom would have repented. Tyre and Sidon (11:21–22) and Sodom (11:23–24) are set above the towns of Jesus’ mighty works [T]. The judgement saying reverses the expected order, as 8:11–12 did [I], high.
  • Revelation is the Father’s gift to “infants”. ἔκρυψας ταῦτα ἀπὸ σοφῶν καὶ συνετῶν, καὶ ἀπεκάλυψας αὐτὰ νηπίοις (“You have hidden these things from the wise and intelligent and have revealed them to infants”, 11:25). νήπιος (“infant”) occurs only at 11:25 and 21:16, where the children praise him in the temple (SBLGNT) [T], verified. ἀποκαλύπτω (“to reveal”) stands only at 10:26; 11:25, 27; 16:17 [T], verified: the last is Peter’s confession, “flesh and blood did not reveal this to you, but My Father”. Synthetic, high for the chain.
  • The Son’s claim is mutual and exclusive. Πάντα μοι παρεδόθη ὑπὸ τοῦ πατρός μου (“All things have been handed over to Me by My Father”, 11:27). ἐπιγινώσκω (“to know fully”) twice: no one knows the Son except the Father, nor the Father except the Son and those to whom the Son wills to reveal him [T]. Plants 28:18: ἐδόθη μοι πᾶσα ἐξουσία (“all authority has been given to Me”) [I], moderate–high.
  • The yoke of the meek King, set against a heavy burden. πραΰς εἰμι καὶ ταπεινὸς τῇ καρδίᾳ (“I am gentle and humble in heart”, 11:29). πραΰς (“meek, gentle”) stands only at 5:5; 11:29; 21:5 (SBLGNT) [T], verified: the meek who inherit, the meek teacher, the meek King on the donkey. φορτίον (“burden”) occurs only at 11:30 and 23:4, where the scribes and Pharisees δεσμεύουσιν … φορτία βαρέα (“tie up heavy burdens”) and will not lift them [T], verified. Jesus’ burden is light; theirs is heavy. Synthetic, high for the chain. ταπεινός (“humble”) occurs only here [T].
  • Rest, twice. κἀγὼ ἀναπαύσω ὑμᾶς (“and I will give you rest”, 11:28) and εὑρήσετε ἀνάπαυσιν ταῖς ψυχαῖς ὑμῶν (“you will find rest for your souls”, 11:29). ἀνάπαυσις (“rest”) occurs elsewhere only at 12:43, where the unclean spirit seeks rest and finds none [T], verified. The next unit opens on the Sabbath (12:1) [T] — the day of rest — so the promise of rest is placed directly before the dispute about the Sabbath [I], moderate–high.

Synoptic check. The woes (11:21–23) and the thanksgiving and Son saying (11:25–27) are double tradition with Luke 10:13–15, 21–22, close in wording. Matthew adds Sodom’s verdict (11:23b–24). The invitation (11:28–30) has no parallel in Mark or Luke: it is Matthew’s own material [T].

Tool 11. Jer 6:16 → 11:29 (high) [S: audit]. The audit confirmed it: Matthew keeps the LXX’s frame (εὑρήσετε … ταῖς ψυχαῖς ὑμῶν, “you will find … for your souls”) and corrects its noun ἁγνισμόν (“purification”) to ἀνάπαυσιν (“rest”), the Hebrew מַרְגּוֹעַ (“rest”), found only at Jer 6:16 in the Hebrew Bible. The text-first reading here agrees and adds one thing. In Jeremiah the next words are וַיֹּאמְרוּ לֹא נֵלֵךְ (“but they said, ‘We will not walk in it’”) — the refusal of the ancient paths [T]. Matthew places the invitation straight after the woes on cities that refused. The source’s refusal and the narrative’s refusal lie side by side [I], moderate–high.

Sir 51:23–27; 6:24–30 → 11:28–30 (moderate — outside the Tanak) [S: audit]. Wisdom’s invitation, her yoke, labour and rest. The audit names Sirach as the companion frame, and this run agrees: σοφία (“wisdom”) at 11:19 has just named the category [T].

Isa 14:13–15 → 11:23 (high — near-verbatim). Source context: the taunt over the king of Babylon, who said “I will ascend to heaven”. Swete 14:15: εἰς ᾅδην καταβήσῃ (“you will go down to Hades”) [T]; Matthew ἕως ᾅδου καταβήσῃ (“you will descend to Hades”). Capernaum is addressed in the words spoken to Babylon’s king. Book usage: first use of Isa 14. Confirms the overview’s row.

Isa 28:12 (Hebrew) → 11:28 (moderate) [S: audit] — the audit’s new candidate: “this is the rest; give rest to the weary”, and “they would not hear”. It strengthens the refusal reading above; held at moderate until audited.

Internal: answers §3:2; §4:17 — the call to repent refused (high, verified). Answers §5:5 — πραΰς: the meek blessed, now the meek teacher (high, verified). Plants §16:17 — ἀποκαλύπτω (high, verified). Plants §21:5; §21:16 — the meek King and the infants’ praise (high, verified). Plants §23:4 — φορτίον (high, verified). Plants §28:18 — “all things handed over” (moderate–high).

Translations. NASB95 renders πραΰς (“gentle”) as “gentle” at all three places (5:5; 11:29; 21:5); the ESV has “meek” at 5:5, “gentle” at 11:29 and “humble” at 21:5 [T], checked in both exports. Pulpit divergence: the ESV breaks the chain into three English words, so the congregation will not hear that the blessed of 5:5, the teacher of 11:29 and the King of 21:5 share one adjective. The NASB95 keeps it.

Pitfall. Preaching 11:28 as a general invitation to rest from busy lives. The weary are those under the heavy burden of religion without the Son (23:4), and the rest is learning from the Son who alone reveals the Father (11:27). The corrective: preach 11:25–30 as one unit, the invitation grounded in the Son’s claim.

Book-overview note: confirms the Jer 6:16 (audited), Sirach and Isa 14 rows. Extends with three verified chains not yet in the Echo Table: φορτίον (11:30 ↔︎ 23:4), πραΰς (5:5; 11:29; 21:5), νήπιος (11:25 ↔︎ 21:16).

23. Matthew 12:1–21 — Lord of the Sabbath; the Servant ⭐

Position. Q1: Jesus has just promised rest to the weary (11:28–29) [T]. Q2: this unit exists here because the promise of rest meets the day of rest at once: Ἐν ἐκείνῳ τῷ καιρῷ (“at that time”, 12:1) ties the Sabbath disputes to the invitation, as the same words tied the thanksgiving to the woes (11:25) [T]. The disputes end in the first plot to destroy him (12:14), and Matthew answers the plot with Isaiah’s Servant, cited at length (12:18–21) [T]. The unit is the hinge where open rejection begins [I], high.

Structure. Grain on the Sabbath (12:1–8); the withered hand (12:9–14); withdrawal, healing and the command to silence (12:15–16); the formula citation (12:17–21) [T].

Text-first findings.

  • The Sabbath cluster. σάββατον (“Sabbath”) stands in 9 verses of Matthew (SBLGNT, 11 occurrences): 12:1, 2, 5, 8, 10, 11, 12; 24:20; 28:1 [T], verified. Seven of the nine are in this unit; the next is the warning about flight (24:20), and the last is the dawn of the resurrection, ὀψὲ δὲ σαββάτων (“after the Sabbath”, 28:1) [T]. Synthetic, moderate–high: the Sabbath question opened here closes at the empty tomb.
  • “Something greater than the temple is here.” τοῦ ἱεροῦ μεῖζόν ἐστιν ὧδε (12:6) [T]. It is the first of three “greater than” sayings, followed by “greater than Jonah” and “greater than Solomon” (12:41, 42), all three in chapter 12 [T]. ἱερόν (“temple”) then appears in the Jerusalem chapters (21:12–23; 24:1; 26:55) [T], verified.
  • Hos 6:6 a second time, now about the “guiltless”. εἰ δὲ ἐγνώκειτε τί ἐστιν· Ἔλεος θέλω καὶ οὐ θυσίαν, οὐκ ἂν κατεδικάσατε τοὺς ἀναιτίους (“if you had known what this means, ‘I desire compassion and not sacrifice’, you would not have condemned the innocent”, 12:7) [T]. ἀναίτιος (“innocent, guiltless”) occurs only at 12:5 and 12:7 (SBLGNT): the priests who “profane” the Sabbath are guiltless, and so are the disciples [T], verified. ἔλεος (“mercy”) occurs at 9:13; 12:7; 23:23 [T], verified: the last is the “weightier matter of the law” the Pharisees neglect.
  • The plot is a συμβούλιον. συμβούλιον ἔλαβον κατ’ αὐτοῦ ὅπως αὐτὸν ἀπολέσωσιν (“they conspired against Him, as to how they might destroy Him”, 12:14). συμβούλιον (“counsel, plot”) occurs at 12:14; 22:15; 27:1, 7; 28:12 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The overview names the chain; it runs from the first plot to the bribe that buries the resurrection [T].
  • He withdraws and heals them all. ἀνεχώρησεν (“He withdrew”, 12:15) — the verb of the Holy Family’s flight (2:14) and of Jesus’ move to Galilee (4:12) [T]. ἐθεράπευσεν αὐτοὺς πάντας (“He healed them all”, 12:15). Matthew alone has πάντας (“all”) against Mark 3:10 πολλούς (“many”) [T].
  • The Servant is the Son of the Voice. ὁ ἀγαπητός μου εἰς ὃν εὐδόκησεν ἡ ψυχή μου (“My Beloved in whom My soul is well-pleased”, 12:18). ἀγαπητός (“beloved”) and εὐδοκέω (“to be well pleased”) each occur only at 3:17; 12:18; 17:5 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. Neither Greek Isaiah nor the Hebrew has “beloved” here [T]. Matthew renders Isaiah’s Servant in the Father’s words at the baptism.
  • Gentiles frame the citation. κρίσιν τοῖς ἔθνεσιν ἀπαγγελεῖ (“He shall proclaim justice to the Gentiles”, 12:18) … τῷ ὀνόματι αὐτοῦ ἔθνη ἐλπιοῦσιν (“in His name the Gentiles will hope”, 12:21) [T]. ἔθνος (“nation, Gentile”) runs from “Galilee of the Gentiles” (4:15) to πάντα τὰ ἔθνη (“all the nations”, 28:19) [T], verified (15 occurrences in 14 verses). κρίσις (“justice, judgement”) stands twice in the citation and four more times in the rest of the chapter (12:36, 41, 42) [T], verified.

Synoptic check. Matthew follows Mark 2:23–3:12 but adds three things: the priests in the temple and “something greater than the temple” (12:5–6); the second Hos 6:6 (12:7); and the sheep in a pit (12:11–12, which Luke has at 14:5). He drops Mark’s “the Sabbath was made for man” (Mark 2:27) and the Herodians (Mark 3:6). He replaces Mark’s summary of crowds and unclean spirits (Mark 3:7–12) with the formula citation [T].

Tool 11. Isa 42:1–4 → 12:18–21 (high — formula quotation, the longest in Matthew). Source context: the first Servant Song. YHWH presents his servant, endows him with his Spirit, and sends him to bring מִשְׁפָּט (“justice”) to the nations — quietly, without breaking the bruised reed, and without failing until justice is established. Text: Matthew’s ὁ παῖς μου (“My Servant”) with no name follows the Hebrew עַבְדִּי (“my servant”). Swete and Rahlfs read Ἰακὼβ ὁ παῖς μου … Ἰσραὴλ ὁ ἐκλεκτός μου (“Jacob my servant … Israel my chosen”) [T]. ὃν ᾑρέτισα (“whom I have chosen”) is not the Greek’s ἀντιλήμψομαι (“I will help”). But 12:21, ἔθνη ἐλπιοῦσιν (“the Gentiles will hope”), is the Greek, against the Hebrew וּלְתוֹרָתוֹ אִיִּים יְיַחֵילוּ (“and the coastlands will wait for his law”) [T]. Mixed, as the overview says. At 12:20 Matthew’s ἕως ἂν ἐκβάλῃ εἰς νῖκος τὴν κρίσιν (“until He leads justice to victory”) matches neither Isaiah text; εἰς νῖκος (“to victory”) renders לָנֶצַח (“for ever”) elsewhere in Swete (e.g. Amos 1:11; Jer 3:5), and Hab 1:4 has וְלֹא־יֵצֵא לָנֶצַח מִשְׁפָּט (“justice never goes forth”) [T]. A Hab 1:4 conflation is a candidate, uncertain. Book usage: Isaiah is the book’s spine. The Servant was cited at 8:17 (Isa 53:4) and will return at 20:28 and 26:28 [I]. OT-to-OT: Isa 42:1 and Ps 2:7 are heard together at 3:17 and 17:5. What it adds: the plot of 12:14 does not derail the mission; the Servant withdraws, heals all, does not quarrel, and brings justice to the nations.

1 Sam 21:1–6 → 12:3–4; Num 28:9–10 → 12:5 (high — explicit appeal “have you not read”). Source context: David, fleeing Saul, eats the bread of the Presence at Nob (Swete 1 Sam 21:6 τοὺς ἄρτους τῆς προθέσεως, “the bread of the Presence”); the Sabbath offerings double the priests’ work. οὐκ ἀνέγνωτε (“have you not read?”) stands at 12:3, 5; 19:4; 22:31 (SBLGNT phrase search) [T], verified. Matthew omits Mark’s “in the time of Abiathar” (Mark 2:26), and so avoids its crux [T].

Hos 6:6 → 12:7 (high). Rahlfs reads καὶ οὐ θυσίαν, as Matthew does; Swete reads ἢ θυσίαν (“rather than sacrifice”). See the overview and unit 17 [T].

Internal: answers §11:28–29 — rest and the Sabbath (moderate–high). Answers §3:17 — the Voice’s words given to the Servant (high, verified). Answers §9:13 — Hos 6:6 a second time (high, verified). Answers §4:15 — the Gentiles (high, verified). Plants §12:41–42 — “greater than” (high). Plants §22:15; §27:1, 7; §28:12 — συμβούλιον (high, verified). Plants §17:5 — the Voice again (high, verified). Plants §28:1; §28:19 — the Sabbath and the nations (moderate; synthetic).

Translations. 12:18 ὁ παῖς μου: NASB95 “My Servant”, ESV “my servant”. Both keep “servant”, though παῖς can mean “child” (it is the centurion’s “servant” at 8:6 and the “boy” at 17:18) [T]. Pulpit divergence: none bearing.

Pitfall. Preaching the Sabbath controversy as Jesus against rules. The text argues from the Law itself (12:5), from the prophets (12:7) and from who he is (12:6, 8), and the healing is “lawful” (12:12). The corrective: preach “greater than the temple” and “Lord of the Sabbath” as the point, and mercy as what the Law always wanted.

Book-overview note: confirms the Isa 42 row (mixed), the Hos 6:6 row, and the συμβούλιον chain. Extends: εἰς νῖκος at 12:20 matches neither Isaiah text — the overview’s “mixed” description should say so. Hab 1:4 is a new candidate, uncertain.

24. Matthew 12:22–50 — The Strong Man, the Unforgivable Word, the Sign of Jonah, My Brothers ⭐

Position. Q1: the Pharisees have taken counsel to destroy him (12:14), and the Servant has withdrawn and healed (12:15–21) [T]. Q2: this unit exists here because the plot now becomes an interpretation. The crowds ask, “Can this be the Son of David?” (12:23), and the Pharisees answer, “by Beelzebul” (12:24) — the charge Jesus foretold at 10:25 [T]. The chapter ends by naming his true family. It divides those who do the Father’s will from “this evil generation” before the parables divide hearers from non-hearers (13) [I], high.

Structure. The healing and the two verdicts (12:22–24); the divided kingdom and the strong man (12:25–30); the unforgivable blasphemy (12:31–32); trees, fruit and words (12:33–37); the sign of Jonah, Nineveh and the queen of the South (12:38–42); the returning spirit (12:43–45); the true family (12:46–50) [T]. “This generation” frames the middle: γενεὰ πονηρὰ καὶ μοιχαλίς (“an evil and adulterous generation”, 12:39) and τῇ γενεᾷ ταύτῃ τῇ πονηρᾷ (“this evil generation”, 12:45) [T].

Text-first findings.

  • “Son of David?” against “Beelzebul”. Μήτι οὗτός ἐστιν ὁ υἱὸς Δαυίδ; (“This man cannot be the Son of David, can he?”, 12:23). The question expects “no” but leaves the door open [T]. Δαυίδ (“David”) stands in 15 verses (SBLGNT, 17 occurrences); “Son of David” is cried by the needy (9:27; 15:22; 20:30–31) and the crowds (21:9, 15), and is finally a question Jesus puts to the Pharisees (22:42–45) [T], verified. Βεελζεβούλ (“Beelzebul”) occurs only at 10:25; 12:24, 27 [T], verified: the charge foretold at 10:25 is now made.
  • “The kingdom of God has come upon you.” ἄρα ἔφθασεν ἐφ’ ὑμᾶς ἡ βασιλεία τοῦ θεοῦ (12:28). This is one of only four uses of “kingdom of God” in the SBLGNT (12:28; 19:24; 21:31, 43), against 32 of “kingdom of heaven” [T] (overview, verified). The rarer phrase stands where the Spirit of God is named: ἐν πνεύματι θεοῦ (“by the Spirit of God”) [T]. The Spirit was put upon the Servant six verses earlier (12:18) [T].
  • The strong man is bound. πῶς δύναταί τις εἰσελθεῖν εἰς τὴν οἰκίαν τοῦ ἰσχυροῦ … ἐὰν μὴ πρῶτον δήσῃ τὸν ἰσχυρόν; (“how can anyone enter the strong man’s house … unless he first binds the strong man?”, 12:29) [T]. John called Jesus ὁ ἰσχυρότερός μου (“mightier than I”, 3:11) [T]. The temptation (4:1–11) is where the binding is first shown [I], moderate.
  • The brood of vipers, and words that judge. γεννήματα ἐχιδνῶν (“you brood of vipers”, 12:34). The phrase occurs only at 3:7; 12:34; 23:33 (SBLGNT) [T], verified: John’s words, here Jesus’ words, and at the last woe. “By your words you will be justified, and by your words you will be condemned” (12:37), and the Pharisees’ words of 12:24 are the case in point [I], high.
  • The sign of Jonah: three days and three nights. ὥσπερ γὰρ ἦν Ἰωνᾶς ἐν τῇ κοιλίᾳ τοῦ κήτους τρεῖς ἡμέρας καὶ τρεῖς νύκτας (“for just as Jonah was three days and three nights in the belly of the sea monster”, 12:40) — Swete Jon 2:1 word for word [T]. Ἰωνᾶς (“Jonah”) occurs at 12:39, 40, 41 (twice); 16:4 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. Luke 11:29–32 has the sign but not the three days; the time saying is Matthew’s [T].
  • “Something greater” twice more. πλεῖον Ἰωνᾶ ὧδε … πλεῖον Σολομῶνος ὧδε (“something greater than Jonah is here … something greater than Solomon is here”, 12:41–42) — after “greater than the temple” (12:6) [T]. Priest, prophet and king are each surpassed [I], moderate–high. Gentiles (Ninevites, the queen of the South) will rise ἐν τῇ κρίσει (“at the judgement”) and condemn this generation — κρίσις (“judgement”) again, after the Servant’s κρίσις for the nations (12:18, 20) [T], verified.
  • “Worse than the first.” τὰ ἔσχατα … χείρονα τῶν πρώτων (“the last state … becomes worse than the first”, 12:45). χείρων (“worse”) occurs only at 9:16; 12:45; 27:64 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. At 27:64 the chief priests use the same phrase about the resurrection: ἔσται ἡ ἐσχάτη πλάνη χείρων τῆς πρώτης (“the last deception will be worse than the first”) [T]. The overview has the echo; confirmed.
  • The family who do the Father’s will. ὅστις γὰρ ἂν ποιήσῃ τὸ θέλημα τοῦ πατρός μου (“whoever does the will of My Father”, 12:50) [T]. θέλημα (“will”) stands at 6:10; 7:21; 12:50; 18:14; 21:31; 26:42 (SBLGNT) [T], verified: the prayer, the house on the rock, the family, the little ones, the two sons, Gethsemane. Matthew makes it “My Father’s will”; Mark 3:35 has “the will of God” [T].

Synoptic check. Matthew combines Mark 3:22–35 with double tradition (Luke 11:14–32). He adds the opening healing of a blind and mute man and the crowd’s “Son of David?” (12:22–23), the tree and words sayings (12:33–37), the three days and three nights (12:40), and “My Father’s will” (12:50). He adds “neither in this age nor in the age to come” (12:32) [T].

Tool 11. Jonah 2:1 (English 1:17) → 12:40 (high — near-verbatim with the Greek). Source context: the LORD appoints a great fish; Jonah is in its belly three days and three nights, then prays and is cast out on dry land. The Hebrew בִּמְעֵי הַדָּג (“in the belly of the fish”) is rendered ἐν τῇ κοιλίᾳ τοῦ κήτους (“in the belly of the sea monster”) in Swete, and Matthew has the Greek wording [T]. κῆτος (“sea monster”) occurs only here in Matthew [T]. Book usage: Jonah appeared in shape, if not by name, at 8:24–25 (see unit 16; candidate). OT-to-OT: Jonah 3:5 (Nineveh repents) supplies 12:41. What it adds: the only sign given is death and return, told in Jonah’s words, and Gentiles who repented at a lesser preacher condemn Israel’s refusal.

1 Kgs 10:1–13 → 12:42 (high — the queen of Sheba). Move 1 only: she came to hear Solomon’s wisdom and blessed Israel’s God. σοφία (“wisdom”) is now claimed for Jesus at 11:19; 12:42; 13:54 [T], verified.

Isa 49:24–25 → 12:29 (moderate — Move 1 only). Swete μὴ λήμψεταί τις παρὰ γίγαντος σκῦλα; (“will anyone take spoils from a giant?”); the LORD answers that he will take the captives of the mighty [T]. The idea fits; the wording does not (Matthew’s ἰσχυρός, “strong man”, is not Swete’s γίγας, “giant”) [T]. Moderate, not in the overview; new candidate.

Internal: answers §10:25 — Beelzebul (high, verified). Answers §3:7; §3:11 — “brood of vipers” and “the stronger one” (high, verified). Answers §12:18 — the Spirit (high). Plants §16:4 — the sign of Jonah again (high, verified). Plants §23:33 — vipers (high, verified). Plants §27:63–64 — “after three days” and “worse than the first” (high, verified). Plants §28:10 — “My brothers” (moderate): the risen Jesus calls the disciples ἀδελφοί μου (“My brethren”).

Translations. 12:40 κῆτος: NASB95 “the sea monster”, ESV “the great fish”. The ESV harmonises with Jonah’s Hebrew; the NASB95 keeps the Greek word Matthew uses [T]. Text: [12:47] is bracketed in NA28 (omitted by א* B L Γ ff¹ k syˢ·ᶜ sa) [T]. The NASB95 prints it in brackets; the ESV prints it with a note. Nothing here rests on it. Pulpit divergence: “great fish” — the congregation will hear Jonah, which is right; it will not hear Matthew’s Greek word, which matters little.

Difficulty. 12:31–32, the sin that will not be forgiven. Function in context: a verdict on the Pharisees’ attribution of the Spirit’s work to Beelzebul (12:24, 28), not a warning about a private sin a believer may fear he has committed [I], high. Pastoral note: the anxious hearer who fears he has committed it shows by that fear that he has not; say so with care [I].

Pitfall. Preaching the true family (12:46–50) as a rejection of Jesus’ mother. The text redefines family around the Father’s will; it does not reject his mother [I], high. The corrective: preach 12:50 as invitation.

Book-overview note: confirms the Jonah, Sheba, brood-of-vipers and χείρων rows. Extends: the θέλημα (“will”) chain (6:10 → 26:42) and the “greater than” triad belong in the Echo Table; Isa 49:24–25 is a new candidate (moderate).

25. Matthew 13:1–23 — The Sower: Hearing and Not Hearing ⭐

Position. Q1: chapter 12 ends with a division inside Israel. The Pharisees plot to destroy him (12:14), call his exorcisms Beelzebul’s work (12:24), and ask for a sign (12:38). Then his mother and brothers stand ἔξω (“outside”, 12:46–47), and Jesus names as his family whoever does the will of his Father (12:50) [T]. 13:1 ties the parables to that scene: Ἐν τῇ ἡμέρᾳ ἐκείνῃ (“on that day”). The phrase stands only here in Matthew (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. Q2: the book has shown a divided response. Now it needs to say why the same word produces opposite results. The sower answers that question before any further rejection is narrated: Nazareth (13:57) and Herod (14:1–12) follow at once [T]/[I]. The unit stands here, and not earlier, because only after chapters 11–12 is there a divided audience for the parable to explain.

Structure.

Verses Section
13:1–3a Setting: out of the house, by the sea, in a boat; the crowds on the shore
13:3b–9 The parable: four soils, closing ὁ ἔχων ὦτα ἀκουέτω (“he who has ears, let him hear”)
13:10–17 The disciples’ question “why in parables?” Given and not given (11–12); Isa 6:9–10 fulfilled (13–15); “blessed are your eyes” (16–17)
13:18–23 The parable explained, each soil a kind of hearer

The unit is a frame [T]. The parable (3b–9) and its explanation (18–23) bracket the reason for parables (10–17), and Isaiah stands at the centre. Hearing is the frame’s thread. ἀκούω (“to hear”) stands 15 times in 13:9–23 (SBLGNT, show('ἀκούω')) [T].

Text-first findings.

  • Understanding is the dividing line, and Matthew makes it so. συνίημι (“to understand”) occurs 9 times in Matthew, in 9 verses (SBLGNT): 13:13, 14, 15, 19, 23, 51; 15:10; 16:12; 17:13 [T], verified. It runs through Jesus’ own diagnosis (13:13: οὐδὲ συνίουσιν, “nor do they understand”) and through Isaiah (13:14, 15). Then it marks the first soil (μὴ συνιέντος, “does not understand”, 13:19) and the last (ὁ … συνιείς, “the one who … understands”, 13:23). Mark’s good soil “accepts” (παραδέχονται, “they accept”, Mark 4:20). Matthew’s good soil “understands” [T]. The parable is about understanding, not only about receiving [I], high.
  • “Because”, not “so that”. διὰ τοῦτο ἐν παραβολαῖς αὐτοῖς λαλῶ, ὅτι βλέποντες οὐ βλέπουσιν (“this is why I speak to them in parables: because seeing they do not see”, 13:13) [T]. Mark has ἵνα βλέποντες βλέπωσι (“so that seeing they may see”, Mark 4:12) [T]. Matthew’s ὅτι (“because”) makes the parables a response to blindness that is already there. In Mark the parables serve a purpose. The NA28 apparatus shows D Θ ƒ¹·¹³ and the Old Latin bringing Mark’s ἵνα (“so that”) back into Matthew (verified) [T]. The change is Matthew’s, and scribes felt it.
  • Isaiah is “fulfilled”, in Jesus’ own mouth, with a verb used nowhere else in the Gospels. ἀναπληροῦται αὐτοῖς ἡ προφητεία Ἠσαΐου (“in their case the prophecy of Isaiah is being fulfilled”, 13:14). ἀναπληρόω (“to fulfil completely”) occurs once in Matthew and in no other Gospel (SBLGNT, verified; elsewhere only 1 Cor 14:16; 16:17; Gal 6:2; Phil 2:30; 1 Thess 2:16) [T]. The narrator’s ten formulas use πληρόω (“to fulfil”). This one is spoken by Jesus, has a present tense, and is not introduced with ἵνα (“in order that”) [T]. The fulfilment is ongoing in this audience [I], moderate–high.
  • “Given” — a gift, and a passive with God as the agent. ὑμῖν δέδοται γνῶναι τὰ μυστήρια τῆς βασιλείας τῶν οὐρανῶν, ἐκείνοις δὲ οὐ δέδοται (“to you it has been given to know the mysteries of the kingdom of heaven, but to them it has not been given”, 13:11) [T]. μυστήριον (“mystery”) occurs once in Matthew (verified) [T]. The saying of 13:12, “whoever has, more will be given (δοθήσεται)”, returns at 25:29. καὶ περισσευθήσεται (“and he will have an abundance”) stands at 13:12 and 25:29 only (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. In Mark the saying comes later (Mark 4:25). Matthew moves it forward to explain the gift [T].
  • A beatitude for eyes and ears. ὑμῶν δὲ μακάριοι οἱ ὀφθαλμοὶ ὅτι βλέπουσιν (“but blessed are your eyes, because they see”, 13:16). μακάριος (“blessed”) stands in 13 verses of Matthew: nine in 5:3–11, then 11:6; 13:16; 16:17; 24:46 (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. “Prophets and righteous” (προφῆται καὶ δίκαιοι, “prophets and righteous men”, 13:17) have longed to see this. Luke has “prophets and kings” (Luke 10:24) [T]. The same pairing returns at 10:41 and in the woes, τοὺς τάφους τῶν προφητῶν … τὰ μνημεῖα τῶν δικαίων (“the tombs of the prophets … the monuments of the righteous”, 23:29) [T].
  • The evil one “snatches”. ἔρχεται ὁ πονηρὸς καὶ ἁρπάζει τὸ ἐσπαρμένον (“the evil one comes and snatches away what has been sown”, 13:19). Mark has ὁ Σατανᾶς (“Satan”, Mark 4:15) and αἴρει (“takes away”) [T]. ἁρπάζω (“to snatch”) occurs in Matthew only at 11:12, 12:29 and 13:19 (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. At 12:29 it is the plundering of the strong man’s house. Here the strong one plunders back [I], moderate. ὁ πονηρός (“the evil one”) returns at 13:38, and it is the petition of 6:13 [T].
  • The thorns are the anxieties of chapter 6. ἡ μέριμνα τοῦ αἰῶνος (“the worry of the age”) and ἡ ἀπάτη τοῦ πλούτου (“the deceitfulness of wealth”) choke the word (13:22) [T]. μέριμνα (“worry”) and πλοῦτος (“wealth”) each occur once in Matthew. μεριμνάω (“to worry”) occurs 7 times in 6 verses: 6:25, 27, 28, 31, 34 (twice); 10:19 (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. The Sermon on the Mount warned against serving two masters and against worry (6:24–34). Here those two things kill the word [I], moderate–high, synthetic.
  • Fruit is the test, as John and the Sermon said. καρπός (“fruit”) occurs 19 times in 14 verses (SBLGNT): 3:8, 10; 7:16–20; 12:33; 13:8, 26; 21:19, 34, 41, 43 [T], verified. ῥίζα (“root”) occurs at 3:10, 13:6 and 13:21 only [T], verified. John’s axe lies at the root (3:10). Here the seed without root withers [T]. καρποφορέω (“to bear fruit”) occurs once, at 13:23 [T].
  • Falling away is named. εὐθὺς σκανδαλίζεται (“immediately he falls away”, 13:21). σκανδαλίζω (“to cause to stumble, fall away”) occurs 14 times in 13 verses. The next use is Nazareth: ἐσκανδαλίζοντο ἐν αὐτῷ (“they took offence at him”, 13:57) [T], verified. It ends with the disciples: πάντες ὑμεῖς σκανδαλισθήσεσθε (“you will all fall away”, 26:31) [T]. θλῖψις (“affliction”) appears first here and then three times in chapter 24 (24:9, 21, 29) [T], verified.

Synoptic check. Matthew follows Mark 4:1–20 for the setting, the parable and the explanation. He changes Mark’s ἵνα (“so that”, Mark 4:12) to ὅτι (“because”, 13:13) and then quotes Isa 6:9–10 in full with a fulfilment formula (13:14–15). Mark only alludes [T]. He adds 13:16–17, which Luke has in another setting (Luke 10:23–24) [T]. Matthew and Luke agree against Mark in the plural τὰ μυστήρια (“the mysteries”) and in γνῶναι (“to know”). Mark has τὸ μυστήριον δέδοται (“the mystery has been given”, Mark 4:11) [T]. Matthew omits Mark’s rebuke “Do you not understand this parable?” (Mark 4:13) [T]. He writes “the word of the kingdom” (τὸν λόγον τῆς βασιλείας, 13:19), a phrase found only here (SBLGNT, verified) and close to his “gospel of the kingdom” (4:23; 9:35; 24:14) [T]. He reverses Mark’s yields to “a hundred, sixty, thirty” (13:8, 23) [T]. And the good soil “understands” (13:23), where in Mark it “accepts” [T].

Tool 11.

Isa 6:9–10 → 13:14–15 (high — formula quotation in Jesus’ mouth). Source context: Isaiah’s throne vision (6:1–8). The prophet is cleansed and sent: “Go and say to this people” (6:9). The commission is to speak a word that will not be understood “until cities lie waste” (6:11). It ends on a stump, and in the Hebrew only, זֶרַע קֹדֶשׁ מַצַּבְתָּהּ (“the holy seed is its stump”, 6:13) [T]. Swete and Rahlfs have no seed clause at 6:13 [T]. The register is judgement, with a remnant at the end. The texts: the Hebrew has imperatives addressed to the prophet: הַשְׁמֵן לֵב־הָעָם הַזֶּה (“make the heart of this people fat”), וְעֵינָיו הָשַׁע (“and smear over their eyes”). And it has וְרָפָא לוֹ (“and one heals him”) [T]. Swete and Rahlfs both turn the imperatives into a description of the people: ἐπαχύνθη γὰρ ἡ καρδία τοῦ λαοῦ τούτου (“for the heart of this people has grown fat”), with καὶ ἰάσομαι αὐτούς (“and I will heal them”) [T]. Matthew stands with the Greek. He drops the opening Πορεύθητι καὶ εἰπὸν τῷ λαῷ τούτῳ (“go and say to this people”), which D and the Old Latin restore (NA28 apparatus, verified). He drops αὐτῶν (“their”) after τοῖς ὠσίν (“with the ears”), where Swete and Rahlfs both have it. He keeps αὐτῶν after τοὺς ὀφθαλμούς (“the eyes”) with Rahlfs, against Swete [T]. Triage: category 1, the LXX’s own interpretation, which Matthew adopts. The Greek’s passive “has grown fat” fits his ὅτι (“because”, 13:13), where the Hebrew’s imperatives would fit Mark’s ἵνα (“so that”) [I], moderate–high. Book usage: Isaiah is Matthew’s main prophetic source. In the prologue he took Isaiah 7–9 (1:23 = Isa 7:14; 4:15–16 = Isa 8:23–9:1), then 40:3 (3:3), 53:4 (8:17) and 42:1–4 (12:18–21) [T]. Isa 6 is the call narrative that opens the Isaiah 6–12 unit, from which 1:23 and 4:15–16 already came [I]. Isa 29:13 follows at 15:8–9, and it is the same people, ὁ λαὸς οὗτος (“this people”) [T]. OT-to-OT: Isaiah reworks this text himself. At 29:9–10 the LORD has poured out a spirit of deep sleep and closed their eyes. The Hebrew root שׁעע (“to smear over, blind”) stands in Isaiah at 6:10 and twice at 29:9; its two other Isaiah uses, at 11:8 and 66:12, are the homonym “to delight” (find.py lemma 8173, verified) [T]/[I]. The Greek of Swete has καμμύω (“to shut the eyes”) at Isa 6:10 and 29:10, and elsewhere only at Isa 33:15 and Lam 3:45 (verified) [T]. Deut 29:3 (Hebrew; Swete 29:4) stands behind both: οὐκ ἔδωκεν Κύριος ὁ θεὸς ὑμῖν καρδίαν εἰδέναι καὶ ὀφθαλμοὺς βλέπειν καὶ ὦτα ἀκούειν (“the Lord God has not given you a heart to know and eyes to see and ears to hear”), in Swete and Rahlfs alike [T]. Matthew’s δέδοται γνῶναι (“it has been given to know”, 13:11) uses the same pair, “give” and “know” [I], moderate. What it adds: Matthew uses Isaiah’s own commissioning text, and the canonical chain behind it (Deut 29 → Isa 6 → Isa 29), to explain why Israel’s response is divided. The Hebrew’s closing image of a holy seed is a quiet companion to a parable about seed. That is uncertain, and it rests on the Hebrew only.

Deut 29:3 (Swete 29:4) → 13:11 (moderate). Move 1: Moses at the Moab covenant. Israel has seen the signs, but the LORD “has not given” them a heart to know “to this day”. Matthew’s “it has been given to you to know” is the reversal for the disciples.

Dan 2:18–47 → 13:11 (moderate — vocabulary). Move 1: in the Greek canon, μυστήριον (“mystery”) renders Aramaic רָז (“secret”) in Daniel 2. There it is used of God revealing the kingdom that will replace the empires. Swete has it at Dan 2:18, 19, 27, 28, 29, 30, 47, in both OG and Theodotion (verified). “There is a God in heaven who reveals mysteries” (Dat 2:28) [T]. The phrase is shared with Mark and Luke, so it belongs to the tradition before it is Matthew’s.

Internal: answers §12:46–50 — “on that day”; outside and inside (high). Answers §3:8, 10; 7:16–20; 12:33 — fruit and root (high, verified).
