Answers §6:24–34 — worry and wealth (moderate–high, synthetic). Answers §12:29 — ἁρπάζω (“to snatch”) (moderate, verified). Answers §5:3–11 — the beatitude form (moderate). Plants §13:51; 15:10, 16; 16:12; 17:13 — understanding (high, verified). Plants §13:57; 26:31 — falling away (high, verified). Plants §25:29 — “to him more will be given” (high, verified). Plants §15:8 — “this people”, the heart (moderate–high). Opening material checked: the fruit-and-axe words of John (3:8–10) are the nearest earlier planting.

Translations. At 13:11 the NASB95 has “mysteries” and the ESV “secrets” for τὰ μυστήρια (“the mysteries”). The ESV loses the word’s link to Daniel 2 [T]. At 13:14 the NASB95 renders ἀκοῇ ἀκούσετε (“with hearing you will hear”) as “you will keep on hearing”; the ESV has “you will indeed hear”. Both convey the Hebrew infinitive-absolute idiom that the Greek copies. At 13:21 the NASB95 has “affliction” and the ESV “tribulation” for θλῖψις (“affliction”). The ESV’s word links better to 24:9, 21, 29, where it is “tribulation” in both. Pulpit divergence: the ESV’s “secrets” (13:11). The congregation will not hear “mystery”, the word that ties the verse to Daniel 2.

Difficulty. 13:13–15, is God hardening Israel? Category: theological. Options: (1) Matthew’s ὅτι (“because”) and the Greek of Isaiah describe a hardness already present. The parables respond to it [I]. (2) The divine passive δέδοται (“it has been given”) and the Hebrew’s imperatives keep God’s sovereignty in view [I]. (3) Both together: the parables both reveal and conceal, depending on the hearer [S]. The text holds 13:11 (God gives) and 13:13 (they do not see) side by side and does not resolve them.

Pitfall. “Four kinds of hearer — work out which soil you are and try harder.” The unit locates the difference in understanding (13:19, 23), which is “given” (13:11), and in the eyes and ears that are “blessed” (13:16). The parable is told to explain a divided response to Jesus, not to set a self-improvement scheme. The corrective: preach 13:10–17 as the centre, the parable as the explanation of 11–12, and the good soil as the hearer who understands and so bears fruit.

Book-overview note: Confirms with something new. The overview’s row for Isa 6:9–10 (“close to the LXX”) is right. Add three things. (1) Matthew follows the Greek’s descriptive passive against the Hebrew imperatives, and that fits his ὅτι (“because”) against Mark’s ἵνα (“so that”). (2) ἀναπληρόω (“to fulfil completely”) is a Jesus-spoken fulfilment, outside the ten narrator formulas and found only here in the Gospels. (3) Isaiah’s own reuse of Isa 6 at 29:9–10 (שׁעע “to smear over”; καμμύω “to shut the eyes”) connects the two Isaiah hardening citations of 13:14–15 and 15:8–9. Extends: the συνίημι (“to understand”) chain (9 verses) belongs among the keynotes of the 11:2–16:20 movement.

26. Matthew 13:24–43 — Wheat and Weeds; Mustard and Leaven

Position. Q1: the sower has explained why the same word meets opposite responses (13:1–23), and named ὁ πονηρός (“the evil one”) as the one who snatches it (13:19) [T]. Q2: the next question is what happens to that division over time. If the kingdom has come, why do the sons of the evil one remain in the field? This unit answers: they remain until the harvest, and the separation belongs to the Son of Man’s angels, not to the servants (13:29–30, 41) [T]. Mustard and leaven answer a second question raised by 13:1–23: how can something so small and so resisted be the kingdom? It stands here, before the private parables (13:44–52), because only after the mixed response of chapters 11–13 does the reader need to be told to wait [I].

Structure.

Verses Section
13:24–30 The weeds among the wheat (to the crowds)
13:31–32 The mustard seed
13:33 The leaven
13:34–35 Narrator’s comment: only in parables; Ps 78:2 fulfilled
13:36–43 In the house: the weeds explained

The weeds are told (24–30) and explained (36–43), with two short parables and a fulfilment formula between them [T]. This is the same frame as 13:3–23, where the parable and its explanation bracket 13:10–17. Each of the three public parables opens with Ἄλλην παραβολήν (“another parable”, 13:24, 31, 33) [T]. The move ἀφεὶς τοὺς ὄχλους … εἰς τὴν οἰκίαν (“leaving the crowds … into the house”, 13:36) turns the discourse from crowds to disciples [T].

Text-first findings.

  • John’s harvest, delayed. τὸν δὲ σῖτον συναγάγετε εἰς τὴν ἀποθήκην μου (“but gather the wheat into my barn”, 13:30), and the weeds are bound πρὸς τὸ κατακαῦσαι αὐτά (“to burn them up”) [T]. This is John’s threshing floor: συνάξει τὸν σῖτον αὐτοῦ εἰς τὴν ἀποθήκην, τὸ δὲ ἄχυρον κατακαύσει (“he will gather his wheat into the barn, but he will burn up the chaff”, 3:12) [T]. κατακαίω (“to burn up”) occurs only at 3:12 and 13:30, and ἀποθήκη (“barn”) only at 3:12; 6:26; 13:30 (SBLGNT, verified). John announced the harvest as imminent. The parable keeps every element and adds one thing: ἄφετε συναυξάνεσθαι ἀμφότερα μέχρι τοῦ θερισμοῦ (“let both grow together until the harvest”, 13:30) [T]. This is the answer to John’s question of 11:3 in parable form [I], moderate–high.
  • The servants are refused. Θέλεις οὖν ἀπελθόντες συλλέξωμεν αὐτά; (“do you want us, then, to go and gather them up?”, 13:28). Οὔ, μήποτε … ἐκριζώσητε ἅμα αὐτοῖς τὸν σῖτον (“no, lest … you uproot the wheat with them”, 13:29) [T]. συλλέγω (“to gather up”) is used five times in this unit (13:28, 29, 30, 40, 41), and in the explanation the subject is the angels: συλλέξουσιν (“they will gather”, 13:41) [T], verified. The gathering is not refused. It is reassigned. ἐκριζόω (“to uproot”) returns only at 15:13, of the Pharisees: “every plant which my heavenly Father did not plant ἐκριζωθήσεται (”shall be uprooted“)”, followed by ἄφετε αὐτούς (“let them alone”, 15:14) [T], verified. The imperative ἄφετε (“let”, “leave”) stands at 13:30; 15:14; 19:14 (SBLGNT, verified).
  • The enemy is named, and the field is the world. ὁ δὲ ἀγρός ἐστιν ὁ κόσμος (“the field is the world”, 13:38); ὁ δὲ ἐχθρὸς … ἐστιν ὁ διάβολος (“the enemy … is the devil”, 13:39) [T]. διάβολος (“devil”) occurs at 4:1, 5, 8, 11; 13:39; 25:41 (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. The tempter of chapter 4 is now the sower of weeds. ἐχθρός (“enemy”) was last heard in “love your enemies” (5:44) [T]. The enemy whom the disciples must not uproot is the devil’s sowing, not their neighbour [I], moderate.
  • The Son of Man sows and the Son of Man sends. Ὁ σπείρων τὸ καλὸν σπέρμα ἐστὶν ὁ υἱὸς τοῦ ἀνθρώπου (“the one who sows the good seed is the Son of Man”, 13:37). ἀποστελεῖ ὁ υἱὸς τοῦ ἀνθρώπου τοὺς ἀγγέλους αὐτοῦ (“the Son of Man will send out his angels”, 13:41) [T]. The phrase τοὺς ἀγγέλους αὐτοῦ (“his angels”) with ἀποστελεῖ (“he will send out”) stands at 13:41 and 24:31 only (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. The kingdom is called τῆς βασιλείας αὐτοῦ (“his kingdom”, the Son of Man’s, 13:41) and then τῇ βασιλείᾳ τοῦ πατρὸς αὐτῶν (“the kingdom of their Father”, 13:43) [T]. τῇ βασιλείᾳ τοῦ πατρός (“the kingdom of the Father”) stands at 13:43 and 26:29 only (verified). The Son’s kingdom ends in the Father’s [I], moderate.
  • “The end of the age” is coined here. συντέλεια (τοῦ) αἰῶνος (“the end of the age”) stands at 13:39, 40, 49; 24:3; 28:20, and συντέλεια (“end”) nowhere else in Matthew (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. The phrase that ends the book (28:20) is first heard as a harvest.
  • The judgement formula. εἰς τὴν κάμινον τοῦ πυρός· ἐκεῖ ἔσται ὁ κλαυθμὸς καὶ ὁ βρυγμὸς τῶν ὀδόντων (“into the furnace of fire; in that place there will be weeping and gnashing of teeth”, 13:42). The second clause occurs six times: 8:12; 13:42, 50; 22:13; 24:51; 25:30 (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. κάμινος (“furnace”) occurs only at 13:42 and 50 (verified). Those thrown in are πάντα τὰ σκάνδαλα καὶ τοὺς ποιοῦντας τὴν ἀνομίαν (“all stumbling blocks and those who commit lawlessness”, 13:41) [T]. ἀνομία (“lawlessness”) stands at 7:23; 13:41; 23:28; 24:12 (verified). At 7:23 it is those who said “Lord, Lord” [T]. The weeds are not outsiders only [I], moderate–high.
  • Small and hidden. The mustard seed is μικρότερον … πάντων τῶν σπερμάτων (“smaller than all the seeds”), and it becomes δένδρον (“a tree”, 13:32). The woman ἐνέκρυψεν (“hid”) leaven in three measures of flour (13:33) [T]. Hiddenness runs through the chapter. ἐγκρύπτω (“to hide in”) occurs only at 13:33; κρύπτω (“to hide”) at 5:14; 11:25; 13:35; 13:44 (twice); 25:18, 25 (SBLGNT, verified). The Father ἔκρυψας ταῦτα (“hid these things”) from the wise (11:25). Here the things are κεκρυμμένα (“hidden”, 13:35). And the treasure is κεκρυμμένῳ (“hidden”, 13:44) [T]. Synthetic: moderate–high. ζύμη (“leaven”) occurs at 13:33 and then only at 16:6, 11, 12, where it is the teaching of the Pharisees and Sadducees (verified) [T]. The same image is used first of the kingdom and then of its opponents.
  • The narrator’s eighth formula. ὅπως πληρωθῇ τὸ ῥηθὲν διὰ τοῦ προφήτου (“so that what was spoken through the prophet might be fulfilled”, 13:35). It is the eighth of the ten narrator’s formulas, and the only one that cites a psalm [T]. See Tool 11.

Synoptic check. Mark’s parable of the seed growing by itself (Mark 4:26–29) stands where Matthew has the weeds [T]. The two share καθεύδω (“to sleep”; Mark 4:27; Matt 13:25), χόρτος (“blade”), καρπός (“fruit”), σῖτος (“wheat”) and θερισμός (“harvest”; Mark 4:29; Matt 13:30). In Mark θερισμός occurs only at 4:29 (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. Matthew’s parable may be built on Mark’s frame. Mark’s sleeper does not know how the seed grows, and Matthew’s sleepers let the enemy in [I], moderate. The mustard seed is in all three. Matthew and Luke agree against Mark in δένδρον (“tree”) and ἐν τοῖς κλάδοις αὐτοῦ (“in its branches”, Luke 13:19), where Mark has ὑπὸ τὴν σκιὰν αὐτοῦ (“under its shade”, Mark 4:32) [T]. The leaven is double tradition (Luke 13:20–21). There Luke has ἔκρυψεν (“hid”) and Matthew ἐνέκρυψεν (“hid in”) [T]. Matthew turns Mark’s summary (Mark 4:33–34) into a fulfilment formula (13:34–35). The explanation of the weeds (13:36–43) is his alone [T].

Tool 11.

Ps 78:2 (Swete 77:2) → 13:35 (high — narrator’s formula quotation). Source context: the opening of Asaph’s long maskil. “Give ear, O my people, to my law (תּוֹרָתִי)” (78:1). The speaker will open his mouth in a parable and pour out riddles “from of old” (78:2), things the fathers told. לֹא נְכַחֵד (“we will not hide”) them from the children (78:4); Swete has οὐκ ἐκρύβη (“it was not hidden”) [T]. The rest of the psalm retells the history of Israel’s unbelief, from Egypt and the wilderness to the choice of David the shepherd (78:70–72) [T]. The “parable” is Israel’s history read for its lesson. The texts: the first line is Swete and Rahlfs verbatim: ἀνοίξω ἐν παραβολαῖς τὸ στόμα μου (“I will open my mouth in parables”) [T]. The second line is not. Swete and Rahlfs have φθέγξομαι προβλήματα ἀπ᾿ ἀρχῆς (“I will utter problems from the beginning”). The Hebrew has אַבִּיעָה חִידוֹת מִנִּי־קֶדֶם (“I will pour forth riddles from of old”) [T]. Matthew’s ἐρεύξομαι (“I will pour forth, utter”) is the verb Swete uses for the same Hebrew verb at Ps 19:3 (Swete 18:3, ἐρεύγεται ῥῆμα, “pours forth speech”). נבע (“to pour forth”) stands at both places (find.py verify 5042, verified) [T]. Triage: category 3, the NT’s own Greek. Matthew renders the Hebrew afresh, using a Greek Psalter equivalent. κεκρυμμένα (“hidden things”) for חִידוֹת (“riddles”) interprets [I]. It may pick up the psalm’s own “not hidden” at 78:4 [I], uncertain. Bracketed text: NA28 prints ἀπὸ καταβολῆς [κόσμου] (“from the foundation [of the world]”). κόσμου (“of the world”) is omitted by א²ᵃ B ƒ¹ e k, Origen and Eusebius (NA28 apparatus, verified) [T]. SBLGNT omits it. Nothing in this finding rests on the word. “The prophet”: Swete and Rahlfs call Asaph τοῦ προφήτου (“the prophet”) at 2 Chr 29:30, where the Hebrew has הַחֹזֶה (“the seer”) [T], verified. א* Θ ƒ¹·¹³ 33 add Ἠσαΐου (“Isaiah”), and Jerome knew manuscripts reading “Asaph” (NA28 apparatus, verified) [T]. Book usage: the eighth narrator’s formula, and the first psalm in that series. Ps 78’s wilderness themes — testing God (Swete 77:18, 41, 56), “can God prepare a table in the wilderness?” (77:19), “bread of heaven” (77:24) — sit close to the feedings and the demand for a sign in 14:13–16:12 [I], synthetic, moderate. OT-to-OT: the psalm retells Exodus 16–17 and Numbers 11 as a mashal. Its ending, David chosen to shepherd Jacob (78:70–72), is the shepherd theme Matthew uses at 2:6 and 9:36 [I], moderate. What it adds: the parables are revelation, not only concealment. 13:11 says the mysteries are given, 13:13–15 says why some do not see, and 13:35 says that what is spoken is “hidden from the foundation”. It is now uttered, as Asaph uttered Israel’s history as a parable.

Dan 12:3 → 13:43 (high) [S: audit]. Rahlfs Theodotion ἐκλάμψουσιν (“they will shine out”), Matthew’s verb, against Swete’s λάμψουσιν (“they will shine”). Text-first extension: the NASB95 prints the clause in small capitals as an OT quotation (“THE RIGHTEOUS WILL SHINE FORTH AS THE SUN”); the ESV does not mark it. The pulpit will not show it.

Dan 3:6 → 13:42, 50 (high) [S: audit]. The furnace of the persecutor becomes the furnace of judgement on the lawless. Matthew has no ἐμ- (“in”) prefix; minuscule 700 adds it at 13:42 (NA28 apparatus).

Mal 3:19–21 (Swete 4:1–3) → 13:40–43 (moderate–high — audit candidate 14, tested here). Source context: the day comes καιομένη ὡς κλίβανος (“burning like an oven”), and πάντες οἱ ποιοῦντες ἄνομα (“all who do lawless things”) will be stubble. For those who fear the name ἀνατελεῖ … ἥλιος δικαιοσύνης (“a sun of righteousness will rise”) (Swete 4:1–2) [T]. Rahlfs has ποιοῦντες ἄνομα (“doing lawless things”) (verified). The Hebrew has כָּל־עֹשֵׂה רִשְׁעָה (“every doer of wickedness”) and שֶׁמֶשׁ צְדָקָה (“a sun of righteousness”) (3:19–20) [T]. Matthew’s sequence is the same: the lawless burned (πυρὶ καίεται, “burned with fire”, 13:40), and then the righteous shining ὡς ὁ ἥλιος (“as the sun”, 13:43) [T]. The words are shared in part (ποιεῖν + ἀνομ-, burning, sun, righteous), and the order is the same. Daniel supplies the verb, Malachi the sequence [I]. Classed as co-text to Dan 12:3.

Zeph 1:2–3 → 13:41 (moderate — new). Source context: the opening oracle of universal judgement. The Hebrew has אָסֹף אָסֵף כֹּל (“I will utterly gather everything”, 1:2) and וְהַמַּכְשֵׁלוֹת אֶת־הָרְשָׁעִים (“and the stumbling blocks with the wicked”, 1:3) [T]. מַכְשֵׁלָה (“stumbling block”) occurs only at Isa 3:6 and Zeph 1:3 (find.py lemma 4384, verified). Swete has neither the gathering nor the stumbling blocks. It reads ἀσθενήσουσιν οἱ ἀσεβεῖς, καὶ ἐξαρῶ τοὺς ἀνόμους (“the ungodly will grow weak, and I will remove the lawless”) [T]. Rahlfs lacks that clause too, and reads ἐξαρῶ τοὺς ἀνθρώπους (“I will remove the people”) [T], verified: a Swete–Rahlfs divergence. Matthew’s συλλέξουσιν … τὰ σκάνδαλα καὶ τοὺς ποιοῦντας τὴν ἀνομίαν (“they will gather … the stumbling blocks and those who commit lawlessness”) stands nearest the Hebrew pair (gather; stumbling blocks with the wicked). Only Swete supplies a word for “lawless” [I].

Dan 4 (Theodotion); Ezek 17:23; 31:6; Ps 104:12 → 13:32 (moderate). Move 1: Theodotion Dan 4:18 (Swete “Dat 4:18”; Rahlfs the same) has ἐν τοῖς κλάδοις αὐτοῦ κατεσκήνουν τὰ ὄρνεα τοῦ οὐρανοῦ (“in its branches the birds of heaven nested”) [T], verified. That is closer to Matthew’s κατασκηνοῦν ἐν τοῖς κλάδοις αὐτοῦ (“nest in its branches”) than the Old Greek (ἐνόσσευον, “were nesting”, Swete Dan 4:9) or Ezek 17:23, where they rest “under its shade” [T]. Ps 103:12 Swete has τὰ πετεινὰ τοῦ οὐρανοῦ κατασκηνώσει (“the birds of heaven will nest”) [T]. In Daniel and Ezekiel 31 the tree is an empire, and it is cut down. In Ezek 17:23 it is the cedar the LORD plants on Israel’s mountain [T]. The wording is shared with Luke, so it belongs to the tradition.

Gen 18:6 → 13:33 (uncertain). Move 1: Sarah’s τρία μέτρα (“three measures”; Hebrew שְׁלֹשׁ סְאִים, “three seahs”) baked as ἐγκρυφίας (“cakes hidden [in the ashes]”) for the visitors (Swete) [T]. Three seahs is a feast-sized quantity. The ἐγκρυφ- (“hide in”) wordplay is suggestive but is not enough to establish the link.

Internal: answers §3:12 — wheat, barn, burning (high, verified). Answers §4:1–11 — the devil (moderate). Answers §5:44 — the enemy (moderate). Answers §7:23 — ἀνομία (“lawlessness”) (moderate–high, verified). Answers §11:25 — hidden things (moderate–high, synthetic). Plants §15:13–14 — uproot; “let them alone” (high, verified). Plants §16:6–12 — leaven reversed (high, verified). Plants §24:3, 31; 28:20 — the end of the age; his angels sent (high, verified). Plants §25:34 — ἀπὸ καταβολῆς (“from the foundation”), at 13:35 and 25:34 only (verified) (moderate). Plants §17:20 — the mustard seed (moderate). Plants §16:28 — the Son of Man’s kingdom (moderate).

Translations. NASB95 “tares”, ESV “weeds” (ζιζάνια). NASB95 “stumbling blocks”, ESV “causes of sin” (13:41). The ESV loses the link to σκάνδαλον (“stumbling block”) at 16:23, where it has “hindrance”, and to 18:7. The NASB95 keeps “stumbling block” at all three. NASB95 “shine forth” (in small capitals), ESV “shine like”. Pulpit divergence: at 13:41 the congregation will hear “causes of sin” and will not hear Peter’s σκάνδαλον (“stumbling block”) anticipated.

Difficulty. Is the field the church or the world? The text says ὁ ἀγρός ἐστιν ὁ κόσμος (“the field is the world”, 13:38) [T]. But the angels gather the weeds ἐκ τῆς βασιλείας αὐτοῦ (“out of his kingdom”, 13:41) [T]. Options: (1) The field is the world. The parable forbids the disciples to purge the world of the wicked before the end [I]. (2) The field is the visible community, and the parable restrains church discipline [S]. But 18:15–17 prescribes discipline, so the parable cannot forbid it [T]/[I]. (3) The Son of Man’s kingdom claims the whole world, so the weeds are in “his kingdom” because the world is his field [I]. The text gives the identification (13:38) and does not resolve the tension.

Pitfall. “The weeds are other people; the church is pure wheat.” The text puts ἀνομία (“lawlessness”) among those who say “Lord, Lord” (7:23), and defines the weeds by their sower, not by their membership. The corrective: preach 13:30 as patience and 13:41–43 as a warning to hearers, with “he who has ears, let him hear” (13:43) as the address.

Book-overview note: Extends and contradicts in detail. (1) The Ps 78:2 row says the second line is “independent of both the LXX and the Hebrew”. Text-first, ἐρεύξομαι (“I will pour forth”) is the Greek Psalter’s own equivalent of the Hebrew verb (נבע, Ps 19:3 / Swete 18:3, gated), so the line is a fresh rendering of the Hebrew. Say “independent of the LXX, nearer the Hebrew”. Add that Swete and Rahlfs call Asaph “the prophet” at 2 Chr 29:30, and flag [κόσμου] as bracketed. (2) The 13:32 rows: the verbal match is Theodotion Dan 4:18 (κατεσκήνουν … ἐν τοῖς κλάδοις, “nested … in the branches”), not the Old Greek 4:9, 18. (3) Add Zeph 1:2–3 (Hebrew, moderate) and Mal 3:19–21 (as co-text, per the audit). Zeph 1:3 is a new Swete–Rahlfs divergence, though one Matthew does not depend on. (4) The overview’s “a mixed community of wheat and weeds” (Presenting situation) should note that the text says the field is the world (13:38).

27. Matthew 13:44–58 — Treasure, Pearl, Net — New and Old; Rejected at Home

Position. Q1: in the house, the disciples have had the weeds explained (13:36–43). The judgement at the end has been told, and the reader has been told to wait [T]. Q2: two things are still owed. The first is what the kingdom is worth to the one who finds it now (treasure, pearl), and the second is the net, the separation stated a second time. The discourse then closes on the disciples’ understanding (13:51) and on the scribe they are to become (13:52). Then, at once, Nazareth. The unit exists here because the parables chapter has to end in two directions: the disciple who understands and sells all, and the home town that takes offence [I]. The narrator’s ἐτέλεσεν (“he finished”, 13:53) puts the hinge inside the unit [T].

Structure.

Verses Section
13:44 Treasure hidden in a field
13:45–46 The merchant and the pearl
13:47–50 The net; the end of the age
13:51–52 “Have you understood?” The scribe discipled for the kingdom
13:53 Formula: “when Jesus had finished these parables”
13:54–58 Nazareth: “Where did this man get these things?” Offence; few mighty works

The three private parables each open Ὁμοία ἐστὶν ἡ βασιλεία τῶν οὐρανῶν (“the kingdom of heaven is like”, 13:44), with Πάλιν (“again”) before the second and third (13:45, 47) [T]. The phrase Ὁμοία ἐστὶν ἡ βασιλεία τῶν οὐρανῶν occurs five times, all in this chapter: 13:31, 33, 44, 45, 47 (SBLGNT, verified). The net (47–50) repeats the end of the weeds (13:40–42) almost word for word, so the discourse closes as its middle did [T].

Text-first findings.

  • Joy, and “all that he has”. ἀπὸ τῆς χαρᾶς αὐτοῦ ὑπάγει καὶ πωλεῖ πάντα ὅσα ἔχει (“from joy over it he goes and sells all that he has”, 13:44); the merchant πέπρακεν πάντα ὅσα εἶχεν (“sold all that he had”, 13:46) [T]. The selling is driven by joy, not by duty [T]. θησαυρός (“treasure”) and πωλέω (“to sell”) come together again only at 19:21: πώλησόν σου τὰ ὑπάρχοντα … καὶ ἕξεις θησαυρὸν ἐν οὐρανοῖς (“sell your possessions … and you will have treasure in heaven”) (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. The rich young man is the man in the field who does not sell, and he goes away λυπούμενος (“grieving”, 19:22), where this man goes ἀπὸ τῆς χαρᾶς (“from joy”) [I], moderate–high, synthetic. θησαυρός (“treasure”) runs from the magi’s treasures (2:11) through 6:19–21 (“where your treasure is”) and 12:35 to 13:44, 52 and 19:21 (8 verses, verified).
  • The net is the weeds again. οὕτως ἔσται ἐν τῇ συντελείᾳ τοῦ αἰῶνος (“so it will be at the end of the age”, 13:49) is verbatim from 13:40, and 13:50 repeats 13:42 word for word [T]. The new word is ἀφορίζω (“to separate”): ἀφοριοῦσιν τοὺς πονηροὺς ἐκ μέσου τῶν δικαίων (“they will separate the wicked from among the righteous”, 13:49). It occurs again only at 25:32 (twice), where the Son of Man separates the nations “as the shepherd separates the sheep from the goats” (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. The net gathers ἐκ παντὸς γένους (“of every kind”, 13:47). The sorting is done by καλά (“good”) and σαπρά (“bad”). σαπρός (“rotten, bad”) is the tree word of 7:17–18 and 12:33 (verified) [T].
  • “Have you understood all these things?” “Yes.” Συνήκατε ταῦτα πάντα; λέγουσιν αὐτῷ· Ναί (13:51) [T]. This is the sixth of nine verses with συνίημι (“to understand”) (13:13, 14, 15, 19, 23, 51; 15:10; 16:12; 17:13, verified). The disciples now claim what Isaiah’s people lacked (13:13–15) [T]. ταῦτα πάντα (“all these things”) runs through the chapter: Jesus spoke ταῦτα πάντα (“all these things”) in parables (13:34), the disciples understand ταῦτα πάντα (13:51), and Nazareth asks where he got ταῦτα πάντα (13:56) (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. The same words are understood in the house and are a scandal at home [I], moderate–high.
  • The discipled scribe. πᾶς γραμματεὺς μαθητευθεὶς τῇ βασιλείᾳ τῶν οὐρανῶν (“every scribe who has been discipled for the kingdom of heaven”, 13:52) [T]. μαθητεύω (“to make a disciple, be discipled”) occurs only at 13:52; 27:57; 28:19 (SBLGNT, verified). γραμματεύς (“scribe”) stands in 22 verses (verified). It is mostly used of opponents, and the exceptions are 8:19 (a scribe offers to follow) and 23:34 (Jesus sends “prophets and wise men and scribes”). Here it is a vocation [T]. He ἐκβάλλει ἐκ τοῦ θησαυροῦ αὐτοῦ καινὰ καὶ παλαιά (“brings out of his treasure things new and old”). This is the form of 12:35: ἐκ τοῦ ἀγαθοῦ θησαυροῦ ἐκβάλλει ἀγαθά (“out of his good treasure brings out good things”) [T]. The order is new before old [T]. καινὰ καὶ παλαιά (“new and old”) stands nowhere else in the NT (SBLGNT, verified).
  • The formula that closes a discourse. καὶ ἐγένετο ὅτε ἐτέλεσεν ὁ Ἰησοῦς (“and it came about that when Jesus had finished”) occurs five times: 7:28; 11:1; 13:53; 19:1; 26:1 (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. At 7:28 the crowds ἐξεπλήσσοντο (“were astonished”) at his teaching, because he taught with authority. At 13:54 the people of his hometown are ἐκπλήσσεσθαι (“astonished”) too (ἐκπλήσσομαι: 7:28; 13:54; 19:25; 22:33, verified) [T]. The same astonishment follows both discourses, but here it turns to offence (13:57) [T].
  • “Whence?” Πόθεν τούτῳ ἡ σοφία αὕτη καὶ αἱ δυνάμεις; (“where did this man get this wisdom and these miraculous powers?”, 13:54); πόθεν οὖν τούτῳ ταῦτα πάντα; (“where then did this man get all these things?”, 13:56). πόθεν οὖν (“whence then?”) occurs only at 13:27 and 13:56 (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. The servants ask the master where the weeds came from. Nazareth asks where Jesus’ wisdom came from [T]. The answer to the first was an enemy. The second is left for the reader [I], moderate. σοφία (“wisdom”) stands at 11:19 (“wisdom is vindicated by her deeds”), 12:42 (“something greater than Solomon”) and here (3 verses, verified) [T].
  • The prophet without honour, and the δυνάμεις. Οὐκ ἔστιν προφήτης ἄτιμος εἰ μὴ ἐν τῇ πατρίδι (“a prophet is not without honour except in his hometown”, 13:57). Jesus names himself a prophet [T]. The next verse uses δύναμις (“miracle, power”): οὐκ ἐποίησεν ἐκεῖ δυνάμεις πολλὰς διὰ τὴν ἀπιστίαν αὐτῶν (“he did not do many miracles there because of their unbelief”, 13:58). Then Herod says αἱ δυνάμεις ἐνεργοῦσιν ἐν αὐτῷ (“the miraculous powers are at work in him”, 14:2) [T]. δύναμις stands at 13:54, 58 and 14:2 in sequence (verified). Two audiences in a row ask what the powers mean and give the wrong answer [I], moderate. ἀπιστία (“unbelief”) occurs once in Matthew (verified).
  • The family named. ἡ μήτηρ αὐτοῦ λέγεται Μαριὰμ καὶ οἱ ἀδελφοὶ αὐτοῦ Ἰάκωβος καὶ Ἰωσὴφ καὶ Σίμων καὶ Ἰούδας (“his mother is called Mary, and his brothers James and Joseph and Simon and Judas”, 13:55) [T]. Matthew has Ἰωσήφ (“Joseph”) where Mark has Ἰωσῆτος (“Joses”, Mark 6:3). The pair Ἰάκωβος … Ἰωσήφ (“James … Joseph”) returns at 27:56, “Mary the mother of James and Joseph” (NA28 and SBLGNT both print Ἰωσήφ there) [T]. Whether the same family is meant is [S] debated. The verbal fact stands, and its significance is uncertain.

Synoptic check. The three private parables and 13:51–52 are Matthew’s alone [T]. Nazareth follows Mark 6:1–6, but Matthew moves it so that it closes the parables chapter. In Mark it follows Jairus’ daughter [T]. He changes Mark’s ὁ τέκτων, ὁ υἱὸς τῆς Μαρίας (“the carpenter, the son of Mary”, Mark 6:3) to ὁ τοῦ τέκτονος υἱός (“the carpenter’s son”, 13:55) [T]. He drops ἐν τοῖς συγγενεῦσιν αὐτοῦ (“among his own relatives”, Mark 6:4). And he changes οὐκ ἐδύνατο ἐκεῖ ποιῆσαι οὐδεμίαν δύναμιν (“he could not do any miracle there”, Mark 6:5) to οὐκ ἐποίησεν ἐκεῖ δυνάμεις πολλάς (“he did not do many miracles there”, 13:58) [T]. Mark’s ἐθαύμαζεν διὰ τὴν ἀπιστίαν αὐτῶν (“he wondered at their unbelief”, Mark 6:6) becomes the reason for the “not many” [T]. Matthew removes the inability and keeps the unbelief [I], high.

Tool 11. No quotation. Allusions are moderate at best.

Sir 39:1–3 → 13:52 (moderate — outside the Tanak). Move 1: Ben Sira’s portrait of the scribe who studies “the law of the Most High”, seeks out the wisdom of the ancients, occupies himself with prophecies, and seeks out ἀπόκρυφα παροιμιῶν (“the hidden things of proverbs”), at home ἐν αἰνίγμασι παραβολῶν (“in the riddles of parables”) (Swete) [T]. Matthew’s scribe, his “hidden” things (13:35, 44) and his parables are the same cluster. Matthew’s scribe is discipled for the kingdom rather than for the ancients [I].

Song 7:14 (Swete 7:13) → 13:52 (uncertain). Move 1: “new as well as old” (חֲדָשִׁים גַּם־יְשָׁנִים, “new ones as well as old”), stored (צָפַנְתִּי, “I have stored up”, gated 6845) for the beloved. Swete and Rahlfs have νέα πρὸς παλαιά (“new with old”), and Matthew has καινὰ καὶ παλαιά (“new and old”) [T]. The pair is common, and the Song is not in view elsewhere in Matthew.

Ezek 47:10; Hab 1:14–17 → 13:47 (uncertain). Move 1: σαγήνη (“dragnet”) is in Swete at Ezek 47:10, where fishermen spread nets on the healed sea and the fish are “of every kind” (Hebrew לְמִינָה, “according to its kind”). It is also at Hab 1:15–16, where the Chaldean gathers men in his nets [T]. The first offers abundance, the second judgement. Matthew’s net carries both, but the wording is not close.

Internal: answers §12:35 — the treasure saying (high, verified). Answers §6:19–21 — treasure (moderate). Answers §13:13–15 — understanding (high, verified). Answers §13:27 — “whence then?” (moderate, verified). Answers §7:28 — the discourse formula and the astonishment (high, verified). Answers §2:23 — Nazareth’s man is ὁ τοῦ τέκτονος υἱός (“the carpenter’s son”). The prologue’s “he will be called a Nazarene” is here the reason for offence (moderate). Plants §19:21–22 — sell, treasure, grief instead of joy (moderate–high, synthetic). Plants §25:32 — ἀφορίζω (“to separate”) (high, verified). Plants §27:57; 28:19 — μαθητεύω (“to make a disciple”) (high, verified). Plants §27:56 — James and Joseph (uncertain). Plants §14:2 — δυνάμεις (“miraculous powers”) (moderate).

Translations. At 13:52 the NASB95 has “every scribe who has become a disciple of the kingdom”, and the ESV “every scribe who has been trained for the kingdom”. The NASB95 keeps the μαθητεύω (“to make a disciple”) chain to 27:57 (“had also become a disciple”) and 28:19 (“make disciples”). The ESV breaks it at 13:52. At 13:44 both have “hidden … hid”. Pulpit divergence: the ESV’s “trained for” (13:52). The congregation will not hear that the scribe of 13:52 is what the Great Commission makes.

Book-overview note: Confirms with something new. The echo-table row on μαθητεύω (“to make a disciple”) and the English row on 13:52 stand. Add: (1) the 13:44 ↔︎ 19:21–22 contrast (sell all, treasure; joy against grief); (2) the discourse-ending formula at 13:53 is followed by astonishment, as at 7:28, but here it turns to offence; (3) Matthew’s change of Mark 6:5 (“could not” → “did not … many”).

28. Matthew 14:1–21 — John Beheaded; Five Thousand Fed

Position. Q1: Nazareth has taken offence, and the δυνάμεις (“miraculous powers”) are the question (13:54–58) [T]. Q2: the next voice to ask about the powers is the ruler’s, and his answer is that John is risen. The forerunner’s death is narrated here, after the hometown rejection and before the bread, because it shows the end of the road the prophet takes (13:57; 14:5) [I]. The feeding then answers Herod’s banquet with a meal in a desolate place. Two meals are set side by side, the king’s and the shepherd’s [I], moderate–high. In Matthew the flashback ends with John’s disciples reporting to Jesus, and “having heard” he withdraws (14:12–13), so the death is the cause of what follows [T].

Structure.

Verses Section
14:1–2 Herod hears: “This is John the Baptist; he has been raised from the dead”
14:3–12 Flashback (γάρ, “for”): arrest, the birthday, the oath, the head on a platter; burial; the report to Jesus
14:13–14 Jesus withdraws; the crowds follow; compassion and healing
14:15–21 Evening: “You give them something to eat”; five loaves, two fish; twelve baskets; five thousand men besides women and children

The flashback opens with Ὁ γὰρ Ἡρῴδης (“for Herod”, 14:3) and closes by returning to Jesus (14:12) [T]. Two meals frame the unit: the birthday feast with the head ἐπὶ πίνακι (“on a platter”, 14:8, 11), and the bread in the wilderness (14:19) [T].

Text-first findings.

  • The second Herod. Ἡρῴδης (“Herod”) occurs in 12 verses of Matthew, all in chapter 2 or chapter 14 (2:1, 3, 7, 12, 13, 15, 16, 19, 22; 14:1, 3, 6; SBLGNT, verified) [T]. The first Herod tried to kill the child, and the second kills the forerunner. Each acts on a king’s fear and a word about a “king” or a “prophet” [I], moderate–high. The narrator gives his title correctly, ὁ τετραάρχης (“the tetrarch”, 14:1, its only use), and then calls him ὁ βασιλεύς (“the king”, 14:9) [T]. The king of 14:9 is only a king in his own court [I], moderate.
  • “Raised from the dead” — the first time. αὐτὸς ἠγέρθη ἀπὸ τῶν νεκρῶν (“he has been raised from the dead”, 14:2) [T]. ἠγέρθη ἀπὸ τῶν νεκρῶν (“he has been raised from the dead”) occurs at 14:2, 27:64 and 28:7 only (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. Herod speaks it falsely of John. The chief priests fear it will be said of Jesus (27:64). The angel says it is true of Jesus (28:7). Verified; synthetic, high for the chain.
  • “Having heard … he withdrew.” Ἀκούσας δὲ ὁ Ἰησοῦς ἀνεχώρησεν (“now when Jesus heard, he withdrew”, 14:13) [T]. ἀκούσας δέ (“now having heard”) with ἀνεχώρησεν (“he withdrew”) stands at 2:22 (Archelaus, son of Herod), 4:12 (John handed over) and 14:13 (John killed) (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. Every time, the threat is Herodian or comes through John [T]. ἀναχωρέω (“to withdraw”) occurs in 10 verses, and at least seven are retreats from danger (2:12, 13, 14, 22; 4:12; 12:15; 14:13; verified) [T]/[I].
  • John’s passion previews Jesus’. Herod κρατήσας … ἔδησεν (“seized … bound”, 14:3). θέλων αὐτὸν ἀποκτεῖναι ἐφοβήθη τὸν ὄχλον, ὅτι ὡς προφήτην αὐτὸν εἶχον (“wanting to kill him, he feared the crowd, because they held him to be a prophet”, 14:5) [T]. The same fear holds back the chief priests over John (21:26, ὡς προφήτην, “as a prophet”) and over Jesus (21:46, ἐφοβήθησαν τοὺς ὄχλους, ἐπεὶ εἰς προφήτην αὐτὸν εἶχον, “they feared the crowds, because they held him to be a prophet”) [T], verified. κρατέω (“to seize”) returns at 26:4, 48, 50, 55, 57, and δέω (“to bind”) at 27:2 [T]. The ruler λυπηθείς (“grieved”) gives way διὰ τοὺς ὅρκους (“because of his oaths”, 14:9), as Pilate will give way to the crowd (27:24) [I], moderate. ὅρκος (“oath”) stands at 5:33; 14:7, 9; 26:72 (verified). The Sermon forbade oaths, Herod’s oath kills a prophet, and Peter’s oath denies Jesus [T]/[I].
  • Compassion, and the disciples told to give. ἐσπλαγχνίσθη ἐπ᾿ αὐτοῖς καὶ ἐθεράπευσεν τοὺς ἀρρώστους αὐτῶν (“he felt compassion for them and healed their sick”, 14:14). σπλαγχνίζομαι (“to feel compassion”) stands at 9:36; 14:14; 15:32; 18:27; 20:34 (verified) [T]. The disciples say ἀπόλυσον τοὺς ὄχλους (“send the crowds away”). Jesus answers, δότε αὐτοῖς ὑμεῖς φαγεῖν (“you give them something to eat”, 14:16) [T]. The disciples were told at their sending, δωρεὰν ἐλάβετε, δωρεὰν δότε (“freely you received, freely give”, 10:8) [T]. Here the giving passes through their hands: ἔδωκεν τοῖς μαθηταῖς … οἱ δὲ μαθηταὶ τοῖς ὄχλοις (“he gave them to the disciples, and the disciples to the crowds”, 14:19) [T].
  • The meal words of the Supper. λαβὼν τοὺς πέντε ἄρτους … εὐλόγησεν καὶ κλάσας ἔδωκεν τοῖς μαθηταῖς (“taking the five loaves … he blessed, and breaking he gave to the disciples”, 14:19). At 26:26: λαβὼν ὁ Ἰησοῦς ἄρτον καὶ εὐλογήσας ἔκλασεν καὶ δοὺς τοῖς μαθηταῖς (“Jesus took bread, blessed, broke it and gave to the disciples”) [T]. κλάω (“to break”) occurs only at 14:19; 15:36; 26:26, and εὐλογέω (“to bless”) with κλάω only at 14:19 and 26:26 (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. ὀψίας δὲ γενομένης (“when it was evening”) stands at 14:15 and also at 26:20, when he reclines with the Twelve (6 verses in all: 8:16; 14:15, 23; 20:8; 26:20; 27:57, verified) [T]. Synthetic: moderate–high.
  • “Besides women and children.” χωρὶς γυναικῶν καὶ παιδίων (“besides women and children”, 14:21) stands at 14:21 and 15:38 only, and Mark has neither (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. Matthew counts as Israel’s wilderness censuses count, by men (ἄνδρες, “men”), and then breaks the count open [I], moderate. χορτάζω (“to be satisfied”) first appeared in the beatitude: ὅτι αὐτοὶ χορτασθήσονται (“for they shall be satisfied”, 5:6). It returns here (14:20) and at 15:33, 37 (verified) [T].

Synoptic check. Matthew abridges Mark 6:14–44 heavily [T]. (1) In Mark Herod ἐφοβεῖτο τὸν Ἰωάννην, εἰδὼς αὐτὸν ἄνδρα δίκαιον καὶ ἅγιον (“feared John, knowing him to be a righteous and holy man”) and protected him, and it is Herodias who wants him dead (Mark 6:19–20). In Matthew, Herod himself is θέλων αὐτὸν ἀποκτεῖναι (“wanting to kill him”), and he fears the crowd, not John (14:5) [T]. Matthew makes the ruler the would-be killer [I], high. (2) Matthew drops Herod’s oath ἕως ἡμίσους τῆς βασιλείας μου (“up to half of my kingdom”, Mark 6:23), the phrase of Esth 5:3; 7:2 (Swete, verified) [T]. The Esther echo is Mark’s, not Matthew’s. (3) Mark makes the death a flashback inside the mission of the Twelve (Mark 6:7–13, 30). Matthew links it to Jesus’ withdrawal (14:12–13) [T]. (4) Mark’s compassion is ὅτι ἦσαν ὡς πρόβατα μὴ ἔχοντα ποιμένα (“because they were like sheep without a shepherd”, Mark 6:34). Matthew omits that clause here because he used it at 9:36 (Num 27:17; see the overview), and his compassion leads to healing, not teaching [T]. (5) Matthew drops the “two hundred denarii” exchange (Mark 6:37) and adds “besides women and children” [T].

Tool 11.

2 Kgs 4:42–44 → 14:16–20 (moderate–high — narrative pattern; shared with Mark). Source context: in a famine (2 Kgs 4:38), a man brings Elisha twenty barley loaves of firstfruits. Elisha says Δότε τῷ λαῷ καὶ ἐσθιέτωσαν (“give to the people and let them eat”). His servant objects, “How can I set this before a hundred men?” The prophet repeats the command with a word of the LORD: Φάγονται καὶ καταλείψουσιν (“they will eat and have some left”). καὶ ἔφαγον καὶ κατέλιπον κατὰ τὸ ῥῆμα Κυρίου (“and they ate and had some left, according to the word of the LORD”) (Swete; Rahlfs identical, verified; Hebrew אָכֹל וְהוֹתֵר, “eating and leaving over”) [T]. Book usage: Elijah–Elisha material is live: John is “Elijah who is to come” (11:14), and 17:10–13 follows [T]. OT-to-OT: the Elisha story is the prophetic repeat of the wilderness manna. Exod 16:18 and Num 11 (πόθεν μοι κρέα, “where am I to get meat?”, Num 11:13 Swete) lie behind both [I]. What it adds: the command δότε (“give”), the servant’s objection, the eating and the remainder are Elisha’s sequence. Jesus’ meal is on a larger scale (5 loaves, 5,000 men, 12 baskets) [T]. The scene casts Jesus as the prophet greater than the prophets, after the death of the one he called Elijah (11:14) [I], moderate.

Ps 78:19–25 (Swete 77:19–25) → 14:15–20 (moderate, synthetic — see unit 26). Move 1: Μὴ δυνήσεται ὁ θεὸς ἑτοιμάσαι τράπεζαν ἐν ἐρήμῳ; (“Can God prepare a table in the wilderness?”, Swete 77:19), and ἄρτον οὐρανοῦ ἔδωκεν αὐτοῖς (“he gave them bread of heaven”, 77:24) [T]. Matthew has the desolate place twice (ἔρημον τόπον, “desolate place”, 14:13; Ἔρημός ἐστιν ὁ τόπος, “this place is desolate”, 14:15) [T]. Ps 78 was cited at 13:35, so it is in the book’s recent view [T].

Internal: answers §2:1–22 — Herod, withdrawal (high, verified). Answers §4:12 — John handed over; withdrawal (high, verified). Answers §5:6 — “they shall be satisfied” (moderate). Answers §5:33–37 — oaths (moderate). Answers §10:8 — “freely give” (moderate). Answers §11:14 — John as Elijah (moderate). Answers §13:57 — the prophet without honour (moderate–high). Plants §21:26, 46 — fear of the crowd that holds him a prophet (high, verified). Plants §26:20, 26 — evening; bless, break, give (high, verified). Plants §27:64; 28:7 — “raised from the dead” (high, verified). Plants §16:9 — five loaves, κόφινοι (“baskets”) (high, verified).

Translations. At 14:9 NASB95 has “Although he was grieved”, ESV “the king was sorry”. λυπέω (“to grieve”) returns of the disciples (17:23; 18:31; 26:22) and of Jesus (26:37). The NASB95’s “grieved” keeps the word nearer its later uses. At 14:19 NASB95 has “He blessed the food” (with “the food” supplied) and ESV “said a blessing”. Neither names an object, and neither is wrong. Pulpit divergence: none bearing.

Book-overview note: Extends. The overview pairs Herod (ch. 2) with Pilate, but Ἡρῴδης (“Herod”) is used only in chapters 2 and 14 (verified). Add the pattern ἀκούσας δέ … ἀνεχώρησεν (“having heard … he withdrew”, 2:22; 4:12; 14:13) and the chain ἠγέρθη ἀπὸ τῶν νεκρῶν (“he has been raised from the dead”, 14:2; 27:64; 28:7). Add 2 Kgs 4:42–44 to the Intertextual Map (moderate–high, shared with Mark), and note that Matthew drops Mark’s Esther phrase.

29. Matthew 14:22–36 — “It Is I”; Little Faith; “Truly God’s Son” ⭐

Position. Q1: the storm of 8:23–27 ended with a question: ποταπός ἐστιν οὗτος (“what kind of man is this?”, 8:27) [T]. The feeding (14:13–21) has just shown the shepherd providing in the wilderness. Q2: this unit exists here because the question of 8:27 now receives its answer from the disciples themselves: Ἀληθῶς θεοῦ υἱὸς εἶ (“You are certainly God’s Son”, 14:33) [T]. It is the first human confession of the Son of God in the book, two chapters before Peter’s (16:16), and it is spoken in an act of worship [T].

Structure. Jesus dismisses the crowds and prays alone on the mountain (14:22–23); the boat in the night and the walking on the sea (14:24–27); Peter on the water (14:28–31); worship and confession in the boat (14:32–33); healings at Gennesaret (14:34–36) [T].

Text-first findings.

  • “Take courage, it is I.” Θαρσεῖτε, ἐγώ εἰμι· μὴ φοβεῖσθε (“take courage, it is I; do not be afraid”, 14:27) [T]. The words are Mark’s (6:50). ἐγώ εἰμι (“I am”) stands in Matthew at 14:27; 22:32; 24:5; 26:22, 25 (SBLGNT phrase search) [T], verified. At 22:32 it is God’s self-naming at the bush; at 24:5 it is the claim of the deceivers who “come in My name” [T]. Synthetic, moderate: in the sea scene the words are a greeting and more.
  • He walks on the sea. περιπατῶν ἐπὶ τὴν θάλασσαν (“walking on the sea”, 14:25). περιπατέω (“to walk”) occurs in Matthew at 4:18; 9:5; 11:5; 14:25, 26, 29; 15:31 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The healed walk (9:5; 11:5; 15:31); here the one who heals them walks on the water [I].
  • Peter, the wind, and a prayer. Κύριε, σῶσόν με (“Lord, save me!”, 14:30) echoes Κύριε, σῶσον (“Save us, Lord”, 8:25) [T]. Jesus answers Ὀλιγόπιστε, εἰς τί ἐδίστασας; (“You of little faith, why did you doubt?”, 14:31). διστάζω (“to doubt”) occurs only at 14:31 and 28:17 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The doubt on the water and the doubt on the mountain are the same word; in both, Jesus acts towards the doubter (stretching out his hand; coming near and speaking) [T]. The overview has the chain; confirmed.
  • Worship and confession. προσεκύνησαν αὐτῷ λέγοντες· Ἀληθῶς θεοῦ υἱὸς εἶ (“those who were in the boat worshipped Him, saying, ‘You are certainly God’s Son!’”, 14:33) [T]. προσκυνέω (“to worship”) is in 13 verses (SBLGNT); after the devil’s demand (4:9) and Jesus’ answer (4:10), it is directed to Jesus [T], verified. ἀληθῶς θεοῦ υἱός (“truly God’s Son”) stands only at 14:33 and 27:54 [T], verified (overview). Mark 6:51–52 ends instead with hearts hardened [T].
  • The fringe of his garment. ἵνα μόνον ἅψωνται τοῦ κρασπέδου τοῦ ἱματίου αὐτοῦ (“that they might just touch the fringe of His cloak”, 14:36). κράσπεδον (“fringe, tassel”) occurs only at 9:20; 14:36; 23:5 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The sick reach for his fringe; the scribes and Pharisees lengthen theirs to be seen (23:5) [T]. Synthetic, moderate–high.

Synoptic check. Mark 6:45–52 has the walking on the sea and “it is I”, but no Peter and no confession; it ends οὐ γὰρ συνῆκαν ἐπὶ τοῖς ἄρτοις, ἀλλ’ ἦν αὐτῶν ἡ καρδία πεπωρωμένη (“they had not gained any insight from the loaves, but their heart was hardened”, Mark 6:52) [T]. Matthew adds Peter on the water (14:28–31), the worship and the confession (14:33), and drops the hardening. The disciples’ insight comes earlier in Matthew than in Mark [T]. Luke omits the episode.

Tool 11. Job 9:8 → 14:25 (moderate — conceptual and verbal). Source context: Job describes God’s power — he alone stretched out the heavens and treads on the waves of the sea. Swete Job 9:8 περιπατῶν ὡς ἐπ’ ἐδάφους ἐπὶ θαλάσσης (“walking on the sea as on dry ground”) [T]. The Hebrew וְדוֹרֵךְ עַל־בָּמֳתֵי יָם (“and treads on the heights of the sea”) has the same picture. Matthew shares περιπατῶν ἐπὶ … θαλάσσ- [T]. The overview has no row; new candidate, moderate (it is shared with Mark).

Ps 77:20 (Swete 76:20); Isa 43:2, 10 (moderate — Move 1 only). The way of the LORD through the sea, and “when you pass through the waters, I am with you … that you may know that ἐγώ εἰμι” [T]. A trajectory, not a quotation [I].

Internal: answers §8:23–27 — the question of 8:27 answered (high). Answers §4:9–10 — worship rightly given (high, verified). Plants §16:16 — Peter’s confession (high). Plants §27:54 — ἀληθῶς θεοῦ υἱός (high, verified). Plants §28:17 — διστάζω (high, verified). Plants §23:5 — κράσπεδον (moderate–high, verified). Answers §1:23 — God with us, on the sea (moderate).

Translations. NASB95 “You are certainly God’s Son!”; ESV “Truly you are the Son of God.” The NASB95 breaks the exact repetition with 27:54 (“Truly this was the Son of God!”), the ESV keeps it (overview English table, confirmed) [T]. Pulpit divergence: favourable — the ESV congregation will hear the two confessions match.

Pitfall. Peter as the hero (“step out of the boat”). The text’s centre is the worship of 14:33, and Peter’s act ends in fear, sinking and rebuke. The corrective: preach Jesus’ hand and the disciples’ confession; Peter is the one rescued.

Book-overview note: confirms the ἀληθῶς θεοῦ υἱός, διστάζω and προσκυνέω chains and the 14:33 / Mark 6:52 contrast in the Christological Trajectory. Extends: Job 9:8 as a candidate; κράσπεδον (9:20; 14:36; 23:5) for the Echo Table.

30. Matthew 15:1–20 — What Defiles

Position. Q1: the crowds at Gennesaret were healed by touching the fringe of his garment (14:36) [T]. Q2: this unit exists here because scribes and Pharisees come ἀπὸ Ἱεροσολύμων (“from Jerusalem”, 15:1) to raise a purity question, and Jesus’ answer moves purity from hands and food to the heart. It clears the ground for the next scene, where he goes to the region of Tyre and Sidon and a Canaanite woman is heard (15:21–28) [T]/[I], high.

Structure. The charge about tradition and Jesus’ counter-charge (15:1–9); the saying to the crowd (15:10–11); private teaching: the Pharisees offended, blind guides, and the explanation to Peter (15:12–20) [T]. An inclusio: ἀνίπτοις χερσὶν φαγεῖν (“to eat with unwashed hands”, 15:20) closes on the charge of 15:2 [T].

Text-first findings.

  • Tradition against God’s command. παράδοσις (“tradition”) occurs only at 15:2, 3, 6 (SBLGNT), and παραβαίνω (“to transgress”) only at 15:2, 3 [T], verified. The charge “your disciples transgress the tradition of the elders” is returned word for word: “you transgress the commandment of God for the sake of your tradition” (15:3) [T]. ἠκυρώσατε τὸν λόγον τοῦ θεοῦ (“you invalidated the word of God”, 15:6) — ἀκυρόω occurs only here [T].
  • Matthew does not explain the custom. Mark 7:3–4 explains handwashing for Gentile readers; Matthew does not (overview, Presenting Situation) [T]. Confirmed.
  • “Blind guides.” τυφλοί εἰσιν ὁδηγοὶ τυφλῶν (“they are blind guides of the blind”, 15:14). ὁδηγός (“guide”) occurs only at 15:14; 23:16, 24 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The charge planted here becomes the refrain of the woes (23:16, 24) [T]. τυφλός (“blind”) is used of the healed (9:27; 12:22; 20:30) and of these guides; the reversal runs through the book [I], high.
  • The plant not planted by the Father. Πᾶσα φυτεία ἣν οὐκ ἐφύτευσεν ὁ πατήρ μου ὁ οὐράνιος ἐκριζωθήσεται (“every plant which My heavenly Father did not plant shall be uprooted”, 15:13) — Matthew’s addition to Mark [T]. It looks back to the wheat and weeds (13:29, ἐκριζώσητε, “you may uproot”) [T].
  • The heart. ἡ δὲ καρδία αὐτῶν πόρρω ἀπέχει ἀπ’ ἐμοῦ (“their heart is far away from Me”, 15:8) and ἐκ γὰρ τῆς καρδίας ἐξέρχονται διαλογισμοὶ πονηροί (“for out of the heart come evil thoughts”, 15:19) [T]. καρδία (“heart”) stands in 15 verses (SBLGNT, 16 occurrences), from “the pure in heart” (5:8) to “from your heart” (18:35) and “with all your heart” (22:37) [T], verified. Matthew’s list in 15:19 follows the order of the Decalogue’s second table: murders, adulteries, (fornications), thefts, false witness, then slanders [T]/[I], moderate–high. Mark 7:21–22 has a longer list in a different order [T].

Synoptic check. Matthew follows Mark 7:1–23 but reverses Mark’s order, putting the Corban counter-charge (15:3–6) before the Isaiah quotation (15:7–9). He drops Mark’s explanation of Jewish custom (Mark 7:3–4) and the narrator’s comment καθαρίζων πάντα τὰ βρώματα (“thus He declared all foods clean”, Mark 7:19) [T]. He adds the uprooted plant and the blind guides (15:12–14; cf. Luke 6:39 for the blind leading the blind), and the closing line about unwashed hands (15:20) [T]. The omission of Mark 7:19 is consistent with a readership that still keeps Jewish practice (overview, Presenting Situation) [I], moderate.

Tool 11. Isa 29:13 → 15:8–9 (high — quotation, introduced by καλῶς ἐπροφήτευσεν, “rightly did Isaiah prophesy”). Source context: Isaiah’s woe on Ariel (29:1–14). The people draw near with mouth and lips while their hearts are far, and their fear of God is “a commandment of men learned by rote” (מִצְוַת אֲנָשִׁים מְלֻמָּדָה). The LORD will therefore do a wonder: the wisdom of the wise will perish (29:14) [T]. Text: μάτην (“in vain”) is the Greek’s, not the Hebrew’s; the Hebrew has “their fear of me is a commandment of men” [T]. Matthew follows the Greek here (category 3 — the NT’s own Greek). Swete and Rahlfs differ: Swete has ἐγγίζει μοι ὁ λαὸς οὗτος ἐν τῷ στόματι αὐτοῦ καὶ ἐν τοῖς χείλεσιν αὐτῶν τιμῶσίν με (“this people draws near me with its mouth, and with their lips they honour me”); Rahlfs lacks the mouth clause: ἐγγίζει μοι ὁ λαὸς οὗτος τοῖς χείλεσιν αὐτῶν τιμῶσίν με [T]. Matthew’s ὁ λαὸς οὗτος τοῖς χείλεσίν με τιμᾷ (“this people honours me with their lips”) is nearer Rahlfs. A further Swete–Rahlfs divergence bearing on Matthew — the fifth, after Hos 6:6, Isa 7:14, Isa 8:23 and Dan 12:3 [T]. Book usage: Isaiah is the book’s spine; Isa 29:14 (“I will hide the understanding of the prudent”) was the audit’s candidate behind 11:25 [S: audit]. OT-to-OT: Isa 29:13–14 and Isa 6:9–10 (13:14–15) together describe hearing without understanding. What it adds: the charge Isaiah laid on Jerusalem is laid on those who come “from Jerusalem” (15:1) [I], moderate–high.

Exod 20:12; 21:16 Swete (Hebrew 21:17) → 15:4 (high — quotation). Swete Exod 21:16 Ὁ κακολογῶν πατέρα αὐτοῦ ἢ μητέρα αὐτοῦ τελευτήσει θανάτῳ (“whoever speaks evil of his father or mother shall die by death”) [T]. Matthew has θανάτῳ τελευτάτω (“let him be put to death”). The verse numbering is Swete’s; confirms the overview’s row.

Internal: answers §5:8 — the heart (high, verified). Answers §13:29 — uprooting (moderate). Plants §23:16, 24 — ὁδηγοὶ τυφλοί (high, verified). Plants §23:25–26 — inside and outside (moderate–high). Plants §15:21–28 — the purity line crossed (high).

Translations. NASB95 “defiles”, ESV “defiles” for κοινόω (5 occurrences in 3 verses: 15:11, 18, 20, SBLGNT, verified). Pulpit divergence: none bearing.

Pitfall. “Jesus abolished the food laws” preached from Matthew. That is Mark’s narrator’s comment (Mark 7:19), which Matthew does not reproduce. Matthew’s point is the heart and the word of God set against human tradition. The corrective: preach 15:19 and 15:6, and leave Mark 7:19 to Mark.

Book-overview note: confirms the Isa 29:13 and Exod 21:16 rows. Extends and corrects: Isa 29:13 is a further Swete–Rahlfs divergence (Matthew nearer Rahlfs, lacking Swete’s ἐν τῷ στόματι αὐτοῦ). Add it to How Matthew uses his Scriptures. ὁδηγός (15:14 ↔︎ 23:16, 24) belongs in the Echo Table.

31. Matthew 15:21–39 — The Canaanite Woman; the God of Israel Glorified

Position. Q1: 15:1–20 moved defilement from food to the heart [T]. Q2: this unit exists here because the boundary just redrawn is now crossed: Jesus withdraws to the region of Tyre and Sidon, and a Canaanite woman’s faith is praised (15:28). He then returns to the sea, heals the crowds, and feeds four thousand. The unit pairs a Gentile at the edge of the table with a crowd fed at the table [T]/[I], high.

Structure. The Canaanite woman (15:21–28); healings on the mountain (15:29–31); the feeding of the four thousand (15:32–39) [T].

Text-first findings.

  • “Canaanite”. γυνὴ Χαναναία (“a Canaanite woman”, 15:22). Χαναναῖος (“Canaanite”) occurs only here in Matthew (SBLGNT), and in the NT only here [T], verified. Mark 7:26 calls her Ἑλληνίς, Συροφοινίκισσα (“a Greek, a Syrophoenician”) [T]. Matthew’s word is the name of the people of the land, and of Tamar and Rahab’s people in the genealogy (1:3, 5) [T]/[I], high.
  • She calls him “Son of David”. Ἐλέησόν με, κύριε υἱὸς Δαυίδ (“Have mercy on me, Lord, Son of David”, 15:22) — the cry of the blind men (9:27; 20:30–31) on a Gentile’s lips [T]. Mark has no title [T].
  • “Only to the lost sheep of the house of Israel.” Οὐκ ἀπεστάλην εἰ μὴ εἰς τὰ πρόβατα τὰ ἀπολωλότα οἴκου Ἰσραήλ (“I was sent only to the lost sheep of the house of Israel”, 15:24). The phrase πρόβατα τὰ ἀπολωλότα (“lost sheep”) occurs only at 10:6 and 15:24 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. Both sayings are Matthew’s own. The restriction is stated twice and then broken twice: here by the woman’s faith, and finally by the commission to πάντα τὰ ἔθνη (“all the nations”, 28:19) [I], high.
  • “Let it be done for you as you wish.” γενηθήτω σοι ὡς θέλεις (15:28). γενηθήτω (“let it be done”) stands at 6:10; 8:13; 9:29; 15:28; 26:42 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The prayer (6:10), three answered requests (8:13; 9:29; 15:28), Gethsemane (26:42) [T]. Two of the three answered requests are for Gentiles: the centurion (8:13) and this woman (15:28) [T]. Her faith is μεγάλη (“great”), as the centurion’s was τοσαύτη (“so great”, 8:10) [T].
  • “They glorified the God of Israel.” ἐδόξασαν τὸν θεὸν Ἰσραήλ (15:31). The phrase stands only here in Matthew (SBLGNT phrase search) [T], verified. The healed are κωφοί, τυφλοί, χωλοί, κυλλοί (“mute, blind, lame, crippled”, 15:30–31) — Isaiah’s catalogue again (cf. 11:5; Isa 35:5–6) [T]. The crowd’s confession may suggest Gentile onlookers praising Israel’s God, but the text does not say where they come from [I], uncertain–moderate.
  • The second feeding is Supper-shaped. ἔλαβεν … εὐχαριστήσας ἔκλασεν καὶ ἐδίδου (“He took … gave thanks and broke them, and started giving”, 15:36). εὐχαριστέω (“to give thanks”) occurs only at 15:36 and 26:27 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. σπλαγχνίζομαι (“to feel compassion”) stands at 9:36; 14:14; 15:32; 18:27; 20:34 [T], verified. Seven loaves and seven baskets (σπυρίς, 15:37; 16:10) against the five loaves and twelve baskets (κόφινος, 14:20; 16:9) [T]. Jesus himself will recall both numbers (16:9–10) [T].

Synoptic check. Mark 7:24–30 and 8:1–10. Matthew adds the title “Son of David”, the disciples’ “send her away” and the saying about the lost sheep (15:23–24), and “O woman, great is your faith” (15:28). He replaces Mark’s healing of one deaf man (Mark 7:31–37) with a summary of many healings on a mountain and the line “they glorified the God of Israel” (15:29–31) [T]. Luke omits the whole sequence.

Tool 11. Isa 35:5–6 → 15:30–31 (moderate–high — the catalogue). Move 1: the desert blooms and the LORD comes to save; the blind see, the deaf hear, the lame leap, the mute sing (Swete τρανὴ δὲ ἔσται γλῶσσα μογιλάλων, “the tongue of the stammerers will be clear”) [T]. Already used at 11:5; here enacted on a mountain [I].

Name freight (Tool 7c). Χαναναία. First canonical occurrence: Canaan, cursed son of Ham (Gen 9:25). Major associations: the people of the land promised to Abraham; Tamar (Gen 38) and Rahab (Josh 2) — both in Matthew’s genealogy (1:3, 5) [T]. Matthew alone uses the archaic name for a woman of the Phoenician coast [T]. The genealogy’s women are answered here (overview, Echo Table) [S], confirmed independently by the name.

Internal: answers §1:3, 5 — the Canaanite women of the genealogy (moderate–high). Answers §10:5–6 — the restriction (high, verified). Answers §8:5–13 — the second Gentile faith praised (high, verified). Plants §16:9–10 — the two feedings recalled (high). Plants §26:26–27 — the Supper-shape (high, verified). Plants §28:19 — all the nations (high).

Translations. 15:26 κυνάρια: NASB95 “dogs”, ESV “dogs”. Both lose the diminutive (“little dogs, house dogs”) [I]. Pulpit divergence: none bearing.

Difficulty. Jesus’ silence (15:23) and the “dogs” saying (15:26) trouble modern hearers. Function in context: the saying states the order of salvation-history — Israel first (10:6; 15:24) — and the woman accepts the order and claims its overflow; Jesus then praises her [I], high. Options: a test of faith; an expression of the historical priority of Israel; Jesus’ own change of mind [S]. The text supports the first two and does not require the third.

Pitfall. Making the woman a model of persistent prayer and stopping there. The point is who she is (a Canaanite), what she calls him (Son of David) and what he gives (the children’s bread to a Gentile). The corrective: preach the unit with 10:6, 15:24 and 28:19.

Book-overview note: confirms the “four women → Canaanite woman” row in the Echo Table. Extends with verified chains: γενηθήτω (6:10 → 26:42), εὐχαριστέω (15:36 ↔︎ 26:27), and “lost sheep of the house of Israel” (10:6 ↔︎ 15:24).

32. Matthew 16:1–12 — Signs and Leaven

Position. Q1: the crowds have glorified the God of Israel and been fed a second time (15:29–39) [T]. Q2: this unit exists here because, after two feedings, the leaders ask for a sign from heaven and the disciples still do not understand about bread. It is the last scene before the confession at Caesarea Philippi (16:13–20): unbelief that demands a sign and dullness that misreads one stand side by side, and the confession is what breaks through [I], high.

Structure. The demand for a sign and the refusal (16:1–4); the leaven of the Pharisees and Sadducees (16:5–12) [T]. Jesus leaves the questioners (καταλιπὼν αὐτοὺς ἀπῆλθεν, “He left them and went away”, 16:4) and turns to the disciples [T].

Text-first findings.

  • Pharisees and Sadducees together, as tempters. οἱ Φαρισαῖοι καὶ Σαδδουκαῖοι πειράζοντες (“the Pharisees and Sadducees … testing Him”, 16:1). Σαδδουκαῖος (“Sadducee”) stands at 3:7; 16:1, 6, 11, 12; 22:23, 34 (SBLGNT) [T], verified: the pair John addressed as a brood of vipers (3:7) return together. πειράζω (“to test, tempt”) occurs at 4:1, 3; 16:1; 19:3; 22:18, 35 [T], verified. After the devil, only the religious leaders “test” Jesus in Matthew. Synthetic, high for the chain.
  • The sign of Jonah again. Γενεὰ πονηρὰ καὶ μοιχαλὶς σημεῖον ἐπιζητεῖ (“an evil and adulterous generation seeks after a sign”, 16:4) repeats 12:39 almost word for word [T]. The sign of Jonah is named but not explained; the explanation was given at 12:40 [T].
  • Leaven is teaching. προσέχετε ἀπὸ τῆς ζύμης τῶν Φαρισαίων καὶ Σαδδουκαίων (“beware of the leaven of the Pharisees and Sadducees”, 16:6, 11). The narrator interprets: ἀλλὰ ἀπὸ τῆς διδαχῆς (“but of the teaching”, 16:12) [T]. ζύμη (“leaven”) occurs at 13:33 and in this unit only (16:6, 11, 12, SBLGNT) [T], verified: the kingdom’s leaven (13:33) and the leaders’ leaven (16:6) are opposites. διδαχή (“teaching”) stands at 7:28; 16:12; 22:33 — Jesus’ teaching astonishes (7:28; 22:33); theirs is to be avoided [T], verified.
  • “Do you not yet understand?” οὔπω νοεῖτε … πῶς οὐ νοεῖτε (“do you not yet understand … how is it that you do not understand?”, 16:9, 11). Jesus recalls both feedings with their separate words for “basket”: κόφινος (14:20; 16:9) and σπυρίς (15:37; 16:10) [T], verified. The rebuke ends in understanding: τότε συνῆκαν (“then they understood”, 16:12) [T]. Mark 8:21 ends on the question οὔπω συνίετε; (“do you not yet understand?”) and no answer [T].

Synoptic check. Mark 8:11–21. Matthew adds the Sadducees, the sign of Jonah (16:4), the weather sayings (16:2b–3, bracketed), the explanation that leaven is teaching, and the disciples’ understanding (16:12). He replaces Mark’s “leaven of Herod” with “of the Sadducees” and drops Mark’s “hardened heart” (Mark 8:17) [T]. The pattern matches 14:33 against Mark 6:52: in Matthew the disciples are slow but not hardened [I], high.

Tool 11. Jonah → 16:4 (high — by reference to 12:39–41). Book usage: the second and last use; Jonah is now a fixed sign [T]. No new OT citation in this unit.

Internal: answers §12:38–40 — the sign of Jonah (high, verified). Answers §13:33 — leaven reversed (high, verified). Answers §14:13–21; §15:32–39 — the feedings recalled (high, verified). Answers §4:1–3 — πειράζω (high, verified). Plants §19:3; §22:18, 35 — the testing continues (high, verified). Plants §16:13–20 — understanding given, and then revealed (moderate–high).

Translations. Text: 16:2b–3 (the red sky at evening and morning) is bracketed in NA28; it is omitted by א B Γ ƒ¹³ 579 syˢ·ᶜ sa and others [T]. Both the NASB95 and ESV exports print it in the running text [T]. Pulpit divergence: none between the versions, but the congregation will hear 16:2b–3 as part of the text; say it is doubtful before building on it.

Pitfall. Preaching “leaven” as sin in general. The narrator defines it as διδαχή (“teaching”, 16:12). The corrective: preach the warning against false teaching that demands signs and misses the sign given.

Book-overview note: confirms the Jonah row and the NA28 bracket note. Extends: the πειράζω chain (4:1, 3 → the leaders) for the Echo Table.

33. Matthew 16:13–28 — “You Are the Christ” — and “Get Behind Me” ⭐

Position. Q1: the rejection movement (11:2–16:12) has divided those who receive from those who refuse. It ends with the Pharisees and Sadducees refused every sign but Jonah’s (16:4), and with the disciples at last understanding about the leaven: τότε συνῆκαν (“then they understood”, 16:12) [T]. The question the movement kept raising — “Are you the one who is coming?” (11:3); “Can this be the Son of David?” (12:23) — is now put by Jesus himself [T]. Q2: the unit exists here because it closes one movement and opens the next. The confession (16:16) is the answer the first half of the book has been building towards. The second ἀπὸ τότε ἤρξατο (“from that time he began”, 16:21) turns the book towards Jerusalem and the cross. The two halves meet in one scene: Peter is called blessed and then called Satan, within seven verses [T]/[I].

Structure. The question at Caesarea Philippi (13–16); the blessing, the rock, the keys (17–19); the command to silence (20); the first passion prediction (21); Peter’s rebuke and Jesus’ rebuke (22–23); the cost of following (24–26); the coming of the Son of Man (27–28) [T]. The hinge is 16:21, where ἀπὸ τότε ἤρξατο (“from that time he began”) repeats 4:17 (SBLGNT: 4:17; 16:21 only, verified) [T]. The unit holds a matched pair: Σὺ εἶ ὁ χριστός (“you are the Christ”, 16:16) from Peter, and σὺ εἶ Πέτρος (“you are Peter”, 16:18) from Jesus; then σκάνδαλον εἶ ἐμοῦ (“you are a stumbling block to me”, 16:23) [T].

Text-first findings.

  • The question is about the Son of Man; the answer is “the Christ, the Son of the living God”. Τίνα λέγουσιν οἱ ἄνθρωποι εἶναι τὸν υἱὸν τοῦ ἀνθρώπου; (“who do people say that the Son of Man is?”, 16:13). Mark 8:27 asks Τίνα με (“who … that I am?”) [T]. Matthew puts the title into the question, so that Peter’s answer names who the Son of Man is: ὁ χριστὸς ὁ υἱὸς τοῦ θεοῦ τοῦ ζῶντος (“the Christ, the Son of the living God”, 16:16) [T]. θεοῦ τοῦ ζῶντος (“of the living God”) stands at 16:16 and 26:63 only, and σὺ εἶ ὁ χριστός (“you are the Christ”) likewise at 16:16 and 26:63 only (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. At the trial the high priest puts Peter’s confession to Jesus under oath, while Peter denies him in the courtyard [T]. Synthetic, verified: high for the chain.
  • Revealed, not reasoned. σὰρξ καὶ αἷμα οὐκ ἀπεκάλυψέν σοι ἀλλ’ ὁ πατήρ μου ὁ ἐν τοῖς οὐρανοῖς (“flesh and blood did not reveal this to you, but my Father who is in heaven”, 16:17) [T]. ἀποκαλύπτω (“to reveal”) stands in four verses of Matthew: 10:26; 11:25, 27; 16:17 (SBLGNT, verified). At 11:25–27 the Father ἀπεκάλυψας αὐτὰ νηπίοις (“revealed them to infants”) and no one knows the Son except the Father [T]. Peter’s confession is 11:25–27 happening: a νήπιος (“infant”) is told by the Father who the Son is [I], moderate–high.
  • The rock and the building. σὺ εἶ Πέτρος, καὶ ἐπὶ ταύτῃ τῇ πέτρᾳ οἰκοδομήσω μου τὴν ἐκκλησίαν (“you are Peter, and upon this rock I will build my church”, 16:18) [T]. πέτρα (“rock”) stands at five places in Matthew (7:24, 25; 16:18; 27:51, 60), and πέτρα with οἰκοδομέω (“to build”) only at 7:24 and 16:18 (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. The wise man ᾠκοδόμησεν … ἐπὶ τὴν πέτραν (“built … upon the rock”, 7:24), and he is the one who ἀκούει μου τοὺς λόγους τούτους καὶ ποιεῖ αὐτούς (“hears these words of mine and does them”) [T]. Jesus builds the church as the wise man builds his house [I], moderate–high. ἐκκλησία (“church”) stands at 16:18 and 18:17 (twice) only, and nowhere in Mark, Luke or John (SBLGNT, verified; positive control: the Matthew search returns both verses) [T]. “My” stands before it: μου τὴν ἐκκλησίαν (“my church”) [T].
  • The gates of Hades are death’s gates. πύλαι ᾅδου οὐ κατισχύσουσιν αὐτῆς (“the gates of Hades will not overpower it”, 16:18). ᾅδης (“Hades”) stands at 11:23 and 16:18 only (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. In the Greek Old Testament ἐν πύλαις ᾅδου (“in the gates of Hades”) is Hezekiah’s phrase for dying (Isa 38:10, Swete and Rahlfs), and Wis 16:13 says God κατάγεις εἰς πύλας ᾅδου καὶ ἀνάγεις (“leads down to the gates of Hades and brings up again”) [T]. The promise is that death will not hold the church, which fits the passion prediction three verses later: ἀποκτανθῆναι καὶ τῇ τρίτῃ ἡμέρᾳ ἐγερθῆναι (“be killed and be raised on the third day”, 16:21) [I], moderate–high.
  • Binding and loosing — on earth, already in heaven. ὃ ἐὰν δήσῃς ἐπὶ τῆς γῆς ἔσται δεδεμένον ἐν τοῖς οὐρανοῖς (“whatever you bind on earth shall have been bound in heaven”, 16:19). The phrase ἐπὶ τῆς γῆς ἔσται (“on earth shall have been”) occurs at 16:19 and 18:18 only, twice in each verse (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. In 16:19 σοι (“to you”) is singular; in 18:18 the verbs are plural (δήσητε, “you [pl.] bind”) [T]. ἔσται δεδεμένον is a future perfect periphrastic: the binding in heaven stands already done [T] for the form, [I] for the force, moderate. The keys (κλεῖδας, “keys”) are a hapax in Matthew (SBLGNT, verified) — see Tool 11 on Isa 22:22.
  • The rock becomes a stumbling stone. Πέτρος (“Peter”) rebukes Jesus — ἤρξατο ἐπιτιμᾶν αὐτῷ (“began to rebuke him”, 16:22) — and Jesus answers Ὕπαγε ὀπίσω μου, Σατανᾶ· σκάνδαλον εἶ ἐμοῦ (“get behind me, Satan; you are a stumbling block to me”, 16:23) [T]. σκάνδαλον εἶ ἐμοῦ is Matthew’s own; Mark 8:33 lacks it [T]. σκάνδαλον (“stumbling block”) stands at 13:41, 16:23 and 18:7 (three times) only (SBLGNT, verified). In 13:41 the Son of Man’s angels gather πάντα τὰ σκάνδαλα (“all stumbling blocks”) out of his kingdom, and in 18:7 Jesus says οὐαί … δι’ οὗ τὸ σκάνδαλον ἔρχεται (“woe … through whom the stumbling block comes”) [T]. The man named as the rock is, a moment later, a stone to trip over. Synthetic: moderate.
  • Satan again, and “behind me” again. Ὕπαγε, Σατανᾶ (“go, Satan”, 4:10) was spoken in the wilderness to the one who offered the kingdoms without the cross. Σατανᾶς (“Satan”) stands at 4:10; 12:26 (twice); 16:23 only (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. ὀπίσω μου (“behind me”) stands at 3:11; 4:19; 10:38; 16:23; 16:24 (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. The phrase runs from John’s ὁ … ὀπίσω μου ἐρχόμενος (“he who is coming after me”, 3:11), through the call Δεῦτε ὀπίσω μου (“follow me”, 4:19), to the rebuke ὀπίσω μου (“behind me”, 16:23) and the condition ὀπίσω μου ἐλθεῖν (“to come after me”, 16:24) [T]. Peter is sent back to the place of a disciple, not out of it [I], moderate–high.
  • The passion prediction names the city. δεῖ αὐτὸν εἰς Ἱεροσόλυμα ἀπελθεῖν (“he must go to Jerusalem”, 16:21) is Matthew’s addition; Mark 8:31 does not name the city [T]. Ἱεροσόλυμα (“Jerusalem”) stands in 11 verses (SBLGNT, verified). It was the city “troubled” with Herod at the start (2:3) and the source of the scribes who came to accuse (15:1) [T]. From here it is the destination (20:17–18; 21:1, 10) [T].
  • The cross is the price of the ψυχή. ψυχή (“soul, life”) stands four times in 16:25–26 (SBLGNT, verified: 16:25×2; 16:26×2). τί δώσει ἄνθρωπος ἀντάλλαγμα τῆς ψυχῆς αὐτοῦ; (“what will a man give in exchange for his soul?”, 16:26) [T]. ἀντάλλαγμα (“exchange”) is a hapax in Matthew (SBLGNT, verified). The question is answered in 20:28, where the Son of Man gives τὴν ψυχὴν αὐτοῦ λύτρον ἀντὶ πολλῶν (“his life a ransom for many”) [T]. Synthetic: moderate–high. The verb ἀπαρνέομαι (“to deny”) stands at 16:24 and then only at 26:34, 35, 75, of Peter (SBLGNT, verified) [T].

Synoptic check. Matthew follows Mark 8:27–9:1 in order and most wording [T]. He adds “the Son of Man” to the question (16:13), “Jeremiah” to the list (16:14), and “the Son of the living God” to the confession (16:16) [T]. Ἰερεμίας (“Jeremiah”) is named only in Matthew in the whole NT (2:17; 16:14; 27:9; SBLGNT, verified). The whole of 16:17–19 is Matthew’s alone [T]. He adds “to Jerusalem” (16:21) and σκάνδαλον εἶ ἐμοῦ (“you are a stumbling block to me”, 16:23) [T]. He changes Mark’s μετὰ τρεῖς ἡμέρας ἀναστῆναι (“after three days rise”) to τῇ τρίτῃ ἡμέρᾳ ἐγερθῆναι (“be raised on the third day”), as Luke 9:22 also does — a minor agreement [T]. He drops Mark 8:38 (“whoever is ashamed of me”) and puts in its place a judgement saying, ἀποδώσει ἑκάστῳ κατὰ τὴν πρᾶξιν αὐτοῦ (“he will repay each according to his deed”, 16:27) [T]. Mark’s “his angels” is “the holy angels”; Matthew’s are the Son of Man’s own: μετὰ τῶν ἀγγέλων αὐτοῦ (“with his angels”, 16:27; compare 13:41; 24:31) [T]. At 16:28 Mark’s “the kingdom of God come with power” becomes τὸν υἱὸν τοῦ ἀνθρώπου ἐρχόμενον ἐν τῇ βασιλείᾳ αὐτοῦ (“the Son of Man coming in his kingdom”) [T].

Tool 11.

Ps 62:13 (Swete 61:13); Prov 24:12 → 16:27 (moderate–high). Source context: Ps 62 is David’s psalm of waiting on God alone, “my rock and my salvation” (צוּרִי, “my rock”, 62:3, 7 WLC). It ends: “power belongs to God; and to you, O Lord, belongs steadfast love, for you repay each according to his work” (62:12–13) [T]. Swete and Rahlfs Ps 61:13: σὺ ἀποδώσεις ἑκάστῳ κατὰ τὰ ἔργα αὐτοῦ (“you will repay each according to his works”); WLC תְשַׁלֵּם לְאִישׁ כְּמַעֲשֵׂהוּ (“you repay a man according to his work”) [T]. Prov 24:12 asks the one who says “we did not know” whether the one who weighs hearts does not know, and ends with the same line (Swete ἀποδίδωσιν ἑκάστῳ κατὰ τὰ ἔργα αὐτοῦ, “he repays each according to his works”) [T]. Book usage: the psalm is not used elsewhere in Matthew. OT-to-OT: Psalms and Proverbs share the line, so it is a proverbial statement of God’s justice in the Ketuvim [T]. Divergence: Matthew has τὴν πρᾶξιν (“the deed”), not τὰ ἔργα (“the works”). κατὰ τὰς πράξεις (“according to the deeds”) is Sir 32:24 Swete only; κατὰ τὴν πρᾶξιν does not occur in Swete (positive control: κατὰ τὰς πράξεις returns Sir 32:24) [T]. This is the NT’s own Greek. What it adds: the line that in the psalm belongs to God (σύ, “you”) is given to the Son of Man, who comes ἐν τῇ δόξῃ τοῦ πατρὸς αὐτοῦ (“in the glory of his Father”). The judgement of God is put in his hands [I], moderate–high.

Isa 22:22 → 16:19 (moderate — not in the overview’s map). Source context: an oracle against Shebna, the steward “over the house”, who carved himself a tomb in the rock (22:15–16). The LORD calls “my servant Eliakim” (22:20), clothes him with Shebna’s robe, and gives him “the government” (22:21). Then WLC: וְנָתַתִּי מַפְתֵּחַ בֵּית־דָּוִד עַל־שִׁכְמוֹ וּפָתַח וְאֵין סֹגֵר (“and I will set the key of the house of David on his shoulder; he shall open and none shall shut”, 22:22) [T]. The oracle ends with the peg torn down (22:25) [T]. Divergence, and it matters: neither Greek text has the key. Swete reads καὶ δώσω τὴν δόξαν Δαυεὶδ αὐτῷ (“and I will give him the glory of David”), with the opening and shutting; Rahlfs has only the glory, without the opening and shutting [T]. מַפְתֵּחַ (“key”, H4668) is gated at Isa 22:22, its only occurrence in Isaiah (WLC, find.py, exit 0) [T]. So if Matthew is drawing on Isa 22:22, he is drawing on the Hebrew, not on either Greek text [I]. Book usage: Isaiah is Matthew’s spine, but Isa 22 is not used elsewhere in the book. OT-to-OT: none in this passage. What it adds: a steward’s authority over the king’s house, given by God, and given to a “servant” [I]. The verbs differ: Isaiah has “open/shut”, Matthew “bind/loose”. That keeps the rating moderate. Rev 3:7 cites Isa 22:22 with κλεῖν Δαυίδ (“key of David”) [T], so the reading of this text as a “key” was alive in the NT.

Isa 38:10 → 16:18 (moderate — the phrase, not a citation). Move 1 only: Hezekiah’s psalm after his recovery. “In the middle of my days I am to go into the gates of Sheol” (WLC בְּשַׁעֲרֵי שְׁאוֹל, “in the gates of Sheol”); Swete and Rahlfs ἐν πύλαις ᾅδου (“in the gates of Hades”) [T]. The phrase names death as a city with gates that close behind the dead. Book usage: none. OT-to-OT: no pairing in this passage.

Internal: answers §4:10 — Σατανᾶς to the tempter and to Peter (high, verified). Answers §4:17 — the second ἀπὸ τότε ἤρξατο (high, verified). Answers §4:19; §3:11 — ὀπίσω μου (high, verified). Answers §7:24–25 — the house on the rock (moderate–high, verified for the words). Answers §11:25–27 — revelation by the Father to infants (moderate–high, verified). Answers §16:4 — Σίμων Βαριωνᾶ (“Simon son of Jonah”, 16:17) is named five verses after τὸ σημεῖον Ἰωνᾶ (“the sign of Jonah”, 16:4). Ἰωνᾶς (“Jonah”) stands in 12:39–41 and 16:4; Βαριωνᾶ is a separate lemma, at 16:17 only (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. Whether the adjacency is meant is uncertain. Plants §18:17–18 — ἐκκλησία and binding/loosing (high, verified). Plants §18:7 — σκάνδαλον (moderate). Plants §20:28 — the price of a ψυχή (moderate–high, synthetic). Plants §26:34–35, 63–75 — the living God and Peter’s denial (high, verified). Plants §17:1 — “some standing here” (16:28) and the next verse, “after six days” (see Difficulty). Opening material checked.

Translations. At 16:18 the NASB95 has “the gates of Hades” and the ESV “the gates of hell” for πύλαι ᾅδου (“gates of Hades”). The ESV’s “hell” points to the place of punishment, where the Greek and Isa 38:10 point to death; and it hides that the same word is “Hades” at 11:23 in the ESV [T]. At 16:19 the NASB95 renders the periphrastic perfect “shall have been bound … shall have been loosed”; the ESV “shall be bound … shall be loosed” [T]. At 16:23 the NASB95 has “a stumbling block”, the ESV “a hindrance”, for σκάνδαλον (“stumbling block”) [T]. Pulpit divergence: three places. The congregation will hear “hell”, not death, at 16:18. They will hear earth’s binding as if heaven then follows, where the NASB95 keeps heaven first. And they will hear “hindrance”, which cuts Peter off from the σκάνδαλον chain of 13:41 and 18:7.

Difficulty. 16:28: “some of those standing here will not taste death until they see the Son of Man coming in his kingdom”. Options: (a) the transfiguration, which follows at once (17:1, “after six days”), and which three of “those standing here” see; (b) the resurrection and the commission, where all authority is given (28:18); (c) the judgement on Jerusalem (24:34, “this generation will not pass away”); (d) the final coming. Matthew’s change from Mark (“the Son of Man coming”, not “the kingdom of God come”) makes (d) look closer, but the placing next to 17:1 makes (a) look closer [I]. Compare 10:23. This should be taken to Logos before it is preached.

Pitfall. “Peter got the answer right, so he was rewarded with the keys.” The text says the answer came from the Father, not from Peter (16:17). And the same man who receives the keys is called Satan and a stumbling block six verses later, for thinking τὰ τῶν ἀνθρώπων (“the things of men”, 16:23) [T]. The corrective: preach 16:13–28 as one unit. The confession is a gift, the church is Jesus’ building (“I will build my church”), and the first lesson after the confession is the cross — for Jesus first, then for anyone who would come after him.

Book-overview note: Confirms the overview’s hinge (4:17; 16:21), the 4:10 / 16:23 echo, the ὀπίσω μου chain, the πέτρα chain, the θεοῦ τοῦ ζῶντος chain and Trap 5. Extends in four places: (1) Isa 22:22 is missing from the Intertextual Map; it should go in under the Latter Prophets at moderate, with the note that the “key” is in the Hebrew and in neither Greek text. (2) Isa 38:10 for πύλαι ᾅδου, moderate, to support the “Where the English hides the Greek” row on “gates of hell”. (3) ἀποκαλύπτω (11:25, 27; 16:17) as an echo-table row (moderate–high). (4) ἀντάλλαγμα τῆς ψυχῆς (16:26) → λύτρον ἀντὶ πολλῶν (20:28) as a candidate echo row.

34. Matthew 17:1–13 — Transfigured; Elijah Has Come ⭐

Position. Q1: the first passion prediction (16:21) and the call to take up the cross (16:24) end with a promise: some standing there will see “the Son of Man coming in his kingdom” (16:28) [T]. The very next words are μεθ’ ἡμέρας ἕξ (“after six days”, 17:1) [T]. Q2: the unit exists here because the cross has just been announced and refused (16:22). The Father’s voice now confirms the one who must suffer, in the words of the baptism (3:17), and adds ἀκούετε αὐτοῦ (“hear him”, 17:5) — hear him, that is, on the subject Peter would not hear [I], high. The descent then ties the glory back to suffering: Elijah has come and suffered, “so also the Son of Man” (17:12) [T].

Structure. The ascent (1); the transformation (2); Moses and Elijah (3); Peter’s offer (4); the cloud and the Voice (5); the fall and the raising (6–8); the descent and the command (9); the Elijah question (10–13) [T]. 17:6–8 is a small ring: they fall on their faces (6), Jesus touches them and says “rise” (7), they lift their eyes and see Jesus only (8) [T].

Text-first findings.

  • A high mountain, twice. ἀναφέρει αὐτοὺς εἰς ὄρος ὑψηλόν (“he brings them up on a high mountain”, 17:1). ὄρος ὑψηλόν (“high mountain”) stands at 4:8 and 17:1 only, and ὑψηλός (“high”) likewise (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. At 4:8 the devil took Jesus to a high mountain and showed him “all the kingdoms of the world and their glory” [T]. Here, after the cross has been accepted (16:21), the glory is shown on Jesus himself [I], moderate–high. Both scenes open with παραλαμβάνει (“takes along”, 4:8; 17:1) [T].
  • The face, like the sun. ἔλαμψεν τὸ πρόσωπον αὐτοῦ ὡς ὁ ἥλιος (“his face shone like the sun”, 17:2) is Matthew’s own; Mark 9:2–3 has the clothes only [T]. ὡς ὁ ἥλιος (“like the sun”) stands at 13:43 and 17:2 only (SBLGNT, verified). At 13:43 οἱ δίκαιοι ἐκλάμψουσιν ὡς ὁ ἥλιος (“the righteous will shine out like the sun”) [T]. The glory promised to the righteous is first seen on Jesus [I], moderate. His clothes λευκὰ ὡς τὸ φῶς (“white as the light”); λευκός (“white”) returns at 28:3 of the angel’s clothing, λευκὸν ὡς χιών (“white as snow”) [T].
  • The Voice repeats the baptism exactly, then adds one command. Οὗτός ἐστιν ὁ υἱός μου ὁ ἀγαπητός, ἐν ᾧ εὐδόκησα (“this is my beloved Son, with whom I am well pleased”, 17:5). The whole sentence stands at 3:17 and 17:5 only (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. Mark 9:7 lacks ἐν ᾧ εὐδόκησα (“with whom I am well pleased”); Matthew makes the two voices identical [T]. ἀγαπητός (“beloved”) and εὐδοκέω (“to be well pleased”) each stand at 3:17, 12:18 and 17:5 only (SBLGNT, verified), so the Son of the Voice is the Servant of 12:18 [T]/[I], high. What the second voice adds is ἀκούετε αὐτοῦ (“hear him”) [T].
