  • Fear, and Jesus comes to them. ἔπεσαν ἐπὶ πρόσωπον αὐτῶν καὶ ἐφοβήθησαν σφόδρα (“they fell on their faces and were greatly afraid”, 17:6). καὶ προσῆλθεν ὁ Ἰησοῦς καὶ ἁψάμενος αὐτῶν εἶπεν· Ἐγέρθητε καὶ μὴ φοβεῖσθε (“and Jesus came to them and, touching them, said, ‘Rise, and do not be afraid’”, 17:7). All of 17:6–7 is Matthew’s own [T]. προσέρχομαι (“to come to”) stands in 50 verses of Matthew (SBLGNT), and Jesus is its subject only at 17:7 and 28:18 (each verse read, verified) [T]. ἐφοβήθησαν σφόδρα (“they were greatly afraid”) stands at 17:6 and 27:54 only (SBLGNT, verified), and at 27:54 the centurion’s fear ends in Ἀληθῶς θεοῦ υἱὸς ἦν οὗτος (“truly this was God’s Son”) [T]. μὴ φοβεῖσθε (“do not be afraid”) stands at 10:28; 14:27; 17:7; 28:5, 10 (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. The mountain of glory and the mountain of commission are framed alike: fear or doubt, then Jesus comes to them and speaks (17:6–7; 28:17–18) [I]; synthetic, high for the chain.
  • “Rise” — the resurrection verb. Ἐγέρθητε (“rise”, 17:7) is the passive of ἐγείρω (“to raise”). Two verses later the same verb is used of the Son of Man: ἕως οὗ ὁ υἱὸς τοῦ ἀνθρώπου ἐκ νεκρῶν ἐγερθῇ (“until the Son of Man is raised from the dead”, 17:9), and again at 17:23 (ἐγερθήσεται, “he will be raised”) [T]. The disciples, lying as if dead with fear, are “raised” by the one who will be raised [I], moderate.
  • Moses first. ὤφθη αὐτοῖς Μωϋσῆς καὶ Ἠλίας (“Moses and Elijah appeared to them”, 17:3). Mark 9:4 has Ἠλίας σὺν Μωϋσεῖ (“Elijah with Moses”) [T]. Matthew puts Moses first in 17:3 and 17:4 [T]. This is the order of the last words of the Prophets in the Hebrew: “Remember the law of Moses my servant … Behold, I will send you Elijah the prophet” (Mal 3:22–23 WLC). Swete and Rahlfs reverse it, putting Elijah first (Mal 4:4–6 Swete; 3:22–24 Rahlfs) [T]. See Tool 11.
  • “Elijah has already come” — and the disciples understand. Ἠλίας μὲν ἔρχεται καὶ ἀποκαταστήσει πάντα· λέγω δὲ ὑμῖν ὅτι Ἠλίας ἤδη ἦλθεν (“Elijah is coming and will restore all things; but I say to you that Elijah already came”, 17:11–12) [T]. οὐκ ἐπέγνωσαν αὐτὸν ἀλλὰ ἐποίησαν ἐν αὐτῷ ὅσα ἠθέλησαν (“they did not recognise him, but did to him whatever they wished”, 17:12) looks back to Herod, θέλων αὐτὸν ἀποκτεῖναι (“wanting to kill him”, 14:5) [T]. Then the narrator: τότε συνῆκαν οἱ μαθηταί (“then the disciples understood”, 17:13). τότε συνῆκαν (“then they understood”) stands at 16:12 and 17:13 only (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. Mark has neither verse; at Mark 9:10 the disciples are still “questioning” [T]. In Matthew the disciples understand, twice in a row [T].
  • John’s fate is the Son of Man’s. οὕτως καὶ ὁ υἱὸς τοῦ ἀνθρώπου μέλλει πάσχειν ὑπ’ αὐτῶν (“so also the Son of Man is going to suffer at their hands”, 17:12). πάσχω (“to suffer”) stands at 16:21; 17:12; 17:15; 27:19 (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. Matthew moves Mark’s “how is it written of the Son of Man that he should suffer?” (Mark 9:12b) to the end of the saying and drops Mark’s “as it is written of him” (Mark 9:13) [T]. The forerunner goes before in death as well as in preaching [I], high.

Synoptic check. Matthew follows Mark 9:2–13 in order [T]. He adds the shining face (17:2), “with whom I am well pleased” (17:5), the fall, the fear, Jesus’ approach and touch, and “rise, do not be afraid” (17:6–7), the word ὅραμα (“vision”, 17:9), and the disciples’ understanding (17:13) [T]. He changes Peter’s Ῥαββί (“Rabbi”, Mark 9:5) to Κύριε (“Lord”, 17:4), and Mark’s ποιήσωμεν (“let us make”) to ποιήσω (“I will make”), with εἰ θέλεις (“if you wish”) [T]. He drops Mark 9:6, “he did not know what to answer”, and Mark 9:10, the disciples’ questioning about rising from the dead [T]. He puts Moses before Elijah [T]. He changes Mark’s present ἀποκαθιστάνει (“restores”, Mark 9:12) to the future ἀποκαταστήσει (“will restore”, 17:11), which is the form of Mal 4:5 Swete / 3:23 Rahlfs [T]. Luke 9:29 also changes the face (τὸ εἶδος τοῦ προσώπου αὐτοῦ ἕτερον, “the appearance of his face became different”), but not with Matthew’s words [T].

Tool 11.

Deut 18:15; Exod 24:16; 34:29–30; 40:29 → 17:1–6 (moderate–high) [S: audit]. The audit’s verdict stands: the two words ἀκούετε αὐτοῦ (“hear him”) come from Mark 9:7, and the case rests on the Sinai setting, which Matthew strengthens (17:1 six days; 17:2 the face; 17:5 overshadowing; 17:6 fear). Text-first extension: the next verse of the source, Deut 18:16, completes the scene. The prophet like Moses is promised κατὰ πάντα ὅσα ᾐτήσω … ἐν Χωρὴβ τῇ ἡμέρᾳ τῆς ἐκκλησίας λέγοντες· Οὐ προσθήσομεν ἀκοῦσαι τὴν φωνὴν Κυρίου (“according to all that you asked … at Horeb on the day of the assembly, saying, ‘Let us not hear again the voice of the LORD’”) (Swete and Rahlfs; WLC בְּיוֹם הַקָּהָל, “on the day of the assembly”) [T]. The people could not bear the voice; God promised a prophet they could hear. At 17:5–6 the disciples hear the voice from the cloud and fall in fear, and the voice points them to the one they can hear [I], moderate–high. The source verse also has ἐκκλησία (“assembly”) — the word of 16:18, six days earlier [T]; that link is uncertain.

Mal 3:22–24 (Swete 4:4–6; Rahlfs 3:22–24) → 17:3, 10–12 (high). Source context: the last words of the Book of the Twelve. In the Hebrew order (WLC): “Remember the law of Moses my servant, which I commanded him at Horeb for all Israel” (3:22); “Behold, I will send you Elijah the prophet before the great and awesome day of the LORD comes” (3:23); “and he will turn the hearts of fathers to sons … lest I come and strike the land with a ban” (3:24) [T]. Both Greek texts put Elijah first and Moses last: Swete Mal 4:4 Elijah, 4:5 ὃς ἀποκαταστήσει καρδίαν πατρὸς πρὸς υἱόν (“who will restore the heart of a father to a son”), 4:6 “remember the law of Moses”; Rahlfs the same order at 3:22–24 [T]. Book usage: Mal 3:1 was cited of John at 11:10, and 11:14 said αὐτός ἐστιν Ἠλίας ὁ μέλλων ἔρχεσθαι (“he is Elijah who is to come”) [T]. 17:10–13 confirms 11:14 in Jesus’ own words and adds John’s death. OT-to-OT: Malachi’s two names, Moses and Elijah, are the two men who met God on Horeb (Exod 33:18–23; 1 Kgs 19:8–13), and Malachi names Horeb in the same verse (3:22 WLC) [T]. Divergence: ἀποκαταστήσει (“he will restore”) is the Greek’s own (Swete and Rahlfs agree); the Hebrew has וְהֵשִׁיב (“and he will turn back”) [T]. Matthew’s verb follows the Greek (Mark’s tense does not) and his order of the two names follows the Hebrew [T]/[I]. The order alone is uncertain as design. What it adds: the two names at the close of the Prophets stand on the mountain beside Jesus. And “the great and awesome day of the LORD” that Elijah precedes is, in this unit, the suffering of the Son of Man (17:12) [I], moderate–high.

Exod 24:13, 15 → 17:1 (uncertain). Move 1 only. In the Greek of Exod 24:15, ἀνέβη Μωυσῆς καὶ Ἰησοῦς εἰς τὸ ὄρος (“Moses and Joshua went up the mountain”; Swete and Rahlfs), and the cloud covers the mountain; the WLC has Moses alone at 24:15 (Joshua is with him at 24:13 in both) [T]. A Greek reader hears Ἰησοῦς go up the mountain of the six days with Moses. No more than that can be claimed.

Internal: answers §16:28 — “some standing here” (moderate; see unit 33, Difficulty). Answers §4:8 — ὄρος ὑψηλόν (high, verified). Answers §3:17 — the Voice verbatim (high, verified). Answers §11:14 — Elijah is John (high). Answers §14:3–12 — “whatever they wished” (high). Answers §13:43 — “like the sun” (moderate, verified). Answers §16:12 — τότε συνῆκαν (high, verified). Plants §28:16–18 — Jesus comes to them; mountain; fear or doubt (high, verified for προσέρχομαι). Plants §27:54 — ἐφοβήθησαν σφόδρα and “God’s Son” (moderate–high, verified). Plants §28:3 — white clothing (moderate). Opening material checked: 3:17 and 4:8 above.

Translations. At 17:7 the NASB95 has “Get up, and do not be afraid” and the ESV “Rise, and have no fear” for Ἐγέρθητε καὶ μὴ φοβεῖσθε. At 17:9 the NASB95 has “until the Son of Man has risen from the dead” and the ESV “until the Son of Man is raised from the dead” for ἐγερθῇ (“is raised”) [T]. The ESV keeps the passive and lets “rise … raised” sound together; the NASB95 loses both. At 17:4 the NASB95 has “tabernacles” and the ESV “tents” for σκηνάς (“tents, tabernacles”). Pulpit divergence: here the pulpit text serves the finding better. The congregation will hear “Rise” and “raised” two verses apart.

Difficulty. 17:11–12: “Elijah is coming and will restore all things”, then “Elijah already came”. Options: (a) 17:11 quotes the scribes’ teaching (17:10) and 17:12 corrects it; (b) both stand: John came as Elijah, and a further restoration is still to come; (c) “restore all things” is fulfilled in John’s call to repentance, and his death is how it was received. The future tense of 17:11 is Malachi’s (Swete and Rahlfs), which tells for (a) or (b) [I]. Take to Logos before preaching.

Pitfall. “Let us build booths and stay on the mountain”: preaching the transfiguration as a model for mountain-top spiritual experience. Peter’s offer is interrupted mid-sentence by the Voice (ἔτι αὐτοῦ λαλοῦντος, “while he was still speaking”, 17:5), and the scene ends with Jesus only and a descent into talk of suffering (17:8–12) [T]. The corrective: preach the glory as the Father’s confirmation of the Son who will suffer, and the command “hear him” as hearing him on the cross of 16:21–24.

Book-overview note: Confirms the merged Deut 18:15 / Exod 24 / 34 / 40 row, the Mal 3:23–24 row, the 3:17 / 12:18 / 17:5 echo and the προσέρχομαι frame (17:7; 28:18), all reached from the text first. Extends: (1) Deut 18:16 (“on the day of the assembly … let us not hear the voice”) belongs in the Deut 18:15 row as the verse that explains why the voice points to someone the disciples can hear (moderate–high). (2) The Mountain frame (Arc Map) can add that ὄρος ὑψηλόν (“high mountain”) stands at 4:8 and 17:1 only (verified). (3) Moses before Elijah (17:3, 4) is Matthew’s change to Mark and follows the Hebrew order of Mal 3:22–23 against both Greek texts; add it to the Mal row as uncertain. (4) τότε συνῆκαν (16:12; 17:13 only) — a disciple-understanding pair that the overview’s “little faith” material could use. (5) The English table could add 17:7, 9 (ἐγείρω), where the ESV is closer than the NASB95.

35. Matthew 17:14–27 — Little Faith; the Temple Tax

Position. Q1: the Voice on the mountain called Jesus “my beloved Son” (17:5), and the descent tied his glory to his suffering (17:12) [T]. Q2: the unit exists here to bring the Son down into the plain and to show what his sonship means there. Below the mountain the disciples cannot heal (17:16, 19). The second passion prediction follows (17:22–23). Then the Son, and those who are sons with him, are said to be “free” from the temple levy, and he pays it anyway, “for me and you” (17:26–27) [T]/[I].

Structure. The boy and the disciples’ failure (14–18); the private question and the answer about faith (19–20); [17:21 is not in NA28 or SBLGNT]; the second passion prediction (22–23); the temple tax and the coin in the fish (24–27) [T]. The unit is joined by sonship: τὸν υἱόν (“my son”, 17:15), ὁ υἱὸς τοῦ ἀνθρώπου (“the Son of Man”, 17:22), and οἱ υἱοί (“the sons”, 17:25–26) [T].

Text-first findings.

  • “How long shall I be with you?” Ὦ γενεὰ ἄπιστος καὶ διεστραμμένη, ἕως πότε μεθ’ ὑμῶν ἔσομαι; (“O unbelieving and perverted generation, how long shall I be with you?”, 17:17) [T]. Mark 9:19 has πρὸς ὑμᾶς (“with/toward you”); Matthew has μεθ’ ὑμῶν (“with you”) [T]. μεθ’ ὑμῶν (“with you”) stands at 17:17; 26:29; 28:20, and μεθ’ ἡμῶν (“with us”) at 1:23 only (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. The book’s Immanuel thread (1:23 → 28:20) passes through a cry of exasperation, and the last word answers it: ἐγὼ μεθ’ ὑμῶν εἰμι πάσας τὰς ἡμέρας (“I am with you all the days”, 28:20) [T]. Synthetic, verified: high for the chain.
  • The disciples’ failure, named. οὐκ ἠδυνήθησαν αὐτὸν θεραπεῦσαι (“they could not heal him”, 17:16), and Διὰ τί ἡμεῖς οὐκ ἠδυνήθημεν ἐκβαλεῖν αὐτό; (“why could we not cast it out?”, 17:19) [T]. The answer: Διὰ τὴν ὀλιγοπιστίαν ὑμῶν (“because of your littleness of faith”, 17:20) [T]. ὀλιγοπιστία (“littleness of faith”) stands only here in Matthew, and ὀλιγόπιστος (“of little faith”) at 6:30; 8:26; 14:31; 16:8 (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. Variant: the Majority text reads ἀπιστίαν (“unbelief”) here, with C D K L W; NA28 reads ὀλιγοπιστίαν with א B Θ ƒ¹·¹³ (NA28 apparatus, verified) [T]. The “little faith” chain depends on the critical text at this one point.
  • Mustard seed and mountain. ἐὰν ἔχητε πίστιν ὡς κόκκον σινάπεως, ἐρεῖτε τῷ ὄρει τούτῳ (“if you have faith like a mustard seed, you will say to this mountain”, 17:20) [T]. κόκκος (“seed”) and σίναπι (“mustard”) stand at 13:31 and 17:20 only (SBLGNT, verified). The seed of the kingdom parable becomes the measure of faith: smallness is not the problem [I], moderate–high. “This mountain” is said just after the descent from the mountain of 17:1–9 [T]. οὐδὲν ἀδυνατήσει ὑμῖν (“nothing will be impossible for you”, 17:20); ἀδυνατέω (“to be impossible”) is a hapax, and its adjective returns at 19:26, παρὰ δὲ θεῷ πάντα δυνατά (“with God all things are possible”) [T].
  • The second prediction: “handed over”. Μέλλει ὁ υἱὸς τοῦ ἀνθρώπου παραδίδοσθαι εἰς χεῖρας ἀνθρώπων (“the Son of Man is going to be handed over into the hands of men”, 17:22) [T]. εἰς χεῖρας (“into the hands”) stands at 17:22 and 26:45 only (SBLGNT, verified), where it is fulfilled: παραδίδοται εἰς χεῖρας ἁμαρτωλῶν (“is being handed over into the hands of sinners”) [T]. The disciples ἐλυπήθησαν σφόδρα (“were deeply grieved”, 17:23). Mark 9:32 has “they did not understand … and were afraid to ask”; in Matthew they understand enough to grieve [T]. ἐλυπήθησαν σφόδρα (“were deeply grieved”) stands at 17:23 and 18:31 only (SBLGNT, verified); λυπούμενοι σφόδρα (“being deeply grieved”) at 26:22 [T].
  • “Then the sons are free.” The collectors of τὰ δίδραχμα (“the two-drachma tax”, 17:24) come to Peter. Jesus asks: οἱ βασιλεῖς τῆς γῆς ἀπὸ τίνων λαμβάνουσιν τέλη ἢ κῆνσον; ἀπὸ τῶν υἱῶν αὐτῶν ἢ ἀπὸ τῶν ἀλλοτρίων; (“from whom do the kings of the earth collect customs or poll-tax — from their sons or from strangers?”, 17:25) [T]. Ἄρα γε ἐλεύθεροί εἰσιν οἱ υἱοί (“then the sons are free”, 17:26) [T]. The argument assumes that the tax is God’s, and that Jesus is the King’s Son [I], high. It follows the Voice of 17:5 within one chapter [T]. The plural οἱ υἱοί (“the sons”) includes Peter [T].
  • “For me and you.” ἵνα δὲ μὴ σκανδαλίσωμεν αὐτούς (“but so that we do not cause them to stumble”, 17:27) [T]. The coin pays ἀντὶ ἐμοῦ καὶ σοῦ (“for me and you”). ἀντί (“for, in place of”) stands at 2:22; 5:38 (twice); 17:27; 20:28 only (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. Its next use is λύτρον ἀντὶ πολλῶν (“a ransom for many”, 20:28) [T]. See Tool 11: the levy itself is the Torah’s ransom money. Synthetic: moderate–high.
  • Τί σοι δοκεῖ … κῆνσον. Jesus’ question to Peter (Τί σοι δοκεῖ, “what do you think?”, 17:25) and the word κῆνσος (“poll-tax”) return together when the Pharisees ask him about Caesar: Τί σοι δοκεῖ; ἔξεστιν δοῦναι κῆνσον Καίσαρι ἢ οὔ; (“what do you think? Is it lawful to give a poll-tax to Caesar or not?”, 22:17) [T]. Τί σοι δοκεῖ stands at 17:25 and 22:17 only, and κῆνσος at 17:25; 22:17, 19 only (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. The two tax questions belong together: one about God’s house, one about Caesar [I], moderate–high.

Synoptic check. The healing follows Mark 9:14–29 in a much shorter form [T]. Matthew drops the scribes’ dispute, the father’s “I believe; help my unbelief” (Mark 9:24) and the answer “this kind comes out only by prayer” (Mark 9:29) [T]. He adds καὶ διεστραμμένη (“and perverted”, 17:17), as Luke 9:41 also does — a minor agreement (see the audit) [T]. He adds ὀλιγοπιστία (“littleness of faith”) and the mustard-seed saying (17:20), which Luke has in another setting (Luke 17:6, of a mulberry tree) and Mark in another form at 11:23 (of “this mountain”) [T]. The second prediction follows Mark 9:31 but replaces “they did not understand” with “they were deeply grieved” (17:23) [T]. The temple tax and the coin (17:24–27) are Matthew’s alone [T].

Tool 11.

Deut 32:5, 20 → 17:17 (high) [S: audit]. The audit’s verdict stands: the Song of Moses supplies both adjectives — γενεὰ σκολιὰ καὶ διεστραμμένη (“a crooked and perverse generation”, 32:5) and γενεὰ ἐξεστραμμένη … υἱοὶ οἷς οὐκ ἔστιν πίστις (“a perverted generation … sons in whom there is no faith”, 32:20; Swete) [T]. Text-first extension: the adjacency check. The previous unit staged a Sinai ascent (17:1–8; Exod 24; 34). This unit is the descent, and Moses’ descent from Sinai found the people in disorder below (Exod 32:15–19) [T] for Exodus. In both a leader comes down from the glory to failure, and in Matthew the verdict on the failure is taken from the Song that Moses spoke about Israel’s later unfaithfulness [I], moderate (pattern, not citation). The contrast matters as much as the likeness: Moses breaks the tablets; Jesus says φέρετέ μοι αὐτὸν ὧδε (“bring him here to me”, 17:17) and heals [T].

Exod 30:11–16 → 17:24–27 (moderate–high; not in the overview’s map for this unit). Source context: when Israel is counted, each man is to give λύτρα τῆς ψυχῆς αὐτοῦ Κυρίῳ (“a ransom for his life to the LORD”, 30:12, Swete and Rahlfs; WLC כֹּפֶר נַפְשׁוֹ, “the ransom of his life”), so that no plague strikes them [T]. The sum is τὸ ἥμισυ τοῦ διδράχμου (“half the didrachm”, 30:13, Swete and Rahlfs), where the Hebrew has מַחֲצִית הַשֶּׁקֶל (“half the shekel”) [T]. Rich and poor give the same, ἐξιλάσασθαι περὶ τῶν ψυχῶν ὑμῶν (“to make atonement for your lives”, 30:15–16), and the silver goes to the service of the tent [T]. Book usage: none earlier. OT-to-OT: Neh 10:33 (WLC; Swete Neh 10:32) sets a third of a shekel yearly for the house of God [T] for Nehemiah. Divergence: the Greek renders the shekel as δίδραχμον (“didrachm”), so the Torah’s half-shekel is, in Greek, “half the didrachm” [T]. That Matthew’s τὰ δίδραχμα (“the two-drachma [tax]”) is this levy is [I]/[S] (the identification is standard, and the sum matches in value) — high as identification. What it adds: the tax the Son does not owe is the Torah’s ransom for the life. He pays it ἀντὶ ἐμοῦ καὶ σοῦ (“for me and you”, 17:27), and three chapters later gives τὴν ψυχὴν αὐτοῦ λύτρον ἀντὶ πολλῶν (“his life a ransom for many”, 20:28) [T]. This joins the audit’s new candidate 12 (Exod 30:12 → 20:28) to 17:24–27 [I], moderate–high.

1 Kgs 18:21 → 17:17 (uncertain). Move 1 only. ἕως πότε (“how long?”) is Elijah’s cry at Carmel (Swete), but the phrase stands in 26 Swete verses, most of them laments in the Psalms and Jeremiah [T]. With Elijah just seen on the mountain (17:3), the echo is possible, nothing more.

Internal: answers §1:23; plants §28:20 — μεθ’ ὑμῶν (high, verified). Answers §13:31 — the mustard seed (moderate–high, verified). Answers §17:5 — the Son, and the sons (high). Plants §19:26 — “nothing impossible” (moderate). Plants §20:28 — ἀντί and the ransom (moderate–high, verified for ἀντί). Plants §22:15–22 — Τί σοι δοκεῖ and κῆνσος (moderate–high, verified). Plants §26:45 — εἰς χεῖρας (high, verified). Answers §4:24 — σεληνιάζομαι (“to be moonstruck”) stands at 4:24 and 17:15 only (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. Opening material checked.

Translations. At 17:15 σεληνιάζεται (“he is moonstruck”) is “he is a lunatic” in the NASB95 and “he has seizures” in the ESV. At 17:17 διεστραμμένη (“perverted”) is “perverted” (NASB95) and “twisted” (ESV). At 17:20 the NASB95 keeps the noun, “the littleness of your faith”; the ESV has “your little faith” [T]. At 17:21 the NASB95 prints “[But this kind does not go out except by prayer and fasting.]” in brackets; the ESV omits the verse (see the overview) [T]. At 17:26 the NASB95 has “the sons are exempt” and the ESV “the sons are free” for ἐλεύθεροι (“free”). Pulpit divergence: none bearing. The ESV’s “free” is the closer word, and its “for me and for yourself” (17:27) loses the plainness of ἀντὶ ἐμοῦ καὶ σοῦ (“for me and you”) but not its sense.

Difficulty. 17:24–27: is the tax the temple levy or a civil tax? Options: (a) the temple half-shekel of Exod 30 (the sons of the King of the temple are free); (b) after AD 70, the Roman tax that replaced it (the fiscus Judaicus) [S]; (c) the story read as a general rule about paying taxes. “The kings of the earth” is the parable’s image, and the τέλη ἢ κῆνσον (“customs or poll-tax”) are its terms. The argument “the sons are free” only works if the tax belongs to God, which fits (a) [I]. The overview notes that the passage is used in dating arguments [S]; nothing in the exegesis depends on the date.

Book-overview note: Confirms the μεθ’ ὑμῶν frame row, the little-faith chain and the Deut 32:5/20 row. Extends: (1) Exod 30:11–16 behind 17:24–27 — the half-didrachm as λύτρα τῆς ψυχῆς (“a ransom for the life”) — should be added to the Torah map for 17:24–27, linked to the existing Exod 30:12 note at 20:28 (moderate–high). (2) The ὀλιγοπιστία at 17:20 rests on the NA28 reading; the Majority text has ἀπιστίαν (“unbelief”); the “little faith” bullet in the Presenting Situation should say so. (3) Τί σοι δοκεῖ + κῆνσος (17:25; 22:17 only) as a candidate echo row. (4) The descent from the mountain to failure (17:14–17) as the Exod 32 pattern — the adjacency case the skill names — moderate.

36. Matthew 18:1–14 — The Little Ones

Position. Q1: the Son has just said that “the sons are free” and has paid the levy “so that we do not cause them to stumble” (μὴ σκανδαλίσωμεν, 17:27) [T]. The disciples have heard twice that he will be handed over and killed (16:21; 17:22–23) [T]. Q2: the fourth discourse begins here, and it begins with the disciples’ question about rank: Τίς ἄρα μείζων ἐστὶν ἐν τῇ βασιλείᾳ τῶν οὐρανῶν; (“who then is greatest in the kingdom of heaven?”, 18:1) [T]. Ἐν ἐκείνῃ τῇ ὥρᾳ (“at that hour”, 18:1) ties the question to the scene before it [T]. The unit exists here because a community built on a Son who will be handed over cannot be ordered by greatness. It must be ordered by the child, by the refusal to cause stumbling, and by the Father’s search for the one who strays [I], high.

Structure. The child in the middle (1–5); woe to the one who causes stumbling, and the hand, foot and eye (6–9); do not despise the little ones, and the straying sheep (10–14) [T]. [18:11 is not in NA28 or SBLGNT.] The unit is framed by ἕνα / ἓν τῶν μικρῶν τούτων (“one of these little ones”, 18:6, 10, 14) [T]. The word σκανδαλίζω / σκάνδαλον (“cause to stumble / stumbling block”) runs through 18:6–9 six times [T] (σκανδαλίζω 18:6, 8, 9; σκάνδαλον 18:7 ×3; SBLGNT, verified).

Text-first findings.

  • The child stands where Jesus will stand. προσκαλεσάμενος παιδίον ἔστησεν αὐτὸ ἐν μέσῳ αὐτῶν (“calling a child, he set him in the middle of them”, 18:2) [T]. ἐν μέσῳ αὐτῶν (“in the middle of them”) stands at 18:2 and 18:20 only (SBLGNT, verified): ἐκεῖ εἰμι ἐν μέσῳ αὐτῶν (“there I am in the middle of them”, 18:20) [T]. The discourse opens with a child in the middle of the disciples and closes its central section with Jesus there [I], moderate–high. ὃς ἐὰν δέξηται ἓν παιδίον τοιοῦτο ἐπὶ τῷ ὀνόματί μου, ἐμὲ δέχεται (“whoever receives one such child in my name receives me”, 18:5) [T].
  • παιδίον — the word of chapter 2. παιδίον (“child”) occurs 18 times in 16 verses of Matthew (SBLGNT, verified). Nine of those occurrences, in seven verses, are in chapter 2, and every one of them names the infant Jesus (2:8, 9, 11, 13 ×2, 14, 20 ×2, 21) [T]. Herod sent to ζητεῖν τὸ παιδίον τοῦ ἀπολέσαι αὐτό (“to seek the child to destroy him”, 2:13) [T]. In this unit the shepherd ζητεῖ τὸ πλανώμενον (“seeks the one that is straying”, 18:12), and it is not the Father’s will ἵνα ἀπόληται ἓν τῶν μικρῶν τούτων (“that one of these little ones should perish”, 18:14) [T]. Herod seeks a child to destroy it; the Father seeks a little one so that it does not perish [I]. Synthetic: moderate.
  • Turn and become small. ἐὰν μὴ στραφῆτε καὶ γένησθε ὡς τὰ παιδία (“unless you turn and become like children”, 18:3) [T]. ὅστις οὖν ταπεινώσει ἑαυτὸν ὡς τὸ παιδίον τοῦτο (“whoever humbles himself like this child”, 18:4) [T]. ταπεινόω (“to humble”) stands at 18:4 and 23:12 (twice) only, and its adjective ταπεινός (“humble”) at 11:29 only, of Jesus: πραΰς εἰμι καὶ ταπεινὸς τῇ καρδίᾳ (“I am gentle and humble in heart”) (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. The greatest in the kingdom is the one who becomes what Jesus is [I], moderate–high.
  • Causing a little one to stumble. Ὃς δ’ ἂν σκανδαλίσῃ ἕνα τῶν μικρῶν τούτων τῶν πιστευόντων εἰς ἐμέ (“whoever causes one of these little ones who believe in me to stumble”, 18:6) [T]. The “little ones” are believers: τῶν πιστευόντων εἰς ἐμέ (“who believe in me”) [T]. “One of these little ones” stands at 10:42; 18:6, 10, 14 (SBLGNT, verified), and at 10:42 the “little one” is a disciple sent out (εἰς ὄνομα μαθητοῦ, “in the name of a disciple”) [T]. The σκάνδαλον (“stumbling block”) of 18:7 is the word Jesus spoke to Peter at 16:23, and the angels gather πάντα τὰ σκάνδαλα (“all stumbling blocks”) out of the kingdom at 13:41 (σκάνδαλον at 13:41; 16:23; 18:7 only, verified) [T]. The disciple who asked who was greatest is the kind of person who becomes a stumbling block [I], moderate.
  • Drowned in the sea. καταποντισθῇ ἐν τῷ πελάγει τῆς θαλάσσης (“be drowned in the depth of the sea”, 18:6) [T]. καταποντίζομαι (“to sink, be drowned”) stands at 14:30 and 18:6 only (SBLGNT, verified). At 14:30 it was Peter, ἀρξάμενος καταποντίζεσθαι (“beginning to sink”), whom Jesus caught [T]. The word is uncommon; the link is uncertain as design.
  • The doublet from the Sermon. The hand, foot and eye sayings (18:8–9) repeat 5:29–30, where they concerned lust; here they concern causing others to stumble [T]. γέεννα (“Gehenna, hell”) stands at 5:22, 29, 30; 10:28; 18:9; 23:15, 33, and γέενναν τοῦ πυρός (“the Gehenna of fire”) at 5:22 and 18:9 only (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. Matthew alone repeats a block of the Sermon in another discourse; the repetition shows the community discourse applying the Sermon’s ethic to life together [I], moderate.
  • Their angels see the Father’s face. οἱ ἄγγελοι αὐτῶν ἐν οὐρανοῖς διὰ παντὸς βλέπουσι τὸ πρόσωπον τοῦ πατρός μου (“their angels in heaven always see the face of my Father”, 18:10) [T]. Matthew’s own. The little ones have standing before the Father, so to despise them (καταφρονέω, “to despise”, 6:24; 18:10 only, verified) is to despise those whom the Father regards [I], high.
  • The straying sheep, not the lost sheep. πλανηθῇ ἓν ἐξ αὐτῶν … ζητεῖ τὸ πλανώμενον (“one of them goes astray … seeks the one that is straying”, 18:12) [T]. Luke 15:4 has ἀπολέσας … ἐπὶ τὸ ἀπολωλός (“having lost … for the lost one”) [T]. Matthew’s verb is πλανάω (“to go astray”); it stands at 18:12 (twice), 13 and then of deceivers in 22:29; 24:4, 5, 11, 24 (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. In Matthew the sheep is a member of the flock who wanders, and the parable’s subject is the Father’s will (18:14) [T]. The neuter participle τὸ πλανώμενον (“the straying one”) is Ezekiel’s (see Tool 11).

Synoptic check. Matthew follows Mark 9:33–37, 42–48 [T]. He drops the argument on the road and Mark’s “if anyone would be first, he must be last” (Mark 9:34–35), which he saves for 20:26–27 [T]. He adds 18:3–4 (turn, become like children, humble yourself) [T]. He drops Mark 9:38–41 (the strange exorcist) and moves the cup of water to 10:42 [T]. He adds 18:7 (the woes on stumbling blocks; compare Luke 17:1) and 18:10 (the angels) [T]. The sheep parable is shared with Luke 15:3–7, but Luke tells it to Pharisees about sinners who repent; Matthew tells it to disciples about the little ones in the flock, with πλανάω (“to stray”) for Luke’s ἀπόλλυμι (“to lose”), and with “on the mountains” for Luke’s “in the wilderness” [T].

Tool 11.

Ezek 34:4, 16 → 18:12–14 (moderate–high; the overview has Ezek 34 only for 9:36 and 25:32). Source context: the oracle against the shepherds of Israel, who fed themselves and not the flock. “The weak you did not strengthen … τὸ πλανώμενον οὐκ ἀπεστρέψατε, καὶ τὸ ἀπολωλὸς οὐκ ἐζητήσατε (‘the straying you did not bring back, and the lost you did not seek’)” (34:4, Swete; Rahlfs ἐπεστρέψατε, “turned back”) [T]. The sheep were scattered ἐν παντὶ ὄρει (“on every mountain”, 34:6) [T]. So the LORD himself will be the shepherd: Τὸ ἀπολωλὸς ζητήσω, καὶ τὸ πλανώμενον ἐπιστρέψω (“the lost I will seek, and the straying I will bring back”, 34:16, Swete and Rahlfs) [T]. Then he promises “one shepherd, my servant David” (34:23) [T]. τὸ πλανώμενον (“the straying one”) stands in Swete only at Ezek 34:4 and 34:16 (verified) [T]. The WLC has הַנִּדַּחַת (“the scattered, driven away”) — the Greek’s “straying” is its own rendering [T]. Matthew stands nearest the Greek (Swete and Rahlfs agree on the participle). Book usage: Ezek 34 stands behind 9:36 (sheep without a shepherd; with Num 27:17, audited) and 25:32–33 (between sheep and goats) [S: audit] for 9:36. 10:6 and 15:24 send Jesus and the Twelve to τὰ πρόβατα τὰ ἀπολωλότα οἴκου Ἰσραήλ (“the lost sheep of the house of Israel”) [T]. OT-to-OT: Ezek 34 itself rewrites Num 27:17 (Moses’ prayer for a shepherd) into God’s own shepherding [I]. What it adds: the seeking that Ezekiel’s shepherds failed to do, and that God promised to do himself, is the Father’s will for the community (18:14). The disciples, who asked about greatness, are put in the place of Ezekiel’s shepherds [I], moderate–high.

Isa 53:6 → 18:12 (uncertain). Move 1 only. πάντες ὡς πρόβατα ἐπλανήθημεν (“all we like sheep have gone astray”, Swete) uses πλανάω of sheep. The verb is common; no other link in the unit.

Internal: answers §2:13 — seeking the child to destroy (moderate, synthetic). Answers §11:29 — ταπεινός (moderate–high, verified). Answers §10:42 — “one of these little ones” (high, verified). Answers §16:23; §13:41 — σκάνδαλον (moderate, verified). Answers §5:29–30 — the doublet (high). Answers §9:36; §10:6; §15:24 — the sheep (high). Plants §18:20 — ἐν μέσῳ αὐτῶν (moderate–high, verified). Plants §19:13–15 — παιδίον (high). Plants §20:26–27 — “first/last … servant” moved from Mark 9:35 (high). Plants §23:12 — ταπεινόω (high, verified). Plants §24:4–5, 11, 24 — πλανάω (moderate). Opening material checked: chapter 2’s παιδίον above.

Translations. At 18:3 στραφῆτε (“you turn”) is “are converted” in the NASB95 and “turn” in the ESV; “converted” adds a technical sense the verb does not carry [I]. At 18:6–9 the NASB95 renders σκανδαλίζω / σκάνδαλον as “cause to stumble / stumbling block”; the ESV has “cause to sin” and “temptations to sin” [T]. Pulpit divergence: the ESV’s “sin … temptations to sin” (18:6–9), with its “hindrance” at 16:23, means the congregation will not hear that the σκάνδαλον of 18:7 is what Peter was called at 16:23. The preacher should supply the link.

Pitfall. “Become like children” read as a call to innocence or trust. The text names one quality: ὅστις οὖν ταπεινώσει ἑαυτόν (“whoever humbles himself”, 18:4), in answer to a question about greatness [T]. The corrective: preach the child as one without rank, received in Jesus’ name, and the “little ones” as the believers whom the strong are tempted to despise.

Book-overview note: Extends: (1) Ezek 34:4, 16 → 18:12–14 — the Swete participle τὸ πλανώμενον (“the straying one”), found only in these two verses, is Matthew’s word against Luke’s ἀπολωλός. Add 18:12–14 to the Ezek 34 row and raise that row from moderate, at least for this use, to moderate–high. (2) The 2:13 echo-table row (ζητεῖν τὸ παιδίον τοῦ ἀπολέσαι) can add 18:12–14 as its inversion (moderate, synthetic). (3) ἐν μέσῳ αὐτῶν (18:2; 18:20 only) as a frame inside the fourth discourse. (4) The English table should add 18:6–9: the ESV’s “sin” and “temptations” for σκανδαλίζω / σκάνδαλον, linked with its “hindrance” at 16:23.

37. Matthew 18:15–35 — Bound and Loosed; Seventy Times Seven ⭐

Position. Q1: the first half of the community discourse (18:1–14) ended on the Father’s will that not one of the little ones should perish (18:14) [T]. Q2: this unit exists here because a community of little ones will sin against one another, and the shepherd’s search for the stray (18:12–13) must become the brother’s search for the brother who sins (18:15). The discourse then turns from discipline to forgiveness without limit, and ends on the heart (18:35). The fourth formula follows (19:1) [T]/[I], high.

Structure. The brother who sins: four steps (18:15–17); binding and loosing, agreement, presence (18:18–20); Peter’s question and the answer (18:21–22); the parable of the unforgiving servant (18:23–35) [T]. ἀδελφός (“brother”) frames the unit: ὁ ἀδελφός σου (18:15) … τῷ ἀδελφῷ αὐτοῦ ἀπὸ τῶν καρδιῶν ὑμῶν (“his brother from your heart”, 18:35) [T].

Text-first findings.

  • The steps aim at gaining a brother. ἐάν σου ἀκούσῃ, ἐκέρδησας τὸν ἀδελφόν σου (“if he listens to you, you have won your brother”, 18:15) [T]. The goal is the brother regained, as the shepherd rejoices over the sheep found (18:13) [I], high. The last step: ἔστω σοι ὥσπερ ὁ ἐθνικὸς καὶ ὁ τελώνης (“let him be to you as a Gentile and a tax collector”, 18:17) [T]. In this book the τελώνης (“tax collector”) is the man Jesus ate with (9:10–11) and called (9:9; 10:3), and who enters the kingdom first (21:31) [T], verified (τελώνης in 8 verses, SBLGNT). The exclusion is serious, and the book’s own tax collector shows it is not final [I], moderate–high.
  • The ἐκκλησία (“church”) twice, and only here and at 16:18. ἐκκλησία occurs at 16:18; 18:17 (twice) (SBLGNT, 3 occurrences in 2 verses) [T], verified. Binding and loosing given to Peter (16:19) is given in the same words to the gathered disciples (18:18): ὅσα ἐὰν δήσητε … λύσητε (“whatever you bind … loose”) [T]. λύω (“to loose”) occurs only at 5:19; 16:19 (twice); 18:18 (twice); 21:2 [T], verified.
  • “I am there in their midst.” ἐκεῖ εἰμι ἐν μέσῳ αὐτῶν (“there I am in their midst”, 18:20) — the middle term between Ἐμμανουήλ … μεθ’ ἡμῶν ὁ θεός (“God with us”, 1:23) and ἐγὼ μεθ’ ὑμῶν εἰμι (“I am with you”, 28:20) [T]. The overview’s Christological Trajectory names it; confirmed. In context the “two or three” are the witnesses of 18:16 and the agreement of 18:19: the promise is spoken over discipline and prayer [T].
  • Seventy times seven. ἕως ἑβδομηκοντάκις ἑπτά (“up to seventy times seven”, 18:22) — Gen 4:24’s wording [S: audit] (Confirmed: ἑβδομηκοντάκις occurs once in Matthew and the NT; Luke 17:4 has only ἑπτάκις, “seven times”). Peter offers the first step, Jesus answers with Lamech’s second [T].
  • Mercy received and refused. οὐκ ἔδει καὶ σὲ ἐλεῆσαι τὸν σύνδουλόν σου, ὡς κἀγὼ σὲ ἠλέησα; (“should you not also have had mercy on your fellow slave, in the same way that I had mercy on you?”, 18:33). ἐλεάω (“to have mercy”) stands in 7 verses (SBLGNT, 8 occurrences): 5:7; 9:27; 15:22; 17:15; 18:33 (twice); 20:30, 31 [T], verified. The beatitude “blessed are the merciful, for they shall receive mercy” (5:7) is the parable in one line [I], high. σπλαγχνισθείς (“feeling compassion”, 18:27) is the verb used of Jesus himself (9:36; 14:14; 15:32; 20:34) [T], verified.
  • The debt. μυρίων ταλάντων (“ten thousand talents”, 18:24) against ἑκατὸν δηνάρια (“a hundred denarii”, 18:28) [T]. The proportion is the point [I], high. ὀφείλω (“to owe”) occurs at 18:28 (twice), 30, 34; 23:16, 18 [T], verified; the prayer’s ὀφειλήματα (“debts”, 6:12) is the noun of the same family [T].
  • “From your hearts”. ἀπὸ τῶν καρδιῶν ὑμῶν (18:35) closes the discourse [T]. καρδία (“heart”) again (see unit 30).

Synoptic check. Luke 17:3–4 has the brother who sins and forgiveness “seven times a day”. Matthew has the four steps, the church, binding and loosing, the two or three, ἑβδομηκοντάκις ἑπτά and the parable, none of which is in Mark or Luke [T].

Tool 11. Deut 19:15 → 18:16 (high — quotation). Source context: the law of witnesses: one witness is not enough; on the evidence of two or three a charge is established. Swete ἐπὶ στόματος δύο μαρτύρων καὶ ἐπὶ στόματος τριῶν μαρτύρων στήσεται πᾶν ῥῆμα (“on the mouth of two witnesses or three every word shall stand”) [T]; Matthew abbreviates it (ἵνα ἐπὶ στόματος δύο μαρτύρων ἢ τριῶν σταθῇ πᾶν ῥῆμα). The phrase is unique to Deut 19:15 in Swete (phrase search) [T], verified. Book usage: Deuteronomy is a live source (4:4–10; 5:31; 22:37). What it adds: the community’s process is Torah’s legal procedure, not private feeling.

Gen 4:24 → 18:22 (high) [S: audit]. Source context: Lamech’s boast of vengeance seventy-seven-fold. What it adds: the line of Cain’s vengeance becomes the measure of the disciple’s forgiveness. The Hebrew value is 77; see Translations.

Internal: answers §16:18–19 — church, binding and loosing (high, verified). Answers §6:12, 14–15 — debts and forgiveness (high). Answers §5:7 — mercy (high, verified). Answers §1:23 — presence (high). Answers §9:9–11 — the tax collector (moderate–high, verified). Plants §28:20 — presence (high). Plants §20:34 — σπλαγχνίζομαι (high, verified).

Translations. 18:22: NASB95 “up to seventy times seven”; ESV “seventy-seven times” (export checked) [T]. The ESV gives the Hebrew value of Gen 4:24; the NASB95 the Greek form (overview English table, v0.2; confirmed). Pulpit divergence: the congregation will hear “seventy-seven”; Lamech’s number is the point either way.

Difficulty. 18:17, “as a Gentile and a tax collector”, is used to justify shunning. Function in context: the last step of a process whose aim is regaining the brother (18:15), set between the shepherd’s search (18:12–14) and limitless forgiveness (18:21–35) [I], high. Preach it within the unit, not apart from it.

Pitfall. Preaching 18:20 as a promise for small gatherings. The two or three are witnesses in a discipline case and agreed petitioners (18:16, 19). The corrective: preach the presence promise as Christ’s authority over the church’s binding and loosing.

Book-overview note: confirms the Gen 4:24 row (audited), the Deut 19:15 row and the presence sequence 1:23 → 18:20 → 28:20. Extends: ἐλεάω (5:7 → 18:33) and λύω (16:19 ↔︎ 18:18) for the Echo Table.

38. Matthew 19:1–15 — From the Beginning; the Children

Position. Q1: the community discourse has just ended on forgiveness of a brother ἀπὸ τῶν καρδιῶν ὑμῶν (“from your hearts”, 18:35) [T]. Jesus has said three times that he goes to Jerusalem to die (16:21; 17:22–23; the third is coming at 20:18–19) [T]. Q2: this unit exists here because the road to Jerusalem now runs through the life of the household. The first test on the road is about the hardest bond to keep: a man and his wife. The answer appeals past Moses to “the beginning”, and the unit ends with children brought for blessing [T]. The heart that must forgive (18:35) is set against the σκληροκαρδία (“hardness of heart”, 19:8) that Moses’ concession made room for [I], moderate–high. The next unit takes up possessions (19:16–30): marriage, children and property follow in order [T]; whether this is a deliberate household sequence is [I], moderate.

Structure. The transition and the move to Judea (1–2); the Pharisees’ test and the answer from Genesis (3–6); Moses’ command or permission (7–8); the ruling (9); the disciples’ objection and the eunuch saying (10–12); the children (13–15) [T]. ἀπ’ ἀρχῆς (“from the beginning”) brackets the debate (19:4, 8) [T]. Both halves end in a saying about the kingdom of heaven: διὰ τὴν βασιλείαν τῶν οὐρανῶν (“for the sake of the kingdom of heaven”, 19:12) and τῶν γὰρ τοιούτων ἐστὶν ἡ βασιλεία τῶν οὐρανῶν (“for of such is the kingdom of heaven”, 19:14) [T].

Text-first findings.

  • The road to Jerusalem passes where the crowds first came from. Καὶ ἐγένετο ὅτε ἐτέλεσεν ὁ Ἰησοῦς τοὺς λόγους τούτους (“and it happened when Jesus had finished these words”, 19:1). ὅτε ἐτέλεσεν ὁ Ἰησοῦς (“when Jesus had finished”) stands at 7:28; 11:1; 13:53; 19:1; 26:1 (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. He goes εἰς τὰ ὅρια τῆς Ἰουδαίας πέραν τοῦ Ἰορδάνου (“into the region of Judea beyond the Jordan”), and ἠκολούθησαν αὐτῷ ὄχλοι πολλοί (“large crowds followed him”, 19:1–2) [T]. πέραν τοῦ Ἰορδάνου (“beyond the Jordan”) stands at 4:15, 25 and 19:1 only; ἠκολούθησαν αὐτῷ ὄχλοι πολλοί (“large crowds followed him”) at 4:25; 8:1; 19:2 only (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. 4:25 has both phrases: the crowds came ἀπὸ … πέραν τοῦ Ἰορδάνου (“from … beyond the Jordan”) just before the Sermon [T]. The last great journey retraces the first catchment [I], moderate.
  • The Pharisees test him as the tempter did. πειράζοντες αὐτόν (“testing him”, 19:3) [T]. πειράζω (“to test, tempt”) stands at 4:1, 3; 16:1; 19:3; 22:18, 35 (SBLGNT, verified): first the devil, then only opponents — Pharisees and Sadducees (16:1), Pharisees (19:3), the Pharisees’ disciples (22:15–18), and a lawyer from among them (22:34–35) [T]. Matthew alone adds κατὰ πᾶσαν αἰτίαν (“for any cause”, 19:3) [T]. That this places Jesus inside a known dispute about the grounds for divorce is [S]. αἰτία (“cause”) occurs at 19:3, 10 and 27:37, where it is the charge over the cross (SBLGNT, verified) [T]; the link is uncertain as design.
  • Scripture before Moses: “Have you not read?” Οὐκ ἀνέγνωτε ὅτι ὁ κτίσας ἀπ’ ἀρχῆς ἄρσεν καὶ θῆλυ ἐποίησεν αὐτούς (“have you not read that he who created them from the beginning made them male and female?”, 19:4) [T]. οὐκ ἀνέγνωτε (“have you not read?”) stands at 12:3, 5; 19:4; 22:31 (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. Then Gen 2:24, and Jesus’ own conclusion: ὥστε οὐκέτι εἰσὶν δύο ἀλλὰ σὰρξ μία. ὃ οὖν ὁ θεὸς συνέζευξεν ἄνθρωπος μὴ χωριζέτω (“so they are no longer two but one flesh. What therefore God has joined together, let man not separate”, 19:6) [T]. συζεύγνυμι (“to join together”) and χωρίζω (“to separate”) occur only here (verified) [T]. ἀπ’ ἀρχῆς (“from the beginning”) stands at 19:4, 8 and 24:21 only (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. Note the reading: ὁ κτίσας (“he who created”) is the NA28 and SBLGNT text, with B Θ ƒ¹ 700; most witnesses read ὁ ποιήσας (“he who made”) (NA28 apparatus, verified) [T]. κτίζω (“to create”) occurs only here in Matthew [T].
  • “Commanded” or “permitted” — Matthew turns Mark round. The Pharisees say Μωϋσῆς ἐνετείλατο (“Moses commanded”, 19:7). Jesus answers Μωϋσῆς … ἐπέτρεψεν ὑμῖν (“Moses permitted you”, 19:8) πρὸς τὴν σκληροκαρδίαν ὑμῶν (“because of your hardness of heart”) [T]. In Mark it is Jesus who asks what Moses “commanded” and they who say “permitted” (Mark 10:3–4) [T]. Matthew gives the opponents the stronger word and Jesus the correction [T]. ἐντέλλομαι (“to command”) stands at 4:6; 17:9; 19:7; 28:20 (SBLGNT, verified); its last use is Jesus’ own: πάντα ὅσα ἐνετειλάμην ὑμῖν (“all that I commanded you”, 28:20) [T]. σκληροκαρδία (“hardness of heart”) occurs only here (verified) [T]. The reading of Deut 24:1–4 as a regulation of something already happening, not a command to divorce, is the plain sense of the source (see Tool 11) [I], high.
  • The ruling, and its exception. ὃς ἂν ἀπολύσῃ τὴν γυναῖκα αὐτοῦ μὴ ἐπὶ πορνείᾳ καὶ γαμήσῃ ἄλλην μοιχᾶται (“whoever divorces his wife, except for sexual immorality, and marries another commits adultery”, 19:9) [T]. At 5:32 the exception is παρεκτὸς λόγου πορνείας (“except on the matter of sexual immorality”) [T]. πορνεία (“sexual immorality”) occurs at 5:32; 15:19; 19:9 only (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. The λόγου (“matter, word”) of 5:32 matches the Hebrew of Deut 24:1, עֶרְוַת דָּבָר (“the nakedness of a thing”, דָּבָר = “word, thing”), where Swete has ἄσχημον πρᾶγμα (“an unseemly thing”) [T]; that Matthew’s phrase reflects the Hebrew is [I], moderate. Text: the SBLGNT adds καὶ ὁ ἀπολελυμένην γαμήσας μοιχᾶται (“and he who marries a divorced woman commits adultery”) with B C* K W and the Majority text; NA28 omits it with א C³ D L (NA28 apparatus, verified) [T]. The SBLGNT and NA28 differ here; nothing in this unit rests on the extra clause.
  • The book’s first divorce was a righteous man’s plan. ἀπολύω (“to dismiss, divorce”) first appears at 1:19: Joseph, δίκαιος ὤν (“being righteous”), ἐβουλήθη λάθρᾳ ἀπολῦσαι αὐτήν (“planned to divorce her quietly”) [T]. In the sense “to divorce” it then stands only at 5:31–32 and 19:3, 7, 8, 9 (SBLGNT, 20 occurrences in all, verified) [T]. Joseph planned what 19:9 allows, for what looked like πορνεία (“sexual immorality”), and was stopped by the angel [I]. The opening chapter already shows the exception in a righteous man’s hands and God’s intervention to keep the marriage [I], moderate.
  • Not all can receive it — “to whom it has been given”. Οὐ πάντες χωροῦσι τὸν λόγον τοῦτον ἀλλ’ οἷς δέδοται (“not all can receive this word, but those to whom it has been given”, 19:11) [T]. δέδοται (“it has been given”) stands at 13:11 (twice) and 19:11 only: ὑμῖν δέδοται γνῶναι τὰ μυστήρια (“to you it has been given to know the mysteries”, 13:11) (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. εὐνοῦχος (“eunuch”) stands three times, in 19:12 only (verified) [T]. The closing ὁ δυνάμενος χωρεῖν χωρείτω (“let the one who is able to receive it receive it”, 19:12) has the form of ὁ ἔχων ὦτα ἀκουέτω (“let the one who has ears hear”, 11:15; 13:9, 43) [T]/[I]. Celibacy “for the kingdom” is a gift, like the understanding of the parables, not a rank [I], moderate–high.
  • The children — and a rebuke that does not stand. προσηνέχθησαν αὐτῷ παιδία ἵνα τὰς χεῖρας ἐπιθῇ αὐτοῖς καὶ προσεύξηται (“children were brought to him so that he might lay his hands on them and pray”, 19:13) [T]. οἱ δὲ μαθηταὶ ἐπετίμησαν αὐτοῖς (“but the disciples rebuked them”) [T]. ἐπιτιμάω (“to rebuke”) stands at 8:26; 12:16; 16:22; 17:18; 19:13; 20:31 (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. At 20:31 ὁ ὄχλος ἐπετίμησεν αὐτοῖς (“the crowd rebuked them”) — the two blind men — and Jesus again receives those who were rebuked [T]. The children come one chapter after the child set ἐν μέσῳ (“in the middle”, 18:2) [T]; παιδίον (“child”) at 18:2–5 and 19:13–14 is the last of its uses (SBLGNT, verified) [T]. τῶν γὰρ τοιούτων ἐστὶν ἡ βασιλεία (“of such is the kingdom”) recalls ὅτι αὐτῶν ἐστιν ἡ βασιλεία τῶν οὐρανῶν (“for theirs is the kingdom of heaven”, 5:3, 10; verified only there) [T]/[I], moderate.

Synoptic check. Matthew follows Mark 10:1–16 [T]. He changes the order of the argument: in Mark, Jesus asks about Moses first and quotes Genesis second (Mark 10:3–8); in Matthew he quotes Genesis first, and the Pharisees bring Moses as an objection (19:4–7) [T]. He swaps “commanded” and “permitted” (see above) [T]. He adds κατὰ πᾶσαν αἰτίαν (“for any cause”, 19:3), the exception μὴ ἐπὶ πορνείᾳ (“except for sexual immorality”, 19:9) and the whole of 19:10–12 [T]. He drops Mark 10:12 (a wife who divorces her husband) [T]. With the children he adds καὶ προσεύξηται (“and pray”, 19:13), and drops three things from Mark: Jesus’ indignation (ἠγανάκτησεν, “he was indignant”, Mark 10:14), the saying about receiving the kingdom ὡς παιδίον (“like a child”, Mark 10:15), which he used at 18:3, and the embrace (ἐναγκαλισάμενος, “taking them in his arms”, Mark 10:16) [T]. ἀγανακτέω (“to be indignant”) occurs in Matthew at 20:24; 21:15; 26:8 only, never of Jesus (SBLGNT, verified) [T].

Tool 11.

Gen 1:27; 2:24 → 19:4–5 (high; [S: audit]). Source context: Gen 1:27 is the creation of humankind in God’s image, ἄρσεν καὶ θῆλυ (“male and female”); Gen 2:24 is the narrator’s comment after the woman is built from the man’s side, “therefore a man shall leave …” [T]. In Matthew both are said by God: ὁ κτίσας … καὶ εἶπεν (“he who created … and said”, 19:4–5) [T]. Text: ἄρσεν καὶ θῆλυ ἐποίησεν αὐτούς (“he made them male and female”) is verbatim Swete and Rahlfs; the WLC has בָּרָא (“created”, gated), which Matthew’s κτίσας (“having created”) renders more closely than the LXX’s ἐποίησεν (“made”) [T]/[I]. For Gen 2:24: Swete reads προσκολληθήσεται τῇ γυναικὶ αὐτοῦ (“shall cleave to his wife”, dative); Rahlfs reads προσκολληθήσεται πρὸς τὴν γυναῖκα αὐτοῦ (“to his wife”, with a preposition); Matthew has κολληθήσεται τῇ γυναικὶ αὐτοῦ, the simple verb with Swete’s dative [T]. All Greek witnesses read οἱ δύο (“the two”), which the WLC lacks (וְהָיוּ לְבָשָׂר אֶחָד, “and they shall become one flesh”) [T]. Matthew stands nearest the Greek, and on the dative nearest Swete; Mark 10:7 has Rahlfs’ πρός. “The two” is a Greek plus on which Jesus’ conclusion turns (οὐκέτι εἰσὶν δύο, “no longer two”, 19:6): a substantive LXX reading, not a translation loss [T]/[I]. Book usage: Matthew’s first words are Βίβλος γενέσεως (“the book of the genesis”, 1:1). In Swete βίβλος γενέσεως stands only at Gen 2:4 and 5:1, and ἄρσεν καὶ θῆλυ ἐποίησεν αὐτούς (“he made them male and female”) only at Gen 1:27 and 5:2 (verified) [T]. So Gen 5:1–2 holds both the book’s opening words and the words Jesus quotes here [T]. Synthetic: moderate as design. OT-to-OT: Mal 2:14–16 reads marriage as covenant and recalls creation (see below). What it adds: Jesus answers a legal question with the order of creation, and sets “the beginning” above Moses’ concession. The book that began with a “genesis” now appeals to the first one.

Deut 24:1–4 → 19:7–8 (high; [S: audit]; also 5:31). Source context: the law does not command divorce. It assumes it (ἐὰν … καὶ γράψει αὐτῇ βιβλίον ἀποστασίου, “if … and he writes her a certificate of divorce”, Swete) and forbids one thing: the first husband may not take her back after a second marriage, ὅτι βδέλυγμά ἐστιν (“for it is an abomination”, 24:4) [T]. The ground is עֶרְוַת דָּבָר (“the nakedness of a thing”; Swete ἄσχημον πρᾶγμα, “an unseemly thing”, 24:1) [T]. βιβλίον ἀποστασίου (“certificate of divorce”) stands in Swete at Deut 24:1, 3 and Jer 3:8 (verified) [T]. Book usage: 5:31 cited the same law in the antitheses [T]. OT-to-OT: the Prophets reuse Deut 24:1–4 of the LORD and Israel. Jer 3:1 asks the Deuteronomic question (“if a man sends away his wife … will he return to her?”) and then calls faithless Israel back; Jer 3:8 says the LORD gave Israel βιβλίον ἀποστασίου (“a certificate of divorce”, Swete) [T]. Isa 50:1 asks Ποῖον τὸ βιβλίον τοῦ ἀποστασίου τῆς μητρὸς ὑμῶν; (“where is your mother’s certificate of divorce?”, Swete) [T]. What it adds: the law Moses gave regulates a broken practice; the Prophets show God himself refusing to treat that certificate as the last word. Jesus’ “from the beginning it was not so” takes the side of the Prophets’ God against the Pharisees’ “Moses commanded” [I], moderate.

Mal 2:14–16 → 19:4–9 (uncertain–moderate). Move 1 only. The LORD is witness between a man and τῆς γυναικὸς νεότητός σου (“the wife of your youth”, 2:14, Swete), whom he has deserted; the WLC 2:16 reads כִּי־שָׂנֵא שַׁלַּח (“for he hates sending away”) where Swete reads ἀλλὰ ἐὰν μισήσας ἐξαποστείλῃς (“but if, hating her, you send her away”) [T]. The two texts point opposite ways, and Matthew uses no Malachi wording. A thematic companion, not a source.

Isa 56:3–5 → 19:12 (uncertain). Move 1 only. ὁ εὐνοῦχος (“the eunuch”) is told not to say “I am a dry tree”; he will have a name κρείττω υἱῶν καὶ θυγατέρων (“better than sons and daughters”, Swete) [T]. εὐνοῦχος stands in 7 Swete verses (Gen 39:1; Neh 1:11; Wis 3:14; Sir 30:20; Est 2:14, 15; Isa 56:3; verified) [T]. The promise fits 19:12 and 19:29, but there is no verbal link beyond the noun.

Internal: answers §4:15, 25 — beyond the Jordan; the crowds that follow (moderate, verified). Answers §1:19 — the first ἀπολύω, a righteous man’s plan to divorce (moderate). Answers §1:1 — βίβλος γενέσεως and Gen 5:1–2 (moderate, synthetic). Answers §4:1, 3 — πειράζω (moderate–high, verified). Answers §5:31–32 — the doublet on divorce (high). Answers §13:11 — δέδοται (moderate–high, verified). Answers §18:2–5 — the child (high). Plants §20:31 — ἐπιτιμάω; the rebuked are received (moderate, verified). Plants §28:20 — ἐντέλλομαι; Moses “commanded” (19:7), Jesus “commanded” (28:20) (moderate, verified). Opening material checked: 1:1 and 1:19 above.

Translations. 19:3: NASB95 “for any reason at all”; ESV “for any cause” [T]. 19:9: NASB95 “except for immorality”; ESV “except for sexual immorality” [T]. 19:11: χωροῦσι is “can accept this statement” (NASB95) and “can receive this saying” (ESV) [T]. Both versions follow the NA28 at the end of 19:9 and at 19:4 (“he who created”) [T]. Pulpit divergence: none bearing. The ESV’s “sexual immorality” is the clearer rendering of πορνεία; the NASB95’s bare “immorality” can be heard as any wrongdoing.

Difficulty. 19:9, the exception clause. Options: (a) πορνεία (“sexual immorality”) as adultery or comparable sexual sin, which permits divorce and remarriage; (b) as a marriage within forbidden degrees (Lev 18), so that the “exception” is not a true marriage [S]; (c) as unfaithfulness during betrothal, which 1:19 illustrates [S]. The text does not define the word, and 5:32 does not settle it. Take it to Logos before preaching; nothing here should be decided from the English.

Book-overview note: Extends: (1) The Gen 1:27; 2:24 row: Matthew’s dative κολληθήσεται τῇ γυναικί agrees with Swete against Rahlfs’ πρός (and Mark’s); “the two” is the Greek’s plus; κτίσας rests on B Θ ƒ¹ 700 and renders the Hebrew בָּרָא. (2) Echo table: βίβλος γενέσεως (1:1) and ἄρσεν καὶ θῆλυ (19:4) meet in Gen 5:1–2 Swete (moderate, synthetic). (3) Add Jer 3:1, 8 and Isa 50:1 to the Deut 24:1 row as the Prophets’ reuse of the divorce law. (4) Add a textual note on 19:9 (SBLGNT and NA28 differ on the second clause). (5) Echo table: 1:19 ἀπολῦσαι ↔︎ 5:32; 19:9 (moderate). (6) The Synoptic table should record Matthew’s reversal of “commanded / permitted”.

39. Matthew 19:16–20:16 — The Rich Young Man; the First Last; the Vineyard Workers

Position. Q1: Jesus has just blessed the children: τῶν γὰρ τοιούτων ἐστὶν ἡ βασιλεία τῶν οὐρανῶν (“for the kingdom of heaven belongs to such as these”, 19:14) [T]. Q2: this unit exists here because the next man to come asks how to earn what the children were simply given: τί ἀγαθὸν ποιήσω ἵνα σχῶ ζωὴν αἰώνιον; (“what good thing shall I do that I may obtain eternal life?”, 19:16) [T]. The answer runs through the camel saying to the parable of wages paid by grace. It is the last teaching before the third passion prediction (20:17–19) [I], high.

Structure. The rich young man (19:16–22); the camel and the needle, and “with God all things are possible” (19:23–26); the reward of the Twelve and of all who leave (19:27–29); “many first will be last” (19:30); the vineyard workers (20:1–15); “the last will be first” (20:16) [T]. An inclusio: πρῶτοι ἔσχατοι καὶ ἔσχατοι πρῶτοι (19:30) and οἱ ἔσχατοι πρῶτοι καὶ οἱ πρῶτοι ἔσχατοι (20:16), in reverse order [T], verified (phrase search). The parable is framed as the explanation of the saying [T].

Text-first findings.

  • “Good” and “the one who is good”. τί ἀγαθόν … Τί με ἐρωτᾷς περὶ τοῦ ἀγαθοῦ; εἷς ἐστιν ὁ ἀγαθός (“why are you asking Me about what is good? There is only One who is good”, 19:16–17). Matthew changes Mark’s Τί με λέγεις ἀγαθόν; (“why do you call Me good?”, Mark 10:18) [T]. ἀγαθός (“good”) returns at the end of the parable: ὅτι ἐγὼ ἀγαθός εἰμι (“because I am generous”, literally “good”, 20:15) [T], verified. The young man asks what good to do; the landowner is the one who is good. Synthetic, moderate–high.
  • “If you wish to be complete.” Εἰ θέλεις τέλειος εἶναι (“if you wish to be complete”, 19:21). τέλειος (“complete, perfect”) occurs only at 5:48 (twice) and 19:21 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The demand of the Sermon (5:48) is now addressed to one man, and the path to it is “follow Me” [T]/[I], high. The overview’s Trap 1 rests on this chain; confirmed.
  • The commandments cited, and love of neighbour added. Οὐ φονεύσεις, Οὐ μοιχεύσεις, Οὐ κλέψεις, Οὐ ψευδομαρτυρήσεις, Τίμα τὸν πατέρα καὶ τὴν μητέρα (19:18–19) — the second table — and Matthew alone adds καὶ Ἀγαπήσεις τὸν πλησίον σου ὡς σεαυτόν (“and you shall love your neighbour as yourself”, Lev 19:18) [T]. Lev 19:18 is cited at 5:43; 19:19; 22:39 (overview row; confirmed).
  • Eternal life. ζωὴν αἰώνιον (“eternal life”) stands at 19:16, 29; 25:46 (SBLGNT phrase search) [T], verified. The young man’s question is answered at 19:29 (those who leave all will inherit it) and at the end of the last discourse (25:46) [T].
  • The throne of his glory. ὅταν καθίσῃ ὁ υἱὸς τοῦ ἀνθρώπου ἐπὶ θρόνου δόξης αὐτοῦ (“when the Son of Man will sit on His glorious throne”, 19:28) — the phrase returns at 25:31, and only there (θρόνος at 5:34; 19:28 twice; 23:22; 25:31, SBLGNT) [T], verified. ἐν τῇ παλιγγενεσίᾳ (“in the regeneration”, 19:28) occurs only here in Matthew [T].
  • “Is your eye evil because I am good?” ἢ ὁ ὀφθαλμός σου πονηρός ἐστιν ὅτι ἐγὼ ἀγαθός εἰμι; (20:15). ὁ ὀφθαλμός σου πονηρός (“your eye is evil”) occurs only at 6:23 and 20:15 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. At 6:23 the bad eye fills the body with darkness, set against treasure in heaven; here it is the grudging eye of the first-hired against the master’s goodness [I], high.
  • “With God all things are possible.” παρὰ δὲ θεῷ πάντα δυνατά (19:26). δυνατός (“possible”) occurs only at 19:26; 24:24; 26:39 (SBLGNT) [T], verified: at 26:39 Jesus prays εἰ δυνατόν ἐστιν (“if it is possible”), and the cup is not removed [T]. Synthetic, moderate.

Synoptic check. Mark 10:17–31 for the young man and the sayings. Matthew changes the opening question (“good teacher” becomes “what good thing”), adds “if you wish to be complete” and “love your neighbour”, calls him νεανίσκος (“young man”, 19:20, 22), and adds the twelve thrones (19:28; cf. Luke 22:30). The vineyard parable is Matthew’s alone [T].

Tool 11. Lev 19:18 → 19:19 (high — quotation). Move 1: the holiness code, love for the neighbour set against vengeance and grudge (Lev 19:17–18). Book usage: cited at 5:43 and 22:39 [T]. What it adds: the summary that will become the second great commandment (22:39) is added to the list the young man thinks he has kept.

Exod 20:12–16; Deut 5:16–20 → 19:18–19 (high — quotation). Move 1 only; the Decalogue’s second table, in the order Matthew also used at 15:19 [I], moderate.

Isa 5:1–7 → 20:1 (uncertain — the vineyard image). The vineyard is Israel in Isaiah’s song, and Matthew will use that song at 21:33. Here the image is the place of work, and no wording is shared [I]. Not claimed.

Internal: answers §5:48 — τέλειος (high, verified). Answers §6:19–23 — treasure in heaven and the bad eye (high, verified). Answers §19:13–15 — the children (high). Plants §21:28–41 — ἀμπελών (the vineyard returns at 21:28, 33–41; verified). Plants §25:31, 46 — the throne and eternal life (high, verified). Plants §26:39 — δυνατός (moderate, verified).

Translations. 20:15 ὁ ὀφθαλμός σου πονηρός: NASB95 “is your eye envious” keeps the eye; ESV “do you begrudge my generosity?” drops it [T] (exports checked). Pulpit divergence: the ESV congregation will not hear the eye of 6:23 here, nor the master’s “good” (ESV “generosity”; NASB95 “generous”).

Pitfall. Reading the parable as a lesson in economics or in late conversion only. The inclusio says it is about first and last, and the master’s question says it is about his goodness. The corrective: preach 20:15 as the centre.

Book-overview note: confirms Trap 1’s τέλειος chain and the Lev 19:18 row. Extends: ὀφθαλμὸς πονηρός (6:23 ↔︎ 20:15), θρόνος δόξης (19:28 ↔︎ 25:31) and the ἀγαθός frame (19:17 ↔︎ 20:15) for the Echo Table.

40. Matthew 20:17–34 — A Ransom for Many; Two Blind Men ⭐

Position. Q1: the vineyard parable ended “the last will be first” (20:16) [T]. Q2: this unit exists here because the reversal is now applied to the Son of Man himself and to those who want the first seats. It brings the third and fullest passion prediction (20:17–19), the request for the places at his right and left (20:20–28), and the healing outside Jericho, the last scene before Jerusalem (20:29–34) [T]. It closes the way to Jerusalem (16:21–20:34) [T].

Structure. The third prediction (20:17–19); the mother’s request, the cup, and the rule of service (20:20–28); two blind men at Jericho (20:29–34) [T].

Text-first findings.

  • “Crucify” for the first time. παραδώσουσιν αὐτὸν τοῖς ἔθνεσιν εἰς τὸ ἐμπαῖξαι καὶ μαστιγῶσαι καὶ σταυρῶσαι (“will hand Him over to the Gentiles to mock and scourge and crucify Him”, 20:19). Mark 10:34 has ἀποκτενοῦσιν (“they will kill”) [T]. σταυρόω (“to crucify”) occurs first here in Matthew (10 verses, SBLGNT: 20:19; 23:34; 26:2; 27:22, 23, 26, 31, 35, 38; 28:5) [T], verified. Matthew drops Mark’s “they will spit” (Mark 10:34) [S: audit]. ἐμπαίζω (“to mock”) stands at 2:16 (Herod “tricked” by the magi); 20:19; 27:29, 31, 41 [T], verified.
  • The places at right and left are given at the cross. εἷς ἐκ δεξιῶν σου καὶ εἷς ἐξ εὐωνύμων σου (“one on Your right and one on Your left”, 20:21). The phrase returns once more: δύο λῃσταί, εἷς ἐκ δεξιῶν καὶ εἷς ἐξ εὐωνύμων (“two robbers … one on the right and one on the left”, 27:38) [T]. ἐξ εὐωνύμων (“on the left”) occurs only at 20:21, 23; 25:33, 41; 27:38 (SBLGNT phrase search) [T], verified. The seats the sons of Zebedee asked for are taken, at his enthronement as King of the Jews (27:37), by two robbers. Synthetic, high for the chain; the reading is [I], moderate–high. The mother who asked is at the cross (27:56) [T].
  • The cup. δύνασθε πιεῖν τὸ ποτήριον ὃ ἐγὼ μέλλω πίνειν; (“are you able to drink the cup that I am about to drink?”, 20:22). ποτήριον (“cup”) stands at 10:42; 20:22, 23; 23:25, 26; 26:27, 39 (SBLGNT) [T], verified: the cup of the Supper and the cup of Gethsemane follow.
  • Rulers of the Gentiles, and the servant. οἱ ἄρχοντες τῶν ἐθνῶν κατακυριεύουσιν αὐτῶν (“the rulers of the Gentiles lord it over them”, 20:25) against διάκονος (“servant”) and δοῦλος (“slave”, 20:26–27) [T]. The rule is grounded in the Son of Man: οὐκ ἦλθεν διακονηθῆναι ἀλλὰ διακονῆσαι καὶ δοῦναι τὴν ψυχὴν αὐτοῦ λύτρον ἀντὶ πολλῶν (“did not come to be served, but to serve, and to give His life a ransom for many”, 20:28) [T]. λύτρον (“ransom”) occurs only here in Matthew [T], verified.
  • The blind see and follow. δύο τυφλοί (“two blind men”, 20:30), Κύριε, ἐλέησον ἡμᾶς, υἱὸς Δαυίδ (“Lord, have mercy on us, Son of David”), and Jesus σπλαγχνισθείς (“moved with compassion”, 20:34) touches their eyes; εὐθέως ἀνέβλεψαν καὶ ἠκολούθησαν αὐτῷ (“immediately they regained their sight and followed Him”) [T]. It repeats 9:27–31 (two blind men, “Son of David”, “have mercy”) [T]. The Zebedees ask for thrones; the blind ask for sight and follow him into Jerusalem [I], high.

Synoptic check. Mark 10:32–52. Matthew has the mother make the request (20:20; Mark 10:35 has James and John), and she προσκυνοῦσα (“bowing down”, worshipping) [T]. He changes “kill” to “crucify” and drops “spit” (20:19). He doubles the blind man and omits his name Bartimaeus (Mark 10:46) [T]. 20:28 is Mark 10:45 word for word [S: audit].

Tool 11. Isa 53:10–12 → 20:28 (high as one component; moderate as the single source) [S: audit]. The audit’s verdict stands: the “many” (רַבִּים, “many”, Isa 53:11–12) is one strand; λύτρον (“ransom”) is not in Isa 52:13–53:12, and its closer texts are Exod 30:12 (λύτρα τῆς ψυχῆς αὐτοῦ, “a ransom for his life”) and Num 3:12. The text-first work here adds one point: the Son of Man’s διακονῆσαι (“to serve”) stands next to the Servant’s “many” in the same sentence, so the Son of Man of Dan 7 and the Servant of Isa 53 meet in one saying [I], moderate–high (Dan 7:14 has “all peoples … should serve him”, Aramaic יִפְלְחוּן; the Son of Man here serves instead) [I], moderate.

Internal: answers §16:21; §17:22–23 — the third prediction (high). Answers §9:27–31 — the two blind men (high). Answers §2:16 — ἐμπαίζω (moderate, verified). Plants §26:27, 39 — the cup (high, verified). Plants §26:28 — “many” (high, verified). Plants §27:38, 56 — right and left; the mother of the sons of Zebedee (high, verified). Plants §21:9 — “Son of David” at the entry (high).

Translations. 20:28 λύτρον ἀντὶ πολλῶν: NASB95 “a ransom for many”, ESV “a ransom for many”. 20:21, 23 and 27:38 — both versions keep “right” and “left” [T]. Pulpit divergence: none bearing.

Pitfall. Making the mother the villain and the blind men the heroes. Both scenes are about what Jesus gives: a cup and a cross to the brothers, sight to the blind. The corrective: preach 20:28 as the centre of the unit, with 27:38 in view.

Book-overview note: confirms the ransom row (audited) and the passion prediction pattern. Extends: the right-and-left chain (20:21, 23 → 27:38) is new and should enter the Echo Table; σταυρόω first at 20:19 (Matthew’s change from Mark) belongs in the arc map note on the third prediction.

41. Matthew 21:1–17 — Your King Comes, Meek ⭐

Position. Q1: the way to Jerusalem has ended with two blind men crying Ἐλέησον ἡμᾶς, κύριε, υἱὸς Δαυίδ (“Lord, have mercy on us, Son of David”, 20:30, 31), healed and following him (20:34) [T]. Three passion predictions have named Jerusalem as the place of suffering (16:21; 20:18) [T]. Q2: this unit exists here because the Son of David must now arrive in David’s city and claim David’s house. He enters as King (21:5), cleanses the temple (21:12–13), heals in it (21:14) and is acclaimed in it (21:15). Every party that the next five chapters will confront is put in place in this one scene: the crowds, the city, the chief priests and the scribes [T]. The judgement chapters (21:18–25:46) need a King in the temple before they can begin [I].

Structure. Preparation: the two animals, “the Lord needs them” (1–3). Formula quotation (4–5). The entry and the acclamation (6–9). The city shaken: “Who is this?” (10–11). The temple: expulsion and the scriptural charge (12–13). The blind and the lame healed (14). The children’s acclamation, the leaders’ anger and Ps 8 (15–16). Withdrawal to Bethany (17). The acclamation brackets the scene: Ὡσαννὰ τῷ υἱῷ Δαυίδ (“Hosanna to the Son of David”) at 21:9 on the road and at 21:15 in the temple, in the same words [T]. ὡσαννά (“hosanna”) occurs only at these two verses in Matthew (three occurrences, SBLGNT) [T], verified.

Text-first findings.

  • The King comes πραΰς (“gentle”). The formula quotation reads ἰδοὺ ὁ βασιλεύς σου ἔρχεταί σοι πραΰς (“behold, your King is coming to you, gentle”, 21:5) [T]. πραΰς (“gentle”) occurs three times in Matthew: 5:5 (οἱ πραεῖς, “the gentle”), 11:29 (πραΰς εἰμι, “I am gentle”) and 21:5 (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The beatitude, the self-description and the entry use one word [T]; the overview’s Echo Table already has this chain. What the text adds here: Matthew keeps πραΰς (“gentle”) and omits Zechariah’s δίκαιος καὶ σῴζων (“righteous and saving”, Zech 9:9 Swete) [T]. The quotation is trimmed to the one word that ties the King to the beatitudes and to 11:29 [I], moderate–high.
  • The quotation is mixed, not “Hebrew”. Matthew’s two animals, ἐπὶ ὄνον καὶ ἐπὶ πῶλον υἱὸν ὑποζυγίου (“on a donkey, and on a colt, the foal of a beast of burden”, 21:5), follow the Hebrew parallelism עַל־חֲמוֹר (“on a donkey”) וְעַל־עַיִר בֶּן־אֲתֹנוֹת (“and on a colt, the son of she-asses”), WLC Zech 9:9 [T] (lemmas 2543, 5895, 860 gated). υἱόν (“son”) renders בֶּן (“son of”), which neither Greek text has: Swete and Rahlfs read ἐπὶ ὑποζύγιον καὶ πῶλον νέον (“on a beast of burden and a young colt”) [T]. But πραΰς (“gentle”) is the Greek’s word; the Hebrew has עָנִי (“afflicted, poor”; lemma 6041 gated) [T]. Matthew takes the animals from the Hebrew and the King’s character from the Greek [T]. This is the pattern the overview found at 12:18–21 (a mixed quotation), not a simple case of “following the Hebrew” [I], high.
  • The two animals are narrated, not only quoted. ὄνος (“donkey”) and πῶλος (“colt”) each stand at 21:2, 5 and 7 only (SBLGNT) [T], verified. 21:7 says ἤγαγον τὴν ὄνον καὶ τὸν πῶλον … καὶ ἐπεκάθισεν ἐπάνω αὐτῶν (“they brought the donkey and the colt … and he sat on them”) [T]. Mark 11:2, 7 and Luke 19:30, 35 have one colt. The narrative is written to match the quotation’s parallelism [T]/[I].
  • The city is “shaken”. εἰσελθόντος αὐτοῦ εἰς Ἱεροσόλυμα ἐσείσθη πᾶσα ἡ πόλις (“when he entered Jerusalem, all the city was shaken”, 21:10) [T]. σείω (“to shake”) occurs at 21:10, 27:51 (ἡ γῆ ἐσείσθη, “the earth was shaken”) and 28:4 (the guards ἐσείσθησαν, “shook”) only (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The verb of the entry is the verb of the cross and the tomb. Synthetic: moderate. The question Τίς ἐστιν οὗτος; (“Who is this?”, 21:10) answers 2:3, where Herod ἐταράχθη καὶ πᾶσα Ἱεροσόλυμα (“was troubled, and all Jerusalem”) at the news of a King [T]. The crowd’s answer, ὁ προφήτης Ἰησοῦς (“the prophet Jesus”, 21:11), is true and too small: the reader knows he is the King of 21:5 [I].
  • The last healing in the book is in the temple. καὶ προσῆλθον αὐτῷ τυφλοὶ καὶ χωλοὶ ἐν τῷ ἱερῷ, καὶ ἐθεράπευσεν αὐτούς (“and the blind and the lame came to him in the temple, and he healed them”, 21:14) [T]. θεραπεύω (“to heal”) stands in 16 verses of Matthew, from 4:23 to 21:14, and 21:14 is the last (SBLGNT) [T], verified. The healing ministry ends where it began to be summarised, with τυφλοί (“blind”) and χωλοί (“lame”) of 11:5 [T]. The verse is Matthew’s alone (Mark 11 and Luke 19 lack it) [T]. See Tool 11 on 2 Sam 5:8.
  • “The wonderful things” and the children’s cry. The chief priests and scribes see τὰ θαυμάσια ἃ ἐποίησεν (“the wonderful things that he had done”, 21:15) [T]. θαυμάσιος (“wonderful”) occurs here only in the whole New Testament (SBLGNT) [T], verified. Its root returns six verses later in the Ps 118 quotation: ἔστιν θαυμαστὴ ἐν ὀφθαλμοῖς ἡμῶν (“it is marvellous in our eyes”, 21:42), the only θαυμαστός (“marvellous”) in Matthew [T], verified. The leaders see the θαυμάσια (“wonderful things”) and ἠγανάκτησαν (“became indignant”, 21:15) [T]. Synthetic: moderate.
  • The cry is a plea turned into praise. Ὡσαννά (“hosanna”, 21:9, 15) transliterates the Hebrew of Ps 118:25, הוֹשִׁיעָה נָּא (“save, please”) [T]. Swete translates it, σῶσον δή (“save now”, Ps 117:25) [T]. Matthew keeps the Hebrew sound, not the Greek gloss [T]. κράζω (“to cry out”) marks the scene: ἔκραζον (“they were crying out”, 21:9) and τοὺς κράζοντας (“those crying out”, 21:15) [T]. The same verb is used of the crowd’s οἱ δὲ περισσῶς ἔκραζον· Σταυρωθήτω (“but they kept crying out, ‘Let him be crucified’”, 27:23) and of Jesus’ last cry (27:50) [T], verified (κράζω in 12 verses, SBLGNT). The crowd of 21:9 is not said to be the crowd of 27:23 [T], and the link is verbal only [I], moderate.
  • “Have you never read?” οὐδέποτε ἀνέγνωτε (“have you never read?”, 21:16) is the first of two in this chapter (21:16, 42), and the phrase stands only there (SBLGNT) [T], verified. Earlier the form was οὐκ ἀνέγνωτε (“have you not read?”, 12:3, 5; 19:4; 22:31) [T]. The sharper adverb comes in the temple [I], moderate.

Synoptic check. Mark has one colt and no formula quotation (Mark 11:1–7); Matthew adds 21:4–5 and the second animal [T]. Mark’s crowd cries Εὐλογημένη ἡ ἐρχομένη βασιλεία τοῦ πατρὸς ἡμῶν Δαυίδ (“blessed is the coming kingdom of our father David”, Mark 11:10); Matthew puts the title on Jesus, Ὡσαννὰ τῷ υἱῷ Δαυίδ (“Hosanna to the Son of David”, 21:9) [T]. In Mark, Jesus looks round the temple, leaves, curses the fig tree and cleanses the temple the next day (Mark 11:11–17); Matthew joins entry and cleansing on one day and moves the fig tree after it [T]. Matthew omits Mark’s πᾶσιν τοῖς ἔθνεσιν (“for all the nations”, Mark 11:17; Luke 19:46 also lacks it) [T]. Matthew alone has the shaken city (21:10–11), the blind and the lame (21:14), the children and Ps 8 (21:15–16) [T]. Luke’s Pharisees ask him to rebuke the disciples (Luke 19:39–40); in Matthew it is the chief priests and scribes, and the objectors are answered from Ps 8 [T].

Tool 11.

Isa 62:11 + Zech 9:9 → 21:5 (high — formula quotation). Source context: Isa 62 is the herald’s announcement to Zion after the exile. The LORD has proclaimed “to the end of the earth”: εἴπατε τῇ θυγατρὶ Σειών (“say to the daughter of Zion”, Isa 62:11 Swete), “behold, your salvation comes” (Hebrew הִנֵּה יִשְׁעֵךְ בָּא, “behold, your salvation comes”; Swete ὁ σωτήρ σοι παραγέγονεν, “your saviour has come”). The people will be called “the holy people, the redeemed of the LORD” (62:12) [T]. Zech 9 begins with judgement on the nations around Israel (9:1–8). Then the King comes to Zion “righteous and saved/saving, afflicted and riding on a donkey” (9:9). He cuts off the chariot and the war-horse and speaks peace to the nations, and his rule runs “from sea to sea” (9:10). The next verse is “because of the blood of your covenant, I have set your prisoners free” (9:11) [T]. The register is a summons to rejoice. Book usage: the ninth of ten narrator formulae (1:22 … 27:9) [T]. Isaiah is the book’s spine; Zechariah is concentrated in 21–27 (overview). The nearest earlier formula, 12:18–21 (Isa 42:1–4), also presents a figure who does not use force: οὐκ ἐρίσει οὐδὲ κραυγάσει (“he will not quarrel or cry out”, 12:19) [T]; the gentle King follows the quiet Servant [I], moderate. OT-to-OT: Zech 9:9’s rider on a donkey recalls Gen 49:10–11, Judah’s ruler “binding his donkey (עִירֹה,”his colt“) to the vine” [I], moderate. What it adds: (1) the omitted words of Isa 62:11 (“your salvation comes”) and of Zech 9:9 (“saving”) are the meaning of the name Ἰησοῦς (“Jesus”) given at 1:21, αὐτὸς γὰρ σώσει (“for he will save”) [I], moderate. The narrator does not need to quote the word: the King who comes is named for it. (2) Zech 9:11’s “blood of your covenant” is the phrase that returns at 26:28 [I], moderate (the overview has Exod 24:8 there). Nearest text: the Hebrew for the animals (υἱόν, “son”, for בֶּן, “son of”); the Greek for πραΰς (“gentle”) and for ὑποζυγίου (“beast of burden”, Swete ὑποζύγιον). Both Swete and Rahlfs read the same [T]. Divergence class: the NT’s own Greek, drawing on both.

Ps 118:25–26 → 21:9 (high — quotation in the crowd’s mouth). Source context: Ps 118 is a thanksgiving liturgy at the temple gates. The king, hard pressed by “all nations” (118:10–12), has been rescued. He asks “open to me the gates of righteousness” (118:19). The stone the builders rejected has become the head of the corner (118:22). The festal cry follows, “save, please … prosper, please” (118:25), and the priests answer, “blessed is he who comes in the name of the LORD; we bless you from the house of the LORD” (118:26). Then “bind the festal sacrifice with cords to the horns of the altar” (118:27) [T]. Book usage: Ps 118 is cited three times: 21:9, 21:42 and 23:39 [T]. ὁ ἐρχόμενος (“the one who comes”) recalls John’s question, Σὺ εἶ ὁ ἐρχόμενος; (“Are you the one who is coming?”, 11:3) [T]; phrase ὁ ἐρχόμενος (“the one who comes”) at 11:3; 21:9; 23:39 only (SBLGNT), verified. OT-to-OT: the psalm’s “stone” verse (118:22) is the one Jesus quotes later in the same chapter (21:42) [T]. What it adds: the crowd speaks the psalm’s entry liturgy, and the gate leads to the temple, where the next scene happens [I]. The same psalm supplies the cornerstone (21:42) and the condition of seeing him again (23:39). Nearest text: εὐλογημένος ὁ ἐρχόμενος ἐν ὀνόματι κυρίου (“blessed is he who comes in the name of the Lord”) is Swete Ps 117:26 verbatim; ὡσαννά (“hosanna”) transliterates the Hebrew [T].

Isa 56:7 + Jer 7:11 → 21:13 (high — γέγραπται, “it is written”). Source context: Isa 56:1–8 opens the last part of Isaiah. It promises that the foreigner and the eunuch who keep the Sabbath and hold fast the covenant will be brought to “my holy mountain”, their sacrifices accepted, “for my house shall be called a house of prayer for all peoples” (56:7); the LORD “gathers the outcasts of Israel” (56:8) [T]. Jer 7 is the temple sermon. Judah trusts “the temple of the LORD, the temple of the LORD” (7:4) while stealing, murdering and committing adultery, and then stands in the house, “Has this house, which is called by my name, become a cave of robbers (מְעָרַת פָּרִצִים,”a cave of violent ones“) in your eyes?” (7:11; lemmas 4631, 6530 gated). The LORD points to Shiloh: what he did there, he will do to this house (7:12–14) [T]. The register moves from welcome (Isaiah) to sentence (Jeremiah). Book usage: γέγραπται (“it is written”) is the seventh of nine (2:5; 4:4, 6, 7, 10; 11:10; 21:13; 26:24, 31) [T]. OT-to-OT: Jeremiah’s Shiloh argument is the logic Jesus will speak in 23:38 and 24:2: the house is left, and no stone left on stone [I], moderate–high. What it adds: the quotation’s Jeremiah half carries the destruction of the house. Matthew cuts Isaiah’s “for all the peoples” [T]. Whatever the reason [I], the welcome of the excluded is enacted in the next verse instead: the blind and the lame come in (21:14) [I], moderate. Nearest text: Swete Isa 56:7 and Jer 7:11 (σπήλαιον λῃστῶν, “cave of robbers”, Rahlfs the same) [T]. Divergence class: translation loss — פָּרִיץ (“violent man”) becomes λῃστής (“robber, bandit”); λῃστής (“robber”) returns at 26:55 and at 27:38, 44, where two λῃσταί (“robbers”) are crucified with him [T], verified.

Ps 8:3 → 21:16 (high — “have you never read?”). Source context: Ps 8 is a hymn to the Creator’s name “in all the earth” (8:2, 10). God founds strength “from the mouth of babes and infants” against his enemies (8:3), then the psalmist asks “what is man … the son of man”, whom God crowned and under whose feet he put all things (8:5–7) [T]. Book usage: Ps 8 returns at 22:44, where Matthew’s ὑποκάτω τῶν ποδῶν σου (“under your feet”) is Ps 8:7’s wording (see unit 43). OT-to-OT: Ps 8:5’s “son of man” given dominion stands beside Dan 7:13 in the book’s Son of Man language [I], uncertain for Matthew. What it adds: the infants’ praise silences “the enemy and the avenger” (8:3); the leaders who object are cast in that role [I]. Nearest text: Swete and Rahlfs verbatim, αἶνον (“praise”). The WLC has עֹז (“strength”; lemma 5797 gated) [T]. Divergence class: substantive — the Greek’s “praise” is the reading the argument needs; the overview already records this.

2 Sam 5:8 → 21:14 (moderate–high — new; not in the overview or the audit). Source context: David takes Jerusalem from the Jebusites, who had taunted that “the blind and the lame” would keep him out. David says that whoever strikes the Jebusites must reach “the lame and the blind, whom David’s soul hates”. Hence the saying: Τυφλοὶ καὶ χωλοὶ οὐκ εἰσελεύσονται εἰς οἶκον Κυρίου (“the blind and the lame shall not enter the house of the Lord”, 2 Sam 5:8 Swete; Rahlfs the same) [T]. The WLC ends עִוֵּר וּפִסֵּחַ לֹא יָבוֹא אֶל־הַבָּיִת (“blind and lame shall not come into the house”; lemmas 5787, 6455, 1004 gated) [T]. The next verses: David dwells in the stronghold and calls it “the city of David” (5:9) [T]. Evidence: τυφλοὶ καὶ χωλοί (“blind and lame”) in that order, nominative, occurs in Swete only at 2 Sam 5:8 [T], verified (positive control: the search returns the verse itself). Matthew uses the same pair in the same order, with ἐν τῷ ἱερῷ (“in the temple”), in a scene where the Son of David has just entered David’s city [T]. Divergence class: substantive in the Greek — “the house of the Lord” is the Greek’s; the Hebrew has only “the house”. So the temple exclusion that Matthew’s scene reverses is explicit only in the Greek [T]/[I]. What it adds: the son of David opens the house that the founding saying of David’s city closed to the blind and the lame [I], moderate–high. This is Matthean material (no Synoptic parallel) and sits inside a Davidic frame two verses either side (21:9, 15) [T]. Not yet audited; recommend for the next audit queue.

Internal: Answers §2:2–3 — the King (βασιλεύς, “king”, 2:2 → 21:5) and the troubled city (2:3 → 21:10) (moderate–high; the overview’s Echo Table). Answers §5:5; §11:29 — πραΰς (“gentle”) (high, verified). Answers §11:3 — ὁ ἐρχόμενος (“the one who comes”) (high, verified). Answers §11:5 — τυφλοί (“blind”) and χωλοί (“lame”) (moderate; the last θεραπεύω (“to heal”), verified).
